A Note on The Bottleneck Reader

This isn’t a “book” per se, but a complimentary set of essays which dives a bit deeper into some of the subject matter covered in the Reality Blind series, and which is still a work in progress. These essays are brief snapshots and commentary on the huge amount of material that comprises a synthesis of state-of-the-art scientific disciplines in 2019. It’s meant to give you the flavor and implications of some of this material as opposed to the bare facts. We hope you enjoy it, understand it, and are moved by it.

NJ Hagens DJ White 8/21/2019

An Introduction to Reality 101

The way things have been the last several hundred years is not the way they have been for the bulk of the human past, nor will be for the bulk of human future. You exist in a near-stroboscopic blip of time in which humanity is churning through millions of years of resources in a one-time pulse. This has ramifications both wonderful and terrible, and we should probably make ourselves aware of them if we are to make self-awareness actually good for anything.

This course is called Reality 101, where we will attempt to present a more seamless and coherent worldview, synthesized by combining many disparate branches of science, culture, and the human condition. These branches are normally thought of separately.

How are the deep past and the deep future relevant to what we do today and tomorrow? How does energy relate to money, money relate to dopamine, dopamine relate to sexual signaling and sexual signaling to the environment? How is happiness related to possessions? Why and how was it beneficial for us to evolve blindness to many aspects of reality? Indeed, what is a human, and what is life? Which key things that our society believes just aren’t so? How is human civilization like a foraging bear? What do humans really eat? What do the expert economists get totally wrong? Do you know the personalities of your invisible slaves? What does “renewable energy” really mean? Are your genes your friends? Do you have a clear idea where your virtual world in your mind ends and the physical world begins? Do you think about how your brain works, and why it does what it does? Why are the big problems of human civilization not being solved? How is human thinking predictably biased? What can we know for sure about the future? And how are these things linked to success and happiness? What would success even be?

You may find this to be a bit of an odd class. In addition to teaching some new connections, it will attempt to teach a new way of thinking and analyzing the world around you, as well as your own inner world. Of knowing yourself, your society, and your species a bit better for what you are, as a way of helping you decide what you’d like to be. To make your own decisions about what success will look like.

It may be a peek into the future, because in our opinion this way of thinking should be taught to students much younger than yourselves, in parallel with all other subjects. However, that hasn’t been done yet, so you may find some things in this class surprising. We all have some things to un-learn.

Connecting all these subjects is not so much a complex thing as an unusual thing. Humans in current society don’t, for the most part, think this way. We are taught subjects quite separately and tend to remember them that way. That’s a good way to become a well-programmed member of a highly social society, but it isn’t very creative. And even though you seldom get graded on it, creativity is more important than we often give it credit for; it’s the difference between competence and brilliance, rote memory versus deep understanding. The really important answers tend not to fall entirely within a single subject or area; they occur when you train yourself to think across subjects and disciplines, letting your mind fit the pieces together. My hope for this class is that it helps you be a sophisticated generalist, with a good - even if hazy - visualization of how everything really fits together. Because everything does fit together. The physical world itself exists as an integrated whole, with everything happening at once. The demarcations we make between areas of knowledge are pretty arbitrary, and our knowledge and very ways of thinking are imperfect by their nature. Once you know how things fit together, have that mental model, you can always find the details by using the internet, or just figuring them out. Conversely, if a person knows a lot of specialized things but has no feel for how they fit together, they may develop isolated concepts and activities which may or may not be useful. Having an integrated mental model is crucial. For instance, if you have a smart phone you’re probably fairly good at using it, and know what tricks it can do, how to add functionality. You probably also know an older person who wants nothing to do with a smart phone because it won’t do tricks for them. The difference is the mental model. You build a virtual model in your head of what and how your smart phone works, and once you’ve done that you can interact with it fluently. Without that mental model, a smart phone is just an inert piece of material. Yet we graduate students out of college without much of a mental model of how the world works. Despite the fact that “how the world works” may be the most important thing to for students (and citizens) to know. Indeed, many of our most famous societal “experts” have expertise in just one narrow area and may have few clues outside that area. We have high hopes for this experimental class. Experimental in the sense that it’s never been taught until recently, and you’re the test subjects. It’s our hope and intention that you benefit greatly from taking this class, both immediately and for the rest of your life. But the reframing of knowledge, the teaching of an altered paradigm for thinking about the world, does need to come with a few caveats.

This synthesis brings clarity to many otherwise-disparate areas of human knowledge – but it also comes with a downside – it also brings those likely human futures into much clearer focus. Some people might consider this a blessing while others see it as a curse, because knowledge can strike sparks against belief, and as members of a social society we tend to believe many things that the people around us do, by default. Many of those things will be shown to be incorrect. And looking at our own species as an evolving thermodynamic system will make clear things that are both exciting and sobering, for we are coming up against natural limits, and that will have human implications. In this way, the information to be covered in this class is existentially challenging, but the human condition has always faced existential challenges of one sort or another which required living humans to rise to them. It’s possible to live a full and happy life while engaging with challenges. However, there’s a psychological adjustment to make when learning Reality 101, and that has to do with the tapestry of pre-existing expectations and beliefs about the future which we have soaked up from the cultural narratives we exist within. We call this the “Christmas pony” effect. If a child comes to mistakenly believe they’re getting a pony for Christmas, they are likely to be disappointed when receiving anything less than a pony, even what they get is something very nice, like a train set or microscope. If their Christmas is to be saved, the imaginary pony needs to be taken to the imaginary glue factory well in advance of the holiday itself. Indeed, this situation foreshadows many of the things we’ll discuss: the roles of belief, energy, and the future. Physical prospects for the future will be the same after you take this class as before, but your own awareness and perspective will have shifted. The degree to which you find this exhilarating or dispiriting will have a lot to do with your current belief in those metaphorical Christmas ponies. And you may have unexamined beliefs you don’t realize you’re carrying around with you coming into this semester. In this class we’ll talk about the limits imposed on reality by physical law and probability, and how to think about the future in a more systematic way than people usually do, as a chaotic system playing out within these strongly bounded limits. Limits which can in principle be known beforehand. And we’ll extend this understanding back to the dawn of life on earth and forward to its end, a direction which is inexorable, but with results that are never inevitable. We’ll describe those hard limits as the bottle of life’s existence, inside which the chaos of contingent paths plays out. And we’ll discuss how energy and resource limits cause the bottle to expand and contract, creating “bottleneck events” which are difficult for life’s complexity to pass through, in the deep past, the deep future, and within our lifetimes. What creates bottleneck events, and to what extent can they be

controlled or anticipated? And if a species like ours can learn to anticipate such events, what practical and ethical questions might arise? The blind dance of evolution has brought us from single-celled bacteria to self-aware personhood, but it has also given us a lot of excess baggage. What things that we think and do originate from this mindless genetic agenda, and what things are truly the product of our conscious reflective minds? Exploring this question will take a lot of twists and turns, into the story of how evolution can - and must - design a brain. And it raises the seldom-asked question of loyalty to the backwards-facing agenda of the gene versus the agendas of empathy and self-aware minds. It turns out that understanding the way we think is utterly necessary to understand and engage in the world. A big part of that is appreciating the difference between our virtual worlds and the physical world. None of us will ever experience the physical world except indirectly. It’s not, even remotely, what we perceive it to be, any more than the picture on an HD TV resembles the stream of digital data which went into the back of the set. The virtual worlds which run as structured information streams within our physical brains are the only reality we can know, and they are not like the physical world, nor would we want them to be. Rather, the realities we each experience are an interface for physical reality, an interface designed by millions of years of backwards-looking evolution to execute successful adaptations to past problems. Brilliantly adapted with pre-programmed hopes, fears, and priorities for a tribal hunter-gatherer existence that we no longer live within. The things which mean the most to us exist within our virtual worlds; and a certain amount of illogic and even delusionality is necessary for our sanity and happiness. Yet unless we are self-aware enough to realize that vital things lie outside our virtual worlds, we are a species not only approaching physical limits, but doing so blindly, and while speeding. Our species has called itself homo sapiens, wise man. As you’ll see in this course, we have not earned that quite yet (more apt perhaps is pan-ignis – “fire ape”). But we may yet earn the title of Sapiens, and if we do it will be because we have understood the way things work in the world and come up with a way for our species to last another million years or more on an intact planet. That’s not the way things seem to be going now. Yet it is still eminently possible. The same evolved biases and fallacies which cause us to make mistakes can also lead us to (erroneously) feel there’s no hope, despite many wonderful things still being possible. Things to try to do that you may think of, that your teachers haven’t. Tag, you’re it. This course is about building blocks - not so much to use in succeeding in a career as about deciding for yourself what success might mean to you, in the context of the

society and world you’ll live in these coming 50 or 70 years. It’s a time period whose events will profoundly determine the shape of life on earth for the next half-billion years, and not incidentally, the total number of human childhoods which will exist in deep time. You’re young, you’re smart, and you have incredible resources at your disposal. Your generation also faces challenges never before faced by any species evolved on earth. There are paths forward which lead to wonderful futures, and paths forward we should avoid. Figuring out which are which is probably a good idea. This century will be a time for creativity, engagement, and heroism. It will also, judged by historical norms, probably feature a lot of strife and weirdness. We hope to give you some tools to help choose a good path. Welcome to Reality 101.

Reality World vs Virtual World

What is commonly referred to as “virtual reality” is just a slight electronic boost to the intensely-distorted virtual reality we already live in. This course is about learning to see past the goggles you were born with, and unlearning some of the incorrect stuff you have picked up from your culture. You’re evolved to disbelieve the things you’re about to be exposed to here. In fact, it’s not an overstatement to say that this course represents a “perfect storm” of unbelievability. The information in this course: - clashes with most of your evolved programming - will create dissonance with many of your beliefs about the world and about yourself - reframes the way you think about your own thinking and conclusions - disagrees with what those around you think and say, and much of what you’re learning in other courses (especially economics) - disagrees with most of your cultural programming - disagrees with conventional expertise and conclusions based on most reductionist science - leads inevitably to conclusions which your mind is pre-programmed to avoid - deals with the future as a real thing - points out that your genes can give bad advice, and aren’t your little friends - draws a hard line in the sand between the virtual and physical worlds - is not socially or politically “correct”

Your other courses teach you things that you don’t know yet. This course will teach you things your species and culture don’t know yet, that the experts running the human world don’t yet know, placing you squarely in a cognitive vanguard which you could well find uncomfortable. After taking it, you may never again see the systems and human activities around you in the same way. Along with the explanatory beauty of this synthesis comes an inability to completely hide from certain logical conclusions. This is a good thing, and necessary for the species to learn, but self-knowledge is a very adult thing, and self-awareness of your species on this level is something inherently new. Until now, the information necessary for this synthesis simply didn’t exist the way it now does. This synthesis is not difficult so much as it is somewhat alien to the default way humans and our cultures now think. Or rather, its difficulty lies in the fact that it requires thinking in ways which are somewhat alien to most of us. We’ll tip our hat to that reality by actually having a (hypothetical, of course) Alien offering color commentary along with the basic presentation. As we go forward, we’ll talk about the Gene Agenda which not only predetermines specific biases, errors, and blind spots in our perceptions, but which is enforced by constant ongoing behavioral rewards and penalties from within the brain itself, which functions to prevent people from diverging from default human thinking. Of course, genes don’t have an “agenda” per se; they’re molecules. However, the attrition of a billion years of gene replication has produced a mindless default “agenda” which shapes human behavior and is very hard to deviate from. But the first big thing to understand is Virtual Reality. You’re familiar with that as a term used with immersive video games. But there’s a lot more to it: the entirety of every human’s perception is virtual reality. We inhabit a virtual “user interface” for the physical world, which is generated within, and by, the tissues of our physical brains. The world we experience is a construct, like a computer user interface which has us click on screen icons instead of directly manipulating binary code in intricate ways. Imagine a black panther, with iridescent green dragon-fly wings swooping in an azure ocean bay grabbing a barracuda with its large panther talons. If you’re like most people you never have heard this particular word combination before, but instantly upon reading it there was an image in your brain of such an imaginary panther. Communicating in this way to others, and anticipating their visualizations and reactions, is central to human experience. But it is purely virtual. When Worlds Collide Our virtual world is only very loosely connected with the physical world. This is a good thing, because the physical world as it actually exists is nothing you’d want to

directly experience, or really even could experience. The version of it inside your head which has 3D depth, color, sensation, feeling, meaning, associations and emotional connections is no more like the physical world than the graphic icons on a computer screen are like lines of binary code or the electrons rushing in and out of billions of transistors billions of times per second. Your sensory inputs are filtered for salience, subjected to logarithmic compression, processed with pattern-seeking algorithms, and scattered to hundreds of always-on modules which are forever outside the sphere of your consciousness. They generate outputs which are assembled into the seamless illusory world that you subjectively experience. And it really is a matter of two qualitatively different worlds: the virtual worlds of human experience, and the physical world. We can each only ever experience our own virtual world and universe; in which meaning, love, satisfaction, music, poetry, challenge, exhilaration, pain, magic stories, contentment and everything else we value plays out. Yet those virtual worlds are critically dependent on the physical world, which obeys entirely different rules. It’s easy to get them confused, and – as we’ll show – with potentially dangerous consequences. Sorting that out is a basic part of this course. As you’ll see, we’re “evolved to be wrong” – often spectacularly wrong - in ways which helped our ancestors survive. Yet we now exist in a context in which we can no longer afford to be spectacularly wrong. Our virtual worlds not only suppress uncomfortable realities, but also overestimate the unpleasantness of anticipated negative events. Most people who lived during the great depression (not in cities) were unaware America was in a Great Depression.1 People living through World War II felt alive and full of purpose despite rationing, curfews and less personal freedom –the fear of ‘being at war’ was offset by collaborating for the greater good and working closely with others on similar team. Research on the neuroscience of ‘dread’ show that people who are afraid of an upcoming event, are willing to actually endure a larger amount of known, advertised pain, in lieu of a smaller, unscheduled variety of pain.2 In essence, our virtual reality can play tricks on us, in both directions.

Culture Medium

Virtual reality is the main software interface provided by the gene agenda. Part of that “gene agenda” we’re born with is a nearly-irresistible need to agree with and conform to those around us, using our beliefs to establish and strengthen who’s in our “ingroup” and who’s not. This means our genetic evolutionary baggage isn’t the end of this virtual confusion, it’s just the beginning, because we take our cues from those around us, and cultural evolution iterates at warp speed compared with gene evolution. It turns out that organizing cultures which allow us to exist relatively

peacefully in large numbers require extremely durable shared beliefs in what are in many ways arbitrary and even nonsensical narratives. The upshot of all this virtual complexity is that we currently do a pretty poor job of understanding physical reality. We tend to use social rules (heuristics) and inappropriate metaphor to model the real physical world, as though we could bend its rules by the force of our beliefs and desires or replace them entirely due to dissatisfaction with their implications. Indeed, just laying out an outline of the ways we think, and think incorrectly, will take up a good slug of the course time. And it may be among the most valuable things you learn. Ever. Yet that’s just the first step: because understanding why the course material, and conclusions, are unbelievable is necessary to make learning about them possible: to take a step away from the gene agenda and consider the ramifications of reality. Your authors have definitely come to their own conclusions about the nature of the human experience, its economy, its ultimate basis and its attainable futures. But these conclusions won’t be taught, because it won’t be necessary: anyone who comes to understand their own mind’s working, and some very basic science about evolutionary psychology, belief, and the rules of the physical universe, will inevitably come to overlapping conclusions, if not the same ones. Yours is the first generation being exposed to this sort of synthesis. It implies stuff which will seem like good news and stuff that will seem like bad news, when contrasted against prevailing cultural beliefs and narratives. A fair number of students who have taken earlier versions of this class have altered their life plans based on what they learned. (note to students: as you can see, we’re using the term “virtual reality” in a way which is accurate, but which is different than the way the term is used in current culture. You may as well get used to this, as we will be re-purposing a lot of terms as well as introducing new ones).

A Probabilistic View of the Future

We have a dysfunctional relationship with the future. There are some things we know about the future. For instance, we can be extremely certain that 200 years from now there will not be a 600-foot marble and granite statue of a grizzly bear in Minnesota. How can we know such a thing? It certainly could happen; there is nothing precluding it in terms of energy or technology. Yet there would be insufficient motivation for humans to do such a thing: to get the permits, to organize around that particular mania, to see it through to fruition... so we can assert with confidence that the future will not contain a 600-foot grizzly bear. And by extension, there exists a large class of other things frequently brought up and discussed in our society which are 600-foot grizzly bears in terms of the incredibly unlikely sequences of events which would be necessary in order to make them a part of the future. Humans tend to be blind to this. Most of our actions are prioritized in favor of the Now. Psychologists call this “hyperbolic discounting” of the future, and it’s a part of what we'll refer to as time blindness. By “time blindness” we refer to the fact that humans don’t think much about the future beyond a narrow, near-term span of time, don’t think about it clearly, and are emotionally detached from it as though it was an imaginary place peopled by

nonentities who don’t matter. To the extent we think about the future at all, we tend to think about it as remote, inexorable and inevitable. This is largely because we are aware of our own physical deaths and have adopted strategies to avoid thinking about the future to quell cognitive dissonance. When we do think about the future, we are subject to an optimism bias when it comes to what we decide to expect. Doing so optimizes real-time happiness. Lottery tickets are a bad investment when considered by probability but a good investment in terms of (temporarily) maintaining fantasy futures. We generally expect our personal future to be better, our family's future to be better, and our world's future to be better. But this doesn't make it true. This is a shame on several levels. Personal success is highly correlated with the ability to defer rewards in time, to be kind to our future selves rather than locking them into predicaments which lower their life quality. But beyond that, we are part of an incredible deep-time story of life, awareness, discovery and recently-dawning wisdom. That story will continue some non-predetermined distance into deep time as we move forward, influenced by your life and its decisions, and the shape of that story should matter to us. We think about the future in a vague way when we think of it at all. We sometimes assume it as utterly unknowable, while at other times we think of it in terms of specific cultural narratives that tend to be relatively certain - even if they conflict with one another. If we look at popular movies, it seems that we’ll either have a Star Trek future with flying cars and universal wealth, or a dystopian apocalypse of some kind. It paradoxically seems like anything can happen, but that we’re just swept along in the current towards some predetermined end. But it’s not true that anything can happen, nor is it the case that the end states are predetermined based on what humans currently know. What we don’t appreciate, most of the time, is the extreme level of contingency which directs our actions and guides our individual fates. The consequences of small happenstances plot a course of eventualities that rapidly diverge from one another. On the day of your conception, any chance movement, a single sneeze by your mother, might have altered which of 100 million sperm fertilized the egg, and whether you would exist. That sneeze, in turn, might have been triggered by a single mote of inhaled dust, whose motion in arriving there was significantly affected by the random Brownian motion of individual atoms. The reality we experience, which seems so solid, has propagated in a nearly impossible way from among nearly infinite possibilities. A dog’s bite, a horse’s whim, a random encounter by an ancestor, a

chance illness by someone would have otherwise altered the event line which became present day. The world around us is the sum result of these accidents. But these circumstances play out in a "bottle." A bottle which represents the bounds of possibility and probability which are set by physical laws and the nature of reality. Gravity will continue to exist. The sun will continue to radiate energy for billions of years. Chemistry will work as it always has. Thermodynamics will continue. We know these things about the future absolutely. The chaotic dance of contingent outcomes is bounded by the impossible and the unfeasibly improbable. It is impossible to predict where a person alive today will be on her 60th birthday, but it will not be on Mars. Mars is outside the bottle. There may or may not be a hurricane in Honolulu next August 30, but if so, it will not have winds of 400mph; such winds are outside the bottle. If you’re on the peak of Mount Everest and trip, there are many possible directions for falling down, but you won’t fall up. If you seal a monkey into a metal cage, it can be impossible to know exactly where in the cage the monkey will be in a year, but it will be inside the cage. So, the future is a mixture of nearly-impossible contingent paths that are impossible to predict far in advance, and these paths are strongly bounded by what is physically and probabilistically possible. Thus, there’s a mixture of certainties and unknowables, and we can know a lot about which are which, and we should. We know that in many ways the future will be reliably like the past. The laws of nature have existed since the universe’s creation and will continue to be reliable. This means that physics and chemistry, energy and thermodynamic disorder will continue to follow the same rules. Gravity, heat, light, energy flows, chemistry... all will behave predictably. So, for instance, we can know that if a wolf on an island needs to survive by eating rabbits, and the rabbits disappear, the wolf will not survive. This doesn’t tell us exactly what the wolf will do in the meanwhile, and its behaviors might be very complex. But without food, it is absolutely predictable that the wolf will starve. So, the wolf has aspects which are both unpredictable and aspects which are entirely predictable. In addition, some things are far more likely than others, because the paths to them are more numerous. The atoms in the air around you are mixed randomly, with about 78% nitrogen and 21% oxygen.3 There is nothing preventing all the oxygen molecules from moving to the ceiling, causing you to pass out. However, that would require very special and improbable things happening, because there are a lot more paths to a well-mixed

bunch of molecules than there are to all the oxygen near the ceiling. It’s so unlikely that one could watch a room for the entire age of the universe without it happening. So even though it’s not impossible for it to happen, the chance is probabilistically negligible, and you don’t need to worry about it. So the future consists of two elements: a slowly evolving “bottle” which contains those things which are possible to attain, and the chaotic dance of happenstance within that bottle. The shape of the first is highly knowable; the second is unpredictable except as a matter of greater and lesser probabilities. Indeed, the best way of thinking about the future is not as a fixed destiny we are traveling towards, but as a set of shifting probabilities. The probabilities of future events gain greater or lesser probability based on what happens in the now, which immediately becomes the fixed past. If Lincoln is assassinated, he will not pursue his plans for reuniting the nation. If dodo birds go extinct because they’re fun for sailors to hit with sticks, all futures contain no dodo birds. Future events inside the bottle are highly contingent on things which alter initial conditions in such a way. This is also true in the case where things are not precluded, but are made less likely. If you commit a felony, your odds of being president are lessened. If you lose fingers in an accident, it will be harder for you to play piano. If 90% of sea turtles disappear due to fisheries, it is more likely the species will become ecologically extinct due to other stressors. These days, weather is forecast probabilistically. That is, you’ll see on the weather report that there’s a 20% chance of rain tomorrow and 35% the next day. This is better than the way they used to do it 50 years ago, when they’d say it would be clear tomorrow and have a 20% chance of being wrong. It is more useful to know the odds of a future occurrence than to have a misplaced sense of certainty. Similarly, without knowing you, we can say that if you’re a pretty normal person, we know the rough odds on how you’ll die. You have roughly a 1 in 6 chance of it being heart disease, 1 in 7 of it being cancer, 1 in 28 of it being a stroke, 1 in 88 of it being in a vehicle accident, 1 in 112 of it being suicide, and so on.4 Yet you can alter these odds. Your 1 chance in 770 of dying on your motorcycle is reduced to 0% if you decide never to ride a motorcycle. If you stay healthy and exercise, you probably alter your odds of heart disease, cancer, and stroke for the better. So instead of sitting in front of the TV all day becoming morbidly obese by eating canisters of Pringles and drinking Mountain Dew, you can eat healthily, take daily walks, avoid tobacco, and many other such things. What you are doing is dynamically altering the odds of negative futures by being conscious of contingent

futures. It’s never certain - healthy people can still be struck by lightning - but it’s the smart way to plan. And it’s the smart way of looking at wider boundary futures too: the futures of your friends, family, nation, species, and living world. The far future seems utterly remote, irrelevant, and scary to us. If asking what a person thinks about what the world will be like in a million years, they are likely to note that the sun will burn up our planet in a billion years, so it doesn't matter. Yet the entire story of complex life is far less than a billion years long, and it is entirely reasonable for a species to last a million years. We are innumerate when it comes to the scale, scope, and shape of possible futures, and shouldn't be. These larger futures are no more visible to you than your own, but they too exist as ranges of contingent possibilities. These are constantly in a state of flux, as various future outcomes become more and less likely based on what we do, and things that happen, in the now. The status of earth's future and life's future is utterly contingent upon the things which happen causally to affect the possible outcomes. These are many, varied, and complex. Some systems important to life are chaotic individually and in aggregate; there is no way to know exactly what will happen. To the extent we are willing to contemplate futures that are less desirable than our hoped-for future, we tend to conflate all futures which seem subjectively depressing to fungible equivalence. Thus, a future in which the stock market goes to zero seems about as bad as one in which the oceans die. However, that doesn't make it true. With some mental effort we can imagine (and plan for) many less-than-perfect futures which would be preferable to others. So when we hear scary things about admittedly scary topics, like ocean acidification, global heating, or oil depletion, these events are not yet certain and will play out in the walls of the bottle – physically, and in reaction to changing conditions of the events that impact them. Ultimately, we can know the shape of what can happen and what can't because we use science and logic and inference to understand the walls of the bottle and path dependence. In sum, the future is best thought of probabilistically. Some future outcomes are highly probable, and others are highly improbable. The odds change constantly depending on what is done in real time. You, reading this, can and will change those odds to a very small, or not so small degree.

The Glorious Story of Life on Earth

Most of our universe’s history, and a lot of its existing neighborhoods, are hostile to life. Early in the universe there was just too much radiation zapping things for life to develop. Near the center of galaxies even today, there are so many supernovas, radiation bursts, black hole x-ray beams and other such things that life would be extinguished pretty quickly after starting. So for a very long time, life was only possible in the distant future, and only there in the quieter neighborhoods in the far reaches of mature galaxies. Even once the energy-zapping quiets down, one needs stars to go through multiple lives and deaths to create heavy elements in supernovas. The heavy stuff we’re made of can only be created in exploding stars. That takes a while. Billions of years. Once you get a cloud of gas with enough heavy elements in it from previous supernova explosions, gravity pulls it together in a spiral, a star forms at the center and solid planets can coalesce around it. But this too is a pretty dangerous place, because after slow accretion, the growing proto-planets whack into each other and get repeatedly melted and pulverized. No life there. Yet the way they get melted, smashed, and pulverized turns out to make a huge difference in what sort of life – if any - might be able to exist in that star system millions of years in the future. The series of accidents which becomes ‘frozen’ into a fixed past determines what can happen. Every collision that happened to our proto-planet became a necessity to the future of life that we see around us now. In planetary formation, some collisions create moons, others bring water, still others bring the heat and metals for a future

magnetic field. At this point, life is still not a good bet to occur. There are innumerably more ways for it to go wrong than go right. Once this bombardment settles and a planet cools down, it still probably won’t have life. It’ll be a gas giant with high temperatures and crushing pressures and frequent asteroid strikes, or have no atmosphere at all, or be way too hot or cold for liquid water due to distance from the star. Or it will be just the right size, composition and distance but not have huge outer planets to suck up most of the ongoing comet and asteroid collisions which would frequently boil the oceans. Even if a planet forms at roughly the right distance from its star, as Earth did; at roughly the right size; as Earth is, and roughly the right atmospheric chemical composition, it still needs a real fine-tuning to look anything like the planet we find ourselves on. Early in its accretion, our planet was nearly blasted in half by collision with a mars-sized object that divided earth into a binary world. Having a relatively large nearby moon - the only one in the solar system - drastically slowed our planet’s rotation through tidal friction with the mantle and seas and stabilized its axial tilt and thus its future climatic variations. It did so while leaving the heavy nickel-iron core largely intact, making possible a magnetic field lasting billions of years to shield the earth’s surface from the electrically charged solar wind, which would otherwise strip away the atmosphere. And the mantle currents created by this circulating metallic core also created plate tectonics to stabilize the CO2 levels via feedback from rock weathering. The random rain of comets onto the surface of the cooling world left just enough water to cover some, but not all of the planet’s surface; had this been otherwise, the plate tectonic thermostat would have been unable to function, and the planet would have veered out of a habitable temperature range. What life needs is special conditions, stability and time. The phenomenon of life is in many ways similar to a whirlwind; matter dancing in a pattern as energy dissipates through it. Self–replicating life is a lot harder to get started than a whirlwind because it’s so much more complex, even in its simplest forms. But once it acquires the ability to replicate, it makes up for this initial improbability by being potentially far more durable than a whirlwind. Further steps – each improbable on their own - can add to this durability. It seems that simple life began almost as soon as the earth had cooled enough to make it possible. That implies that simple life happens fairly easily in the scheme of things IF the special conditions are met. It also implies that life probably began multiple times as our planet was cooling but was sterilized out of existence by large asteroid impacts boiling the seas. We’re descended from the life which began after the last impact-boiling of the seas, as is all other life we know about.

It might seem like earth life was home free after getting to that unlikely point in the game. Nope. The universe makes life possible but doesn’t cut it any slack. Life needs a lot of things to “go right” for it to continue. It needs usable energy sources and nutrients, protection from radiation, a fairly narrow band of temperatures, a lack of toxic compounds, a liveable range of acidity, and much else. It has been a popular recent conception (James Lovelock) that life automatically made the earth into “Gaia”, a pan-species super-organism which acts to nurture life; in effect, life changing the planet to make it continually better for itself. A comforting notion. And humans have a soft spot for comforting narratives like a “mother nature” which strives toward a paradisiacal end. However, one can make at least as good a case for the opposite conclusion: that life tends to run itself off a cliff. In point of fact, it does both things. Sometimes the random dance of living interactions tends to create or enhance more stable conditions, and sometimes it upsets the whole game board. This is because evolution, by its nature, only looks backwards. It passes on the adaptations which led to survival in the past, sort of like a lobotomized zombie bus driver who doesn’t care if he crashes and whose only view is the rearview mirror. Physical life has no intentions; there is no ultimate goal; it’s a relatively complex process but utterly automatic: it doesn’t care where it’s going or where it ends up. It’s just something that happens due to the rules governing energy and matter, like a rock rolling down a hill or a raindrop falling from the sky. So while there have been processes which have tended to stabilize the earth’s environment, such as a large moon and a plate tectonic thermostat to keep atmospheric CO2 levels roughly stable (and the ocean at a livable pH), there have also been phenomena left over from the primordial solar system bombardment to periodically stir things up, like huge volcanic eruptions and occasional asteroid impacts. Life itself gets into the act of messing things up. The development of photosynthesis is something we appreciate today, but at the time it was apocalyptic, because oxygen was a toxic gas to most life which originally existed on earth. Oxygen pollution nearly wiped out all this original life as it changed the chemistry and mineralogy of the planet’s surface, atmosphere, and oceans. And by drawing out so much CO2 and methane from the air and replacing it with free oxygen, it helped cause the earth to freeze into a snowball from poles to equator for huge spans of time, during which periods our simple & tiny ancestors probably only survived near deep-ocean vents beneath miles of ice, independent of photosynthesis. Only the volcanoes eventually

melted this frozen world by slowly outgassing more CO2. Yet, when those volcanoes got widespread and active, in turn, they also wiped out life by overheating the planet and acidifying the seas, again with CO2, which is the main actor in atmospheric heat retention. When the planet heats from too much CO2 and associated gases, the poles warm far more than the equator. This leads to a drastic lessening between the temperature of the poles and equator, and that means that the energy flows which drive ocean currents and atmospheric wind diminish, and they slow or stop. The oceans become acidified, stratified and anoxic, and the primordial life driven from the surface by the oxygen of photosynthesis has its own unconscious revenge, creating toxic H2S hydrogen sulfide gas which kills off critters that breathe oxygen.

(Source) There has been a somewhat fractal pattern of extinction events of greater and lesser severity in the deep time since life started, but five planetary mass extinctions stand out. These are extreme events - a mass extinction in which 50% or more of species are lost means that every last individual of that species dies out. If a species almost dies out, it’s not counted even if most of its members do. These near-miss events avoided extinction but were certainly bottlenecks for much of large complex life of the day. The common theme to these planet-wide extinction events is CO2 and methane heating and its follow-on effects like anoxic oceans and H2S poisoning. (until recently

it seemed that the last event, which famously featured an asteroid or comet whacking the earth and killing the dinosaurs, was the exception; but it now seems that a CO2 heating event had begun 150k years prior to the asteroid’s arrival). So, it seems a CO2-driven marine mass extinction was well underway, and earth ecosystems especially vulnerable, when the asteroid fell. The point of this narrative is that as durable and adaptable as life can be, its course has been absolutely determined by many catastrophes and a mix of good and bad luck. From mishaps large and small, 99.9% of all species to ever exist are now extinct. We humans might logically be a fan of these past catastrophes, because we inherited our chance at existence only because prior life was killed off so many times, leaving vacant ecological niches. Yet we’d better also learn (quickly) the lessons clearly written in rocks, fossils, and ice. It has been only a few decades that these lessons have been known at all, and only now is the scientific community starting to appreciate them. To the average human, they remain fairy tales. One of the lessons we should heed is that life in general is durable and quite resilient. Given time and opportunity life grabs hold and persists. However, the kind of life which is durable is usually simple life which reproduces wildly and quickly. Bacteria, algae, scum, slime and muck. Worms, jellyfish, and their ilk. Even once the sun boils the oceans away in the future – and it will – bacteria in deep rocks may well survive an additional billion years or more. But is that really what we care about when we think of life? One thing we perhaps fail to appreciate is that most life is, has been, and always will be simple, single-celled life. Long after complex life is gone, the bacteria and algae will exist on earth. Bacteria and pond scum have not all “turned into” squirrels and rabbits and left their single-celled ways behind. Rather, large complex life is a specialization which can exist – temporarily -when conditions are exceptionally stable and benign, with only slow and gradual environmental changes. The reason that’s an important point is that WE are some of the largest and most complex life, and the other species we need to exist are mostly ALSO large complex life. That’s a dimension we seldom think about when it comes to mass extinctions. Like, the largest terrestrial animal to come through the KT bottleneck of 65mya weighed about 2 pounds. All mammals, including us, are evolved from critters smaller than that. If nothing else, that should make us less sanguine about future changes. Here’s the math: big, long-lived, slow reproducing animals die off when earth undergoes rapid changes. The faster the change, the smaller the size of the complex critters that make it through. They only exist in very special conditions. Large complex life is fragile, pond scum isn’t.

So special conditions, stability and luck turn out to be what determines the existence, and molds the shape, of life. Life seems common to us, because in our experience we’ve found it almost everywhere on the planet. But, now that we’re starting to look off-earth, we don’t see it anywhere. There’s a whole lot of “nobody home” in the universe around us, near as we can tell so far. And the more we learn, the more that makes sense, because so much has to go improbably right for life to exist and persevere. Since our universe is so unbelievably ginormous, there’s almost certainly other life somewhere. However, large complex life may be very, very, rare; since a whole slew of things have to happen just right for it to occur, evolve and remain. Single-celled life probably comes into existence reasonably often if a planet has liquid water; we can infer this since it seems to have begun here about as soon as our planet had cooled enough. But it took billions of years for complex life to form from simple life; which means that the likelihood of it happening was so low that it well might not have happened at all before our sun toasted the planet. Some events, like the chance hybridization of two utterly dissimilar single-celled organisms which became a chimerical organism with a nucleus and mitochondria, seemingly happened only once, in billions of years of single-celled organisms existing in the seas. Without that accident, no animals, no us. But the rarest thing of all is one of the tricks that chimera’s descendants can do: make a brain. And - jumping ahead in the semester a bit - given many millions of years of the right random accidents, a big enough brain can - it turns out - generate a virtual world. Virtual worlds contain thoughts, feelings, qualities, meaning, love, and understanding. They can also, at least potentially, do another trick: steer futures by looking ahead in the physical world. Yet as the most complex aspect of a tall house of complex cards, these virtual worlds – conscious minds - are the most fragile emergent creation of life’s quickening, a relatively recent occurrence on a planet well into middle age. Species in Earth’s history which acquired the ability to reproduce wildly have always done so, resulting in overshoot and dieback, and sometimes resulting in crashing the planetary system and scrubbing out large complex life. There has never been a creature we know of which looked very far into the future to inform its species behavior in the now or had the capacity to research and comprehend the glorious story of life on earth. But a creature like that is probably a good idea. Any candidates? SOME SUMMARY POINTS:

Conditions have to be met for life to have a chance at starting and surviving. It probably happens only relatively rarely that life forms on a planet and is given billions of years to continue. -To the best of our knowledge, life started only once, about 3.8bya. All life we have ever encountered is descended from that chance event. All life on earth is not only related to us, but closely related. We share more than half our DNA with mushrooms. They are our siblings. - It happened nearly as soon as the world had cooled off enough, so the formation of simple life may be relatively common in the universe. - Single-celled life is the main story of life. Multiple-celled life is not the result of a progression, but the expansion of life into broader niches. To a good first approximation, (almost) all life is single-celled, always has been and always will be. - Single-celled life came up with many innovations and continues to. The evolution of photosynthesis tapped a huge direct power source which changed earth's surface chemistry and made possible extensive biomass powered by the sun. Life has always been about tapping energy flows to create organized structure in opposition to entropy. It always will be, though we forget this. - Large complex life was created by symbiotic associations of single-celled animals; this was the origin of chloroplasts in plants and mitochondria in animals. Each independent critter kept its own separate DNA, but they came to be hybrid organisms, making possible the energy flows that make large plants and animals (respectively) possible. It took a long time after life began for mitochondria (and thus animals) to emerge; and it may be a nearly impossible occurrence in the universe. But without it, nervous systems, muscles, rapid movement, senses and brains could not have come into being. - No one way of organizing complex life is necessarily any better than any other. In the Cambrian explosion, a huge diversity of different forms of life emerged. The survivors were largely selected by random chance, the luck of timing and sequence as the environment and life danced with each other. If the movie were to be played again with only minor changes, life on earth might look very different. - Our lineage traces back through worms, fish, reptiles, mammals, primates, monkeys, apes. Each new species was an unlikely response to competition and environmental change.

Geology and paleontology gives us true, non-speculative answers about what happened in the past; and the picture sharpens with time as we read this story. Examining what happened on earth in the past is a powerful window on how it may react in the future - The long-term story of complex life is steered as much by catastrophe as by stability. ~99.9% of all species ever to live are now extinct (or speciated). - Many families and species perished in 5 planetary mass extinction events; and we have created a 6th. - The first things to go in a mass extinction are the long-lived, large, complex creatures high on food chains with low reproductive rates. (yes, we are in this category) - These events were abrupt in geologic terms but extremely slow in human terms - Science is now converging that the main driver of the past 5 mass extinctions was CO2 levels SOME PERSPECTIVE/ LOOKING AHEAD TO NEXT SECTIONS (Reality 101 students: below are some implications (and conclusions) that derive from the above, which include aspects you have not yet been exposed to - we will discuss most of as the semester unfolds. Don’t worry about grasping the complexity of this synthesis yet, but in the back of your mind, put some sticky notes.) - When we arrived on the scene as human, it was into a world of fantastic biological wealth. We are a part of that, and it is a part of us. We have always defined ourselves by the other large complex species we interact with. - We have also more often than not killed off the large complex species we interact with. Our niche as modern humans has been to not be satisfied by a single niche. As we exhaust each niche, we try opening another. - Very late in the story of life came self-awareness, consciousness. It took 3.7by for life to wake up and think about itself abstractly, and to begin to understand the universe. Yet the strong evolved drives which guided life before abstract thought was possible left little leeway for a self-aware mind to explore. - Mankind's cleverness at opening new niches finally tapped the dead remains of fossil life from earlier eras. This grew human biomass by an order of magnitude and granted

a bolus of temporary energy wealth, with which humans created industrial society run by the energy of long-dead organisms. This enabled us to reach out and take anything we wanted, leading, mostly out of our direct sight, to mass extinctions. It became clear that our continued actions would also heat the biosphere and acidify the oceans, leading to planetary conditions unlike those which allowed us to evolve. Locked into the compelling agenda of reacting in accordance with the agenda of the gene, we have decided en masse not to worry much about it. - No future civilization on Earth, human or otherwise, will ever experience such a pulse of industrialization, because the geological and biological concentrations of non-renewable resources are being scattered to the winds (and into space), leaving only lower quality concentrations. Future civilizations may additionally have to get by on a planet with unstable climate, slime oceans, and natural energy flows from a diminished global ecosystem. - But a step as important as the origination of chloroplasts or mitochondria is now being developed and tested: the emergence of self-aware minds as a force which can separate themselves from the unconscious agenda of the gene. Early results of the test scores suggest starkly failing grades. At stake is the future of large complex life which now exists, and all conscious beings. Whether consciousness and self-awareness would evolve again, after a human/carbon cataclysm, would be constrained in many ways, and involve not a little luck. In evolutionary history, high intelligence has not been a strong attractor. Due to the controlling contingent influence of initial conditions, this century, and what we do during it, will to a huge extent determine the fate of complex life on earth for the rest of earth's history. A future without dolphins, elephants or owls means all futures will be lacking those creatures. - The ability of the brain to rationalize and deny, in furtherance of temporary gratification and immediate reward, is huge. Most people never aspire to do anything but exist and react. Yet these brains are all the experiment of complex life has to work with. The fate of all conscious evolved life of earth now hinges on the way cave-man brains understand and respond to the real world.

The Web of Life on Earth

“We are here because one odd group of fishes had a peculiar fin anatomy that could transform into legs for terrestrial creatures; because the earth never froze entirely

during an ice age; because a small and tenuous species, arising in Africa a quarter of a million years ago, has managed, so far, to survive by hook and by crook. We may

yearn for a ‘higher answer’– but none exists.” - Stephen Jay Gould

Earth’s life has been evolving for nearly 4 billion years. It has occupied niches from the small and simple to the large and complex and everything in between. During the last several hundred million years, our planet’s tree of life has spread into myriad wonderful and fascinating trunks, branches and twigs. From the prehistoric-looking denizens living near the bottom of the ocean to hive-like mole rats in the arctic, from the 80,000 plant species in the Amazon to thousands of species found within an inch of dirt in your backyard, scientists have catalogued that we share this planet with nearly countless other species of creatures – the conventional estimate being 8.3 million (- yet since so many are small creatures in the soil and in the oceans, some scientists estimate Earth holds as many as a trillion unique species).5,6 Humans share

enormous DNA commonality with every one of earths living organisms– almost 99% with chimpanzees and bonobos, some 90% with cows, 80% with cats, 70% with mice, 60% with fruit flies, and even 50% of our DNA is the same as found in a banana7,8,9,10! It is a profound truism that we share a common ancestry (and a current Earth environment) with a huge number of other living creatures. KEY POINT: We are genetically related to every living thing on Earth

The Universe Doesn’t Care – And neither does Evolution

How did this all happen? Was our human shape, form and behavior the long range ‘objective’ of evolutionary processes? Or did new phylogenetic scaffolding build on top of existing structures in ad hoc ways as the pulse of life expanded outward to new niches and opportunities, reacting to changing contexts? We humans think and communicate in stories, because stories are easier to both process and remember. We’ll get around to discussing that, and its implications, later in the course. But right now, in OUR story we’re in the chapter about deep time and evolution. These are possibly the greatest stories ever told, and we (as a species) are understanding their details pretty well these days. Which is why it’s important to mention that our culture tends to get aspects of these stories wrong in peculiar (and important) ways. The birth and evolution of our universe, and the subsequent birth and evolution of life on earth, are easy to romanticize as a majestic series of events designed to bring us - or something very much like us - into existence. Astronomical and geological evolution, and subsequently biological evolution, are just a logical continuum: a “march of progress” from inanimate matter to microbes to mammals. But instead the common story is that the universe has played out its destiny in seeking the crown of creation: the hairless social fire apes, a.k.a. humans. It can certainly look that way… that there was an intentionality and purpose behind our seemingly meteoric ascent. That’s what happened, right? Here we are, reading and writing and communicating magnificent stories in all our glory, the dominant creature on a beautiful, rich planet. But this narrative is simplified, misguided, and ultimately a bit dangerous. A gene doesn't care if it survives any more than a raindrop cares if it

falls. They are each just bunches of chemicals obeying the laws of physics, chemistry and biology. Evolution and its products are an accumulation of billions (or trillions) of events which just happened to work out a certain way. Our penchant to retroactively imbue this process with direction and intentionality is part of our natural pull towards comforting and complete “teleological” stories. Teleology is a belief that events have reasons for happening, and that the ‘universe’ seeks out desired end states. A teleological story is one which imbues false purpose and directionality. Oxygen exists so humans have something to breathe: an apple falls from a tree because it wants to, tornados are attracted to trailer parks, and (from the perspective of a dog) humans exist to throw Frisbees and provide snacks –are all examples of teleological thinking, which demands that there be a final cause or purpose. That evolution occurred in order to produce us, or that the universe exists to produce life, are similar such stories.

The universe doesn’t actually care about life. It has no brain to care with. It has no preference for life, nor does it cut it any slack. This is important to realize, and it fits together with the fact that life itself sometimes makes a world better for life, and sometimes causes life to be wiped out. The universe’s only big favor to life is that it doesn’t preclude it. Most parts of the current universe are utterly inimical to life. The entire universe was prohibitively hostile to life in the deep past and will be for the huge majority of the deep-time future. There’s a tiny window of time in which the

universe has stars, light, heat, energy flows and elements in the right proportions and proximity to - rarely - allow conditions which make possible the emergence of life. If and when that happens, it is barely possible that a preposterously unlikely series of events will make possible highly energetic life with something like brains, and then barely possible that those brains will encounter circumstances under which evolution causes them to grow large enough to think abstract thoughts. And then after some time the universe will kill them again, with no more prejudice than when it allowed their creation. Life will only be possible for a very small part of this universe’s duration. Earth has had life on it for nearly 4 billion years.11 It has had simple brains of one sort or another since worms developed them around 550 million years ago (mya), so even those first brains took a while.12 An unbelievably long while. Brains didn’t automatically evolve to get bigger and smarter, because brains are costly in resource and energy terms. It took the next half-billion years to evolve something as smart as a chicken. Only fairly recently, and as a result of a number of re-sets due to mass extinction bottleneck events, did the mammals get a chance at becoming large, with a few of them growing larger, more complex brains. Only VERY recently (as far as we know) has the world seen things like elephants and whales and apes with large brains which are self-aware and capable of complex abstraction. These exist ONLY in a small subset of quite large mammals, which wouldn’t exist at all if not for the utterly random crash of an asteroid into an enormous fossil oil reservoir 65 mya, which finally cleared the non-avian dinosaurs out of the big-critter niches. If not for that random crash, abstract thoughts like you are reading wouldn’t exist, and mammals would still likely be small bug-eaters skulking in the dinosaurs’ shadows. There is pretty much NOTHING more random than two enormous rocks banging together after 13.8 billion years of gravitational interaction. Yet upon that chance (and many others) rested the existence of large mammalian brains, nerve-dense neocortices, a perception of the universe and self, and Honors courses. Evolution is the result of the mechanism of natural selection, in which traits which allow an organism to survive and reproduce are passed on and proliferate in a population. If we understand that the universe doesn’t care, it’s a short step to grasping that evolution doesn’t care either. Far from evolution seeking us out, it has staggered in a random drunkard's walk for 3.8 billion years with no more intentionality than the steel ball in a playerless pinball game, until it accidentally hit upon our combination of traits, and we accidentally woke up. If we disappear, the odds are high that nothing much like us will evolve again. Evolution is clueless, mindless, directionless, and not even smart enough to be stupid. So how about a non-romanticized example of evolution: take a new box of Raisin Bran and shake it. You’ll observe that the longer you shake it, the more the raisins concentrate near the bottom. The smaller and more spherical raisins get there first,

followed by the larger spherical raisins, followed by the large irregular raisins followed by the small partly mashed weird-shaped raisins that the raisin-bran people get bargain rates on because they're weird-looking. You’ve just simulated evolution. That's not even a metaphor, that's actual fitness selection, in which the arbitrarily-designated "fitness" of some raisins got them to the bottom of the box (analogous to the “today” of evolution) while other raisins were left behind. In that who-gives-a-damn context, "raisin-race fitness" is determined by size, density, relative sphericity, surface wrinkles, and microscopic texture enabling van der Waals force and friction, as well as the specific shape and size context of that exact assemblage of rodent excreta, bug legs, and whatever else found its way into the bran-flake machine. One could develop a cosmology around the "bottom of the box" being a preferred location, but preferred by who? Not the raisins. And this is exactly what genes do. They aren’t trying to replicate into the future. They aren’t trying to do anything. Some of them move more successfully through the aggregate "stuff happens" energy-pumped physical-world phase space than others, and others don’t. That's it. Rinse and repeat, and ta-daaa! Here we are 3.8 billion years later, the raisins in the planetary box - lucky as hell to exist, serial lottery winners all of us (and, ironically, betting it all on the next lottery ticket, as though wild luck were a dependable part of our inherent nature). Evolution has no goal. Its about which adaptations survive, and brains can just as easily evolve to get smaller (and stupider?) as they can to get larger and smarter. One human species, Homo floresiensis, apparently did just that, as they emigrated to an island ecosystem, natural selection favored smaller and less energy intense bodies (and brains).13 Interestingly, our OWN brains have gotten about 10% smaller in the last 10-20k years, as we have moved toward deeper and more complex social networks, offloading individual processing and specialization to our “cultural cloud.14 If that trend continues for another 10k years, we’ll be back to the brain size of a homo erectus and still shrinking (so study while you can!). Evolution is not a “march of progress.” The notion that evolution has a direction, and that we're "it," is a teleological fallacy. Funny word, but one worth learning, because it's a fallacy we humans fall into very often, along with hundreds of others, many of which we’ll learn about in the weeks ahead. KEY POINT: Evolution is a process without a destination or purpose.

From Fitness Maximizers to Adaptation Executors

What causes the things we do to feel good (or feel bad)? Is it random, or are we responding to some software in our brains that we (humans) all share? It turns out that the hurdles and bottlenecks that our great grancestors x1016 generations– all the way back through other primates, other mammals and simpler organisms– successfully navigated, have a direct bearing on what we do, what we like and what we strive for during our own lives. Why does something feel bad or good to us at all? It’s because the ancestors who “felt good” about doing things which happened to enhance their relative fitness at that time survived to pass on these tendencies and the behavioral rewards inherent in their particular brain structure. Sex feels great. Eating high-energy-content food feels great. Being a high-status tribal member feels great. Hating outgroups feels great. And killing large prey (and outgroup members during wartime) feels great. This is, to some of us, such an uncomfortable thing to hear that it feels incorrect. Our ability to recognize the way our own brains function is limited because our conscious minds can access only the output results of the more-powerful brain regions which influence us, and not the processes they use to arrive at those results. If we look back through deep time, the organisms that were shaped and sculpted to what they look (and act) like today, did so via the relentless gauntlet of ‘relative fitness’. In almost all animal generations there is born a surplus of offspring relative to what the ecosystem can provide for. Either food and water scarcity, or predation, or disease, or accidents mean that the number of organisms (insects, fish, animals, what have you) that live to die of old age is a very small percent of the total. It is largely the beneficial genetic mutations (and chance), causing small advantages during the organism’s life that allow incrementally better survival (and incrementally more success at mating and producing offspring). Relative fitness quite simply is the survival and/or reproductive rate of a genotype (genetic make-up) or phenotype (observable traits) relative to the maximum survival and/or reproductive rate of other genotypes in the population. It is from this theme that the (in)famous phrase by Richard Dawkins ‘The Selfish Gene’ emanated.15 He didn’t mean that there was a gene for selfishness, (or that genes themselves gave a damn) but rather that whatever happened during an organism’s life that advanced its survival/mating success relative to others of its species meant that its genes (or genotype) would be more highly represented as a % of total genes in the next generation than they were in the generation when it was born. This means that evolution is about ‘fitness maximization’ when looking backwards (which is the only direction evolution can be seen/experienced: fitness exists only in

retrospect). We (and our dogs, and the spiders in our basements and the coyotes in our backyards, etc.) are products of a long series of ‘what worked better’ in each particular generation of that species that came before, multiplied and stacked on top of each other. How would this really work? Let’s say there are 1,000,000 generations of history in what led to a biologically modern coyote. In generation #536,488 there was an extreme famine, and only a few of coyotes with the most efficient metabolisms made it through the coyote bottleneck of that era. Later, in generation #697,793, there was a series of hard winters, and only the coyotes with the mutation to grow thicker fur when the weather turned cold survived. And in generation #945,400, there was a disease found in mice that would transmit itself into the coyote gut and lead to fatal diarrhea and illness – only the few coyotes that were immune to this bacteria made it through. If we come to generation 1,000,000 – today – all living coyotes will: a) have extremely efficient metabolisms, b) grow thick fur when it is cold outside and c) be resistant to that certain form of mice-originating bacteria – even though these coyotes might live in a warmer climate, with plenty of food, and no mice. This is because these coyote genotypes made it through the various bottlenecks in their evolutionary history – these traits (for now) are ‘locked in’ the coyote genotype – irrespective of what the current environmental conditions might be: hot/cold, plentiful vs little food, etc. A quick note on relative vs. absolute fitness. If, in a coyote generation, ALL coyotes survived and reproduced healthy litters of 8 coyote puppies who all live full lives, reproduce themselves and die of old age, the absolute fitness (how many survived) of these coyotes was very high, and the differential reproduction of their parents was zero. They all survived and bred. Relative fitness only comes into play when something new or unique (or lucky) allows one individual or line to have differential survivability/mating success. This can occur when the population is huge, or, in some cases, very small, allowing only a few individuals through some environmental bottleneck. Seen through the backward lens of deep time, it is primarily the accretion products of relative fitness that have shaped and sculpted the living things we see around us today. Let’s move from coyotes to dogs. Dogs, originally from wolf stock, have been artificially selected for various traits for companionship or work for humans. I (Nate) have a golden retriever, Quinn. If I throw a ball for him, he ecstatically will run and fetch it back to me – for hundreds of times in a row if I keep throwing it. His ancestors who were more fired up to retrieve birds or game were selected by their owners to breed. Fitness, in the historic golden retriever sense, was artificially maintained by whatever traits the dog’s owners preferred. When Quinn sees a ball or Frisbee thrown,

his neural response is one of joy – as if this is what he was born to do – in his case, this is pretty close to the truth. His breed has a series of historical adaptations that make his behavioral response retrieving, as opposed to digging, or fighting, naturally pleasing and rewarding. So, let’s move to humans. All of us alive today are the product of a long series of historical environments and bottlenecks in which certain genotypes survived and mated. When we look back at this long series of lives lived and babies born, we know that in each particular generation, those who had a slight advantage in survival and mating were more likely to be our ancestors than those without those preferences or traits. In fact, many in the pre- and post-hominid populations did not breed at all, and hence are not our ancestors. We are, all of us reading these words, quite simply, descended from the best of the best (or perhaps more correctly the luckiest of the luckiest) at surviving to be old enough to mate, and successfully rearing offspring. Looking back at this history, ours is a long series of ‘relative fitness’ in each sequential trial (life). But, (and here is the important punchline) we do not think about maximizing fitness during our own lifetimes. We do not go through life calculating the odds of each situation to improve our survival chances. We do not use each encounter with the opposite sex to produce more offspring. (though the goal of some of these encounters is to go through those motions). Although we sometimes appear to be pursuing fitness on behalf of our genes, we really are just executing our evolved neural programs that were shaped in environments of our ancestors in ways that give us the same ‘feelings’ (read: neurotransmitters, hormones, endocrine cascades, etc) that our ancestral blueprint locked in as they were successful at surviving and passing on their templates through past bottlenecks. Organisms (including humans) are adaptation executors, not fitness pursuers. We go through our lives pursuing activities in today’s world that are completely (in most cases, bizarrely) different than our great grancestors on the Tanzanian savannah – but obtaining similar feelings of pleasure, motivation, pain, and avoidance, etc. as they did under vastly different historical circumstances. As we’ll see in a few weeks, the activities leading to survival, higher status, sex and anything central to our ancestors achieving relative fitness in prior eras, ‘feel’ especially strong to us. One final note, with a bit wider boundary. Absolute fitness is the measure of an organism’s fitness independent of other individuals. If you’re a chicken, and you lay 1024 viable eggs in your life, your fitness was 1024. Relative fitness, on the other hand, is absolute fitness divided by mean fitness. Thus, if the mean fitness of the population was 982 eggs then the relative fitness would be 1024/982 = 1.04.

Absolute and relative fitness are most often relevant when discussing the ontogeny of a single organism. But species also compete with each other for resources, habitat and niches – therefore it also makes sense to discuss relative fitness between species. Every species to exist since the dawn of life has been fit enough to survive at that time and in that situation. Yet 99.8+% of those species are now gone, because they were eventually insufficiently fit relative to competitors and changing contexts as time and accidental events marched forward. For instance, a large part of earth’s current marine animal biomass are the pteropods (marine snails) known as sea butterflies, which evolved after the last major mass extinction (KT) and have been very fit in the conditions which have recently prevailed.16 However, the sea butterflies’ shells are now dissolving in high-latitude seas due to increasing CO2-caused acidity which causes their aragonite shells to fizz away. What was “fit” in the oceans of your grandparents may not be fit in the oceans of your grandchildren, because another 20-40 years of continued fossil fuel burning will (if it occurs) render aragonite chemically impossible for (at least) hundreds of thousands of years. This, in turn, will alter the relative fitness of animals which evolved to be dependent on pteropod-intensive food webs, compared to jellyfish or other ocean creatures that seek food unrelated to pteropods.17 KEY POINT: We don’t seek to maximize our fitness, we execute adaptations via doing things that feel good or “right” to do. THINKING AHEAD FURTHER IN THE SEMESTER So how does this relate to Reality 101? In this class I will oft suggest that it’s time to move towards a consciously-designed agenda of the self-aware mind and away from the agenda of the gene. But there really isn't an "agenda of the gene" any more than there's an "agenda of the raindrop" - it's a useful metaphor, and we largely communicate with one another in metaphors because there's no other way to quickly communicate a complex idea with language. The agenda of the gene simply means that while walking our own path, we gravitate towards the behaviors and actions that give us ‘feelings’ that our ancestors (on the road behind us that has disappeared) got as they successfully navigated survival and mating challenges on their own paths. That's one of the problems with following what "feels good" to do: those feelings were designed to feel ‘good’ by the same sort of process of attrition that gets some raisins to the bottom of a box before others. Again, not a metaphor, the same sort of process. We are by default relying on that process to pull a miracle out of a hat as we reproduce ourselves past the 10 billion mark ‘eating fossils,’ as though our genes have a plan.

Before we were born, our ancestral gene trajectory functioned to make the maximum numbers of copies of themselves. That was its sole distinguishing characteristic, which is as intention-free as the crystallizing of a snowflake. The agenda of the gene therefore pushes for the largest-possible biomass of human existence, while our quality of life is not even a consideration. This is important to realize. While our brains have evolved so we “feel better” when we do things that happened to help our ancestors survive, “feeling good” is not a design goal of any sort. Our genes will always strive towards 20 billion miserable humans on earth in lieu of 2 billion happy ones. Because being hungry, having (and making) babies, being fair to other in-group tribal members all occur in the same way that water flows downhill. That’s how the fitness game has always worked. Here’s the catch. Going along with the gene agenda feels good. Eating junk food, blaming outgroups, acquiring more and better possessions than our neighbors... all of this feels great. Pursuing a different agenda not only has no evolved deep-brain reward circuits; it runs afoul of evolved brain aversive systems. Going on a diet feels terrible. Loving our enemies feels messed-up.
