Living an austere life feels like a sacrifice. So, the behavioral dice are loaded in favor of the gene agenda, and that’s what we see writ large in the world around us. Our species may only make it to the trillionth human childhood if we start doing things which make logical sense, but don’t initially feel good to us. And how do we do that!? (we’ll come back to that question as the semester continues). Just as that shaken cereal box will wind up with the raisins on the bottom, the result of our population overshoot is as predictable as yeast replicating in a wine bottle. We are not going to evolve away from requiring food. Once we have used up the dead fossil stuff, we'll have to go back to eating the things we used to eat, and what’s available at that time will only be a fraction of what our hugely enlarged population needs. Yet there's a small "but." We have brains which can in principle understand where the agenda of the gene is heading to, and the broad shape of the inevitable results. It is not impossible that we could anticipate this and modify our behavior to achieve something new in the history of life: a forward-looking fitness based on understanding the physical world (hope for such ‘intelligent foresight’ is why I’m teaching this class and we’re writing this book). The problem - and it's a big problem - is that we are deeply programmed. Our brains evolved to get us to the bottom of the raisin bran box faster than the other raisins. All the things that make us "feel good" are about that journey. All the things we are pre-programmed to care passionately about are linked to our long-ago evolved reptilian limbic systems, while the abstractions of the "conscious" part of our neocortices have

no such deep links. We will gun each other down over road rage or being snubbed for a promotion, but the gradual loss of a natural world just causes us to blog. The mindless progress of life across the ages has created a world of incredible wonder and diversity. Our current economics consider this to have zero value, but in our minds we (most of us) realize otherwise. Swimming over a coral reef, walking in a rainforest with its sounds and smells, hiking a desert, we are surrounded by our deep-time kin, the other evolved species to have survived until now. They are not our enemies or our resources, they are our family; the things most closely related to us in a near-infinite universe, the things that have made the world we recognize. There’s so much we don’t know yet. How does an elephant, a dolphin, a macaw see the world? What does a 250-year-old bowhead whale think about, under a ceiling of ice, seeing with reflected sound, and thinking with a brain more than twice the size of ours? What really exists, unknown, in the oceans we’re dredging to rubble at the end of multi-mile-long steel cables? We are genetically adapted to ignore those things in favor of consumption and reproduction, like yeast in a wine bottle, and our brains come pre-loaded with rewards and disincentives weighted to make that continue to happen. But it hasn’t happened yet. And we are capable of learning and understanding the way our brains work... a central part of Reality 101. KEY POINT: Blindly following the ‘agenda of the gene’ leads to ecological problems

Section II - Human Behavior

The story of deep time is our story: we are a product of deep time, and both our physical and mental existence is inexplicable without understanding this. As we approach the year 2020, we finally have enough pieces of the puzzle to see the big picture, and it’s a picture which is quite unlike the stories we have previously told about ourselves. For we have arrived in a modern world with barely-altered ‘caveman' brains, optimized for small tribal survival and scarce resources on the African savannahs. Understanding how we think, and why we act in the ways we do individually and collectively, is a necessary step in the big synthesis. If we are to really know anything about the world around us, we need to know ourselves; to know where our minds might take us we need to understand where they’ve been and why.

Enter the Gene Agenda

Humans are special, but we are still mammals - and primates. Our brains and behaviors are products of what worked in our past just as much as our kidneys or feet are. In effect, we are ‘adaptation-executors’ that go through our lives trying to replicate the emotional states of our successful ancestors. Some interesting (and relevant) problems arise when the physical world we’ve described merges with humans trying to attain the same ‘feelings’ as our ancestors. To describe these challenges, may we present Mr. Gene Agenda (shown at left, walking forward with its eyes looking backward). The “gene agenda” we are referring to is symbolic of unconscious preferences and proclivities we are born with. Among them are: Status – We intensely care about relative status. In

a globalized modern culture with marketing, and advertising, we compete for resource intensive stuff. (Status can also be linked to respect, ethics, and community, instead of things!!) Supernormal stimuli – Modern technology can hijack our neural reward system, which was designed for much slower daily experiences. We can (and do) become addicted to the ‘unexpected reward’ of the next encounter, episode, or email, and at an ever-more-rapid pace Me vs Us, Us vs Them – We are products of competition against others for status and resources, but also of cooperation with others to meet shared challenges. We are intensely groupish. This is why social media today is about the ‘messenger’ rather than the message. Cognitive biases – We didn’t evolve to have a veridical view of our world. We think in words and images which are disconnected from our physical reality. This results in myriad cognitive biases. Rationality is the exception not the rule. Time Bias – We naturally – biologically - care about the present very much more than the future. We make promises to change ‘tomorrow’ until tomorrow becomes ‘today’. Our neocortex can imagine them, but we are emotionally blind to issues like climate change, sea level rise and biodiversity loss. Denihilism – Our challenges are: complex, in the future, abstract, scary, have no immediate answers, and few on TV or in politics are discussing these things. The interconnected challenges of the human predicament appear as the perfect storm for

the human brain. To resolve the disturbing dissonance the brain attempts to ignore or deny the evidence of the risk. The consensus trance-We use ineffective social sorting mechanisms to solve complex physical world problems. The above list is a short summary of our inherently programmed behavior tendencies – our ‘gene agenda’. Hardly any of us understand and admit that our evolutionary heritage is highly relevant to sustainability and upcoming cultural responses. Why? How did the gene agenda come about? Can we do a ‘work-around’ this agenda? Here’s a very brief summary: Of course, “genes” don’t have an agenda; they’re molecules. They care no more about whether you survive than a raindrop cares if it falls. But your genes have programmed an agenda: your “behavioral defaults” have been shaped by genes undergoing differential survival under harrowing circumstances in tens of millions of years of prehistory. This agenda exists for good reason: the de facto “gene agenda” greatly preceded the evolution of brains, and for that matter complex life. It’s a molecular mechanism to make sure that you are likely to make decisions, have motivations, and interact with the world as closely as possible to the way your ancestors did. It was created and shaped by their surviving and reproducing when those around them didn’t. Understanding that there’s something which can be usefully referred to as the “gene agenda” is a first step in grokking the systemic predicament that our species is facing. Having a brain means that one has some behavioral flexibility, and that can be valuable. But brains had to evolve as ‘add ons” to the gene agenda which already existed. If their flexibility departs greatly from the gene agenda, it is also departing from the actions and reactions which conferred survival upon one’s ancestors. Thus, the more potential autonomy conferred by an evolving brain, the more that autonomy must be held in check lest it diverge from crucial programming and die out. The way this is handled is that brains offer behavioral rewards and punishments deep in their core structure, to reward “gene agenda” actions and to deter actions which run counter to the gene agenda. The way we and other animals subjectively experience this is “good feelings” and “bad feelings.” Going along with the gene agenda feels great and is highly rewarded. Eating sugar, hurting enemies, agreeing with tribe-members, having sex, gaining social status, getting at least our tribal “fair share” of stuff, escaping predators, nursing babies and many other such actions; all are immediately rewarded in the specialized parts of the limbic system via neurotransmitters like dopamine, oxytocin and endorphins.

Actions, thoughts, which are NOT part of the gene agenda do NOT feel good in the same deep way, and generally run afoul of punishments. For instance, being hungry feels bad, being thirsty feels really bad. Letting yourself get injured HURTS. (Think how quickly you learned not to bite your tongue while eating). Going without sex feels bad; disagreeing with tribe-members feels bad. Having dissonant thoughts (inner conflicts with the gene agenda) feels REALLY unpleasant. This means that while conscious, self-aware animals like humans have some potential to depart from the gene agenda - and this has had survival value - it’s very difficult to do. New ideas arise in the neocortex, and the neocortex is forever disconnected from the deep reward systems which make things “feel” important to us. The gene agenda isn’t confined to the brain’s reward & punishment systems; it also derives from the fact that the world we experience is NOT the world in which we physically exist. We inhabit and experience a “user interface universe” (UIU) which is wholly generated by our physical brains using greatly-modified inputs from our senses. This UIU is infused with color, emotion, meaning, sound, love, hate, ambition, triumph, disgrace, pleasure and pain, good and evil. It corresponds to the physical world in ways that were useful in the genes’ past. However, it is no more an accurate representation of the physical world than the screen of your cell phone is “like” the billions of transistors and electrons obeying quantum rules in billions of computing cycles per second inside it. This “user interface universe” evolved just as your body did, for the reproductive success of genes, and it’s skewed in many ways toward survival and utility, and away from veridical (truthful) reflection of physical reality. For instance, your brain has hyperactive face-detection and will see faces in random patterns, because missing a real face has disproportionate consequences to imagining an unreal face. We also have hyperactive agency attribution, for much the same reason: we tend to infer malign intent and plots where none may exist. When you add that to a further list of inborn “gene agenda” cognitive fallacies, biases, and illusions as long as your arm, you wind up with a brain that is pretty loosely attached to what’s actually going on in the physical world. We’re biased to perceive our modern world in terms of small-tribe hunter-gatherer salience. This worked great for small tribes on the African Savannah, which is still what we’re mostly programmed to be; but we’re now in new territory. We get “emergent effects” from our various misperceptions which make it difficult to make good decisions about reality. As one example, the combination of face detection, agency attribution, stimulation via supernormal ideation, consensus belief and “thinking in words” has created tens of thousands of supernatural mythologies for which we have historically killed and died;

and with no relief in sight; from Aztec sun sacrifice to Easter Island “stone heads” to neoclassical economics. The common thread is that they feel good to believe and have organizing value to societies larger than 150 members, creating more competitively-formidable superorganisms (as our nations, societies, corporations, religions and alliances have turned out to be). But aren’t genes our friends? We wouldn’t be here without them! So do we owe some debt of loyalty to our genes? One occasionally hears such sentiments expressed. And that’s foolish, because genes aren’t our little friends. They program solely for reproductive success because that’s all evolution COULD program them for. This is usually accomplished at the cost of mortality and (in conscious species) misery, because its sole method is over-reproduction and attrition. We are, during a brief one-time period of energy excess, insulated to an unprecedented degree from this attrition; but it will begin a return to old Malthusian norms during your lifetime, as increasing physical-world “energetic remoteness” of resources forces us inexorably back toward steady-state existence. History is full of accidents which led to our existence. One might as well honor the Chicxulub meteor as honor their genes… especially since the meteor is no longer dangerous, while the gene agenda IS. Logically, we owe no debts to random accidents. Genes accidentally produced the consciousness we experience, and the virtual universes we experience it within, and will be perfectly “happy” to get rid of those things in the future if it helps reproduction. Already our brains have shrunk about 10-20% in the last 20k years as we have relied more on large groups and less on individual thinking, and our genes will be just fine if we evolve to have walnut-sized brains, get pregnant by age 4, and eat cockroaches, which is a far more robust ecological niche than long-lived brainy ape (and one better-suited to a changing, hotter world). Not caring much whether that happens is, again, “gene agenda”: it’s an abstract idea about the future with no way to reach our limbic systems. Pretty much ALL negative-feeling future possibilities are similarly discounted, even those which are more likely than not. For instance, most humans choose to believe that their conscious existence will not end when their bodies perish; so it’s perhaps no surprise we choose to believe all sorts of lesser dissonance-avoiding stories. And there again, relieving cognitive dissonance is “gene agenda”, because dissonance causes stress and stress challenges health and tribal functionality. We’re evolved not to be too happy or too sad, but to dwell in a metastable homeostasis in which we are maximally motivated by the brain’s deep evolved reward and aversion systems.

We’ve met the enemy, and he’s inside us So if there have been so many past benefits to Gene Agenda, what’s the problem? Well, the problems are many and we’re running into them all right about now, due to a couple centuries of fossil-energy-driven exponential growth hitting a lot of limits at about the same time. The short answer is that the very same “gene agenda” which brings a conscious species like ours into existence and power over nature, will - if it continues to be dominant - tend to wreck the world’s carrying capacity and environment, killing most of that species off and leaving it with a relatively grim future. Only if such a species can transition from “gene agenda” to a more sapient ecologically-aware agenda can that be avoided. That must, of course, be done before the damage is locked in. And so far, all popular narratives to the contrary, we aren’t doing that. We’re 99.9% Gene Agenda, and those humans (99.9% of them) connected to the global economic system are pedal to the metal in nearly every dire consumptive and destructive variable which can be graphed. To a good first approximation, we’re still nothing BUT gene agenda. So far, we have come a long way hitching a ride on what is essentially a runaway train; but without the wisdom to jump off before the crash it may all come to nothing. Thus, anyone who understands “Energy Primacy” (see next section) as the basis of life, and the broad outlines of a science-synthesis view of reality, must conclude that the “gene agenda” is something we need to try moving beyond, and quickly. It’s a full-speed train heading for a bridgeless chasm. The authors strongly suggest that humans attempt a sapient, ecologically-informed deep-time ethic of life so that the human experiment on earth can last a long time and involve a lot of happiness, discovery, and trillions of childhoods in the garden this planet can be. This is still possible but will NOT become likely until and unless we make it so, and it could easily become impossible during your lifespan due to the cumulative weight of irrevocable bad decisions. An agenda to die for… So to sum up, the problems with the Gene Agenda, as a plan for the human species going forward, include: - It is mindless, with no intentionality whatsoever. It not only cannot plan for the future, it has no mind to know there IS a future. It’s just replicating molecules which have accidentally made life and can just as easily unmake it.

It is antithetical to rational thought and reasonable ethics, fogging our collective minds with a “consensus trance” of selective reality-blindness. - It is deeply compelling, with roots in the limbic system of every brain which curtail and enslave our freedom of thought. It feels right and passionately important because it was designed to by millions of years’ attrition. Its rewards are blissful, and addictive and its punishments are immediate: it is hugely difficult and stressful to overtly depart from. - The gene agenda is the deepest “common denominator” of human behavior, shared by all. That means that behavior which “feels good” to the limbic system is ratified by the social approval of those around us, and seeking their approval is another part of the gene agenda. It will tend to dominate any consensus decision-making system and drive out rational analysis. - It will always strive for 20 billion starving, warring humans with destroyed ecosystems, rather than 2 billion happy peaceful humans with sustainable living practices. - Its reliance on the brain’s mirror-neuron system means we empathize with one suffering child, but are wired to ignore a million suffering children. One is “felt” as an individual, the other is “felt” as a concept with no strong roots. Thus, to run a world we need better empathy than the “gene agenda” is wired for. - The gene agenda causes us to heavily err towards uncritical belief in unrealistically-optimistic future outcomes. This is a spectacularly bad bit of agenda for a world-controlling species focused on short-term gain. - Not understanding energy, thermodynamics, or science, the Gene Agenda rewards us when we find “others” to hate and blame for what were entirely foreseeable problems, because it feels good and confers tribe solidarity to do so. In a species armed to the teeth with nuclear missiles and heading into a period of drastically declining resources with utterly no clue as to why, this is another dire default. If this “gene agenda” is so powerful, is it realistic to hope - even in principle - that it can be challenged? Yes. There are strategies we will recommend, and these have been proven to work, by us and some others. And even if that were not the case, there is little choice: we must find and employ an alternative, an “agenda of self-aware mind” which can in crucial applications supplant the ultimately-suicidal “feel good” agenda our brains came pre-programmed with. And it must happen quickly, during the next few decades of your lifetime.

We hope this brief introduction of the concept has helped you see that “Gene Agenda” exists and is at this point our species’ principal adversary. This is a new and original framing, but one with deep scientific roots and explanatory value which we hope you’ll adopt in your thinking. There are abundant ecological examples of species which overshoot the sustainable carrying capacity of an environment due to a temporary bolus of resources. This corrects itself in a “dieoff” phase during which the population stabilizes at a far lower level or is extinguished. It is emblematic of our “gene agenda” that we can know this to a scientific certainty yet be blissfully confident it doesn’t apply to us.

Delusional R Us

Part 1: Introduction Part 2: The Gift and Curse of Mind Modularity Part 3: The Power of Crazy Part 4: Delusional R Us: Conclusion

Part 1 - Introduction

“The most common of all follies is to believe passionately in the palpably not true. It is the chief occupation of mankind.” - H. L. Mencken

“Reasoning was designed by evolution to help us win arguments.” -Hugo Mercier and Dan Sperber

“We apply fight-or-flight reflexes not only to predators, but to data itself.” -Chris Mooney

DJ has a single, one-of-a-kind bumper sticker on his car: “Humans Are Delusional”, which may sum up a lot about our current predicament(s). It’s probably reasonable to say we don’t so much have an energy problem, a pollution problem, a population problem, a biodiversity problem, a resource problem, or a climate problem so much as a brain problem. Your authors Nate & DJ are delusional. They admit it. And by realizing it and beginning to understand the nature of this delusionality, they consider themselves

several steps ahead of where they were before they learned of it. They’re not particularly delusional relative to other members of their species (though that’s always open to debate!). Rather, they’re delusional simply because they’re homo sapiens: an evolved delusionality comes free with a human genome and upbringing. Clearly, we’re not talking about “delusionality” in the usual sense you hear it. Indeed, the usual definition of “delusional” is a person who considered to be self-deceiving or insane. We will instead show that there really isn’t even any such thing as self-deception, only an evolution-honed modularity of mind. And the definition of “insanity” is “not behaving in a normal manner”, so if the majority does it, it can’t be insane. So surely our use of “delusional” is no worse than that. We have been told many times that the word “delusional” is a terrible choice to use in these descriptions: it seems pejorative, negative, insulting to human nobility and achievement. But we keep coming back to it cause it’s just so darned accurate, and because a little metaphoric cold water splashed in our collective faces may not be a bad idea at this stage. So how are we re-tasking the word “delusional” for the purposes of waking our species up to smell the coffee? We’ll show that humans are delusional: - genetically, with a built-in landscape of biases and behavioral rewards/punishments skewed heavily toward the mindless “agenda of the gene” and away from logic and clear analysis; - due to an evolved mind module central to our consciousness which continually invents stories and makes us passionately believe in them; - culturally, with organizing beliefs based significantly around evolved mythologies of various types; - due to limited ability to deviate from the consensus beliefs of other humans we interact with; - in a reductionist manner due to the micro-division of expertise caused by ultrasociality; - in believing that narratives (stories) capture the essence of the real world; - relative to dealing with the real physical world, mis-using social sorting routines and heuristics to parse physical-world propositions and situations, confusing virtual-

world phenomena and rules with those of the physical world, and having selective blindness to many aspects of the real world; - as to the nature of our own minds and the way it thinks, with misplaced certainty in a very fallible process which is much more constrained than it feels; - relative to what we think we are; - relative to the scope and complexity of the predicaments we have worked ourselves into. And this is a very truncated list; we could easily have included a couple hundred bullet points. We’re Nate and DJ, and we’re delusional. As they say, the first step is admitting you have a problem.

Part 2 – Human Cognitive Biases – An Overview

In order to understand the human predicament, the most important thing to understand is the human. A key part of this book is an attempt to explain the particular handicaps of the human brain and why our blind spots and biases, in aggregate, make it difficult to plan and implement benign futures. We have broken this into several segments:

An introduction to and examples of common human biases and fallacies 2. An explanation of why our minds are ‘modular’ but portray a feeling of unity

and belief 3. An explanation of the power of metaphor that crosses the borders of fantasy 4. Summarizing the opportunities and constraints of our human delusionality

Let’s get started. The following personalities are generalizations (though each one is based on people we know and some of them are verbatim emails). We expect you might recognize aspects of each of these characters as people in your own lives. When asked the following question: ‘What do you think about the future, and mankind’s ability to overcome the challenges of living on a planet with finite resources, like oil and gas?’

Their responses are given below.

“Sir, the scientific world has known since the 1950s that oil is not a “fossil” fuel. It is a natural product of the Earth created as a result of core thermal interaction with the mantle. I challenge you to consider the possibility that oil is not a fossil fuel and is being regenerated continuously. I know this phenomenon well because I studied in graduate school under the best known Russian geologist on these matters who now oversees a government laboratory in Moscow. Where I now live in Louisiana, old dry wells are now being reopened because oil is returning to those holes. Oil is plenty useful stuff, but the earth is creating more of it everyday.”

“For all intents and purposes on human time scales, our resources are not limited at all. Whenever mankind has faced a challenge, we have proven the naysayers wrong by somehow overcoming it– the Manhattan Project, walking on the moon, Y2K, etc. We are smart (and getting smarter), developing technology that can better access and improve the energy and ore extraction processes. We are becoming more efficient at utilizing materials. We combine hard work, intelligence and free markets and come up with answers. That’s what humans have always done and always will do. Sure, there will be recessions and maybe a depression or two this coming century, but people alive in 2100 (and after) will have higher living standards, and access to technology that we can only dream of today. Sure, we joke about flying cars and things like that, but there is really no limit to human ingenuity – those worrying about resource shortages and global environmental sink capacity are doing society a disservice, leaking confidence away from those working on real solutions. If a commodity – like oil – gets scarce its price will eventually go up, and the higher price will incentivize new technology to pull out more resources or alternative sources. Please remember that oil is just a commodity – the true juggernaut of the human condition is our ingenuity and inventiveness.”

“You are wasting your time with that question and wasting your time with this god damn book. Humanity is doomed. We are no smarter than yeast. Look at what we’ve done to the biosphere. Even if we could figure out a way to live without oil it’s too late. Runaway climate change is around the corner. Methane burps from the permafrost, destabilized methane hydrates, positive feedbacks from melting ice caps and lower albedo etc. are going to cause Man’s extinction well before 2100 and probably before 2050. And well before then we will have World War 3 over resources, and those remaining will revert to our core nature, every man for himself, loss of social order, food shortages, etc. I expect cannibalism to be widespread in Europe and North America within a generation. All you can do in the meantime is enjoy each day, build your bunker with guns, whiskey and tradeables, and stay away from economists!”

“Yea, our oil situation has nothing to do Obama's policy or the way the EPA, the DOJ, etc. fleece American corporations with random acts of sodomy. America got what it deserved....a free spirit prez that doesn't know diddly poo. Ohhhh, but Costco is still hiring! Our lame president will handle all these oil and environmental problems. Ohhh right after he goes on the Mike and Mike show and makes basketball picks. Obama will go down as the worst president in the history of this great country. Mark my words. Oh and you ask about oil. Where is it headed next? If you’re a so called expert let me know, I need to make some shekels.”

“I don’t know much about oil or natural resources but I really don’t need to. God put them there for us to find and use. If you read Genesis 1:28 all the fishes of the seas and trees in the forest are there for our dominion. I expect it’s the same with oil and gas and whatever we discover next. I can’t imagine that He would put resources there, for us, if they were going to run out! In any case, I think society overly worries about the future – everything we do is part of God’s plan. We need to just pray and trust in the Lord. I know I do and I rarely feel anxious at all. It is amazing to see the wonder of how it all fits together on God’s green earth. God bless you and God bless America.”

“The earth is a big place! Too much to worry about in my own life to spend time thinking about things I can’t control or influence! Humans will figure stuff out, or we won’t. I’ve read nothing in the newspapers or Fox news or CNN to suggest there is any serious problems with oil or other resources. As far as oil, if I had to guess, I’d say we have, well, about 1 million years left 2 million years? +/-? That’s just a guess though, I’ve never thought about it. But if everyone just gets off their butts and gets jobs, I expect we’ll arrive at the future in good shape.”

“I saw this show, I think on the History Channel, that showed some Tesla fellow from Europe had developed a perpetual energy machine a long time ago – but the government put the clamps down because if energy was free or too cheap to meter there would be no energy industry or war industry. It’s all about profits man! If we ever really have resource issues, I expect the government will resurrect that Tesla machine and other secret devices that are hoarded by the Rothschilds and their cronies. There is a backup plan for it all but only the rich will benefit. I do think science is pretty cool though– I love the History Channel.”

Do you recognize some of these themes in your daily interactions with friends and colleagues, or perhaps some things you yourself might think/say? What do these people have in common? Well, we can list at least 3 things:

Each of them is descended from the best of the best at surviving, procuring resources and mating successfully over thousands of human generations. (we know they mated successfully because to be our ancestors they had to, by definition, mate…)

Since there is little overlap, each of their beliefs about oil and our resource future is unlikely to represent the reality of our physical world. At most one of them could be ‘right.’

Each of them (probably) ‘feels’ as confident in their world views as you do in yours, or as the authors of this book feel in ours.

Few of the people answered the question directly and completely but focused on their perception of one aspect of the question and spoke to that.

A common theme was to redirect the ‘fault’ of a potential resource/environmental crisis towards some sub-group of society (a politician, the unemployed, the naysayers, etc.).

We can also guess at some additional things:

Presenting each of these people with facts, data, charts, handouts, or books that refute their world view will not persuade them. If anything, it may trigger them to dig in their heels, use motivated reasoning, and even more strongly defend their viewpoints.

When these people, (or any people), get stressed about money, or their job, or family or anything in life that adds to anxiety and worry, these beliefs about the world are likely to intensify and become more rigid.

If these people encounter someone with similar beliefs about the world, they are likely to befriend them, irrespective of other nominal differences. We gravitate towards people who agree with us.

With this personality/belief sample as a springboard, this chapter will explain the prevalence (and likely origin) of our beliefs, biases and logical fallacies. Before we get to that synthesis though, let’s do a quick run-through of some commonly known human biases. By the way, here is how the authors would have answered that question about oil and our resource future.

“Our human economy is fully based on energy and natural resources. The level of wealth and complexity of society is directly related to energy surplus (benefits less

costs measured in natural resource terms instead of dollar terms). As we gradually burn through the really high-quality basins we will likely go to costlier, dirtier, harder to get to resources. There is plenty of oil left – but the increasing cost of extracting it – both in energy and environmental terms - implies a long-term decline to human benefits (lower standards of living, higher percentage of poverty, or both), increasing environmental concerns (global heating, acidifying oceans, and increasing stress on wild nature) and more risks of social disruption. Technology can potentially slow this decline, but most technology has been going to ‘apps’ and ‘gadgets’ that ultimately require higher baseline use of quality primary energy. Mankind will have to become ‘wiser’ as opposed to ‘cleverer’ to meet the mounting challenges and a general trend of living with less each year instead of more. But we might be wrong.”

Below is a brief overview of some prevalent human biases - do you recognize any of these?

COGNITIVE DISSONANCE Cognitive dissonance is where holding a particular belief is too ‘dissonant’ with other aspects of one’s life, and the dissonant belief is smoothed, removed or modified so that the dissonance (pain, stress, fear, anxiety etc.) is removed.18 Consider an example (made famous by Jared Diamond, in “Collapse”). “Consider a narrow river valley below a high dam, such that if the dam burst, the resulting flood of water would drown people for a considerable distance downstream. When attitude pollsters ask people downstream of the dam how concerned they are about the dam’s bursting, it’s not surprising that fear of a dam burst is lowest far downstream and increases among residents increasingly close to the dam. Surprisingly, though, after you get to just a few miles below the dam, where fear of the dam’s breaking is found to be the highest, the concern then falls off to zero as you approach closer to the dam! That is, the people living immediately under the dam, the ones most certain to be drowned in a dam burst, profess unconcern. That’s because of psychological denial: the only way of preserving one’s sanity while looking up every day at the dam is to deny the possibility that it could burst. If something that you perceive arouses in you a painful emotion, you may subconsciously suppress or deny your perception in order to avoid the unbearable pain, even though the practical results of ignoring your perception may prove ultimately disastrous. The emotions most often responsible are terror, anxiety, and grief.”

CONFIRMATION BIAS Confirmation bias is when we selectively search our environment for evidence and information to confirm what we already think.19 Implicitly, we tend to ‘ignore’ and

therefore not spend any time seeking information that does not support our beliefs. If I believe in UFOs I am going to frequent websites and buy books about UFOs and will not buy (or read) literature debunking the existence of UFOs. Basically, the human mind tends to only test the predictions of our personal hypotheses by using data that will support them. We all do this to some degree, and it is why the scientific method culturally evolved –to test ALL potential hypotheses. You might guess that those who deny risks of human-induced climate change tend to only read literature on why climate changes naturally, and without human influence (which is true but only part of the story). Similarly, those very worried about global heating tend to gravitate towards articles and news spelling out the grave dangers of continuing to burn fossil carbon. (and the internet makes this bias especially insidious, as an articulate passionate blogger carries as much weight as an atmospheric scientist). In effect, we humans are cherry pickers because it gives us confidence and reduces dissonance. (Note, after a while you will see this is a common theme with many of our biases.)

SOCIAL/GROUP BIASES In-group bias is one of a cluster of cognitive biases known as group biases. Essentially, it is exhibited whenever someone gives preferential treatment to members of a group to which he or she belongs. One of the interesting things about this bias is that it has been demonstrated in randomly assigned groups as well as more organized ones; for example, in-group bias will be exhibited by members of groups which have been created through a coin toss, and also by members of the same sports team, or people of the same religion.20 Do you like your social group’s friends better than their perceived adversaries? Do you like your state’s sports team better than its rival? These are (small) examples of the sociological phenomenon of in-group bias, where we give preferential treatment, often irrationally so, to members of a group to which we belong (and we each belong to lots and lots of groups). In our own groups we attribute our success to our own smarts and personalities, but our mistakes we blame on environmental circumstances and bad luck. But in 'out groups' we attribute their successes to luck (or unsavory actions like cheating) and their failures to their personalities or lack of intelligence, etc. We also tend to lump outsiders together using broad swath descriptions, while treating in-group members as unique and valued individuals. Think for a moment why such favoritism for our in-groups - whether they are political or collecting rocks- is so prevalent and intense (hint: it has evolutionary origins).

We are social creatures. We do not act or make decisions in a vacuum but look to others to inform our behaviors. This social pressure to conform has been studied intensely and has various subcategories: 'groupthink', the bandwagon effect, and the conformity effect among others.

Groupthink occurs when a group of people possess an evolved imperative for harmony and conformity within the group, and thus make dysfunctional or incorrect decisions. The members of the group try to reach a consensus while suppressing conflict and dissenting viewpoints and without critical looks at alternative viewpoints and outside sources. Groupthink became famously known during the Cuban missile crisis when President Kennedy’s advisors all agreed with his analysis and statements - a group consensus that brought us perilously close to a nuclear war. Kennedy afterwards required all people to speak their minds to the limits of their individual expertise even if the information was unpopular (see chapter on ‘We Solve Physical Problems Using Social Sorting Mechanisms’).21

The bandwagon effect is a phenomenon when the rate of adoption of beliefs and ideas increases more when they have already been adopted by others - something you are probably familiar with in high school. If everyone at school wears blue one day, and you wear green, you will immediately feel anxious and nervous that you don't belong, -and not belonging historically came with steep penalties.

More generally, we will go to extreme measures just to fit in - this is called the conformity effect. In the famous Ashe experiment, a subject was seated (last) after 7 'confederates' and was given a series of questions. It was shown that over 2/3 of the time, the subject would give the wrong answer when the other people in the room all agreed on a different right answer. Would you say that Line C in Exhibit 2 is the line most like the line in Exhibit 1? 66% of people tested did.22 When they were interviewed after the experiment, most of these people acknowledged they didn't really believe their answers but went along with the group consensus for fear of being ridiculed or thought to be weird. How much of what is occurring in modern society is a giant version of the Ashe experiment?

AUTHORITY BIAS

We have a strong tendency to listen to and believe statements coming from authority figures like priests, politicians, professors and CEOs. Though the why of this is yet to be sussed out, Richard Dawkins believes it is an adaptive byproduct of children who unquestioningly followed adult instructions during the thousands of generations of our ancestral environment.23 Presumably, the penchant for adults to easily believe things that they are confidently told is a carryover from the children who did NOT eat the berries, touch the snake, or swim over a waterfall as they were told not to– these children survived to have children of their own. Though not authority figures necessarily, we also trust confident and charismatic people and will prefer their predictions even if we know that other more boring, uncharismatic analysts have better track records!

Irrespective of its origins and as uncomfortable as it sounds, we DO inherently believe in authority figures, as the famous and controversial Milgram experiments evidenced.24 Sixty-five percent of volunteers delivered what they thought were fatal doses of 450 volt electric shocks to human subjects while being calmly encouraged to continue by the experiment 'administrators' (doctors in lab coats). The other 35% of participants still delivered high voltage shocks to the point of unconsciousness in the subject but refused to administer the 'highest level' shocks. Interestingly, none of these 35% insisted that the experiment itself be terminated, nor left the room to check that the victim was okay without first asking for permission. So much for independent thinking. In interviews prior to the experiment respondents predicted that only the most 'sadistic' 1.2% of participants would be willing to hurt another participant with electric shocks, yet 100% of the participants DID administer the shocks. The power of authority figures is indeed strong.

OPTIMISM BIAS

Individuals have a tendency to be overly optimistic -we are taught from an early age to 'look at the bright side' and that 'every cloud has a silver lining', and therefore it’s natural that the majority of people discount 'pessimistic' viewpoints and worldviews. Adults are particularly vulnerable to self-deception when comparing their own intelligence and attractiveness to others. Research has shown that we systematically exaggerate our chances of success, believing that we are more competent and more in-control than we really are. Eighty-eight percent of people think they are better drivers than average, ninety-four percent of professors believe they are better at their jobs than the average professor, etc. (By definition, almost half of those surveyed are 'overly optimistic').25

There are good neural explanations for being optimistic. Even if the pessimistic view may be more accurate, the stress of incorporating that particular negativity into one’s

worldview releases a cascade of stress-activated hormones that can seriously compromise a person’s health. In addition, pessimism can lead to depression, which suppresses the normal functioning of important neurotransmitters such as serotonin, which in turn can lead to reduced physical activity, mood swings, and a number of other physical symptoms and diseases.26 Optimistic attitudes also reduce secretion of cortisol, a stress hormone that inhibits the immune system, as well as increase production of helper T-cells.27 The placebo effect is a well-known but little-understood medical phenomenon that improves patients’ physical response with no actual medication. In depression patients, placebos increase wellbeing by an average of 30-50%.28 Apparently, when we 'think' positively that something is helping us medically, even if it’s a sugar pill, it 'works'.

IDEOLOGICAL IMMUNITY

"Educated, intelligent, and successful adults rarely change their most fundamental presuppositions," stated social scientist Jay Snelson. The more knowledge a person has gained and the more effort they have put into establishing their beliefs and theories, the greater their confidence in their views about the world. Psychologists have shown that the higher one’s intelligence (as measured by IQ, admittedly not perfect), the greater one’s ability to defend one’s established viewpoint.29 Furthermore, the higher one’s IQ, the SMALLER the ability to consider alternative points of view. Ergo the higher one’s intelligence the higher the chance of resistance to one’s current ideology and belief. It couldn't be that our intelligence came about not to solve problems as much as to win social arguments for status, could it? (Nate says, the day I finished my PhD I 'felt' smarter, even though that of course was not the case, I was probably dumber by that point).

POST HOC ERGO PROPTER HOC

Post hoc ergo propter hoc literally means 'after this, therefore because of this' in Latin - it can logically be written as 'B follows A, therefore A caused B'. This is an extremely common fallacy which you might be familiar with hearing your teacher admonish 'correlation doesn't imply causation.' Because the rooster crows at dawn, and then the sun rises, does not mean that the rooster caused the sun to rise. There are countless examples in our culture where people see Event B follow Event A and assume a (incorrect) relationship. Our brains are easily tricked in these ways.

In addition to the brief sample above, here is a longer list of human cognitive biases:

(Note: Reality 101 students, no need to read or remember any of these -the point is only THAT THERE ARE SO MANY).

Ambiguity effect – the tendency to avoid options for which missing information makes the probability seem “unknown.” Anchoring – the tendency to rely too heavily, or “anchor,” on a past reference or on one trait or piece of information when making decisions (also called “insufficient adjustment.”) Attentional Bias – the tendency of emotionally dominant stimuli in one’s environment to preferentially draw and hold attention and of a person to neglect relevant data when making judgments of a correlation or association. Availability heuristic – estimating what is more likely by what is more available in memory, which is biased toward vivid, unusual, or emotionally charged examples. Availability cascade – a self-reinforcing process in which a collective belief gains more and more plausibility through its increasing repetition in public discourse (or “repeat something long enough and it will become true”). Backfire effect – Evidence disconfirming our beliefs only strengthens them. Bandwagon effect – the tendency to do (or believe) things because many other people do (or believe) the same. Related to groupthink and herd behavior. Base rate neglect or Base rate fallacy – the tendency to base judgments on specifics, ignoring general statistical information. Belief bias – an effect where someone’s evaluation of the logical strength of an argument is biased by the believability of the conclusion. Bias blind spot – the tendency to see oneself as less biased than other people. Choice-supportive bias – the tendency to remember one’s choices as better than they actually were. Clustering illusion – the tendency to see patterns where none actually exist. Also referred to as “patternicity” by author Michael Shermer. Confirmation bias – the tendency to search for or interpret information in a way that confirms one’s preconceptions. Congruence bias – the tendency to test hypotheses exclusively through direct testing, in contrast to tests of possible alternative hypotheses. Conjunction fallacy – the tendency to assume that specific conditions are more probable than general ones. Conservatism or Regressive Bias – tendency to underestimate high values and high likelihoods/probabilities/frequencies and overestimate low ones. Based on the observed evidence, estimates are not extreme enough. Contrast effect – the enhancement or diminishing of a weight or other measurement when compared with a recently observed contrasting object. Denomination effect – the tendency to spend more money when it is denominated in small amounts (e.g. coins) rather than large amounts (e.g. bills).[14] Distinction bias – the tendency to view two options as more dissimilar when evaluating them simultaneously than when evaluating them separately.

Empathy gap – the tendency to underestimate the influence or strength of feelings, in either oneself or others. Endowment effect – the fact that people often demand much more to give up an object than they would be willing to pay to acquire it. Exaggerated expectation – based on the estimates, real-world evidence turns out to be less extreme than our expectations (conditionally inverse of the conservatism bias). Experimenter’s or Expectation bias – the tendency for experimenters to believe, certify, and publish data that agree with their expectations for the outcome of an experiment, and to disbelieve, discard, or downgrade the corresponding weightings for data that appear to conflict with those expectations. Focusing effect – the tendency to place too much importance on one aspect of an event; this causes error in accurately predicting the utility of a future outcome. Forward Bias – the tendency to create models based on past data which are validated only against that past data. Framing effect – drawing different conclusions from the same information, depending on how that information is presented. Frequency illusion – the illusion in which a word, a name or other thing that has recently come to one’s attention suddenly appears “everywhere” with improbable frequency (see also recency illusion). AKA “The Baader-Meinhof phenomenon”. Gambler’s fallacy – the tendency to think that future probabilities are altered by past events, when in reality they are unchanged. Results from an erroneous conceptualization of the Law of large numbers. For example, “I’ve flipped heads with this coin five times consecutively, so the chance of tails coming out on the sixth flip is much greater than heads.” Hard-easy effect – Based on a specific level of task difficulty, the confidence in judgments is too conservative and not extreme enough Hindsight bias – sometimes called the “I-knew-it-all-along” effect, the tendency to see past events as being predictable at the time those events happened. Hostile media effect – the tendency to see a media report as being biased due to one’s own strong partisan views. Hyperbolic discounting – the tendency for people to have a stronger preference for more immediate payoffs relative to later payoffs, where the tendency increases the closer to the present both payoffs are. Illusion of control – the tendency to overestimate one’s degree of influence over other external events. Illusory correlation – inaccurately perceiving a relationship between two unrelated events. Impact bias – the tendency to overestimate the length or the intensity of the impact of future feeling states. Information bias – tendency to seek information even when it cannot affect action.

Irrational escalation – the phenomenon where people justify increased investment in a decision, based on the cumulative prior investment, despite new evidence suggesting that the decision was probably wrong. Just-world hypothesis – the tendency for people to want to believe that the world is fundamentally just, causing them to rationalize an otherwise inexplicable injustice as deserved by the victim(s). Loss aversion – “the disutility of giving up an object is greater than the utility associated with acquiring it”. (see also Sunk cost effects and Endowment effect). Mere exposure effect – the tendency to express undue liking for things merely because of familiarity with them. Money illusion – the tendency to concentrate on the nominal (face value) of money rather than its value in terms of purchasing power. Moral credential effect – the tendency of a track record of non-prejudice to increase subsequent prejudice. Negativity bias – the tendency to pay more attention and give more weight to negative than positive experiences or other kinds of information. Neglect of probability – the tendency to completely disregard probability when making a decision under uncertainty. Normalcy bias – the refusal to plan for, or react to, a disaster which has never happened before. Observer-expectancy effect – when a researcher expects a given result and therefore unconsciously manipulates an experiment or misinterprets data in order to find it (see also subject-expectancy effect). Omission bias – the tendency to judge harmful actions as worse, or less moral, than equally harmful omissions (inactions). Optimism bias – the tendency to be over-optimistic, overestimating favorable and pleasing outcomes Ostrich effect – ignoring an obvious (negative) situation. Outcome bias – the tendency to judge a decision by its eventual outcome instead of based on the quality of the decision at the time it was made. Overconfidence effect – excessive confidence in one’s own answers to questions. For example, for certain types of questions, answers that people rate as “99% certain” turn out to be wrong 40% of the time. Pareidolia – a vague and random stimulus (often an image or sound) is perceived as significant, e.g., seeing images of animals or faces in clouds, the man in the moon, and hearing hidden messages on records played in reverse. Pessimism bias – the tendency for some people, especially those suffering from depression, to overestimate the likelihood of negative things happening to them. Placement bias – tendency to believe ourselves to be better than others at tasks at which we rate ourselves above average (also Illusory superiority or Better-than-

average effect) and tendency to believe ourselves to be worse than others at tasks at which we rate ourselves below average (also Worse-than-average effect Planning fallacy – the tendency to underestimate task-completion times. Post-purchase rationalization – the tendency to persuade oneself through rational argument that a purchase was a good value. Primacy effect – the greater ease of recall of initial items in a sequence compared to items in the middle of the sequence. Pro-innovation bias – the tendency to reflect a personal bias towards an invention/innovation, while often failing to identify limitations and weaknesses or address the possibility of failure. Pseudocertainty effect – the tendency to make risk-averse choices if the expected outcome is positive, but make risk-seeking choices to avoid negative outcomes. Reactance – the urge to do the opposite of what someone wants you to do out of a need to resist a perceived attempt to constrain your freedom of choice. Recency bias – a cognitive bias that results from disproportionate salience of recent stimuli or observations — the tendency to weigh recent events more than earlier events (see also peak-end rule). Recency illusion – the illusion that a phenomenon, typically a word or language usage, that one has just begun to notice is a recent innovation (see also frequency illusion). Regressive Bayesian likelihood – estimates of conditional probabilities are conservative and not extreme enough Restraint bias – the tendency to overestimate one’s ability to show restraint in the face of temptation. Selective perception – the tendency for expectations to affect perception. Semmelweis reflex – the tendency to reject new evidence that contradicts a paradigm.[46] Social comparison bias – the tendency, when making hiring decisions, to favor potential candidates who don’t compete with one’s own particular strengths. Status quo bias – the tendency to like things to stay relatively the same (see also loss aversion, endowment effect, and system justification). Stereotyping – expecting a member of a group to have certain characteristics without having actual information about that individual. Subadditivity effect – the tendency to estimate that the likelihood of an event is less than the sum of its (more than two) mutually exclusive components. Subjective validation – perception that something is true if a subject’s belief demands it to be true. Also assigns perceived connections between coincidences. Unit bias — the tendency to want to finish a given unit of a task or an item. Strong effects on the consumption of food in particular. Well-travelled road effect – underestimation of the duration taken to traverse oft-traveled routes and over-estimate the duration taken to traverse less familiar routes.

Zero-risk bias – preference for reducing a small risk to zero over a greater reduction in a larger risk. Actor–observer bias – the tendency for explanations of other individuals’ behaviors to overemphasize the influence of their personality and underemphasize the influence of their situation (see also Fundamental attribution error), and for explanations of one’s own behaviors to do the opposite (that is, to overemphasize the influence of our situation and underemphasize the influence of our own personality). Defensive attribution hypothesis – defensive attributions are made when individuals witness or learn of a mishap happening to another person. In these situations, attributions of responsibility to the victim or harm-doer for the mishap will depend upon the severity of the outcomes of the mishap and the level of personal and situational similarity between the individual and victim. More responsibility will be attributed to the harm-doer as the outcome becomes more severe, and as personal or situational similarity decreases. Dunning–Kruger effect an effect in which incompetent people fail to realize they are incompetent, because they lack the skill to distinguish between competence and incompetence Egocentric bias – occurs when people claim more responsibility for themselves for the results of a joint action than an outside observer would. Forer effect (aka Barnum effect) – the tendency to give high accuracy ratings to descriptions of their personality that supposedly are tailored specifically for them, but are in fact vague and general enough to apply to a wide range of people. For example, horoscopes. False consensus effect – the tendency for people to overestimate the degree to which others agree with them. Fundamental attribution error – the tendency for people to over-emphasize personality-based explanations for behaviors observed in others while under-emphasizing the role and power of situational influences on the same behavior Halo effect – the tendency for a person’s positive or negative traits to “spill over” from one area of their personality to another in others’ perceptions of them (see also physical attractiveness stereotype). Illusion of asymmetric insight – people perceive their knowledge of their peers to surpass their peers’ knowledge of them. Illusion of transparency – people overestimate others’ ability to know them, and they also overestimate their ability to know others. Illusory superiority – overestimating one’s desirable qualities, and underestimating undesirable qualities, relative to other people. (Also known as “Lake Wobegon effect,” “better-than-average effect,” or “superiority bias”). Ingroup bias – the tendency for people to give preferential treatment to others they perceive to be members of their own groups.

Just-world phenomenon – the tendency for people to believe that the world is just and therefore people “get what they deserve.” Moral luck – the tendency for people to ascribe greater or lesser moral standing based on the outcome of an event rather than the intention. Outgroup homogeneity bias – individuals see members of their own group as being relatively more varied than members of other groups. Projection bias – the tendency to unconsciously assume that others (or one’s future selves) share one’s current emotional states, thoughts and values. Self-serving bias – the tendency to claim more responsibility for successes than failures. It may also manifest itself as a tendency for people to evaluate ambiguous information in a way beneficial to their interests (see also group-serving bias). System justification – the tendency to defend and bolster the status quo. Existing social, economic, and political arrangements tend to be preferred, and alternatives disparaged sometimes even at the expense of individual and collective self-interest (see also status quo bias). Trait ascription bias – the tendency for people to view themselves as relatively variable in terms of personality, behavior, and mood while viewing others as much more predictable. Ultimate attribution error – similar to the fundamental attribution error, in this error a person is likely to make an internal attribution to an entire group instead of the individuals within the Cryptomnesia – a form of misattribution where a memory is mistaken for imagination. Egocentric bias – recalling the past in a self-serving manner, e.g., remembering one’s exam grades as being better than they were, or remembering a caught fish as being bigger than it was. False memory – a form of misattribution where imagination is mistaken for a memory. Hindsight bias – filtering memory of past events through present knowledge, so that those events look more predictable than they actually were; also known as the “I-knew-it-all-along effect.” Positivity effect – older adults remember relatively more positive than negative things, compared with younger adults. Reminiscence bump – the effect that people tend to recall more personal events from adolescence and early adulthood than from other lifetime periods. Rosy retrospection – the tendency to rate past events more positively than they had actually rated them when the event occurred. Self-serving bias – perceiving oneself responsible for desirable outcomes but not responsible for undesirable ones. Suggestibility – a form of misattribution where ideas suggested by a questioner are mistaken for memory.

Telescoping effect – the effect that recent events appear to have occurred more remotely and remote events appear to have occurred more recently. Von Restorff effect – the tendency for an item that “stands out like a sore thumb” to be more likely to be remembered than other items. Choice-supportive bias: remembering chosen options as having been better than rejected options. Change bias: after an investment of effort in producing change, remembering one’s past performance as more difficult than it actually was. Childhood amnesia: the retention of few memories from before the age of four (odd, isn’t it?) Consistency bias: incorrectly remembering one’s past attitudes and behaviour as resembling present attitudes and behavior. Context effect: that cognition and memory are dependent on context, such that out-of-context memories are more difficult to retrieve than in-context memories (e.g., recall time and accuracy for a work-related memory will be lower at home, and vice versa). Cross-race effect: the tendency for people of one race to have difficulty identifying members of a race other than their own Cryptomnesia: a form of misattribution where a memory is mistaken for imagination, because there is no subjective experience of it being a memory. Fading affect bias: a bias in which the emotion associated with unpleasant memories fades more quickly than the emotion associated with positive events. Generation effect (Self-generation effect): that self-generated information is remembered best. For instance, people are better able to recall memories of statements that they have generated than similar statements generated by others. Google effect: the tendency to forget information that can be easily found online. Humor effect: that humorous items are more easily remembered than non-humorous ones, which might be explained by the distinctiveness of humor, the increased cognitive processing time to understand the humor, or the emotional arousal caused by the humor. Illusion-of-truth effect: that people are more likely to identify as true statements those they have previously heard (even if they cannot consciously remember having heard them), regardless of the actual validity of the statement. In other words, a person is more likely to believe a familiar statement than an unfamiliar one. Leveling and Sharpening: memory distortions introduced by the loss of details in a recollection over time, often concurrent with sharpening or selective recollection of certain details that take on exaggerated significance in relation to the details or aspects of the experience lost through leveling. Both biases may be reinforced over time, and by repeated recollection or re-telling of a memory. Levels-of-processing effect: that different methods of encoding information into memory have different levels of effectiveness.

List-length effect: a smaller percentage of items are remembered in a longer list, but as the length of the list increases, the absolute number of items remembered increases as well. Misinformation effect: misinformation affects people’s reports of their own memory. Misattribution: when information is retained in memory but the source of the memory is forgotten. Modality effect: that memory recall is higher for the last items of a list when the list items were received via speech than when they were received via writing. Mood congruent memory bias: the improved recall of information congruent with one’s current mood. Next-in-line effect: that a person in a group has diminished recall for the words of others who spoke immediately before or after this person. Osborn effect: that being intoxicated with a mind-altering substance makes it harder to retrieve motor patterns from the Basal Ganglion. Part-list cueing effect: being shown some items from a list makes it harder to retrieve the other items. Peak-end effect: that people seem to perceive not the sum of an experience but the average of how it was at its peak (e.g. pleasant or unpleasant) and how it ended. Persistence: the unwanted recurrence of memories of a traumatic event. Picture superiority effect: that concepts are much more likely to be remembered experientially if they are presented in picture form than if they are presented in word form. Serial position effect: items in the middle are the least likely to be remembered Self-relevance effect: that memories relating to the self are better recalled than similar information relating to others. Source Confusion: misattributing the source of a memory, e.g. misremembering that one saw an event personally when actually it was seen on television. Spacing effect: that information is better recalled if exposure to it is repeated over a longer span of time. Stereotypical bias: memory distorted towards stereotypes (e.g. racial or gender), e.g. “black-sounding” names being misremembered as names of criminals. Suffix effect: the weakening of the recency effect in the case that an item is appended to the list that the subject is not required to recall. Suggestibility: a form of misattribution where ideas suggested by a questioner are mistaken for memory. Telescoping effect: tendency to displace recent events backward in time and remote events forward in time, so that recent events appear more remote, and remote events, more recent. Tip of the tongue phenomenon: when a subject is able to recall parts of an item, or related information, but is frustratingly unable to recall the whole item. This is thought

an instance of “blocking” where multiple similar memories are being recalled and interfere with each other. Verbatim effect: the “gist” of what someone has said is better remembered than the verbatim wording. Von Restorff effect: that an item that sticks out is more likely to be remembered than other items Zeigarnik effect: uncompleted or interrupted tasks are remembered better than completed ones.30

WHAT THE ?!!!?? This is but a sampling of the hundreds/thousands of biases in human thinking that have been observed and quantified. What is going on here? Why did this come about? Aren't we the smartest organism on the planet? Is rationality (making the right decision given access to the relevant scientific facts) the exception, not the rule? It turns out that humans tend to be delusional in well-established ways, for good evolutionary reasons – these reactions to everyday things by our ancestors were adaptive. However, the evolved mental 'shortcuts' that served us well in dominating the world are ill-suited to our long-term well-being now that we have dominated it. Let's explore how this came about, how it all fits together, and how such knowledge might improve our lives and possible human futures.

Part 2 - The Gift and Curse of Modular Minds

As you read this sentence, your thoughts and conclusions about it are affected by how dirty your computer is, the font I chose, how comfortable you are, the hardness of your chair, the pheromones in the air, the contents of your stomach, the things you plan to do after reading this, who else is in the room and what your relationship with them is, whether there's a color picture on the page, a black and white picture or none, your mental assumptions of Nate and DJ, the things in your peripheral vision, the sounds in the background, whether you just had a fight with your girlfriend, the vague personal past associations you have with words in the sentences, what happened the last time you were in this room, and thousands of other tiny neural modules speaking at once. This parallel processing in your brain considers nothing irrelevant, and folds it all in to your real-time experience and conclusions. If asked, you will invent reasons for your conclusions - and believe them - without knowing even in principle what, why, or how your brain’s modules perceived and communicated what they did. And every moment of your life is constructed this way. Well, here's one thing you probably don't know about yourself: you don't have a "self" in the sense that it feels like you do. That's a story, albeit a convincing one, told by a

single brain module among many. You've often heard the phrases "he's deceiving himself," "you know better than that," or the general term ‘self-deception.’ These terms arise from our subjective feeling that there is a little spirit in our heads which is "you" - the captain of the ship, the executive in charge, the mahout astride the elephant. It feels like the senses bring in accurate information about the physical world and our little voice inside assembles and organizes this info and makes (mostly) rational decisions about it, like a tiny Captain Kirk on the bridge of a starship. But actually, that little voice in our heads is more like a junior copy editor for a supermarket tabloid, having no knowledge of the real world at all, just story ideas from contributors he's never met, and a mandate to get an exciting paper out to those supermarkets every day which look good and sell. To do his job best, the facts not only aren’t important, they slow him down. Modern science has shown that the entire reality we experience is an after-the-fact rationalization, strung together by our little tabloid editor, based on input that has only a passing relation to reality. Our minds, like our bodies, were sculpted by evolution, and evolution did not set out to build an all-purpose computer capable of understanding the real world. Thus, a primitive life-form far back in our evolutionary lineage would have only had a few sensory tricks: eating, simple smelling, detecting light from dark, and being able to stand still or run. Such a critter would not need descriptive meta-information about the environment, such as the earth being round, the existence of gravity and oxygen; nor would it need to integrate and reconcile its different brain systems to create an abstract, cohesive template of the whole world. Moreover, in addition to reacting in the correct direction, evolution was about reacting quicker than those around you. The fewer parts you can use to make a processing system, the faster it is. So our ancestral critter separately evolved processing modules, using bunched local groups of neurons for eating, moving toward or away from light, running or standing still, smelling, etc. To work in the real world, these systems had to all operate at the same time, in parallel. Predators and prey won’t wait around while an animal chooses which of her skills and senses to activate. Fast-forward to the better part of a billion years later, and those adaptations still work (as a trillion cockroaches can attest!). But as evolution expanded life and complexity, a few branches of the animal tree led to lifeforms which could support larger brains. These brains evolved with excruciating slowness, which is the natural pace of evolution. When a new ability was adaptive, it was tacked on top of and in front of the existing neural scaffolding of prior successful adaptations. A subsystem that originally evolved to smell things might get better at reacting to the smells which proved salient to the critter’s survival, but such a “smelling module” wouldn’t additionally evolve to perform something entirely unrelated, like detecting the sound of approaching

footsteps. Doing so would just have slowed down a perfectly good evolved smell module by getting it to do something entirely different. As such, the smell module would be conserved over time, and a new brain modality would be added. Modern airliners don't actually need pilots; they can take off, fly to their destination and land perfectly well. They have nothing LIKE an understanding of the world; they have utterly unconscious heuristics which cause them to do certain things at certain times, consult both GPS and doubly-redundant inertial navigation devices, radar to avoid whacking into things, and various programmed responses to conditions airplanes have encountered in the past and are thus likely to encounter in the future. However, the plane doesn't understand the world, see the world, or even ‘know’ a world exists. And that’s what evolution has done with our large and spectacularly successful human brains as well. Just like the plane, most of the process of thinking is just like the cockpit machinery in that aircraft. Our brains are large aggregates of context-triggered brain modules; IF this happens, DO this. We don’t have to think about breathing, speeding or slowing our heartbeat, priming our nerve potentials preceding movement, topping off our muscle cells with ATP, enacting peristalsis, emitting the right amount and kind of saliva, maintaining our blood sugar, balancing upright to walk, or maintaining thousands of other processes which keep our bodies working, and that’s a darn good thing since a central self would be swamped if it had to do those things consciously. A whole lot of things have to go right for a brain to see a flower and know it’s a flower. In other words, most of the ‘thinking’ the brain does is kept invisible to our consciousness for the same reason we don’t have to consciously excrete insulin after a meal: it would bombard a conscious mind with overwhelming stimuli to the point of helplessness. Our brain does these bodily tasks for us quickly, frugally, and quietly. Modern neuroscience terms this functional modularity ‘fast and frugal heuristics.’ One relevant phenomenon resulting from our modular minds is that we tend to go overboard in attributing agency (agency = the existence of an entity (person, animal, monster, deity) behind some movement, action or phenomenon). It was extremely valuable in evolutionary history to detect predators, enemies, dangers, and emergent threats to social and mating status. So much so that the downside of "false positives" was disproportionately far greater than "false negatives". This created critters who erred greatly on the side of detecting dangers and enemies, and often attributing agency where it didn't exist. There was a negligible cost to thinking a pile of grass was a lion, but a very large cost to concluding a lion was just a pile of grass. Our visual

systems are primed for predator and face recognition, so we see predators and faces in random shapes. Moreover, we generalize this to infer conscious agency for many occurrences in the physical world unconnected to living systems. As such, most humans have a thriving coterie of imaginary friends and enemies, despite the fact that this is technically insane. Many people have a whole pantheon of angels, saints, and other entities they believe in. Many also believe their dead family members are with them and consult them. Some people threaten their cars. Most people believe in the “invisible hand of the marketplace” as a guiding force in current society, etc. Since our default mental mode is to conform with our tribe, our social relationships with invisible friends, enemies, and abstractions are generally considered more important by the human species than the ecological details of our physical world. What could possibly go wrong with that? Human brains have morphological modularity (dedicated functional substructures like the optic lobe, medulla, cerebrum, limbic system, cortex, neocortex, and R and L lobes); functional/heuristic modularity such as dedicated sub-areas which have been assigned by evolution to carry out certain kinds of sub-processing like language, face recognition, snake recognition, etc; and contextual program/story modularity, such as Christianity, biology, conspiracy, aspects of self-image, cultural narratives, personal narratives, etc.31 This modularity exists to prevent neural processing bottlenecks: if everything the brain processed had to be run through a single "you", you'd be so slow that sparrows could prey on you. Our modules process all sensory information heavily and forward only the filtered results between modules. What emerges from this unique neural machinery is nothing less than our reality, our “selves,” and the biological basis of bullshit. Brain researcher Michael Gazzaniga notes, "Consciousness is not a single generalized process. It's an emergent property that arises locally out of hundreds or thousands of widely-distributed specialized systems and modules. From moment to moment, different modules compete for attention and the winner serves as the neural system underlying that moment of conscious experience. We don't experience a thousand chattering voices but a unified experience. Consciousness flows easily and naturally from one moment to the next with a single unified and coherent narrative. The reality we experience emerges out of a particular specialized system I call the 'interpreter', which is specialized to the brain's left hemisphere. It is the trigger for human beliefs which constrain our brain."
