It turns out the “conscious self” we are aware of is a small subsystem in the left hemisphere of our brain which creates a seamless illusion of “self”, which probably evolved to make us more effective in social interactions. This module is entirely unaware of the other modules as their inner workings are invisible to it. Our conscious

minds have no more access to the roots of our quick decisions than they have to knowing about the secretion of hormones by our pituitary. For example, learning that an optical illusion isn’t real still doesn’t make it go away, because the little specialist module which misinterprets what it sees doesn’t understand words or communicate – it sends its results on a one-way line. There is no way you can “introspect” your way into the working of the limbic system or take conscious control of your blood’s salt balance. We have no conscious access to the cognitive modules which make us feel a negative impression of someone due to a faint background odor in the room or because they look like someone who scared us when we were two years old. The kicker to all this is that since the “interpreter” module has NO knowledge of (or access to) the other modular sub-processes of the brain, but only to the outputs (decisions) made by thousands of babbling idiot subsystems, it just makes stuff up, typically settling for a self-consistent story that reflects most favorably on the individual tribe-member in question: YOU. As such, we live inside self-constructed virtual worlds of post-hoc confabulation. This is the story which is communicated to those around us. It can be pretty much entirely BS, but it’s our BS and we are evolved to believe it absolutely, without question. Even if we frequently (and retroactively) change this story, our feeling of belief and confidence in it seldom wavers. So, one high-level module, out of thousands, acts as an “interpreter” of events, and is tasked with making our experience of the world into an internally-consistent story. Yeah, that sounds wacky, but it's true. It can be directly tested in people who have had an operation to cut the connection between brain hemispheres. There’s a large bundle of nerves called the corpus callosum which acts as a high-speed conduit for information-sharing between the brain’s left and right hemispheres, and it turns out that some kinds of seizure disorders can be cured by the drastic step of severing this bundle of nerves. What results is that each side of the brain becomes a separate person, entirely unaware of the other, each with a specific subset of the sensory information and motor control the original person had, and these 2 ‘people’ have entirely different personalities. By asking questions of these patients and feeding different sensory information to the separate minds, one can see the left-hemisphere “interpreter” in action, creating a made-up reality by filling in the gaps. For instance, one can show the right hemisphere a picture of a dog and the left hemisphere a picture of a bird and tell the right hemisphere to draw a picture of what it sees (with the left hand, which it controls). One can then ask the right hemisphere why the left hand drew a picture that looks a lot like a dog. Rather than saying “I don’t know”, (the correct answer), the left hemisphere makes up a plausible story, such as “when I last saw a bird it was being chased by a dog.” It’s the interpreter’s job to make us believe we know, and thereby

to help us convince those around us with the best story it can come up with. It clearly doesn’t just do this between hemispheres, but with all the structurally and functionally independent processing centers of the brain. Instead of “interpreter,” we might just as well call this module the “Public relations” module, the “press secretary,” or the “storyteller.” What emerges is that the interpreter creates a self-serving story and believes it utterly, even though it has literally no idea why a quick decision was made or action taken. Our "sense of self" that arises from this "interpreter" (or "executive secretary") module stitches our inner voices into inferences and explanations that create a narrative that is socially advantageous to the individual. These stories may have little to do with truth or reality, the sole requirement is that they reflect well on the individual in a social context and are plausible to other tribe members. The most efficient way of deceiving other apes is to sincerely believe your own lies. There are good evolutionary reasons for this: it’s hard to lie without detection. There are many physiological changes which occur when active lying is taking place (as the best poker players know), and it also requires that one keep track of multiple complex storylines (as devotees of soap operas know). Therefore, to the degree possible, it is most efficient and advantageous for an organism to really believe what it says. This eliminates any "tells" which might prevent successful beneficial-to-the-self lying. But as you might guess, it makes it difficult for us to do non-social thinking, or to believe facts which do not fit well within such self-helpful story lines. Additionally, our narratives are exclusively post-hoc, created from whole cloth after decisions have already been made. And, though these narratives are what we generally refer to as ‘reality’, they are only weakly anchored in physical reality if at all. Having multiple modules means that we can sincerely hold many mutually incompatible beliefs in our heads. This works because they're context-triggered. We don't reconcile our beliefs: they exist in parallel. So one may not believe in evolution when it comes to creation of life on earth, but still believe it’s a good idea to get a flu shot every year due to the influenza virus evolving virulence in a competitive evolutionary dance with the human immune system. If evolution didn’t exist, we wouldn’t need annual flu shots. But the human mind does not reconcile its conflicting beliefs produced by different modules, because with modular beliefs you get the adaptive benefits of belief while avoiding the downsides of the extended implications of such beliefs. Being human means, for the most part, never having to reconcile your ‘beliefs’. To further complicate things, most of our modules don't synthesize, while the real world does nothing but. Our brains can shift focus quickly, but we mainly do one

thing at a time. In school we learn our subjects separately - Spanish, math, psych, etc., and that's the way they stay. Yet that isn't the way things happen in the real world of complex adaptive systems. So: 1. our ‘self’ is actually hordes of independent idiot-savant mini-brains and modules

who for the most part have no knowledge of what the other brains do, or that other modules even exist.

Our interpreter module seamlessly creates stories that are socially acceptable to inputs it wasn’t privy to.

We believe this process was decided rationally by us and is the ‘truth.’ Our brains work this way, and well, simply because this was adaptive to our ancestors and continues to be adaptive to us as individuals today. But you, dear reader, are in the first generation of creatures in a billion years to learn this information about the actual nature of your mind. This wasn’t known before, and now it is. We are specifically evolved to make up stories and utterly believe them. That has historically helped our fitness as tribal primates. In a species which is now controlling an entire planet, it’s an interesting (and relevant) factoid to grasp. We are each of us an enormous committee of specialized idiot-savants ensconced in a single brain case, experiencing a virtual world which is created for us by an amalgamation of parallel simple processing systems, integrated by unconscious processing, and interpreted for social purposes by a small, recently-evolved module which - unaware of what the rest of the brain has actually considered - creates ever-changing stories about the world and the self. (While writing this paragraph, it strikes me that it’s no wonder we haven’t understood this process!) So where does that leave us? We have modular minds, structurally and functionally, plus an illusion of unity, knowledge, and certainty. Yet the upside of recognizing all this (call it ‘self-aware modularity) might be significant. This mind modularity is a new and alien-feeling concept, but it is clearly central to our humanity. We need stories to arrange our lives and perceptions by, need a sense of ourselves, need to function in one body in the modern world despite being a committee of idiot-savants playing by obscure, stone-age rules. Understanding ourselves in this way could remove the stigma from believing two conflicting things at once, which is often a practical necessity to live as a well-adjusted social human. If we cut ourselves some slack, we can with a bit of discipline have magic coexisting with science in the stories of our lives. We might choose our beliefs without requiring that they be logically congruent with one another, to be happy socially while being responsible ecologically. It might be possible to be a

Christian atheist or scientist magician, or any other combination of beliefs which allowed us to live happily and sustainably in the future. To actually love our enemies. Our human minds are the most complex weavings of information in the known universe. Indeed, each of them constitutes a virtual universe which comprises the “reality” we subjectively experience. But none of us evolved to possess a mental module with a task of accurately understanding the world. Things feel “right” based on the agenda of the gene, for a good reason. They feel right because in the past they have aided our ancestors’ survival, not because they are correct. This is a good thing, since physical reality would suck to experience directly, while our virtual version of it can be delightful. However, it is becoming clear that collective delusion about the physical world is leading to (big) problems. So if we live in retroactively-confabulated worlds and are evolved to passionately believe our own made-up stories based on the outputs of a huge internal committee of anonymous enfranchised savants and imbeciles, what do we do? Well, one choice is to believe this is not the case and carry on, which is probably the default. Another option is to be open to becoming aware of our own cognitive biases, questioning our firmly held beliefs, where they came from, and why they feel so strong. And hopefully it will help us appreciate that our “interpreter” module has at least opened the door to kinds of thinking which did not evolve with the brain, but via human culture. Things like logic, mathematics, science, and an ethic based on ecological first principles rather than feelings. These thoughts don’t come naturally, and they lack the evolved power of feelings, but they can potentially filter our thinking in useful ways, and open our beliefs, and ultimately actions, to change. This speaks to us as individuals. However, in a super-social species, our brains possess some other relevant neural machinery that was adaptive to our ancestors, which we politely refer to as ‘The Power of Crazy’.

Part 3 - The Power of Crazy

Once upon a time, there was a species which told stories to itself. Some of the stories were very clever, others were very fanciful; still others contained wisdom about the experience of life and love, or insights into the physical world around them. This served the species well as it expanded into a functional social hive, but there was a catch: the species couldn't easily tell its stories apart. As it conquered more and more of the earth, wiping out predators and opponents, and pretty much everyone born survived to middle/old age, the lives of its members became increasingly detached from the physical consequences of believing fanciful stories, and a convergence of

evolved mental traits led to the pervasive belief in stories which were in some ways wildly at odds with long-term survival needs. And this species is….us. So why are we a story-telling species? How is the "story" a central part of being human? Members of a species need to communicate with one another. Members of species that live in groups, even more so. And extremely social species –like our own – greatly depend on ongoing communication between individuals. As we developed into Homo sapiens, evolution increasingly favored verbal communication over simpler forms. Human speech evolved from making sounds to making words and ultimately to stringing together complex and nuanced word groups into sentences and speeches. We started using words to represent things and actions, and to invoke references and metaphors to convey abstract concepts. This conveyance of abstractions was (and remains) adaptive. Speech is a "serial" communication system – each additional word sharpening the texture and meaning of the image/concept conveyed by the preceding words. For example, “The eggs of the Thunder Turtle are as bright as a thousand sunrises and bigger than the early moon and they smell like crushed red flowers.” Metaphor drastically speeds up communication by invoking comparisons with complex things that already reside in another person’s mind. "That thing I saw was like a leopard but bigger. And brown." "If that rock falls on you, it will be like when the mammoth stepped on Uncle Paz." Narrative immediately creates a virtual reality in the mind of others (did you just envision a woolly mammoth stepping on a caveman?). In order to work, narrative communication is inherently imaginative when referring to things and concepts. It doesn't need to be perfect; the real-world standard for a competitive advantage is fast and good enough. It doesn't matter that the way one person envisions a leopard may not be exactly the same way as their friend, or whether each has the exact same virtual memory of a smashed uncle. As purely imaginary creations of the brain, narrative and metaphor are by definition untethered to physical reality. As such, this type of communication has no built-in correction mechanisms for corresponding to reality. Sentences could be constructed which evoked vivid pictures in the mind of both the speaker and listener, pictures of “crazy” things which had no real counterparts in the physical world. This wasn’t just confined to communication. As humans became more socially verbal, it became more efficient to not only communicate in metaphorical narrative but to think using the same language/syntax used in that communication. This ability allowed the thoughts of one mind to be quickly and approximately communicated to

another. Communication using words which invoke metaphors takes advantage of the shared experience and great similarity between tribe-members’ minds. Most members of a small tribe will have very similar memories – many of them visually, auditorily, tactilely, and olfactorily complex – and the ability to refer to these memories and concepts in another brain by triggering that brain with a short phrase is powerful and “good enough” even though no two people remember the crocodile stealing the gazelle from the leopard in exactly the same way (since no complex situations will be exactly alike). Visual thinking can be extremely powerful, but there is no practical way of transmitting visual-cortex abstractions quickly between minds. Pantomime? Drawing a picture in the dirt? A picture, the saying goes, is worth a thousand words; which is another way of saying it would take a thousand words to describe a simple mental picture. When running from a pride of lions or chasing a gazelle, it made evolutionary sense for tribe-members to think in ways which were capable of being quickly communicated, and that meant an internalized language in the mind. As an alternative to actually transferring a complex, information-dense physical experience somehow, the ability to refer to concepts that already exist in the mind of the listener allows the speaker to insert subjective emotion and imagery merely by creating a rough facsimile in the others imagination. Consider how this skill would have provided advantages to individuals and tribes on the Pleistocene (and to those of us reading these pages even now). And, the actual thoughts received by the listening human did not need to be the same as those of the speaking human – the communication just had to be “good enough” to get the job done. There’s an old joke about a hillbilly who fell through the floor of an outhouse and yelled “Fire!” After he was rescued, he was asked why he yelled “fire!” And he responded, “Well I didn’t reckon anybody would come if I yelled “shit!” If your friend says that flying standby on Podunk Airlines is like being the new kid in a Turkish prison, it’s a pretty vague metaphor, but your interpreter module will make some sort of sense of it... or try its best. It probably doesn’t matter that you have to imagine what that means: you get the gist that he didn’t like it. Orangutans and bonobos do not have this skill. So if by "crazy" we mean objects, abilities, situations, entities, relationships and propositions which don't exist in the physical world, it's clear that it's not easy to sort "crazy" from real. The funny thing is that, for most of human existence, this turned out not to be a problem. The narratives which were obviously contradicted by the evidence of our senses were corrected. "Antelope dead at bottom of cliff, so antelope

can't fly like owl." Those which never required obvious correcting based on physical evidence became harmless nonsense beliefs which varied from tribe to tribe, spun into tall tales and legends which gave hunter-gatherers something to do with their large amounts of leisure and campfire time. A bit of crazy was a small price to pay for a workable abstract communications toolkit between tribe members. And yet the "power of crazy" ultimately turned out to be hugely important in human history, allowing us to navigate fundamental bottlenecks on socialization and cooperation. All social primates face processing limits with brain size relative to the number of interconnected nodes/relationships in a group. In our species with a 1300-1400 cubic centimeter brain, an individual can keep tabs on and maintain real relationships with about 150 other humans (Dunbar’s number).32 All things being equal, larger organisms are more formidable competitors than smaller ones. Tribes cohesively functioning as a ‘superorganism’ would be more formidable still. But populations larger than 150 or so could not easily coordinate actions because as numbers increase, it becomes less feasible to maintain effective “social grooming,” which requires familiarity and trust etc. Among other things, our finite neocortical capacity limits our ability to accurately detect falsehoods and form alliances when interacting with too many people due to the time investment needed to familiarize with each individual. Furthermore, larger and less cohesive groups would be less efficient at everyday tasks because individuals could ‘free ride’ and cause instability. Ergo, most ape groups are constrained by a maximum size, and this been the case for humans through most of our history. Enter the power of crazy. It turns out that thinking in metaphor-laced narrative opened the door to super-socialization. It does this through:

a. the attractive appeal of supernormal ideation (hat is, thinking about made-up things which have qualities that “peg the meter” of supernormal stimulation inside a brain). e.g. “The Thunder Turtle is bigger than a mountain and poops lightning, and flies so fast it cannot be seen.”

b. the agreement by group members upon beliefs such as imaginary entities and

authority figures, enabling one to rely on predictable actions by others based on their holding these beliefs. e.g. “We both know that the Thunder turtle favors our tribe and will reward us if we do his will. I know that you know that he sees our thoughts so neither of us will cheat.”

c. reshaping individual self-image in accordance with a belief in these shared mythologies. e.g. “I am a member of the Thunder Turtle clan and cannot be easily harmed. If I am harmed, the Thunder Turtle will make me well.”

This transition to entirely virtual communication conferred behavioral cohesiveness in our ancestral groups that could not have been achieved by using purely physical descriptors of real world situations. Imagining and then communicating imaginary things and weaving narratives around them was only limited by our ability to creatively visualize in our heads. These narratives then merge with others and evolve into ever more coherent-seeming and awe-inspiring stories – even crossing over into mythologies. Using an evolutionary lens, the more stimulating, inspiring, calming, enraging, etc. story elements were ‘fitter’ and thus were highly conserved in a virtual process analogous to gene-sharing in viral evolution. The "game theory" calculation (correct behavioral cost-benefit action to take) changes if you know that the members of all tribes which believe in the Thunder Turtle share the same understanding of the Thunder Turtle's desires, because all believers in the Turtle will dependably punish cheaters who disobey those desires lest the Thunder Turtle punish them. A shared narrative and mental visualization provides a reliable basis for trust and cooperation with less-known extended tribal members living in different territories. Aligning your own goals with those of the Thunder Turtle was a path to a larger and more powerful group than the ~150 humans in close proximity would allow. And, moving another level up the hierarchy, an alliance of tribes which all believe in the Thunder Turtle could in turn form a more formidable superorganism than smaller groups of tribes, and the resultant cohesion and coordinated fast response would in no way depends on the Turtle intervening, or even existing! Thus the power of crazy had (and can have) very practical results. Indeed, being "crazy" in this sense may be a necessary quality enabling a belief system to confer these benefits. An arbitrary supernatural belief system that empowers its believer cannot include things which are subject to obvious falsification, such as "Thunder Turtle shamans bleed purple when stabbed." They need to be impossible to check, and to have a good fit with our grab bag of evolved cognitive biases, fears, and lusts. Moreover, if a supernormal narrative does not "peg the meter" of imagination, it is subject to being replaced by one which does. Thus, mythologies successful at organizing at larger population levels evolve toward significant disconnect from the physical world, and toward imaginative hyperbole. Believing in the supernatural turns out to be enormously effective as a social glue, and one of the chief powers of crazy is that one's own concept of self evolves side-by-side with the organizing narratives. This is so powerful it can enable army-ant style

selflessness in order to benefit strangers. For instance, Japanese kamikaze pilots truly believed they were descended from the Sun god, were representing a holy emperor, and that by crashing their planes into the ships of the ‘monkey-people’ they would earn favor and live forever. Our hunter-gatherer tribes have come a long way. As world population and wealth increased, first newspapers, and more recently social media and internet talk shows and news, have expanded our narrative communication at warp speed along billions of human neural nodes. And the most common mythology of our time may be the faith in progress and markets. Abstract financial alchemy and arcane econometrics formulae underpin our institutions and cultural expectations. Our modern democracies have no Plan B beyond growing indefinitely on a finite planet. Such is the power of crazy.

Part 4 - Delusional R Us – Conclusion

The statement that "humans are delusional" refers to a core fallacy: the belief that humans perceive and understand the physical world the way it actually exists. No animals actually understand this, so it's only a fallacy because we assume that we do. But can any species really be delusional? All are evolved to successfully reproduce, and by doing that, they have met the test of survival and evolution. We’d say that all species are delusional relative to having a perfect understanding of the world, and that it usually doesn’t matter. A housefly knows nothing of plate tectonics, it just knows how to find dog poo, avoid dragonflies and spiderwebs, and mate, and that suffices. Yet if a border collie believed it could successfully land a jumbo jet, that would be pretty delusional. Not as delusional as if it were a Dalmatian, but pretty delusional, and fraught with no small consequence for the dog and its trusting passengers. “You can do it, girl!” “Arf!” As we enter the 21st century, we’re in a roughly analogous situation to beginning a dead-stick night landing of a 747 with a confident dog in the pilot’s chair chewing on the throttle knobs, because while we have minds which are wonderfully well-evolved to exist in small tribes of hunter-gatherers, we have surged in population and impact to the point that those evolved systems are entirely inadequate to the current careening crop of emergent predicaments we have created. But border collies are trainable, and so are we. This Honors seminar would be rather pointless if there was no possibility of altering the course of events, and I hope to show that by understanding ourselves and our world according to logical “first

principles,” we can derive a good overall view of the world which is not skewed by evolutionary baggage, and can learn the discipline to work with the minds we actually have. A thermostat might do a great job of keeping your house between 55 and 68 degrees but expecting it to also give stock market advice would be delusional. The thermostat isn't delusional, but outside its realm of functional competence, it would be effectively useless. Similarly, we have used the word "delusional" with respect to humans believing their mental constructs represent the physical world as it actually exists and making decisions about the physical world as though that were true (for instance, deciding that sea level can't rise because a particular God wrote a book saying the world would never flood again). So, the principle delusion is that the brain characteristics which have evolved to enable past survival and reproduction are equally well-suited to making decisions about the physical world now that our species is the top predator and controlling the world's biomass, chemistry, temperature, climate, and other systems. So do we intend “delusional” to be pejorative, another way of saying “stupid,” a snarky way of putting down those who haven’t come to the same conclusions we have? Emphatically, no. In fact, a fair degree of what we term delusion is probably necessary to be sane, and certainly necessary to enjoy being a human, and find happiness. It’s a gift. The physical world we exist within is not what you’d want to perceive directly. It’s a place without color, texture, meaning, love, humor, or anything else we value. Our minds present us with a virtual world vastly different, with all these wonderful things added to it, with our senses and brains logarithmically compressing wide ranges of real-world variation in brightness, loudness, smell, touch, etc. into subjectively linear experiences. It’s hardly surprising, then, that we treat our virtual simulation as real: it’s the only thing we will ever, can ever, experience. In our virtual worlds, magic exists, and anything is possible. Which is a great way to live as a member of a hunter-gatherer tribe. But perhaps it’s not as well matched to overseeing a planetary ecology we currently dominate but don’t collectively understand (arf!). It may be that in a far future where humans have sagely avoided a thousand kinds of disaster, delusion will become celebrated for the central role it plays in the quality of human life, and will be pursued in full realization that we are delusional, and that’s grand. If so, there will have to be a demarcation in each mind between what IS and IS

NOT delusional relative to the realities of the physical world, or else have a subset of the population acting as “designated drivers” to run things using delusion-filtered thinking, to allow the rest to enjoy the bliss of unfettered delusion without wrecking the planet, our resources, or each other. So Nate & DJ are not only delusional, we spend most of each day knowing we're being delusional because that’s where the fun, camaraderie, enjoyment and pleasure exist. However, we’ve found that it’s possible, in principle and in practice, to index our delusions - to identify, acknowledge, and correct for evolved bias, known fallacies, and the usual human blind spots; and to use delusion-filtered thinking to understand and deal with physical-world reality. Doing so continues to be an interesting journey, full of “mind bombs” which reframe the stuff we always thought we understood. But that’s not all there is to it. There’s also an emergent quality to the process: gradually seeing the way the puzzle parts start to fit together in understanding why the world is the way it is, and what we can know about the future. Our physical world does really exist as a cohesive whole with everything happening at once: to some extent, if we don’t at least dimly understand how the parts fit together, we don’t understand anything. Delusion is necessary to be human and to enjoy being human, yet non-delusional thinking is necessary for some classes of decision, in order for humanity to continue to exist in deep time rather than crashing into limits. We need both for different uses, so we’d better learn to tell them apart.

Time Bias and the Fourth Dimension

We possess a certain evolved cognitive bias which the psychological literature refers to as the discount rate because of our tendency to discount the value of things we expect to receive in the future relative to the value of things we expect to receive immediately. We prefer to call this phenomenon the time bias, in hopes of moving its discussion out of academia and into mainstream thought. It seems a perfectly reasonable name for it, because it does relate to time, and it is indeed a bias: rather than making logical decisions about the future, we are perpetually pulled towards the immediate by an ancient yet intact hunter-gatherer instinct which keeps telling us that “a bird in the hand is worth three in the bush.”

How did the time bias evolve? First, the vicissitudes of fortune and the general uncertainty of life have conditioned us to take advantage of opportunities quickly, lest they be lost to us forever. In nature, most organisms do not live as long as their biological potential, making long-range planning on their part problematic. Therefore, their emotions and instincts drive animals to pursue short-term goals such as eating and mating. These are automatic behaviors, rooted in older brain regions such as the limbic system, and they are inherently myopic: as long as they remain active, the future carries little weight. Indeed, most species have no concept of “future” whatsoever, and humans are still developing one. In our planet's past, had you waited to eat, that food might have ended up in another's belly instead of yours—or you might have ended up in another's belly! In a healthy ecosystem, resources necessary for life do not go long unclaimed.

Second, the risk in deferring immediate consumption by betting on the future comes in many specific forms: risk of spoilage, risk of destruction and risk of your own non-survival, among others. For example, a healthy 30-year-old male in the US has a 7.96% chance of failing to survive until his 50th birthday. The list goes on and on: risk of loss of access, risk of government expropriation, risk of obsolescence, risk of you forgetting what it is you are waiting for?...

Time bias is cross-linked with another bias: loss aversion. It feels better to us to avoid a loss than to realize an equivalent or a greater gain.33 The thought of giving up a doughnut we have feels more important than the thought of getting an extra doughnut.

Time bias is strengthened by our lack of certainty about the future. You can eat all of your seed corn now, or plant some of it in hopes of reaping a harvest next year, but if you think that you might starve before then, then it makes more sense to you to just eat the seed grain. If a friend offers to buy you two doughnuts tomorrow in exchange

for eating your one and only doughnut today, you might wonder whether she really means it, whether she will remember her promise tomorrow, whether she will even be here tomorrow, whether she will have the money to buy the two doughnuts, whether donuts will be available tomorrow, and so on.

In all, it just feels a lot more important to us to get something sooner than later. Such feelings have come from evolutionary adaptations passed down to us from our ancestors, who didn’t analyze such things at all. They did what they had to in order to survive, which was to err on the side of the immediate. And since they did survive, they passed on their tendency to favor the immediate. These feelings are the bias. Keep in mind that nearly all of our decisions are made on the basis of feelings rather than rational thought.34 The processes that give rise to these feelings aren’t even conscious. We rationalize our irrational decisions after the fact, if at all.

Are you starting to see how these biases can lead to problems such as obesity, hoarding, and mindless consumerism? In the unnaturally plentiful conditions of an industrial economy, the same strong urges that helped our ancestors survive can easily drive us to excess, even imperiling our survival.

Our current society does little to help us control and moderate these urges. If it feels good to immediately spend your entire paycheck on things that make you feel good right here right now—fast food, alcohol, tobacco, drugs, entertainment, massages, status symbols, toys—then what is to stop you? But this focus on immediate reward can lead to a dysfunctional lifestyle: over time, it takes an ever-increasing amount of consumption to achieve the same level of euphoria. This is called the hedonic ratchet effect, and it leads one to develop ever-larger, entirely unnecessary or even harmful habits.35

We can be said to inhabit a three-dimensional (3D) world—but only if we disregard the fourth dimension: time. It is easy to imagine a person's past as being 4D: not only can we see what that person was doing at any single point in time, but from our vantage point in the present we can trace out the entire four-dimensional shape of her life as it has played out. Your own past life will also have a discernible 4D shape, since you know about your own past. But since we cannot see the 4D shape of the future we tend to fall back to 3D, leading us to concentrate on immediate gratification rather than long-range planning. Most of the time this works. But it is just as useful to consider the future as 4D as it is the past, especially when it comes to large-scale phenomena: economies, civilizations, species and ecosystems. Life unfolds in 3D, but it leaves a 4D trace in our minds, which we can, in principle, project into the future using our imaginations to actively plan out our 4D future.

Most of us already know how to do this in our own lives, to some extent. We learn to defer rewards available to us in 3D reality in order to have a more successful life as

viewed in 4D. Saving part of our paycheck makes us more resilient to unexpected problems. Controlling our appetites to avoid becoming morbidly obese gives us better chances in the future. We restrain ourselves from killing someone in anger because we can see, with our 4D vision, that this action is likely to result in severe negative consequences.

To the extent that we can defer gratification, we tend to do this more as a culture than as individuals. Our culture encourages us to learn how to ride a bike, study foreign languages, pursue a college degree, save money, eat healthy, etc. We also build things that provide benefits beyond our lifetimes, like cathedrals, institutions, social contracts (in theory). None of this necessarily implies having a sophisticated vision of the future. Your parents did not independently deduce that you should learn to tie your shoes, ride a bike or go to college; they just followed a cultural template.

In the most extreme examples of deferred gratification, people defer their rewards until after they are dead and buried. Those who believe that their consciousness will survive after they are dead, and that the greatest possible reward will be given to them once they “shuffle off this mortal coil,” can defer their reward permanently. Even though this is a nonsensical proposition— in order to be realized, reward has to be perceived by a living brain—its behavioral effects on individuals and populations can be profound. It may be seen as a culturally evolved workaround to the time bias and other such bugs in our genetic programming, and in many ways, it is a useful “behavioral patch” for impulsivity.

Such supernatural and non-rational behavioral patches are ultimately framed by selfish individual motives. In addition, they are subject to an inherent limiting factor too: they are not based on logical thinking and observation—that is, they are not based on bias-free thinking—and this makes them useless for defining and attaining any specifically desired complex-system or ecological realities. If Homo sapiens were to live up to the name, some of us would need to learn how to chart a path for our collective futures by relying on facts rather than feelings, deferring immediate gratification based on realistic 4D aspirations and waiting for the good feelings to follow later.

The good feelings we seek now are no different than the emotions that helped ancient hunter-gatherers survive, but the activities done now to attain those feelings just don’t align with the original evolutionary world and aren’t consistent with 4D planning. We can derive satisfaction from music, art, sports, games, social events and much else— all the things that do not automatically cause problems in the future. Moreover, we can learn to care, and to derive a deep satisfaction from being a responsible part of a much larger story, knowing that our efforts on behalf of innumerable future childhoods can make them possible, and happy. These efforts would not be on behalf of just our

future children: in deep time, the same choices that make future human childhoods possible also allow other large complex species to continue to exist.

Case in point: since we have come to exist as a species, there have been fewer than 100 billion human lives lived. How many will there be in total, in 4D, and what will their quality of life be? This question is never posed, even though it is perhaps the most important consideration for a self-aware species. Nor is it a particularly abstract question: it will, one day, have a specific, concrete answer.

Most mammalian species persist for at least 5 million years.36 If we follow this pattern, we have many millions of years left, and if we exceed this expectation— because we are, after all, an unusually adaptable species, able to exploit numerous ecological niches—we could perhaps persist for tens of millions of years, and many trillions of lifetimes left to play, discover, create, and experience. This sounds like science fiction, because we can’t really imagine it. And because we can’t imagine it, and it doesn’t feel important, we don’t even try. We live with a time bias, favoring people who have already been born, and assign to future humans the status of nonentities—mere abstractions—because it feels like the thing to do.

From a 4D perspective, by around the year 1800 AD the only thing we had to do to attain trillions of human childhoods over millions of years was to follow a simple dictum: “Don’t wreck the planet.” This is probably the first time you have ever heard this point made in this way, because it comes from an alien perspective that has been hiding in a human blind spot. Even if we set our sights on just the first trillion human lives, that would already be ten times as many humans as have ever lived since we evolved. These are the lives we may be condemning to nonexistence by using up all the non-renewable natural resource concentrations as fast as we can and by wrecking the biosphere and the populations of wild animals. These lives may feel abstract, but your own existence doesn't seem particularly abstract, and it wasn’t so long ago that you had the exact same status as they do.

Will the future of the Earth allow for even one trillion more human childhoods? That is doubtful, if we continue on our current “business as usual” trajectory of digging up and burning up the planet. Our best science tells us that we are pushing toward a hothouse climate that the Earth hasn't experienced in 55 million years, entirely unlike the one we evolved in.37 It would seemingly be an overheated world with parched lands and stagnant oceans full of jellyfish, worms, and slime. So, will there be room for ten billion more human lives in this world? Or 50 billion? Or 110 billion? This question will eventually have a specific, precise answer—an answer significantly determined by what we do, and do not do, in the coming decades.

We could decide that the future has an importance that transcends our own lives. The idea of a trillion happy human childhoods is not only grounded in reality, but it is also

wonderful enough to act as a highly motivating supernormal stimulus. That is, it is possible for us to decide that the goal of enabling these future lives rather than preventing them is a very good goal. Then we would gain pleasure simply by aiming for it. We are not evolved to think this way, but neither does evolution preclude such thinking.

The question of whether we should learn to unselfishly defer rewards to the future has deep ramifications for our species’ existence, and the quality of that existence. The agenda set by our genetic programming is to perpetuate a feelings-based system geared toward maximum reproduction, maximum power, and maximum consumption in the now, and to cultivate a lackadaisical, see-what-happens attitude toward the future. It will always favor giving birth to 20 billion starving humans over 2 billion happy ones. But the agenda set by the rational mind can take into account quality of life and 4D time and can—in principle—chart a path that optimizes our species’ 4D existence, both quantitatively and qualitatively. The agenda set by our genes may feel good, but if we pursue it, it will not get us to a trillion childhoods, and it will wreck the planet as a habitat for large complex life for a very long time.

Yet our failure to plan for the future is an understandable thing: in the 3.5-billion-year history of life on Earth no organism ever thought particularly far ahead until quite recently, when fairly advanced brains came on the scene.

Plants and animals can only focus on the present moment. Even a squirrel gathering acorns for the winter is instinctively focused on the all-consuming “now” of doing what feels right to do immediately. It doesn’t think about the coming winter, nor does its DNA have any concern about a coming winter. Being a squirrel implies hiding acorns during autumn, and the more acorns it hides, the better it feels. It is following the unconscious agenda of its genes and needs no concept of the future. The first squirrels to accidentally hide some acorns turned out to be more likely to survive and leave progeny that was increasingly driven to hide acorns. The fact that squirrels only ever find a small fraction of the acorns they hide illustrates the blindness of this accidentally evolved mechanism. It could be re-framed as the oak trees being really in charge and are breeding squirrels to better serve them by burying their acorns and not digging them up. Periodically, the oak trees stop producing acorns for a season, allowing just the particularly burying-obsessed squirrels to survive—

Like squirrels, we have a lot of things that feel right for us to do, too, for much the same sorts of reasons.

A lot of perfectly functional animals don’t have a sense of “now.” They exist in a mindless world of causality, sequence, duration, and path-dependence without the need of a “now,” reacting instinctively, never once stopping to puzzle out cause and effect. Considerations of past and future never come up.

Evolution has shaped our brain to react in ways that worked in the past, and to form associations between short-term causes and effects. This requires hardly any conscious effort at all. Thinking about what could happen in the future is a very new activity for our species. It was only once our brains gained the ability to construct mental models of possible futures that this could even become an option, and that is probably a relatively recent development in the history of the triune brain, long after the emotion and reward systems fully evolved. The very notion of the future is probably similar to the notion of a tool: a relatively recent emergent property of larger, modular brains passing some cognitive complexity threshold.

We are alive at a very special time—not only on the cusp of huge changes, for good or ill, but a time that offers possibilities beyond what life has evolved to do. But for now, they are only possibilities—improbable ones.

Few governments, businesses or institutions plan beyond a year or two—five years at the outside. In effect, they impose a threshold of relevance, beyond which nothing is considered to matter. This attitude turns nearly all of the future into a single, gigantic blind spot. The ramifications of this blindness are huge, but we are so blinkered ourselves that it’s not obvious to us—unless we take a step back.

Consider: the devices we use to help us live our lives could have been designed to last a very long time, yet our landfills are full of perfectly functional computers and phones that were considered cutting edge just five years ago. What’s the harm? There isn’t much harm, provided we can convince ourselves that anything we don’t see doesn’t count, that nothing past a five-year horizon matters, and that dollars of profit are the only criterion that determines success.

Even when we look at scientific reports that describe the effect human activities are having on the Earth, we see that the longest period they generally consider is 100 years—a little more than one human lifetime. All the climate change forecasts and estimates of ocean acidification, sea level rise, loss of species and so on, end at 2100, even though the effects they describe will be felt in 2200, 2500, 3000, 5000, and many will have ramifications a million years out. That doesn't seem particularly sane, but it feels right because nobody after 2100 matters. The transient effects of CO2, for instance, are far less significant than its systematic, long-term effects on the planetary climate, but we only pay attention to what will happen fairly soon. We could do otherwise, but we don’t, because we are still making our individual and societal decisions based on how things feel to a stone-age brain, reinforced by modern cultural goals and mores.

The way we think about time can make a big difference in the way we operate. For instance, a calculation was made about whales in the middle of the 20th century: the remaining great whales reproduced more slowly than the interest paid on money in the

bank. If great whales could only reproduce at, say, 5% per year, and money in the bank could earn 7% per year, it made more business sense to get rid of the “low value” whales by wiping them all out, putting the money from catches in the bank to accrue interest, and then recycling the whaling ships. And that, to a large extent, was exactly what was done. However, making biosphere decisions with million-year consequences based on five-year financial plans was shortsighted to say the least.

Yours could be the first generation to seriously consider active engagement with deep timelines for our species, for other large species, and for the planet: the first to lead a conscious 4D existence. This prospect is not of just a revolutionary cultural breakthrough, but a first for life on earth, and unique to our species. It would represent a break from the agenda of the gene, which is centered on feelings-driven consumption and reproduction, and a shift to the agenda of mind that rationally maximizes what we value, such as people, happiness, and healthy ecosystems.

The new agenda would lead to a very different set of assumptions and conclusions about the wisest courses of action, and therefore it would initially come into conflict with those cultures and institutions that set a time relevance threshold. If all of a sudden, we were told that we should reasonably share what we have with future multitudes, our tendency would be to resist, because it wouldn't feel right to us; it would feel like a loss. We would be tempted to treat those who espouse such notions—along with the hapless inhabitants of the future—as a hostile out-group. That is why we chose to call it the time bias rather than the discount rate: it has implications beyond a simple economic preference for immediacy. It is one of the greatest prejudices with which our species must contend, worse than the racism and slavery of past centuries, which divided peoples, for this prejudice divides the entire human race from its future. Our current societies exist within a consensus temporal solipsism which devalues the future by default.

If our species and our world are to thrive, we need to learn to use our brains in ways that are at odds with what they have evolved to do. This is no minor task; but by reading this, and by thinking about it, you will have at least opened the door to this possibility.

Using Social Sorting Mechanisms to Solve Physical World Problems

Our brain carries with it a vast array of algorithms that were constructed (adapted) to solve problems of our ancestral past. The higher impact these had on survival (and mating) success, the more prone to ‘firing’ they are in related situations today. Imagine thousands of generations of hominids living in tribes of 25-150 people on the savannah in Africa. In addition to finely tuned fight-or-flight reflexes, agility and quick responses to fighting and killing game, etc, among the most important traits that made our ancestors our ancestors was being the best of the best at improving their mating prospects, both in quantity and quality. The results of this sexual competition were highly linked to social status. As a fish swims in water, so do we humans swim in a world of social status. And, just as the fish has no need to be aware of what water is or how it works, we don’t typically recognize how social rules, social acceptance and social status influence and constrain our daily behaviors. We are evolved social primates, and although our sociality tethers long predate the hominid line (think grooming in other primates), the importance of social cohesion and connectivity in humans exploded after we developed tools and language and had to compete/war with other tribes. Just as with most animals in nature, in our ancestral environment, those men (and women) who successfully moved up the social hierarchy improved their prospects for access to resources and improved their mating prospects. Those at the bottom of the status rung not only possessed fewer mating opportunities but many did not mate at all. Status among our ancestors was probably linked to those attributes providing consistent benefits to the tribe: hunting prowess, strength, leadership ability, storytelling skills etc. In modern humans, status is defined by what our modern cultures dictate. As we

are living through an era of massive wealth from mining ancient sunlight 10 million times faster than it was sequestered, pure physical prowess has largely been replaced by digital wealth, fast cars, high political connections, etc.

Getting a compliment from a high status person, getting an email from a popular (and attractive) man/woman at our workplace or school, winning an award for some accomplishment, these are all things that give us bursts of happiness and motivation (that probably exaggerate their real impact on our lives). In short, status seeking (and equally; avoiding situations that reduce status or social approval) is one of the loudest-shouting modules in the human brain. As such, personal rank and public perception hold a lot of influence over 'affecting the future' and 'a healthy planet'.

Solving Physical Problems with the Wrong Module

In the last 200 years or so a lack of predators and other historic dangers, and the effect of wealth and modern medicine, have served to greatly diminish any immediate penalties associated with misunderstanding the nature of our physical world; while social status has remained a strong driver of human behavior. Other than overtly risky behavior like base jumping, surfing during a hurricane, or drug experimentation, today’s world offers few immediate physical risks if one toes the social line and goes with the social flow. Short of illness or serious dysfunctionality, it is very difficult to starve or do damage to oneself in contemporary Western societies, because our wide social network protects our members even from themselves. Among other things, this has resulted in social status and conforming being sufficient for survival, with most physical-world considerations mooted. In a study called the “smoke filled room,” 3/4 of people when left alone in a room would report smoke unexpectedly coming under a door. And for good reason, since dying in a building fire is one of the few major immediate dangers we are still subject to. However, if in a room with two other people who ignored the smoke, 90% ignored it too. Basically, the risk of minor social awkwardness was more disturbing than the threat of fire. It’s almost as if our physical-world concerns are considered adequately dealt with if others have the same information. Clearly this makes little sense from afar, but it’s what we do, up close, every day. Our social “consensus trance” overrides other considerations, and as such we don’t notice it. We are wired to be more worried about not conforming to the social signals which upvote or downvote our daily social hierarchy scorecard than to other more relevant risks, often even to immediate physical risks. \*\*I once (wrongly) thought I was having a heart attack and my most pressing thought was being embarrassed for the people I was with at the restaurant. The thought I might be ‘dying and never able to be in another restaurant forever’ was outshouted by my social anxiety.

Our evolved genetic and cultural tendency is to pass information on to the tribe, and then observe whether the tribe gets agitated. If the tribe gets agitated, some behavior then ensues which resolves the agitation, and we go along with it. This has led to what might be termed the “enlightenment” model of social activism, in which the individual goal is not a specific physical-world system state but only the transmission of information. It tacitly assumes that if real problems exist it is due to information not being effectively transmitted to tribe members. And if the tribe doesn’t get agitated, then the information probably wasn’t important. But modern ‘tribes’ aren’t trained in physical world details beyond what happens in their kitchen or garage. This theme underlies the default assumptions of current social and environmental activism, and as such highlights their core weakness. Most humans have weak science and math skills, which take some study and practice to master; and even those who have some training are ill-equipped to assess super complex real-world propositions. So, in most cases in our society, we deal with such issues the same way we deal with smoke under a door: by observing the reactions of our contemporaries. There’s a reason we do this: the risk of being at odds with our social group shouts very loudly inside our brains. It is usually the greatest risk we face, and it certainly feels like it, with a direct hotline to our emotional limbic system in the core brain. But the result is that we use social routines to sort physical propositions. If we get new information, we parse it, in effect, by posing unseen binary neural tests: “who is the information coming from?” “does my in-group believe this?” “does a known (and hated) outgroup believe it?” “Who wants me to believe this and what is their motivation?” And this neural routine and the resultant answers/reactions generally suffices for questions of childhood vaccination, environmental issues, and concerns about future energy supplies. Even those who are science experts mostly use such social filters. All this might not matter much on the large scale, except that our additional evolved module for fairness means that many modern governments are at least in theory run by the popular vote. But based on our social sorting propositions, not only do people feel entitled to their own opinions, they feel entitled to their own facts and rules of nature. In modern America this results in the opinion of a bartender or a bus driver being expected to count on a scientific question such as ocean acidification, as though one could vote on the way nature and the universe works. Thus, bartenders and bus drivers - who may be innocent of actual knowledge - expect one another to have opinions on climate change and any other combination of words they see in the news. Where do they get these opinions? Perhaps from taking night-courses in physics and

atmospheric chemistry- but most of the time just by absorbing and repeating the opinions and beliefs of their friends, family and in-group. It is in this way that presidential candidates may win votes by raising their hands and proudly noting that they don't believe in evolution! This is one reason modern democracy doesn't deal well with environmental predicaments, especially long term ones. It's our eusocial Achilles heel of "fairness ad absurdum". The evolutionary hangover of adaptations from seeking social status in small groups during the Pleistocene is that glue-sniffing pimps and child soldiers often have pronounced and vocal opinions on the 6th great extinction and other complex topics. And on the other end of the spectrum, a large % of environmental and social advocacy ends up just assembling and networking famous people but accomplishing little to nothing that’s actually effective. Even in my own public talks about our societal and planetary future, I (Nate) notice myself socially conforming. I often worry about what my audience wants to hear, rather than stating the most accurate physical description of an issue. To myself, I tell 90% of the truth (I am human, and self-deceive, of course). To my best friend I tell 80% of the truth – for some things I withhold because I know she’ll disagree or will get upset. In a card game with 5 friends I’ll tell 60% of my ‘truth’ because one of them works for Monsanto, and another just suffered a family tragedy and I don’t want to overly concern him about future events. In a lecture to 100 people, I tell 40% of the physical truth, because there are a lot of strangers who I don’t want to offend with what might seem controversial statements if they haven’t studied them. And so on, as one expands the size of the human group. Imagine a politician speaking to an arena of 40,000 people. Our core brains are motivated by feelings. And in a nation of hundreds of millions, making decisions with “feelings”, does not handle physical world complexity very well. \*\*Note: our Reality 101 lectures are organized around scientific articles – so my lectures are based on ‘truth’, but if we stray too far off topic, this same dynamic is at work, though it lessens as I get to know you students. Our current institutions and politicians require stories that involve ‘growth’, at the same time our biophysical science informs us growth is almost over. The disconnect between the social and the physical stories is what is causing us anxiety now and disruption in the near future. Quite simply, it's hard to be a self-aware actor while interacting with other humans. That's OK, but one needs to be aware of this phenomenon and balance it, because the agenda of anticipating social reaction really is the evolved agenda of the gene. If ‘we’ as a species are going to navigate the next century and beyond on a living world, we need to see humans and our systems ecologically as well as socially. This realization doesn’t shout loudly other than in quiet moments by ourselves when we can imagine the closing scenes of our movie.

Section III - Understanding Energy and Economy

It’s not an overstatement to note that this human century is, and will be, defined by the intersection of copious excess energy and human behavior. The way our evolved brains, perceptions, wants and stimulation interact with a temporary energy extravaganza has been the main story of humanity for the last hundred years and will be for the next hundred; and the ramifications will determine which direction the human story takes and how long it lasts. Energy is (just about) everything. And our culture is energy blind.

Energy, Candy, and Reality

(by author DJ) Laying out this book seemed fairly straightforward on first blush. Why, it was just a matter of explaining some of the basic concepts necessary for the synthesis to make sense. Then as we started making lists of the things that science shows to be true, but that human society in general doesn't understand or believe, a funny thing happened. The list grew and kept growing. But it's not that these things are obscure or complicated, a lot of it is just stuff we forget as we grow up. I never thought much about energy as a kid, other than craving sugar with a fierce intensity, and going hyperactively nuts once I found any. There was pretty much nothing I wouldn't eat if it had sugar in it. My parents kept the sugar away from the kids mostly, so my obsessed little brain focused on the sugar-centric holidays: Christmas, Easter, Valentine's Day, Halloween; during which usually-scarce sugar would be on colorful display in an array of treats. Of course, the reason I lusted after sugar so much is that I was evolved to. Candy was a supernormal stimulus which pegged the ancient motivation meter in my skull. I was never offered candy to murder anyone, and in retrospect that's probably a good thing. Yet in the pursuit of candy I unknowingly learned a lot of lessons about humans and energy which are just as true today as they were then, looking for Easter eggs amid the

venomous snakes and giant snapping turtles of my swamp-adjacent Indiana back yard. Firstly, I learned about scarcity and intermittence. If it wasn't a sugar-centric holiday, sugar was hard to come by and unreliable as an energy source. I once found an entire crate of mildewed apple-flavored jell-o concentrate in the basement which my mom had bought marked down to a half-penny a package because it was horrible, and then stored in the basement because it was horrible, presumably as standby food for the aftermath of a soviet invasion. I spent several months in the dark, disconsolately gumming those horrid humidity-congealed globs of discolored gelatin stuck to waxed paper and have the aftertaste of mildew and fake apple still in my mind 63 years later. I also discovered, about a year later, a gallon of sweet Mogen-David wine in a low cupboard which I swigged gulps of over several weeks until it was gone, tipping the jug I was too small to lift. But those were exceptions to what was a very sugarless status quo. My human brain was well-adapted to intermittence of supply, times of lean and times of plenty. Which meant it had a raptor-like focus on sugar holidays. The ostensible rationales for the various sugar-related holidays seemed obligately deranged; Giant rabbits who pooped chocolate eggs to commemorate a Jewish guy rising from the dead. Uh, ok. Dressing up as monsters and threatening the neighbors to give us sugar (ok, I kinda followed the logic there). A whole season of gifts and candy to celebrate that same Jewish guy being born, by bringing a tree indoors and pretending that a fat guy riding flying reindeer would be coming down the chimney with bags of wrapped gifts. For no particular reason. The exchange of heart-shaped notes bearing pleasantries along with candy. Other than wild implausibility and a nearly psychedelic level of non-sequitur from one to another, the rationales seemed to only really be linked by candy. But I didn’t much care. The candy was the important part. Yes, I'm going to link elements of the energy plight of humanity to lessons I learned grubbing for sugar as a child. Easter is as good a holiday as any to pick for this purpose. I would wake on Easter morning, open my sleepy eyes and briefly re-integrate my personality before an urgent message would bubble to the surface of my reality: SUGAR DAY! and I would leap to my feet. I was certain to get sugar that day; but the amount and quality were still very much up in the air. There would be lavish Easter baskets hidden in various parts of the house with the names of each child on them, and Easter eggs hidden throughout the yard.

Clearly, the first target was the best and highest-quality concentrations of energy: the Easter baskets. As I was the oldest, largest, and earliest-waking child, the first thing I'd do was find the Easter baskets of all four children. I'd then re-hide mine in a good place, like down in the basement with the spiders and creepy paintings. Then I'd figure how much I could pilfer the other kids’ baskets without detection, which ranged from about 20-30%, and stash that stuff with my basket, by which time the other kids were waking. At that point, since they felt candy-wealthy, I could bargain with them for part of their remaining candy, trading various non-candy items and dubious promises for a portion of what they had, which would likewise be secreted away. By that time, the grown-ups would be awake, and their authority would halt any additional pilfering or trading, and the hunt would move to the back yard. It was an interesting yard for it, because there actually were eggs of various birds and reptiles – which might reasonably take umbrage - scattered among the hidden stuff that was left over from filling the Easter baskets, and there was also a very real race with the hornets, ants, and what-the-hell-is-that-thing for the sugar. My parents generally had hidden stuff in the dark after a few home-made beers so had no idea where anything was or how much was out there except in general terms. So, we'd find the obvious candy but then retreat to our hoards lest they be raided. I'm telling you this story because I'm going to make a case that it's usefully analogous to the availability of fossil fuels to humans. Of course, in this metaphor I represent the USA, and my siblings represent other nations, which is why I wound up with a disproportionate share of candy and the resulting energy to do more mischief and get my way more often than not (the other nations just hated my freedom). I suppose my parents represent the Rule of Law, the Geneva Convention, and that stuff, Jesus represents Manifest Destiny, and the never-seen Easter bunny is the invisible hand of the marketplace. Or something. The first thing you'd eat was the gooey filled chocolate eggs. They were the most energy-dense and digestible, melt-in-your-mouth wonderful and would impart enough manic energy to climb wallpaper with sticky fingers like child-size screaming geckos. The next day you'd be down to the hollow chocolate bunnies, which were also very good, high-octane brain food with that hint of extra caffeine that really sets up a sugar buzz. The day after that, it was the chocolate-dipped marshmallow bunnies wrapped in foil, which weren't bad, but weren't really premium because in those days some of the foil never did come off and you had to swallow it, after getting zings from your teeth as you chewed. After that, things got a bit more grim, because Indiana was and is a humid state in the spring, and all that was left in the Easter baskets was jelly beans and some tasteless rock-hard colored sugar shells with fluffy sugar inside. The fake waxed-paper "Easter grass" would have stuck all that stuff together into a disgusting

sticky mass with the artificial colors bleeding on one another. One had to swallow a fair bit of waxed green paper to get the jelly beans down, and by the time you were willing to try cracking open one of the sugar shells with your little jaws, they looked as if they'd grown green hair. They were almost as useful for putting down siblings' shirt-necks as for eating, and they'd be found heavily licked and discarded for weeks afterward, adhering to the carpet. So by the second week after Easter, the sugar was gone... except for anything which might have remained undiscovered in the yard. So at that point forays would be launched, collectively and independently, to scout for remaining candy in a yard known to be the abode of monsters, in which one didn't usually reach into holes in the ground. During this phase, which lasted a week or so, there would be some genuine discoveries of candy. The foil-wrapped marshmallow bunnies, generally a third-line candy on Easter morning, were prized because the foil did a fair job of keeping insects off and out, which after a couple weeks of rain was a reasonable criterion. Any jellybeans found were more a matter of "I dare you" than a serious competition to eat them. But still we managed to eke out another week of sporadic sugar intake before the times got lean again and we had to sneak a stick of gum here and a spoon of syrup there. So what lessons did this teach which are salient to our fossil fuels? - You can only eat what you find. My dog understands this, but the fact that hardly any large new oilfields are being discovered hasn't filtered into the common wisdom. The filled Easter baskets have long since been emptied, but most Americans think the USA is now a net oil exporter. Not even close.38 The world's existing infrastructure was built using light sweet crude from enormous accessible oilfields, which was so cheap as to be nearly free. - You can only eat it once. Once you eat it, it's gone. Already, the sophistication of this parable has leapfrogged neoclassical economics, which believes that demand creates energy and that resources will always be found if the price is right. I literally seethed with demand during the lean months, but it didn't make any candy appear. I had no money, so it didn't matter that the stores had candy. - Concentrations of energy are finite and unevenly distributed, and mostly found already What is our oil doing underneath all those foreigners? There is such a thing as "abiotic oil", but nobody has ever found enough to make it useful. Fossil energy comes from ancient dead plants and tiny animals, hence the name. After a certain point in time, it's

hardly worth looking for candy because you reach a point of diminishing returns. Which is why most oil companies are no longer actively looking for new oilfields. They know that what's left is the equivalent of ant-covered jellybean remnants and rained-on marshmallow peeps. - The most aggressive competitors get to eat the most candy The resources of weaker nations don't do them much good and can cause stronger nations to take an unhealthy interest in them. Want to trade your candy for my old toys? Younger siblings are SO fun to play with... - The quality of an energy source can vary While it's all called "candy", there is a lot of difference between fresh Cadbury eggs and stale hairy jellybeans. - The best stuff gets eaten first, so the quality always goes down, and always will No humans eat the worst candy first. You eat the BEST candy first, then the next best, and so on. That means that the sweetest energy sources are exploited first and are then gone. Rinse and repeat. They actually call the stuff we built the USA on "light sweet crude." But coal and oil deposits aren’t renewed annually like Easter eggs. They are a one-time deal. The flush times are over, and we’re coasting on infrastructure we built when it was nearly free. - The biggest energy deposits get found and eaten first, so new discoveries get smaller and smaller The big concentrations (the Easter baskets) - are where everyone goes first. Only once those play out is it worth looking for other places. There are no more super-giant oilfields on earth.39 We've already drilled the good places, now we're doing the equivalent of sticking our hands into suspicious holes in the backyard (I know something about this; in addition to having been an aggressive sugar-seeker in my earlier life, I was an oil exploration geophysicist in my early 20’s. Life is a journey, I guess). - Sometimes an energy source is so marginal that it's barely worth using, taking more energy than it's worth and making a disgusting mess

Once the holiday candy runs out, it'll mean gumming moldy Jell-O packages in the basement again, which is metaphorically equivalent to mining the tar sands. We've always known they were there but haven't been hard-up enough to actually eat them. - Energy and wacky ideas travel together. At any given time, a significant proportion of the population believes that Easter candy comes from giant chocolate-pooping pink rabbits and that Christmas candy is delivered by flying reindeer via an immortal fat man in a red suit coming down the chimney. This is a fair parallel to the general state of energy knowledge in the USA, where we not only have a right to our own opinions, but apparently to our own facts. So we say "drill baby drill" as though the process of drilling creates oil reservoirs, and when oil prices go up we assume it's a conspiracy of some kind. We think about energy in the same magical terms really young kids think about candy, while being similarly uncertain as to its origin and prospects. - no kid saves his good candy It's not human nature to save stuff for the future, and it's even less human nature to save for the future when you're in the throes of an energy rush. We know that it's a long long time until the next sugar holiday, but we don't care. Candy! - nobody worries about diabetes until after they have it. We believe what we want to believe.

The Moon, Cheese and Cleverness

Humans have visited the moon, and many of us enjoy imagining humans eventually travelling to other places in the solar system. There have been proposals for permanent moon bases, and it turns out that the way we think about such plans can provide insights into some of the energy and materials realities we’ve recently come to take for granted. This essay will describe what might be called the "fallacy of cleverness" - basically the mistaken belief that human cleverness is the ultimate basis for our economy and survival, a central assumption currently underpinning our economic systems. The idea of visiting other planets provides a good demonstration of our unconscious assumptions. On the moon, an unprotected human's blood would boil at our usual 98.6 degree body temperature, because water doesn't exist as a liquid in a vacuum. If we jump into the air, we travel as if in slow-motion under the 1/6 G gravity field of the moon. If we drop a feather and a hammer, they will hit the ground at the same time. If we make a footprint in the soil, it may still be there thousands of years later, looking fresh. It's a very different place, and intuitions about how things happen aren't applicable when one is on the moon. The temperature can be -300F at night and +200F in the daytime. About every three minutes we'd see a flash of light in one eye or another from a cosmic ray blasting through our eyeball from some angle, unscreened by an atmosphere. Again, it's a very different place subject to different rules, which is why astronauts had to train a long time to unlearn earth habits that might get them killed on the moon.40

These issues become very relevant if one thinks about doing more than just landing on the moon and driving a golf cart around to sightsee. Some really brilliant people - astrophysicist Stephen Hawking for one - have advanced the notion that we will need to move to Mars since we're making the earth unlivable (due to eventual climate and other changes). They are correct that we are degrading earth's natural systems, but incorrect that it's within our capacity to terraform another world, for reasons including but not limited to those above. What are some of the issues beyond the simple (or not really simple at all) ‘getting there and landing’? Well, you'd need to dig underground some ways to provide radiation protection so your lunar crews didn't all get genetic damage and shorten their lives due to cancer. But how do you dig? Well, you'd need digging implements, and there's no way to build them on the moon, so you'd need to bring them with you. Oh, and spare parts, and a lab to fix them in. And you'd need power for them. No fuels lying around, so you'd either need to ship in some sort of power like a nuclear reactor - difficult - or solar panels, which would also have to be made on earth and shipped to the moon very delicately. If one breaks, you don't order another one, you do without. And you have to bring your air and food with you. Want to reclaim oxygen from the soil? You need to find and mine lunar ice, which may be deep underground, which requires a lot more power. Want to grow some of your own food? You need bigger caverns and lights, more water, etc., though that will provide something else that's good to have: oxygen. So, it turns out that just to dig a hole in the ground big enough to live in would probably cost trillions of dollars. Those trillions of dollars, in turn, would come from the economies of earth by NOT DOING trillions of dollars’ worth of other stuff, like fixing bridges and making peoples' lives easier. Furthermore, it turns out that the only way of building things on the moon is to bring pre-built energy and complexity from the earth. There would be essentially zero prospect of ever building and fueling nuclear reactors, or making solar panels, on the moon itself. For that you need fossil energy and a geologically active world which has concentrated materials for you to use. Indeed, building a moon base, if we decided to do so, would probably be done with 98% earth-sourced fossil energy or not done at all. And it wouldn’t be cheap. Even best-case, once a huge earth infrastructure was built to do huge numbers of large-scale rocket launches, it’d cost between roughly $50,000-200,000 per pound to get stuff from the earth to the moon. We visited the moon in an era of much cheaper energy and have so far found fiscal excuses not to go back because money has become our (imperfect) yardstick for energy cost these days. Life taps energy flows. Energy is what creates life, movement, food and manufactured stuff. That's what makes it possible for us to live, and what makes it possible for our
