technology to move things around and transform them. There is cleverness involved in inventing technology, but most technology is about using cheap fossil energy in ever-more creative ways. Cleverness has been used in finding energy flows to tap, and then inventing technology to use it to offer brain-pleasing activities. Cleverness itself is only valuable to an organism under very special circumstances, which may be one reason why large brains have rarely evolved in the last 4 billion years. Cleverness to find energy only works when there is energy around to be found, and some practical way to put cleverness to work. An astronaut stranded on the moon would soon die even if she had an IQ of 300, because cleverness isn't magic. Cleverness allows us to gradually increase human knowledge and collaborate with other humans, working with what is available to us. If Einstein had been born in the year 1800 AD, he might have made a minor name for himself as a clever guy, but he would not have discovered relativity. The general advance of human knowledge would not yet have given him the conceptual tools to work with. Similarly, Darwin wouldn’t have discovered evolution by natural selection without Britain’s new energy surplus from harnessing coal that financed scientific voyages to South America. In order to work, cleverness is dependent on context to work.

The problem with our current cultural assumptions is that they tend to forget that energy is the fundamental driver of all life and technology. Psychologists are famous for running rats through mazes to test their cleverness. They can motivate them by putting a food reward like cheese at the end of the maze. If the rat solves the maze, she

gets the cheese. If many generations of rats have always found cheese at the end of every maze, one could forgive them for believing that a cheese reward was a fundamental property of mazes; that all winding paths are defined by cheese at the end. The idea of a maze without cheese would be both counter-intuitive and disturbing. And that's a bit like the situation we're in. For the last 30,000 years we've been on a helluva roll. A virgin planet full of forests, wild animals, seas of fish, metal ores concentrated by billions of years of geologic history, and easily-accessible fossil carbon and hydrocarbons that burn. During that time, cleverness and super-socialization of human populations have allowed us to jump outside our original evolutionary niche and capture larger and larger portions of the biosphere for our own use. Yet such super-socialization is achieved not with cleverness, but with shared belief in abstract - even crazy- organizing principles. Apes and humans in prehistory were never able to cooperate in groups larger than the number of close relationships which could be maintained, which in primates is proportional to brain size and for humans is about 150.40 Yet the breakthrough of believing in the beliefs of others meant that reliable cooperation could extend outside such a tiny tribe. It made possible abstract currencies, trade, and large alliances, and in the process made Homo sapiens into a much larger and more formidable superorganism. The important thing was not whether the beliefs were true (they in theory could be entirely delusional, but whether others would not easily abandon these beliefs. Thus, alongside cleverness evolved an unwillingness to easily change our organizing beliefs. This worked great for killing off the other species of humans and about all other large animals we came across. The only protection against humans was to be smaller, faster, and terrified. Yet it became a problem once we were in control of the whole planet, as we now are. So these days a core part of our durable shared beliefs is that (1) cleverness is the fundamental resource of humanity; (2) that all mazes can be solved by sufficient cleverness; and (3) that the world, and universe, is organized in such a way that there will always be cheese at the end of each maze. That is, we believe - without saying so directly - that the laws of the universe were designed in such a way that our species faces no fundamental barriers between designing flint arrowheads and using antimatter to travel through wormholes in starships. With due apologies to religions, this is a religious belief; based on faith not fact. Of course, this metaphorical cheese we're talking about is non-renewable resources and particularly low-entropy energy sources. Over the last couple hundred years - since utilizing fossil energy - we have seemed a lot cleverer to ourselves. That's

because we had an untapped planet, hundreds of millions of years of concentrated solar energy in coal, oil, and gas, and a healthy and diverse natural world of bounty. And now our beliefs about our world would take for granted unlimited energy and materials, and complex social and physical networks which have been slowly built up for hundreds of years. Yet the way we've gotten to a 7+ billion population has been by pumping fossil water and using fossil energy to grow more food. Called the "green revolution," it basically uses fossil energy and technology to keep alive far more people at once than would otherwise be supportable. So as long as we have unlimited energy and fresh water, we're okay, right? Of course, as we bend energy to our own purposes, we need a lot of technology, which in turn requires a great deal of materials. We've used up most of the pure, cheap metal ores and fertilizers, which means it now takes ever-more energy to purify the remaining materials. But still, if we have unlimited energy we're okay, right? If you have enough energy you can get around the problem of poor-quality raw materials. If we wipe out fish populations nearby, we can dredge the ocean floor of the most remote parts of the earth... for a while. Technology and energy make it possible. But it's not just energy. If we want to build a modern wind turbine, we need neodymium from China. In order for that to happen, we need a complex network of financial agreements, fossil-fueled mines, and people willing to go down into the mines and refine the ores. That all needs to be purified and shipped to its destination. We assume that will continue to exist, because like the rats, we assume that cleverness + time = cheese. Presumably, rat economists would poo-pooh the idea of cheese-less mazes, since the invisible hand has always allocated cheese in response to cleverness in their lifetimes. In our case, we additionally believe that cleverness + time = wealth. Like the rat economists, our economists now (with serious faces) predict that by the year 2075, the USA will produce the same GDP now produced by the entire world.41 This is not some borderline guess, this is what the chief economists predict as reality. They can do this because they are entirely in the thrall of the belief in belief which tied our tribes together so long ago. And they believe that belief is more fundamental than energy and resources. In this case, the belief is in economic theories which state that demand creates energy and money runs the world. Indeed, that belief is what is currently determining the agenda of nations and the fate of your children, should you choose to have them. Despite it being, well, absurd.

Presumably, our moon-base plans were flawed from the outset because we didn't think to send economists, who would make the moon into a habitable world using only belief and the free market. Oxygen, water, power and resources would pop into existence when demand was sufficient and be allocated fairly among the moon's ever-growing population. Yep. But we could learn a thing or two from the idea of a moon base. Energy and resources have to come from somewhere, and they need a built infrastructure to work. There are many - indeed most - situations in the universe where cleverness won't accomplish anything, because there is no cheese to find. If you're on the moon building a radiation shelter and your power-drill breaks, and you don't have a spare transmission, you die. If on earth, you order another one over the internet and just do something else until it arrives via FedEx a day or two later. So, we tend to think about projects from the point of view of what we're used to, and not from conditions we might have on the moon. And that's the case with energy. Despite there being more and smarter scientists alive than ever before in history, there have been no new kinds of usable energy discovered in nearly 100 years, and none are expected. Everything that our industrial system now produces is an outgrowth of fossil energy, even windmills, solar panels, and nuclear plants. Yet fossil energy and its side-effects are inexorably degrading the living world's resources and making it less habitable. Our scientists are clever enough to see that disaster is looming on many fronts, yet our belief in belief causes us to adhere to the organizing myths of our tribes instead of our scientists. Thus, we simultaneously ignore our scientists’ best advice while assuming a higher price signal will naturally spur discovery of unlimited energy and resources forever. Good scientific news must be true, and bad scientific news must be false. Right? Until recently, our main energy forecasting institution (EIA) based their future energy supply forecasts on demand – however much demand would be expected to achieve a level of growth (population and consumption) 20 years in the future, that was how much energy would arrive!42 What could possibly go wrong? Our analogy with the moon is not complete fantasy. If one was given the challenge of building and maintaining a new power infrastructure with zero reliance on fossil energy, one would face many of the same problems of our moon-base astronauts. If one tried to build solar panels from scratch (even here on earth) using only solar panel output, it likely couldn't be done; the same is true for all other "renewable" energy devices, which are better thought of as "fossil fuel extenders" in the sense that the product "hamburger helper" will stretch a pound of hamburger by padding it out with macaroni.

Yet the fate of our societies depends on making such a switch and making it smoothly and quickly. Currently, we're still listening to the myths of economists who think demand creates energy, and, of even more concern, we each believe the things about energy that our friends believe. We are likely to find out the difference between cleverness and wisdom, the hard way.

GDP, Jobs, and Fossil Largesse

“Smokestacks darkened the skies. No place was sacred, no landscape safe from the insatiable creature’s thirst for more energy to serve his God of Growth.”

from Overdevelopment, Overconsumption, Overshoot

First, some review of relevant points from earlier in semester:

BASIC 1. Fossil carbon compounds are incredibly energy dense, as their formation and

processing was done by geologic forces over deep time. One barrel of oil contains about 1700 kWh of work potential.43 Compared to an average human work day where 0.6kWh is generated, one barrel of oil, currently costing under $50 to global citizens, contains about 10.5 years of human labor equivalence (4.5 years after conversion losses).44

As such, these ‘fossil slaves’ are thousands of times cheaper than human labor. Applying large amounts of these ‘workers’ to tasks humans used to do manually or with animals has generated a gargantuan invisible labor force subsidizing humanity – building the scale and complexity of our industry, population, wages, profits, etc.

GDP - what nations aspire to - is a measure of finished goods and services

generated in an economy. It is strongly correlated with energy use -since 1965, an additional 100 units of economic growth has required on average 99.5 additional units of energy.45 'Burning stuff' (measuring how much primary energy

is consumed) is a reasonable first approximation for GDP globally. GDB -Gross Domestic Burning.

Thus, outside of nuclear and hydro, ~98.5% of ‘labor’ in human economies is

done by oil, coal and natural gas (measured by joules of output). Due to this cheap embodied labor residing in fossil carbon compounds, the average human being in 2016 enjoys 14x the goods and services as the average human being in the year 1800. (the average American=> 49x).

Regionally and nationally this relationship can decouple if the ‘heavy lifting’ of

industrialization is done elsewhere, and the goods (and embodied energy) imported (e.g.,. from China). The relationship between global energy use (which is ~87% fossil fuel based) and GDP over the past 50+ years remains tightly linked.46

The common political mantra that higher GDP creates social benefits by lifting

all boats has become suspect since the 2008 recession and ‘recovery.’ For the first time in the history of the USA, we now have more bartenders and waitresses than manufacturing jobs.47 In order to maximize dollar profits, it often makes more sense for corporations to mechanize and hire ‘fossil slaves’ than to hire ‘real workers.’ Real income peaked in the USA around 1970 for the bottom 50% of wage earners.48

GDP only measures the 'goods' and doesn’t measure the 'bads' (externalities, social malaise, extinctions, pollution). Actually, natural disasters like oil spills and hurricanes are ostensibly great for GDP\*\* because we have to build and burn more stuff to replace the damaged areas. (\*\*Note, only to a point – once a country – e.g. currently Haiti or the Philippines - cannot afford to replace what was lost, then natural disasters become a sharp negative to GDP as infrastructure underpinning future GDP is lost and can’t be rebuilt)

Since 2015, the tight correlation between energy use and global GDP

be eroding – we have grown at 3% but only grown energy use by 1.2% in those years. This is giving hope that we can grow the economy without corresponding growth in resource consumption – termed ‘decoupling’. There are several temporary factors contributing to this phenomenon a) an asset bubble leading to capital gains not using resources (yet), b) a shift from coal to more efficient natural gas (byproduct of shale oil drilling), c) some small shift of renewably generated electricity, and d) some very small efficiency gains. But the largest

discrepancy is in the GDP and inflation numbers themselves, overstating the former and understating the latter. In the intermediate (and long) term, there can be no significant decoupling from GDP and energy use because everything that generates GDP requires energy.

On an ‘empty planet,’ pursuing GDP in order to gainfully employ people (and

distribute money so they could buy needs and wants) seemed to make sense. However, on an ecologically full planet pursuing GDP with no other long-term plan is using up precious natural capital stocks just to maintain momentum and provide people brain-pleasing neurotransmitters.

There are numerous alternative measures to GDP that incorporate well-being

and happiness and subtract environmental ills. But it won’t be easy to switch objectives from GDP to e.g. G.P.I. or G.H.I. (Genuine Progress or Happiness Index) because the present creditors will expect to be paid back in real GDP ($) rather than happiness certificates. Still, over time, strict metrics of success based on consumption alone are likely to change.

There will likely be a growing disparity between ‘jobs’ (occupations that provide

income and contribute to the global human heat engine) and ‘work’ (those tasks that need to be accomplished by individuals and society to procure and maintain basic needs). However, at 2018 USA wage rates, moving from $20 per barrel (the long-run average cost for oil), to $150 per barrel, the army of energy slaves declines from 22,000 per barrel to under 3,000 – meaning at $150/barrel, we’ll get 7.5 times less useful work! At this price, the economy shrinks and therefore much more work needs to be accomplished via efficiency improvements, real humans, or making do with less.

Our institutions and financial systems are based on expectations of continued

GDP growth perpetually into the future. Current OECD (2015) forecasts are for more than a tripling of the physical size of the world economy by 2050. No serious government or institution entity forecasts the end of growth this century (at least not publicly).

Okay. Let’s unpack all of this a bit.

Often in the news today, you'll hear people talking about job growth and job creation like it's a good thing. Everybody wants a good job, right? The more jobs we have to do, the better off we are!

Yet if you kick open an anthill or a beehive, the insects will not be grateful for the sudden boost in job creation, and they will effectively utilize the cross-species

language of biting and stinging to inform you of this opinion. From this we may infer that insects don't understand economics.

Alternately, it could it be that ants - having honed their behaviors for 130 million years and having attained a total biomass we have only recently (and temporarily) matched - might be in tune with some deep realities about jobs, energy, and the embodied cost of building complexity.

Because this is Reality 101, let’s ask some basic questions. What ARE jobs, really? How do they relate to energy and wealth? How do we keep track of whether we’re richer or poorer? We all kinda feel like we know. And (as a general rule) whenever “we kinda feel that we know” is the case, we should probably take a closer look.

To do so, we’ll first need to add a few things to our story about ants. We need to revisit our invisible energy slaves, discover what “freaks out” capuchin monkeys, and think about what wealth actually is.

Energy Slaves again As you recall - and as we’ll discuss in greater detail as the course goes on - every American has over 250 invisible energy slaves working 24/7 for them.49 That is, the labor equivalent of 250 human workers, 24/7, every day of the year, mostly derived from burning fossil carbon and hydrocarbons (54 barrel of oil equivalents of oil, coal and natural gas, X 4.5 years of human labor energy output in each-this number increases by 10-20 barrels if we include the energy embodied in our imported goods). Every American thus has a veritable army of invisible servants, which is why even those below the official poverty line live, for the most part, lives far more comfortable and lavish with respect to energy and stuff than kings and queens of old (but obviously not as high in social status). Being long dead and pulled from the ground - and thus a bit zombie-esque - these energy slaves don’t complain, don’t sleep, and don’t need to be fed. However, as we are increasingly learning, they do inhale, exhale, and leave behind waste. Since they’re invisible, we don’t think about these fossil helpers any more than we think about nitrogen (which happens to be 78% of what we breathe in, but hey, it’s just “there”, so why think about it?) Same with our 250 energy helpers. The extent we think about them is when we fill up at the pump or pay our electric bill – and then only as an outlay of our limited dollars.

We use the “slave” metaphor because it’s really a very good one, despite its pejorative connotation. Energy slaves do exactly the sort of things that human slaves and domestic animals previously did: things that fulfilled their masters’ needs and whims. And they do them faster. And cheaper. Indeed, it probably wasn’t a big coincidence that the world (and the USA) got around to freeing most of its human slaves only once industrialization started offering cheaper carbon labor replacements.

The other reason it is apt – our culture is still discussing the ramifications of slavery 150 years ago today – and our ancestors will still be discussing the impact of fossil slaves – both the lack of their labor and the impact of their exhalation – 150 years from now.

The things we value are created bya combination of human and energy-slave work plus natural capital (minerals and ores, soils and forests, etc.). There are huge amounts of embedded energy in the creation and operation of something like an iPad and the infrastructure which makes it work. When we tap our screen to view a kittycat picture, the image is pulled from a furiously spinning hard drive which may be halfway around the planet, propelled by some fossil slaves, and routed through data centers which are likewise fueled. The internet -via laptops and cell phones -uses over a seventh of the world’s electricity - that’s a lot of energy slaves.50 The infrastructure itself has taken decades to build, and requires constantly increasing energy to maintain. But we don’t think much about that either.

So the internet is infrastructure we have invested energy in, just like a built anthill has been invested in with ant labor. If the internet (or an anthill) was destroyed and needed to be rebuilt, that situation would certainly create jobs. But it would also require a lot of energy, raw materials and work. Ants don’t have energy slaves, so they don’t want more work to do. They are dealing with finite energy inputs in their ecosystem. If more energy (ant-labor) is devoted to rebuilding the anthill, less energy is then left to care for the larvae, forage for food, and defend the hive.

Energy slaves don’t care either way about job creation. (Being zombies and all). But why do we?

Everybody wants a good job. Remember this, because it’ll come up again and again in Reality101: evolution works with what it’s got. It’s a stepwise process, and each step is based on what was available in the step before. This is true both for biological and social evolution. That’s why there are no animals on the Serengeti with wheels: there’s no viable path to evolve wheels from feet, because even if there was a way of designing animals that had wheels, there are no viable intermediate stages. Hold that thought…

Now in times past, a human’s career, their societal function, was largely about their own individual labor and skills. A blacksmith worked with metal. A cooper made barrels. A shoemaker made shoes. Others made furniture, cloth, or other valuable commodities. Farmers created food. Preachers preached. Others did simpler labor like digging ditches or cutting down trees. The relative value of their labor was roughly set by how much other humans valued the end product of such labor, so a skilled blacksmith might be able to trade his services for more status and better

accommodations than a ditch digger. Thus, it became an integral part of human culture that the products of some work were considered more valuable than others. It became a mark of social status and pride to have such a career. Hold that thought too, we’ll be coming right back to it.

Cue the Screaming Monkeys. “Equal Pay for Equal Work” is currently the slogan for those opposed to sexual discrimination, which is usually characterized by women getting paid less than men. And it’s a sentiment which has deep roots in the ape and even simian mind.

If you give capuchin monkeys the “job” of doing a nonsense task in exchange for a reward, they will happily do it all day long as long as they keep getting a reward - cucumber slices. But if a capuchin sees the monkey in the next cage get a (better tasting so higher value) grape while it still gets a cucumber slice, it’ll go ape, throwing the cucumber slice in the face of the experimenter in a rage. It gets the same cucumber slice it has been happy to work for before, but it no longer wants it, because it no longer feels fair in comparison to its cage mate’s effort and reward. Instead, it wants the experimenter and the other monkey to be punished for this inequity (we watched this video of Frans deWaals experiment in class).51

Think for a moment how central this monkey reaction is to the human world around you. We’ll come back to it later in the course and will refer to the term “capuchin fairness” because a similar mechanism turns out to be behind a great deal of human behavior. We’re outraged at the notion of somebody getting more reward than we do for doing the same thing. Indeed, many large-scale human institutions now stress perceived fairness of process over quality of end results. (A prominent example might be the US Congress). Moreover, this monkey-business also reiterates the concept of relative wealth being more important to a monkey mind (and a human mind, it turns out) than absolute wealth, which is kind of nuts, but that’s monkeys for you.

It turns out that our brains are simultaneously trying to optimize two different, and somewhat incompatible pursuits, both of which have deep evolutionary roots in our social species. One is energy gathering and wealth creation: obtaining food, procuring clothing and shelter – basically optimal foraging theory applied to the human biological organism. The other is equitable social distribution and transparency of process. A tribe of hunter-gatherers needed to cooperate as a mini super-organism to get food and defend territory and stand together against competitors. But within the tribe, an individual’s success depended on its getting a reasonable share of what the tribe had. We’re descended from tribe-members who insisted on at least their fair share, as is every living capuchin, so it’s not surprising it’s such a strong feeling. But when both of these instincts are operating

simultaneously, in an era where our species happened upon a buried treasure of fossil pixie dust, some interesting practices emerged…

Ok. Ants. Monkeys. Energy Slaves. So where did “jobs” come from? A funny thing happened on the way to the Anthropocene. To an ever-increasing degree over the last two centuries, wealth has been created more by fossil slaves than by human labor, significantly more - and it’s at its all-time peak about now. (you’ll have the information to derive this yourself by the end of this course).

If you don’t believe that, try hiring a bunch of people to push you and your SUV around hundreds of miles per week with their own muscles and see what it costs you, and then see how little it costs you to buy the same work in a tank of gasoline. In fact, the vast majority of the tasks and stuff that used to be done by human labor is now done by fossil slaves and the infrastructure they have enabled. The slaves have also made shipping nearly free, so any actual human labor we need to produce this stuff can also be hired in the cheapest places on earth (under essentially slave labor conditions), and shipped to us by planes, trains, ships and trucks for next to nothing. So rather than buying furniture from local artisans, we make local firms compete with furniture made halfway across the world which is cheaply shipped to a local store. To a good first approximation, the USA doesn’t make anything anymore (well, movies…).

We have amassed a huge amount of wealth, even if much of it is dumb stuff like plastic toys and salad shooters and things that quickly break. There are so many things we think we want, so we get them. We eat salads with fresh veggies which may be grown 5000 miles away and air-flown to our stores by energy slaves running the planes, refrigerators, trucks, and stores. The average dinner travels over 1500 miles to get to your plate in USA.52

We increasingly buy disposable everything - used once and tossed away. Most everything is short-life these days; when your authors were young an electric fan was expected to last 20+ years. Now if it lasts 2-3 before you toss it, that’s about par for the course. Planned obsolescence exists because it’s “good for GDP.” A new dishwasher now lasts 6-8 years when it used to last 12-16, because they now have integrated cheaper electronics that fail. Our GDP has become tethered to rapid product-replacement cycles keyed to our short attention spans and our enjoyment of buying new things. This creates “jobs” for car salesmen, advertising executives, etc., but has tilted the scales in favor of “useless GDP” rather than real societal utility. We know how to make things with high quality that last, but due to time bias and the financialization of the human experience, such an objective is relatively unimportant

in our current culture. Many people get a new phone every 18 months with their cell plan, and perfectly functional ones wind up in the landfill.

But how should we distribute the largesse of the energy slaves? Does everyone get equal shares? Do we take the total number of dollars (which is the way we count such things) created by energy-slave work and divide them equally among the population?

Heavens no. We haven't even acknowledged that the energy slaves are responsible.

Rather, with a bit of help from opportunism, social evolution co-opted the pre-existing “work for pay” concept into an uneven distribution system that “felt” fair. These days there are a lot of jobs in the USA, which keep us very busy not making much of anything of long term value. We do advertising, hairstyling, consulting, writing, and a lot of supervising of the things our fossil slaves do. We don’t care all that much what we’re doing as long as we feel we’re getting paid at least as well for the same task as the other capuchins – er... people - around us, and that with our compensation we can buy things that give us pleasing brain-reward experiences. These days in this culture, a “good job” is defined by how much it pays, not by what it accomplishes. Many people would consider it an optimum situation, a great job, to sit in a room for 40 hours per week and make $100,000 per year, just pulling a lever the way a capuchin does for a cucumber slice. You know they would (would you? Think about it. Now think about how that compares to the career you’re currently planning). And that’s where the perceived equality is: the equality of inconvenience. The 40-hour work week is a social threshold of inconvenience endured, which is now what we keep primary social track of rather than the productive output of a person’s activity. In 1930, John Maynard Keynes predicted that wealth would increase 600% in the next century (by 2030, which is only 11 years away) and because of this wealth, people would only need to work 15 hours per week.53 He was right about our wealth increase, but paradoxically, we are working longer hours than ever! Because socially, everyone who isn’t a criminal is supposed to have a job and endure roughly equivalent inconvenience. Any segment of society which went to a 15-hour work week would be treated as mooching freeloaders and be pelted by cucumber slices and worse.

In a society in which all of us are basically idle royalty being catered to by fossil slaves, why do we place such a value on “jobs”? Well, partly because it’s how the allocation mechanism evolved, but there also exists considerable resentment against those who don’t work. Think of the vitriol with which people talk about “freeloaders” on society who don’t work a 40-hour week and who take food stamps. The fact is, \most of us are freeloaders when it comes down to it, but if we endure 40 hours of

inconvenience per week, we meet the social criteria of having earned our banana pellets even if what we’re doing is stupid and useless, and realized to be stupid and useless. Indeed, a job that’s stupid and useless but pays a lot is highly prized.

So “jobs” per se aren’t intrinsically useful at all, which is why ants don’t want more of them. They’re mostly a co-opted, socially-evolved mechanism for wealth distribution and are very little about societal wealth creation. And they function to keep us busy and distract us from huge wealth disparity. We’re too busy making sure our co-workers don’t get grapes to do something as radical as call out and lynch the bankers. Keeping a population distracted may well be necessary to hold a modern nation together.

And because most of our wealth comes from invisible, mute slaves we don’t even think about; it isn’t clear to us that what we’re actually doing in current economies is distributing the wealth they create. That means we can now have wild disparities in pay, as long as it “feels like” others are doing something qualitatively different. The amount paid to a wall street vice president is hugely greater than that paid to a college professor, which in turn is greater than that paid to an environmental campaigner. This has pretty much nothing to do with the relative worth of each function to society, and everything to do with how well-connected such jobs are to the flow of energy-slave-created wealth. Yet if higher pay is received by someone in another “tribe” who we don’t directly interact with, we don’t feel the urge to scream and throw our paycheck. We just wish we had a “better” job.

If we reflect on the possibility that we have en-masse simply accepted the premise that the job is somehow paid what it’s worth, we arrive at some disturbing conclusions. Is a teacher, farmer, or fireman really of less value to society than a real-estate flipper? The amounts paid for jobs have been allowed to float freely, detached from actual societal value as the degree of political connectedness of those with such jobs varies. The vast majority of our wealth comes from primary natural capital in tandem with fossil slaves and from the fruit of empire; jobs are mostly an ad-hoc mechanism for distributing this wealth unequally in a way which effectively conveys the illusion of egalitarian process.

For now, are most of us just idle princes and princesses in a fossil-slave kingdom, none of us really at huge risk, and mostly doing things which have little net value? And what happens when our fossil slaves grow wings and fly away into the atmosphere? What will the princes and princesses do then? That’s just Gross.

This leads us to the story of how we keep track of our wealth and productivity and success. How DO we keep track of that collective wealth anyways? Well for real wealth, mostly we don’t. The value of a healthy ecosystem, clean air, seas full of fish, fresh drinkable water… love, joy, happiness and fulfillment… all these things our market system considers to be of essentially zero value. Likewise, armadillos, dolphins, hummingbirds, rainforests… you get the idea.

But our economists have a metric called “gross domestic product” (GDP) which is what our society uses to roughly keep track of our ‘success’. It represents the dollar value of all finished goods and services produced in a time period (typically a year) within a nation’s borders. Since that other stuff- you know, the natural world- doesn’t consist of finished goods and services, it isn’t counted (now if you kill the hummingbirds and make them into ornaments for hats, or turn armadillos into ashtrays, they then can be added to GDP because they’re now products which are “finished”!).

The fact that parts of the environment which have been “finished” are considered more valuable than parts which are “unfinished” is one way in which GDP sets a fairly screwy default value in our current world. It’s a tacit societal value system: anything without a transacted money value isn’t part of GDP. So, a nation which chops down all its trees to sell to another country for firewood has a better GDP than one which leaves its trees standing. It’s a funny way to figure wealth, but it’s what we’ve got. And oh, by the way, we’re betting everything on it.

GDP is based on money transaction (money is, roughly speaking, a claim on future energy), and since most current wealth is created by our fossil energy slaves, GDP is directly tied to the energy burned by society. Indeed, it has recently been shown that GDP is tied to fossil fuel energy, and thus CO2 emissions, in a way which may be described very simply by treating human society as essentially a giant heat engine. In other words, a very simple model which treats human civilization as an essentially mindless consumptive system - a thermodynamic amoeba in search of energy - suffices to match the GDP with the quantity of energy burned.

And over the last 100 years, our burning of energy, and thus our world GDP, has gone through the roof. The number of dollars representing the wealth created from the burning has also increased, and exponentially so in the last 50 years, and since the 2008 crisis, even faster.

It may be reasonable to reflect that during this same period, sometimes called The Great Acceleration, the planet has been largely laid to waste, extinction events have accelerated, many world fisheries are badly depleted, and the ecosystems which sustain large complex life on earth have been progressively compromised. Yet we

continue to grow the scale of the heat engine to accomplish the primary objective of the modern human economy: to maximize dollars and jobs.

Bear in mind that what we’re doing - if we get right down to it - is converting trillions of watts of fossil-slave energy into a few watts of pleasing stimulation inside our brains. (alternately: tiny amounts of brain-reward chemicals) And the side-effect of this process is all around us. Mountains of waste, acidified oceans, altered climates, pollution, species and population extinctions, and mischief. Here we use “mischief” as the general term for things humans do en-route to pleasing themselves, which may include building racetracks, using disposable diapers, making waste baskets out of elephant feet, overbuilding fishing fleets, throwing out electronics every two years to replace them with new ones, etc. It doesn’t “feel like” waste at the time. But if you ask someone in 200 years what percent of fossil magic was wasted, they will likely say “all of it,” because not much useful fossil fuel (or anything previously built with it) will likely remain.

The ubiquity of fossil slavery during our lifetimes has caused us to conflate wants and needs. Most of what we “feel like” we need these days is nothing we evolved to need. Consumerism is driven largely by social competitiveness and suggestive advertising. Most capuchins – er…, people - find it more important to have a bigger house than their neighbors, than to have an even bigger house in a neighborhood where it’s the smallest one. Relative wealth - it’s not just for monkeys (we and the monkeys like fairness, but it feels more fair if we’ve got stuff at least as good as the people we interact with).

And this signaling of status is important socially and sexually. A lot of the things we feel we need are just for show.

And do you remember the “hedonic ratchet” effect from earlier discussions on bias, heuristics, fallacies and delusion? To get the same mental stimulation we got yesterday, we require the expectations of ever-increasing reward. That means more money and more energy slaves. Or at least the expectation of same.

Happiness is not correlated with wealth beyond having the basics of life covered. Most of the things which actually make us happy, joyful, and fulfilled are in our virtual mental worlds, and not in the physical world at all. A Filipino may have only a small percent of the number of energy slaves as an American, but be every bit as happy, and surveys have shown that to be true.61 It’s quite possible to be “poor” and happy. Equally, it’s quite possible to be rich and miserable. Our brains are even primed for it, seemingly.

So where does this leave us?

Well, you already know that our amoeba-like heat-engine of an economy is wrecking the earth, acidifying the seas, melting the polar caps, causing what could become the greatest mass extinction in 65 million years, and throwing our future into doubt.

But at least we have our good ol’ energy slaves to continue creating GDP. Right?

Thing is, the energy slaves will soon be going away forever. In the last 30 years we’ve burned a third of all fossil energy that has been used since it was discovered thousands of years ago. Since your authors have been alive, humans have used more energy than in the entire 300,000 year history of Homosapiens.54 We are just now passing through the all-time peak of liquid hydrocarbon availability, which is the chief driver of our economies due to its special attributes.

Each year, basically from now on, most of us will have fewer fossil energy slaves marching behind us. You’d think this wouldn’t make much difference, right? Since they’re invisible anyhow? But in fact, it’ll make a great deal of difference, because we’re heading back into times – either gradually or suddenly, but inexorably - in which human labor makes up an increasing percentage of the total energy we have available. One day human (and perhaps animal) labor will again be the majority of the work done in human societies – just like it is in an anthill.

And this will happen in the context of a more used-up natural world. Rather than being able to catch dinner by throwing a hook in the nearby ocean, the nearest healthy schools of fish may be ten thousand miles away in Antarctica, and hard to get to without dirt-cheap energy slaves to make giant refrigerated ships to pursue and move them around for us. The copper mines will be mostly used up. The inorganic phosphate deposits we used to make fertilizer, mostly gone. And so on.

Or rather than “gone,” let’s use the more accurate term energetically remote. That is, there will still be loads of “stuff” underground, but it won’t be the very pure ores of yesteryear. It’ll be stuff that requires digging up a huge amount of rock for a tiny amount of whatever we’re after. Because (remember the Easter candy story) we always use the best stuff first. Yet we’ll be going after worse and worse ore with fewer and fewer slaves. And the heavy breathing of the fossil slaves will have pulled our seas and climate back towards conditions in which they were born - a hellish primordial world of toxicity.

This all raises the question - or at least should - of whether it might not be a good idea to set the fossil slaves free and let them rest, since they’re going away soon anyhow and when they do, we will really need a livable planet. They don’t need jobs, and we don’t need dollars for happiness. Yet this flies in the face of capuchin

entitlement and evolved mechanisms for brain reward, which – in effect - take our current societal arrangements for granted. As our fossil slaves eventually retire – childless –we might have to rediscover the difference between jobs and work, just like the ants.

On GDP, Stone Heads and Babies

“Can you think of any problem in any area of human endeavor on any scale, from microscopic to global, whose long-term solution is in any demonstrable way aided, assisted, or advanced by further increases in population, locally, nationally, or globally?” Al Bartlett

So other than using up non-renewable resources and degrading the natural world, what other consequences can there be when maximizing GDP is our plan for the future?

Well, for one thing, it can lead us to really screwy societal choices.

For instance, in the infamous Easter Island culture, there was an organizing belief in belief that all food, resources, and other good things came from their dead ancestors, and that the way to make your dead ancestors happy was to build giant statues for them. This was actually not that different an organizing concept from GDP, in that both exhibit a near-hallucinatory level of disconnect from physical reality and ecology.

As ecological changes on Easter island worsened due to rats cutting into food supplies, it “made sense” to vastly ramp up the production of giant stone statues, making them ever-bigger (and hence presumably more pleasing to the dead ancestors... “too big to fail”, perhaps...). This was a colossal undertaking for a stone-age people using human muscle power and required a lot of wood for rollers and leverage. So they cut the trees down, which caused erosion to begin washing away their productive farmland.

The worse things got, the harder they worked making stone giants. The final generation of stone giants never left the quarries - they were too big to move. As a part of this process, eventually the last large tree was cut down, which made sense based on their organizing beliefs, but was in retrospect not a good plan. It not only

meant their fertile soil washed away but meant they could no longer make boats to go fishing. So, they starved, fought, and suffered a lot as their populations crashed.

For the Easter Islanders, erecting these stone monuments was an example of “jobs” masquerading as “work” - basically tasks done for social-obligation reasons that did not provide actual biological or group-fitness benefits. (Do you think there may be modern-day equivalents?)

Today it’s easy to joke about these islanders and their “giant stone heads” as a high point in the history of human doofus-ness. Yet our adherence to GDP is a similarly skewed metric, equally detached from the realities of ecology, from human happiness, and from the potential for future generations with decent life quality. On a much larger scale, we too are eroding farm land (which these days is largely a dead medium used to hold the seeds in place and receive industrially-produced fertilizer and pesticides), destroying the ability to get fish (by wiping out fisheries), and, because of our numbers, mucking things up to a degree the Easter Islanders never reached.

We’ve already mentioned that - due to being blind to the energy slaves who do nearly everything for us - we now tend to conflate “jobs” with “work”, where “jobs” are just a social distribution mechanism for energy-slave largesse - an entitlement entwined with social status - and “work” is what is necessary to temporarily improve an individual’s, group’s, nation’s, or species’ circumstances.

We’ve also noted that we have folded “planned obsolescence” into most built consumer devices, so they break more quickly and require replacement, tuning their life-cycle to human whims and brain rewards rather than to real utility. Mostly we don’t really even want or expect gadgets to last as long as they used to; as long as we can afford it, we want the newer, cooler, stuff. And advertising helps keep our culture primed for it.

The fact is, we have designed a social system that requires growth. Money –really a claim on future energy and resources – comes into existence irrespective of whether such future energy and resources will be available. Each year we need growth in a household/city/state/nation/world to service and pay off monetary loans that were created previously. No serious government or institutional body has plans for anything other than continued growth into the future. Growth requires resource access and affordability but starts first with population and is driven by population increase.

So, right as our energy slaves are about to start going away forever, leaving 7-10 billion humans without the things they have come to take for granted, our nations have decided the answer is to make more babies! Yep, to raise GDP you need more demand for toys, diapers, teachers, etc… more jobs, because more jobs means more transactions which means more GDP! More GDP means “growth”, so growth is good! China has just reversed its 1-child policy, which prevented massive starvations and slowed the horrendous assault on China’s environment.55 Many other nations, such as Japan, Germany, and Sweden, are now offering bonuses for getting pregnant. In Denmark advertising firms are encouraging couples to have more babies for the good of the economy via sexy commercials.56

Paradoxically, as traditional drivers of GDP growth – development of virgin land, credit expansion, low cost fossil fuels, and groundbreaking innovation- wane in their impact, there may be renewed incentives proposed not to shrink our population as ecological wisdom would advise, but instead to grow it! Currently we are having (as a species) over 120 million babies per year.57 This works out to over 335,000 human babies born every day – compared to a total extant population of all the other Great Apes (bonobos, chimpanzees and gorillas) of about ~500,000!58 Since ‘demand” is considered a quasi-magical force in current economic theory, babies are considered to be good for business (yet children brought into the world now for GDP reasons will face some real challenges in their lives. Nate and DJ decided not to do that for a host of reasons).

China is building massive empty cities now.59 No kidding. Cities with nobody in them, ready to be moved into by the bonus babies to grow GDP. That’s edging perilously close to building giant stone heads.

When you get right down to Reality101 and the intermediate human future, this is actually worse than building giant stone heads, because stone heads don’t suffer,

reproduce, or require further environmental degradation to provide for their needs. In many real ways, the world and human species would be far better off if we immediately moved from GDP to “giant stone heads” as a metric for success (and say, doesn’t that imply to you that we might even do better than giant stone heads, if we put our minds to it?).

GDP sets a money value on everything in the natural world and in human experience, and the most important things are currently valued at or near “zero.” Yet as we’ve seen, GDP is currently tied to the work of fossil slaves, who will be gradually flying away. There’s no way, even in principle, for “growth” such as we’ve recently seen to continue indefinitely, and considerable data points to it ending quite soon. GDP will begin a long decline because it’s tied to finite realities in the physical world.

The good news, of course, is that GDP is a really goofy metric for success, just as “giant stone heads” was (though to give the Easter Islanders their just due, at the time they had no evidence their belief was delusional, while in 2016 we have demonstrable proof that the conclusions of neoclassical economics are refuted by basic science). If we decide that we value happiness, quality of life, and a healthy planet with uncounted thousands of human generations left, we could in principle jettison GDP and do things differently.

It won’t be easy, only necessary. It’ll be easier to fail than succeed, for the societal inertia of a raging amoeba hungry for growth is a hard thing to change. Nothing much depends upon it other than the human destiny and the fate of complex life on the planet.

Learn to see the giant stone heads around you and think about them.

Receding Horizons and Peak Everything

Key points/Overview \*\* 1. We extract various low-entropy materials from the earth to keep our industrial society going, from metal ores to fossil carbon and hydrocarbons. \*\*2. The distribution of natural resources is such that for each there is a characteristic discovery and utilization curve, and these curves may be averaged to give a rough prediction of the future availability of that resource. This is straightforward, yet we disbelieve it. \*\* 3. For any such resource, the available flow will rise, peak, and subside as the easiest and purest resources are used first. \*\*4. The most important of these are the energy resource peaks, because as the pure ores get used up, it takes increasing energy to refine the worse ores into usable purity. \*\*5. Some elements are only recovered only as side-effects of other mining and will become unavailable if the current scale of mining stops. \*\*6. Pretty much all aspects of human society have gone exponential - a lot of hockey stickshapes in: population, consumption, production and environmental impact - which means that energy and materials flows will peak this century. Many peaks (e.g. fish, and oil in over 80 countries) are already in the past.

\*\*7. We're currently coasting on infrastructure which was built at peak energy and peak ores. This infrastructure will need to eventually be repaired, replaced, or done without, but there will be a lag period from the peaks before this becomes critical. The benefits from applying huge (and still growing) numbers of fossil slaves to increase complexity are already declining; and the horizons will keep receding for a long, long time. Yet we’ll likely continue to look at the new iPhones and not at the rusting pipes underground. \*\* 8. Complexity costs a lot in energy and materials to maintain. To continue, our civilization will eventually have to simplify, either in a pre-planned way or in a cascade of failures. \*\* 9. The good news is that happiness - at least for individuals - need not peak. It is part of our virtual worlds; as long as we have enough stuff to be healthy, we can be happy. But we are currently being very careless with that possibility and are not extending the “we” very far into the future. Our species was born into a world which seemed to offer limitless resource abundance. And it was pretty much true. Forests, wildlife, fertile plains, seas full of fish, and all existing on a scale many orders of magnitude larger than human populations. Like a mouse discovering an entire pizza, the enormity of bounty was wonderful and stunning, and it seemed to exist only for us to consume and enjoy. Ever since that time, when the earth’s ability to provide for us far exceeded the needs and wants of our numbers, we have continued to hold onto our beliefs about the unlimited plentitude of the natural world. The problem is, this is no longer the situation. It just “feels” true because our (American) bellies are mostly full at the moment, courtesy of our hard-working fossil slaves. Yet most of the fossil slaves still sleeping in the earth will take more energy to wake up than they’d be able to give back once awakened. Despite their large numbers, we never had the potential of waking more than a small subset of them (enough to give our species a turbo boost and give the squeeze to other species, but small in relation to the total amount of ‘buried sunlight’ on our planet). The reason our bellies are full is that we’ve become accustomed to eating fossil energy ourselves. Only one in eleven calories going into our mouths from the global food-creation system comes from photosynthesis. Most of the nitrogen in our bodies’ proteins is fixed in factories which use natural gas to create ammonia, which is in turn dumped – along with pesticides, herbicides, mined phosphate and potassium, on largely sterile soils to make plants grow.60 (Fun trivia fact – this process was invented

by the same guy who is known as “the father of chemical warfare” for his work in gassing allied troops in WWI). That’s where the 7.5 billion humans around us came from, and the reason the current terrestrial vertebrate biomass of earth is enormously expanded relative to all historic levels. In effect we are using the sun and soil to turn fossil energy directly into crops and livestock, and more and more people (what could possibly go wrong?) So technically we’re testing out a radically new ecological niche: Homo detritivoris. And it’s the largest-scale test of anything, ever. We are not made of the same things our grandparents – those creatures of solar flows – were, and our existence flows from a radically different set of contingencies (in addition to fossil slaves who breathe and emit wastes, we have also added more living animal tissue than the earth previously supported, far outweighing the wild species we have done away with).61 Our modern cornucopian beliefs are formalized in neoclassical economics - the myopic religion of our global economy - which believes that human demand and cleverness, not energy and materials, are the ultimate creative forces. The priests and shamans of neoclassical economics believe there is literally nothing in the universe for which a decent substitute won’t appear if we want it badly enough. Yet as unrealistic as these beliefs are, these priests and shamans are allowed to drive, vote, and wander around unrestrained, and - oh yeah - we are using their ‘wisdom’ as the entire basis for our civilization’s decisions. But the reality is that other than air, we have always had to invest scarce energy to get everything else (and yes, there are places in the world that you can even buy air from vending machines now, if you want it without a heavy dose of toxic air pollution). In a recent essay draft, we introduced the concept of energy remoteness. That is, we are effectively separated from the things we need and want by barriers of energy, time, materials and complexity. With enough cheap energy you can obtain materials, build complexity and save time, so our current reality boils down to the energy distance between our society and various things we need and want. For instance, there is both gold and uranium in common seawater, but the concentrations are so low one would need great amounts of low-cost energy to concentrate such rare minerals for human use. Energy cost and availability is the driving constraint of human output. In effect, there is an “energy trade” for anything we want that we don’t already have. This has been the case since long before there were humans. It has been the case since before there were spines. Foraging efficiency is about whether a critter can capture enough energy in its travels and other actions to replenish the energy it expends, plus do all the energy-intensive things it needs to do to reproduce: grow, heal, find a safe place to live, get water, etc. The ones which do, and which aren’t otherwise taken out

of the game by other factors, survive. The ones which don’t, don’t. This has always been the case with animal species, and always will be. And we are animals. Ergo, we should pay attention. We believe - that is, the “we” of our current societal economic narratives - in the cornucopian story of “demand creates supply” ex nihilo (Latin for “out of nothing”). Not satisfied with that level of crazy, we have “doubled down” and also believe that we will not only do this indefinitely, but that we will continue to increase our output year after year indefinitely. Hence the use of the word “growth” as the target of all current economic programs and our political parties. Growth feels like a good idea because we get there by having sex, cuddling babies, eating, acquiring more possessions, and burning stuff – and all of these activities “feel good” and are very easy to do more of, given the means. In our tribal hunter-gatherer days we evolved a neural “hedonic ratchet” because it served us well to expand into new niches. It’s still a part of us. That is, we require the expectation of ever-larger reward to get the same level of pleasure we used to get out of less. To get pleasing brain rewards from anticipation of the future, we need to always expect better things in the future than were available in the past – in our culture this now translates to more stuff, more gadgetry and more fuel -- but alas, more waste and CO2 emissions! Neoclassical economics promises that this is guaranteed to happen and is thus a good mythological “fit” for our unconscious decision-making brain modules. (An “ecological economics”- commitment and implementation - would make a helluva lot of sense, and could be pursued. But at its core it’s displeasing to the agenda of the gene, and so far we aren’t doing that.) In addition to “continual growth” being impossible even in principle, there are some basic first-principle realities which show that things will be, must be, soon heading in the opposite direction from what we’ve come to expect. Do you remember the “best first” principle from the Easter egg story? Humans use the best stuff first because it is less energetically remote. That is, it is purer (requires less energy to transform into what we want), more concentrated where we find it such as in very rich ores, and easier to get to in terms of where it happens to be. There’s no question why we would use this stuff first. It’s basic foraging efficiency. Any animal goes for the best stuff first. A bear will not eat berries while it can get salmon. A human will not suck cactus juice out of a cactus while there are cans of cola in his fridge.

So, we used the stuff with all those great properties first. But that stuff existed in those concentrations only due to the gradual processes of geological time, in which a million years is an eye-blink. It will not re-concentrate itself in the lifetime of our species. By the “can’t have your cake and eat it too” principle, this means what we’ve used is gone now, and over time we have been left with progressively less-pure, less-concentrated, and harder-to-access resources. As you might imagine, that has been going on a long time, pretty much from the get-go, and the scale of accessing those resources is now unprecedented. So why wasn’t there an immediate and accelerating cost incurred in pursuit of this energetic remoteness? The answer, of course, is that there was. Our resources, other than renewable biological systems, have been getting more energetically remote since we began using them. In the 1930s, the oil was barely under the surface in Pennsylvania and Texas and created huge gushers that spewed oil under pressure high into the air. Today we drill in water 20,000 feet deep, and drill 5000 feet further through solid rock to get oil.62 A new well in the Bakken Field of North Dakota requires 100 train-car-loads of sand and hundreds of trucks of water, as well as high energy input in diesel oil and natural gas. We are mapping the high arctic so when it is ice free, we can drill more holes under the Arctic ocean to get at what was once unthinkably remote and off-limits. These are all aspects of energy remoteness. Drilling holes in the ground to locate ever-smaller and more problematic oilfields is inherently unsustainable – and it’s getting costlier. But by adding more overall fossil energy slaves to the workforce every year, we obfuscated these underlying trends of depletion. Not only did we keep pace with the progressing energy remoteness of resources, we made it seem like they were even getting cheaper and more abundant – like our “demand” was creating them. That was entirely an illusion based on the largesse from fossil slaves existing in a human blind spot, invisible as water to a fish. Yet once the slave labor starts going away - and that has indeed started - we wind up with something of a double whammy, because we have ever-fewer energy slaves chasing what are, by historical standards, really crappy, diffuse, and remote raw materials. That is, we have engineered a predicament\*\* of potentially rapid transition to unaffordability - or even unavailability - all while continuing to believe that “demand” is causing things we want to pop into existence. (\*\*Note: a “predicament” is different from a problem, in that problems have answers, while predicaments are just something you need to try making the best of. A person

hanging from his fingernails 40 stories above the asphalt has a problem. Her friend who has already started falling has a predicament. The first person can try to climb back into the window. The friend has no such clear, reductionist option.) And we really haven’t discussed here the significant energy cost of maintaining complexity, or the fact that it inevitably reaches a point of diminishing returns after a society turns to increasingly complex solutions. Rust never sleeps, and even in the world’s richest nation burning more energy per capita than almost every country, the USA isn’t currently fixing our bridges, pipelines, roads, railroad tracks, sewage systems, or any of that stuff beyond the bare minimum (gee, what could go wrong there?). We assume, to the extent we think about it at all, that our “demand” will fix things if they ever break, as many times as necessary. We even believe that when that happens it will be a good thing because it’ll create jobs.

Receding Horizons and Imaginary Futures

Another real-world manifestation of progressive energetic remoteness is what we call “receding horizons”, and it’s something else you won’t have heard about outside Reality101. There’s sort of a societal “kick the can down the road” response to intractable issues, because we tend to assume that things will always be easier in the future. After all, that has been true about many things up until recently (due to our adding more fossil slaves each year), so the result has become a central belief of our culture without our understanding the cause. If we believe that GDP, energy, and wealth will be hugely higher in the future, and that science will know more, then it only makes good sense to put off any really difficult things for those fifty billion future rich people to deal with in the cornucopian future. So, for instance, we just store the dangerous “spent” nuclear fuel rods in swimming pools next to existing reactors rather than doing something to make them less dangerous. So, we’re “kicking the can” to the rich miracle-workers we believe will exist in a generation or two, who will have some sort of miraculous deus ex machina (intervention) plan for putting things like nuclear waste right. That’s another reason (on top of lack of affordability) we’re not fixing our bridges and sewage systems. Let those rich future people do it with their fusion generators and anti-gravity boots! Ah, but those rich people of the intermediate future are a myth, and a dangerous one. In reality they’ll be far more likely to be impoverished, sweaty, hungry, and hate our guts to the extent they think about us at all. They will, after all, be the ones who increasingly get stuck with the check and the responsibilities after the energy slaves

go back to sleep, leaving things on the planet largely screwed up...the ultimate Ponzi scheme. And receding horizons will likely mean they won’t have fusion generators, either. Of fusion plants it has been said “it is the energy of the future - and always will be.” When DJ was a kid, they figured to have commercial fusion reactors within 40 years. DJ is now 65, and it’s still supposedly 40 years away. Receding horizons. The complexity required to affordably contain an energy-generating fusion reaction simply does not lend itself to being solved, despite the huge amounts of money being thrown at it. There are too many intractable problems which would have to be solved simultaneously, and they are problems of the sort which would require ongoing access to the level of societal complexity we now have – or better. That would require energy and resource inflows at increasing levels, but that will not persist (it turns out that demand, on its own, could never create much of anything in the first place). Commercial fusion is thus - ironically - becoming ever more energetically remote as an actual deployable solution even as we fail to solve the fundamental technical and practical problems which have always stymied it. We don’t need to look at things as exotic as fusion to see receding horizons. They are receding all around us as liquid fuels start to fluctuate between unaffordable and unavailable. Just look at energy-intensive industries and you’ll see corporate mass extinctions, consolidation, cost-cutting, and red balance sheets. When we were younger, half of the seats on airliners were empty and the seats were bigger (so it wasn’t unusual to be able to just lie down across 4 seats), you could fly a supersonic airliner if you wanted to, and the choice of airlines was stunning, with many flying the same routes and tempting you with gourmet meals and free perks. That stuff has gone away as consumer-based airlines fight a losing battle to continue existing in any form at all. If your grandkids see contrails in the sky, it’ll likely be military planes or foreign royalty. The moon is farther away than it was when we visited it. Not the physical 1.5 inches farther away it gets every year due to tidal interaction with the earth. It is hugely more energetically remote than it was when we watched a Saturn V rocket leap skyward over our heads in 1970. That means it’s more fiscally remote, as well. While it would be physically possible, we simply can’t afford to do it now, and couldn’t build another Saturn V if we wanted to; we’d need to start from scratch because we didn’t maintain the infrastructure to build them. It’s a very strange and profound feeling to look at the moon and know there were once people on it. It may be that nobody born since the 70’s will ever know what that’s like. Receding horizons.

As successful as Gordon Moore’s conjecture was, Moore’s Law (that the number of transistors per inch on integrated circuits will double each year since 1970s) has been used to predict all sorts of things which will almost certainly not happen, such as humans uploading their brains into robot bodies in the relatively near future, achieving immortality. In preparing this lesson draft, DJ suggested that we offer a corollary to Moore’s Law, noting that things which can’t be solved exclusively by ever-smaller transistors don’t obey Moore’s Law even if we want them to. So for instance building a bridge, building a dam, building and launching a Saturn V, building railroad tracks, fixing toilets – that is, creating macro things that exist and function in the physical world rather than in virtual computer worlds, will always cost more as the energy slaves go away. That is, physical projects get progressively harder to accomplish as we progress into a future in which there is less “net energy” available to humans each year. If you learn to see receding horizons, you’ll start seeing them everywhere. They aren’t hidden. We just ignore them. Manufacturing wages, adjusted for inflation, peaked in 1973.63 Median household wages peaked in 1999.64 The benefits from super cheap fossil pixie dust have already been experienced. Now things which comprise our human world are more complex, require more energy inputs, and are generally harder for most Americans to afford. A lot of your generation are living with their parents, while their parents didn’t have to live with theirs. An independent life is more energetically remote than it used to be. Again, look around and you’ll see it. Yet you’ve probably not heard anyone in the news, or even in science articles, talk about “receding horizons” and “energetic remoteness” until now. So while we don’t know exactly how the future will roll out, we do know some things about it absolutely. Resources - including fossil carbon and hydrocarbons themselves - will be more energetically remote. It will be not easier, but harder to deal with those pools of hideously-radioactive fuel rods in swimming pools, kept from going critical by pipes and pumps which are rusting year by year. Hundreds of potential “Chernobyls” in the heartland, not due so much to poor reactor design as to belief systems which are unmoored to physical reality, and a disconnect with the ability to think reasonably about the future. One reason among many to acquaint the world with Reality101. But there’s a more basic observation we can make, and be absolutely sure it’s correct, because it derives from the most basic physical reality.

Peak Everything

You’ve heard about “peak oil” in your class lectures. It’s funny that in the existing cultural mythology of growth and neoclassical economics, “peak oil” is considered to have been a crazy idea which has been debunked. This is far from the truth. Peak Oil is a physical principle which has been widely misunderstood: anything finite which you’re extracting from natural sources will gradually get harder to find. This is an obvious outgrowth of using the “best first”, which might just as well be called “worst last.” This implies that there will be an availability curve which initially goes up, as you use the best stuff, and then goes down as you get to the worse stuff. The “peak” is the peak flow rate of oil output. It doesn’t matter what you do, this will occur. Natural resources follow a normal type distribution over time that has a peak and a decline –often in the shape of a ‘Verhulst function’ (similar to a normal curve) with roots in biology and population dynamics. This dead-simple concept is so utterly disbelieved by so many educated, authority-holding people that it’s creepy. In the 1960s we bought gasoline for 18 cents a gallon. Or “premium high octane” for 19 cents. Everybody we knew had V8 engines then, because 8-10 mpg was no big problem, and why wouldn’t you want a huge amount of power? That stuff is more energetically remote now. And when you kids get to be the age of your authors, you will be telling codger stories about being able to get gasoline in any quantity you wanted, any day of the week, just by paying for it. Receding horizons. But they’re not just receding for oil, although due to its energy density and transportability, oil is the “master resource” that makes our global economy work, so is arguably the most important. The same sort of logic applies to everything else which requires energy to obtain or manufacture. Which means everything in the physical world, pretty much. Hawaii will be a lot more energetically remote when you can’t get there by a nonstop jet; the world will begin seeming larger again. As we’ve said, all resources have been getting more energetically remote since we started mining them (or in the case of ecological resources, since we started using them faster than they could replenish). This means that “peak mammoth meat” came and went 15,000 or so years ago. Peak whales was probably in the 1950’s. Peak fish was probably near 2000. Peak (traditional crude) oil was about 2005. This begs the question, then, about other stuff we need, which will all peak and decline as well.

Peak phosphate. Google it and think about it. The “green revolution” which keeps human population expanding is completely reliant on inorganic fertilizer, which absolutely requires mined phosphate. It’s finite and is getting more energetically remote. Even if nothing else is considered, that implies that the amount of food from the “green revolution” will also peak, and that in turn has ramifications for the amount of living animal biomass - humans and livestock - which may be supported. There's currently a fierce debate about just how long our supply can last. "Peak phosphorus" doesn't get a lot of buzz, but it should. In a recent essay in Nature, Jeremy Grantham, a billionaire who also runs an environmental foundation, put the case bluntly: Our phosphorus use "must be drastically reduced in the next 20-40 years or we will begin to starve."65 Ergo, finite phosphate is a prime example of the progressive energy remoteness we are discussing in this essay. “Demand” cannot create a phosphorus substitute. Our muscle cells use ATP (adenosine triphosphate) to function and always will. Peak copper. Peak high-grade coal. Peak societal complexity. Peak air travel. Peak car travel. Peak physical wealth. Peak stuff-that-needs-refrigeration. Peak cheap shipping. And inevitably, Peak humans. It’ll happen this century. The United Nations projects population to peak at 9.2 billion in 2075.66 Deutsche Bank projects it to be in 2055.67Another study says 2070. Of course, all these studies use econometrics that erroneously conflated the dollar value of fossil fuels with the work value. They also assume that if we demand enough energy it will automatically appear. They also assume continued 1.5-2.0% growth for the entire century. There is no shortage of scientific reports noting that peak humans will happen, but they don’t stress the point that it will be a one-off all-time high, and specifically don’t talk about the shape of the curve after peak. An example of physical world problems being solved via social sorting mechanisms? Think about it.

Peak Happiness?

The intent of this course is not to depress you or make you feel despondent. Just the opposite, actually, but we also can’t get to where we need to go by bullshitting you. Our society will need to - at some point - begin making rational, adult decisions if a way can be found for that to happen. Those decisions must be informed by physical-reality first-principles, and by the fact that there are some things that we DO know about the future, because they cannot be otherwise. They form the walls of the bottle of our human and earthly existence, which is ultimately subject to the limits inherent in physical reality.

Again, though, to end on a slightly upbeat note, lets reflect again that we do not have to remain zombies in thrall to the gene agenda. We can chart a course - at least in principle - to a 4D future of trillions of human childhoods on an ecologically-healthy planet. There is utterly nothing to stop us other than the inertia of a belief system which now exists in billions of brains. Physical wealth must decline, but happiness could - in principle - rise at the same time. GDP isn’t true wealth, it’s just a slave census. That we need all this stuff is a lie, and a harmful lie. Think of the best times you’ve ever had, the most wonderful, memorable experiences. Were those good times about wealth and stuff or were they about human interaction with friends and loved ones, or an experience in the natural world? Human history has been punctuated by times of strife, and indeed most human lives of recent history were lived in conditions far more austere and hopeless than anything we now face, as we are unable to wake sufficient slaves to indefinitely keep growth going. Yet those people wrote classic literature and music, loved and laughed, created and discovered. Challenges can bring us together and give us focus and direction. That’s the thing: there’s a real cornucopia of infinite abundance at our disposal: the wealth of possibilities and experience within our virtual worlds, which are not inherently tethered to physical limits. Our physical brains only consume about 20 watts of power, probably less than those of our Neolithic ancestors.68 That power requirement won’t ever rise, yet within our minds many sorts of magic are possible. Myth, subjectivity, belief and tall stories are a problem when they cause us to make bad decisions about the physical world, but they are exactly what enable us to find happiness as humans; to aspire, to care, to create and to love unselfishly. The highs and lows of experience with our virtual worlds – the only worlds we can ever experience – do NOT require huge and growing energy supplies, and piles of manufactured stuff. Just enough food, resources, safety and friendships to live a good and satisfying life. And DJ and I would add nature to that list. And that could go on for millions of years, if we stop making insane physical-world decisions prodded by our evolved feelings. High stakes indeed. Learning Reality101 means learning to see the actual shape of the human and earthly reality, and that’s a lot to deal with, particularly since we have been told very different stories about the world for most of our lives. We’re learning the realities before learning how to cope with knowing about them. That’s the mixed blessing of being the first to learn something new. But this course will get to that as well. Would we be working this hard if we thought it was hopeless? Nope.

Peak Oil and the Immediacy Fallacy

So, your authors have noted that oil availability, energy availability, and therefore raw resource availability must eventually peak and decline. This is obvious from: - the fact that a finite non-renewable resource gets used up, by definition.
