As they say, you can’t have your cake and eat it too. - the fact that “best first” extraction policies by humans mean that remaining resources necessarily get progressively more “energy remote” requiring increasing energy to extract - the fact that you have to discover something before you can use it, and oil discoveries peaked long ago; we’re currently using it up 6x faster than discovering more.69 - detailed analysis of actual global oil production projects it peaking and declining precipitously.70 - human energy use is rising exponentially. The exponential function has a way of finding limits quickly.71 Your authors have also noted that this peak and decline will have a large effect on current human systems. This follows from: - for over 200 years, our societies have adapted to having more energy to burn through each year, and treat that as automatically true into the indefinite future; - our economies have evolved with energy being essentially free with a trivial acquisition side-cost like tipping a waiter, and are not prepared to pay the far higher prices which will be increasingly required; - our energy agencies believe that “demand” creates energy, and forecast its availability solely on that basis, not on the basis of what exists in physical reality; - during temporary oil or energy shortfalls in the past, all hell breaks loose;72 - our drive toward energy efficiency has not focused on energy resilience in systems. - the mechanisms we use to tap kinetic energy flows (euphemistically known as “renewables”) can’t (yet) be built, maintained, and replaced with their own energy output. (more on that in coming essays) - due to the displacement of human labor and technology with vast amounts of cheap energy inputs, even moderate price increases in energy substantially reduce benefits (wages, profits, low prices) to human economies. Though many aspects of our economic situation remain open questions, the above list, we feel, is not really debatable. Despite a lot of arm-waving by those who substitute wishful thinking for science (and economic myths for physics and thermodynamics), there is extensive literature on this which has never been refuted.

The open question is When. When will this occur? If you ask people you know, as we have, you get many answers. Famous economist Julian Simon believed there is no reason we will face energy or materials shortages for billions of years. He was famous, we’re not. Many millions of influential people believe the same thing. If parsing real-world decisions by social-status heuristics rather than science, he’d be the pony to bet on. Except, we note that he was delusional about the real world to the point of functional insanity. The “experts” picked by a society can thus have consequences. Some people, including some of our friends and relatives, guess that there’s oil (and therefore other resources we can extract and transport with oil) to supply a growing economy for thousands of years. Others agree that it must eventually peak and decline, but believe that’s a century or three away. Still others expect it to happen, but not for 50 years or so. Your authors, using the tools of scientific synthesis and correcting for known human bias, see it peaking quite soon and declining steeply thereafter. Oil in conventional deposits has already hit a maximum and has been flat since 2006. Total global extraction is still increasing but it is the costlier, harder to extract liquids which used to not be considered oil that is making up the difference. But to believe in a near term (e.g. before 2030) peak and decline in oil extraction is still an extreme minority opinion in the human world. Clearly, the “when” will have an enormous impact on human society, the earth’s environment, and about every other issue which exists, so it really needs to be unpacked. For instance, the Yellowstone super-volcano erupts about every 600,000 years with enough force to entirely screw up most of North America. It last erupted 640,000 years ago.73 If we thought it was going to erupt again in 10 years, our society would need to do a lot of things very differently than if we thought it would erupt in 10,000 years (though arguably, that would be a lesser problem than a weaning from oil). One idly imagines Julian Simon, if he were still alive, buying retirement property on top of the caldera, to keep it from erupting by the sheer force of his “demand” that it not do so (indeed, one wonders how neoclassical economists reconcile the existence of personal death with the magical power of human demand, though we may not really want to know...) It’s a tough thing for non-scientists to deal with: lacking technical expertise themselves, regular people need to fall back on the advice of “experts”; and not having the necessary background to assess which expert is more likely to be right, they use social status as a proxy filter. This often works: Einstein seldom steered people wrong. However, social status can also elevate a Julian Simon, due to perceived successes of him and those like him during what was historically a very aberrant period (the shaman who seems to have the most successful rain dance generally does very well in any tribe).

In addition, the very nature of “expertise” in current society can steer us wrong. Our society has developed along ultrasocial lines, with extreme specialization being what currently wins prizes and tenure. We thus can find ourselves in the thrall of various famous idiot-savants - brilliant in some narrow esoteric specialty but too dysfunctionally systems-dumb to make ramen in a microwave or do their own laundry - consulted by presidents on how to run the world. Even an excellent scientist may only be conversant with a narrow slice of a small sub-discipline, and potentially be utterly clueless about a systems-level synthesis of science. For instance, Stephen Hawking, the famous black hole physicist, believes humans will need to terraform Mars in order to escape an earth which we will make uninhabitable. He may well be correct about the “uninhabitable earth” because due to conclusions that follow from basic knowledge of physics, but the complex steps required to make Mars livable are probabilistically impossible with the energy and time available to humans. This leads to those such as famous industrialist Elon Musk, who follows Hawking’s conclusions, literally saying: “Fuck earth. Mars is the future,” and making seductively promoted but incredibly naive plans for going there and writing off the earth, despite the fact that any reasonably competent high school student understanding basic logical synthesis could prove that it will not happen. Why do we think that the availability of oil will soon decline? This conclusion is conclusively documented at the archived website theoildrum.com, which author Nate curated for some years, and which collected information from actual scientists about depletion rates, exploration, and the gritty details of resource extraction and complex systems. It’s highly recommended, but much of that is pretty dense reading for non-specialists. So briefly: - we know, to a very good approximation, how much oil there is. - new discoveries have petered out to the point that little effort is being spent looking, and you can’t produce what you haven’t discovered. - the flow rates of existing fields are well-known, and when added together, show the flow of conventional “cheap” crude oil maxing around 2008 and steeply declining afterwards. - the so-called “new oil” is expensive to produce and its wells deplete quickly. Even adding a bunch of those to world oil supplies still shows an imminent peak and decline in world supplies, just offset by a few years. The counter-arguments, are, basically: - that can’t be true because it would be a real bummer;

economists say that human demand can create anything indefinitely if they demand it hard enough; - we don’t remember that happening before, so it can’t happen any time soon; - famous people say it won’t happen; - a respected guy I know says it won’t happen - technology will figure something out; - we don’t really need oil anyhow, we can use other energy. Of these counter-arguments, the first five are clearly specious, in the “flying pink unicorns” category. If that’s not clear, start the accompanying slide deck from #1 again. Most technology is about ways of using more energy. There is no technological way to get around the laws of physics or thermodynamics. Most “good news” tech discoveries reported in popular media are interesting in some ways while being untenable from an energy standpoint. They would require a booming oil economy and fiscal subsidies to work at all, similar to the ethanol boondoggle. Moreover, in a free-market economy such as humanity now possesses, technological efficiency gains tend to increase total energy use (Jevons Paradox). There is no new technology on the horizon, or even theorized, which stands ready to replace the oil our existing economies mostly run on. There is a growing theme – especially as it relates to self-driving electric taxis -that we won’t need oil anyway. As we’ll see in a later essay, so-called “renewables” are actually primarily fossil-fuel extender mechanisms and most of them only create electricity, which presently is about 20% of our energy use. It is undoubtedly highly useful energy in many ways, but it won’t work for most of what we currently do and expect. To the extent our infrastructure could be converted to do so, that entire existing global infrastructure would have to be replaced by an electric one, an investment of hundreds of trillions of dollars (Wild guess that but prove us wrong...) and a large portion of the remaining global oil flow… and that’s not even remotely happening. Electricity just doesn’t substitute well for oil: recently the perhaps ironically-named “solar impulse” airplane tried to fly around the world to highlight the bright future of renewables. Not only did it take well over a year - slower than an 1800’s sailing ship - but it carried only one passenger, cost $170 million, and had to be entirely rebuilt halfway through when its batteries fried themselves.74 It also used two different pilots, so the only thing which actually made it around the world was the name “solar impulse.” If oil supplies diminish, the same thing will happen to airliners as happens to hummingbirds when the flowers go away: they stop functioning.

When making decisions that have important real-world implications, such as whether the economy may be expected to collapse into an unprecedented depression, and worse, during your lifetime, it might be useful to consider a precautionary principle: What are the consequences of being wrong, versus the benefits? It’s clear now from atmospheric and oceanographic science that the burning of fossil carbon must soon cease, in order to prevent a mass extinction of complex life which humans might not long survive, so even if there were unlimited supplies of oil, even if it were created by angels in response to human demand, we’d still have to very soon, and dramatically, alter our societies to prevent an extreme diminishment of the human future. The difference between doing this and just collapsing from unexpected oil shortages might be likened to the difference between an airliner’s controlled emergency landing, and the sort of landing one might expect with nobody in the cockpit. So behaving as if we needed to drastically cut back on oil and fossil carbon is necessary anyhow unless we want to be sociopathic with regards to our grandkids and every other species more complex than scorpions and jellyfish. As we noted early in this book, humans don’t primarily have an energy problem, a resource problem, a population problem, or a climate problem: we have a brain problem from which the others emerge. To the extent we parse this “oil depletion” issue based on what we wish were true, what popular people say, and what mythology predicts, we are following a poorly-designed gene agenda which emerged by random chance, for cavemen. To the extent we deal with it based on data and science, we need to go with what they tell us. They tell us that the global oil flow will begin diminishing very soon, and that the trend will continue indefinitely. \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_ So are we right about this? Or are we subject to an emergent bias we’re calling the “Immediacy Fallacy”? As we’re all aware, sometimes a perfectly intelligent human will latch onto a notion and interpret all data in such a way as to confirm that notion. Thus, we saw a fair number of scientists, for many decades, profess that smoking was not unhealthy. It didn’t hurt that most of them were smokers rationalizing their own habit, or that there was a lot more funding available for pro-tobacco results than anti-tobacco results. The Immediacy Fallacy is a bit different: it can happen when an honest person learns so much about an exciting or scary event they see arising in the future that it looms disproportionately large, acquiring a counter-factual and illogical immediacy. In other words, it acquires such a large footprint in their minds that it “feels like” it will happen very soon, even if the facts don’t bear that out. Some people in perfect health begin fearing death at age ten, because it’s a big scary thing. There’s an entire cult of

psuedo-science which claims that we will soon experience a “technological singularity” in which our personalities are uploaded into immortal robots, and that it will occur within the lifetimes of many people now alive. (no and no). There’s also a popular speaker named (XXX XXXXXXXXX) who believes that humans will go extinct from climate change within a few decades (and when pressed on an email discussion list, said 5 years ago that his own neighborhood would be too hot for human life within 5 years). He is widely believed, and publicly projects the vibes of a good guy, but he’s delusional and harmfully so. By compressing the time scale of some very real problems down by a couple orders of magnitude, he comes up with an inevitable immediate human (and planetary) apocalypse, and of course the conclusion that nothing can be done for humanity - stick a fork in us, we’re done - aside from taking paid speaking engagements. This fallacy might also lie in wait for some students being abruptly exposed to Reality101 – in effect, an entire education shoehorned into a single honors course, because it doesn’t “fit” anywhere else yet. It’s natural to feel some overwhelm... however, that doesn’t mean things are hopeless. It just means taking a bit of time to put it all into perspective. It’s possible to know all this stuff and live a happy, non-depressed life, even a more-productive one than would have been lived otherwise; and these various scenarios will take a long time to play out. Have your authors succumbed to this fallacy, seeing a near-term oil peak because they have learned so much about oil and energy in their research? (After all, one is a PhD who ran a high-profile science & energy website and the other was formerly an oil geophysicist). Well, you can make your own mind up there. For what it’s worth, we don’t just study energy, we study the intersection of all sciences, with a big focus on evolutionary psychology and human delusion. This makes us VERY circumspect about our conclusions, even to the extent of pointing out a potential bias that hasn’t been described elsewhere. What we wish to be true, what “feels like” it should be true, what those around us believe to be true, really needs to take a back seat to science and real-world data. So that’s what we think. As they say in the car ads, “your mileage may vary”.

Renewable Energy - The Right Answer to the Wrong Questions

\*\*Energy is fundamental in nature. Plants, animals and ecosystems rely on energy surplus to grow, survive and replicate. The main difference between humans and non-human animals is that in addition to using food energy “inside the body” (endosomatically) as all animals do, we also use non-food energy outside the body (exosomatically). Our exosomatic reach naturally skyrocketed upon finding and accessing large stores of fossil sunlight. We have no evolved instincts which limit such exosomatic flows.

\*\*As we will learn going forward, there is no sentient overmind (or even fiendish cabal) directing our species forward – but rather, the aggregation of our individual choices (in a social system that maximizes quarterly earnings). The emergent effect is akin to a giant Amoeba, metastasizing across the planet with new roads, new buildings, and new biomass, mostly on the backs of the fossil slaves. Unless something fundamental about individual and collective human behavior changes, this “emergent amoeba” will not voluntarily stop slurping low-entropy goodies (minerals + carbon) until it can no longer find or access them.

\*\*Since our species started to walk upright, humans have used ‘technology’ (spear points, bows and arrows, wheels, plows, computers etc.) to solve problems. In the modern day, technology does some amazing things for us, but at its core, most new technology functions by channelling ever-more energy into complex solutions, raising

the minimum primary energy requirement for aggregate society via the ‘rebound effect’ and other phenomena.

\*\*Efficiency and designing cheaper items a) allows us to use more of that item and b) gives us more money/energy to spend on other things that contribute to GDP. If a product is brought down in price - for any reason - to a minor part of the discretionary budget of an average individual, the common consumption of this item quickly becomes a perceived new “need”. Due to human brains being embedded in a culture of advertising and relative status, much of the benefits of technology ‘rebound’ into more total consumption.

\*\*The continually-increasing access to better and/or more primary energy coupled with technology has created what economists term a “virtuous cycle,” growing the size and scale of modern human economies, which allows for new inventions and adds to social and physical complexity. This in turn requires a larger energy spigot to keep things running without recession or dislocation.

\*\*Because of the accelerating depletion of the less-costly oil and gas, the long trend of “more net energy each year” is peaking and the trend will change to gradually less net energy (after accounting for energy requirements to obtain/deliver energy) each year. As it does, the cycle may become less virtuous - in fact, it will become increasingly problematic.

\*\*The mechanisms popularly referred to as “renewable energy” are mature, viable and amazing. It is clear that such technologies can do many things for our species. But with the above backdrop, scaling them is – at present - primarily growing the size and complexity of the global economy, functioning primarily as ‘FFEMs (Fossil Fuel Extension Mechanisms). (Note; the annual demand increase for electricity globally in 2018 alone was almost double the total installed capacity of solar PV of all prior years/decades). If “raising GDP” remains our standard for success, these FFEMs may just wind up building a larger and less-resilient societal Rube Goldberg machine.

\*\*Econometrics models that assume taxing or eliminating fossil fuels from an economy will still allow economic growth, tend to conflate the dollar value and the labor value of $1 of primary energy. This is a fallacy shared by all sides of current debates. Given the embodied energy in fossil carbon, $1 of it removed from an economy removes

20 of ‘work’. Our fossil energy slaves are the principle drivers of the modern physical economy, yet economists ignore them.

\*\*Renewable energy is misnamed. An apple tree is renewable. A chicken is renewable. Solar panels and wind turbines tap continual flows from wind and sun using mechanisms which need to be repeatedly rebuilt using materials, energy and

infrastructure; the mechanisms themselves are no more renewable than a pickup truck. Currently, what is referred to as “renewable energy” requires fossil fuels, rare minerals, and complex global financial guarantees. It is therefore, at best repeatable, not renewable.

\*\*The direct and indirect solar flows which are tapped as “renewable energy” are principally kinetic energy which we convert into another kind of kinetic energy: electricity. This must be used either instantaneously, converted to potential energy in some sort of storage system, or wasted. This is in contrast to fossil energy, which is entirely chemical potential energy. So there’s a fundamental difference between the various things we’re calling “energy”, and that can lead to muddy thinking.

\*\*Only about 20% of our end-use energy in the USA is electricity (the rest being heat, transportation fuel, etc.) The vast majority of reasonably high energy gain “renewable energy” is electric, not liquid fuels. With a near term peak in oil production, and oil extraction costs up around 300% in past 20 years, liquid fuels may become society's limiting input. This changes the outlook and context of renewable energy considerably. Over time some/many processes can be electrified, but presently these ‘rebuildables’ could, at most, contribute only a portion of that 20% at our current scale. What is our long term goal?

\*\* Even when we contemplate replacing fossil energy, we tend to assume its benefits will remain. Though technology on electric shovels and mining equipment is advancing, no solar panel has ever been created exclusively with solar power. No wind generator has ever been created exclusively with wind power. No fission plant has ever been created and fueled using fission energy, and it is far from a certainty that any of these things will ever be done.

\*\*Renewable energy mechanisms are now mature technology and some have gotten significantly cheaper, but no combination of depleting fossil fuels and new ‘renewables’ can continue economic growth and the system of financial claims supporting it for much longer. The implication is that new institutions and policies for post-growth economies are at least as important as the focus on swapping out electricity sources.

\*\*Though it is essential that some industrial processes have 24/7 access to electricity and fuels to remain productive, for residential systems, this (currently perceived) need is more of a ‘want’ for most individuals, a convenience.

\*\*We can construct a viable and potentially wonderful and stable society around rebuildable energy-tapping mechanisms. (It is a certainty that we will eventually

attempt do this out of necessity if we’re able to). But it will look nothing like our current civilization with 70:1+ exosomatic footprints.

\*\* New and better technology itself doesn’t save ecosystems, other species, or oceans.

Endo vs Exo, Yo!

We live in a thermodynamic universe. For some reason the visible universe started out about 14 billion years ago really small, dense, and organized, and has been getting bigger and more disorganized ever since. If you can imagine something a tiny fraction of the size of a single electron - which of course you can’t - with a mass about like a bowling ball, you’ve got it. But it didn’t stay that size for long. The so-called “big bang” happened immediately, in which space inflated, mass multiplied, and stuff happened, eventually including us, and it’s still banging after all these years. (Did you ever wonder where the big bang happened? Which direction in the sky to point to? Well, it happened right here in this classroom. Every point in the universe is where it happened. It’s just all bigger now, and a helluva lot cooler.) It’s almost an understatement to say that life requires energy, because in a real sense life is a kind of energy flow. It’s a sort of parasitic and catalytic process, like a whirlwind. By interposing itself in an energy gradient, life can do the trick of throttling energy flow while creating and maintaining complexity. The rules of thermodynamics require that the universe ALWAYS gets more disorganized on average. But there’s a loophole exploited by life: as long as the amount of order life creates is significantly less than the amount of disorder being created by the energy

flows it taps, the rule isn’t violated. That’s how dissipating sunlight can turn into the ecological complexity we see around us. Life finds energy gradients, and by tapping their flow can keep itself both existing and multiplying. The biggest source of living energy to the earth is, as you know, photosynthesis. The use of certain wavelengths of light by plants forms chemical energy which can be then used by the plant for general tasks of life. Animals do a different trick - they eat plants, and thus acquire the chemical energy they need to do their more active thing. And plants and animals complement each other well, since each gives off what the other needs to breathe - O2 and CO2 respectively. Using this chemical energy as fuel for plant and animal cells may be termed “endosomatic” energy use, meaning “inside the body”, and that’s the only way plants and animals have used energy, (as far as we know) until very recently, when the phenomenon of “exosomatic” energy use (outside the body) became a major factor. For the purposes of this essay, we won’t split hairs about things like insects enslaving other insects, or toxoplasmosis protozoans hijacking mammal brains, because even though these are cases where a species arguably directs energy outside its own body, it is still endosomatic energy (in the host’s body) which is being directed. What we’re introducing here is energy use which accomplishes goals for individuals of a species without ever passing through a living body – exosomatically. It turns out, with the exception of Siberian avalanche monkeys, humans are the only species who use exosomatic energy in a directed way. Such uniqueness is all the more impressive when we admit we just made up the “avalanche monkeys” because such a species would be really cool. (We did not make up the stuff about mammal brains being enslaved by protozoans, and you have a 1 in 5 chance of already being infected by it, higher if you own a cat.) Deep in our ancestral past, humans acquired a unique symbiosis with non-life energy flows: we discovered how to use fire. For the first time, an animal could derive benefits from energy flow entirely outside its physical body. Those benefits might have been incredibly wasteful, such as burning down a forest to catch a few deer, but they were benefits nonetheless, at no cost to the animal itself. In a way we became sort of a hybrid organism which controlled two energy flows, one which passes through our bodies as food calories, and one which we control externally. A Homo erectus carrying a burning torch was thus a chimerical creature of dual energy flows… but with instincts evolved only for the inner flow.

The magic wand of exosomatic energy could do many indirect things which helped leverage endosomatic calories. Cooking food meant it was easier to chew and digest, making available more endosomatic calories and requiring fewer to be used for digestion. Campfires kept wild carnivores away in the night and/or helped even the odds between their night vision and ours, leading to less endosomatic energy being expended. The warmth of a fire meant that maintaining body temperature on a cold night did not have to come entirely from the energy of food. It was a very useful symbiosis. What could go wrong? Unlike finite endosomatic energy – (we get tired when our bodies run out of chemical energy) - there were no limits on how much ‘exo’ energy we could use as long as there was stuff to burn. Indeed, its very utility led to the success of the hominid species which used it, as we expanded into more ecological niches. Which led to more fire, in an expanding catalytic process. Forests were gradually cleared of burnable trees and the human habitat became less wooded, as human cultures figured out ever-more things which could be done with fire: heat, light, work, fuel, etc. Eventually, running out of things to burn above ground spurred the discovery of vast amounts of burnable stuff under the Earth’s surface. Accessing the treasure trove of fossil hydrocarbons would gradually, but inexorably, blow the previous ceiling off our exosomatic energy use. The once naked ape could now construct tall buildings, fly to the moon and subdue the natural world. There's no instinctive throttle on our exosomatic magic wand. So in addition to turning oil and gas into huge amounts of food, we use our industrial base to combine fossil carbon with minerals and resources into stuff, and the stuff into brain rewards and waste. We build bigger yachts, faster cars, and taller buildings because they're cool (and we can). We throw things away because new ones stimulate us more. We ignore the world in which we evolved because for now we have air conditioning, pizza and video games. If you’re like an average American, you will consume about 100 times as many calories outside your body as inside.75 We’ve gone “exo” in a big way. Yet if we put on our “time bias” correction goggles, it’s clear that due both to the finite nature of oil, coal, and natural gas, and to the negative effects their burning has on planetary ecology, this short temporary pulse of hyper-exosomatic-energy use will gradually, but with certainty, come to an end. Humans will probably continue to use exosomatic energy as long as we’re human, there will just be a lot less of it due to there simply being fewer things around to burn.

So - can technology and human ingenuity indefinitely continue our access to unlimited exo-energy? It sure has so far, right?

Technology and energy

“A technological and economic development which does not leave in its wake a better world and an integrally higher quality of life cannot be considered progress.” (Pope Francis, Laudato Si)

Technology has been defined in a number of ways. At its core it is a problem-solving response that humans have turned to since our hunter-gatherer beginnings. The definition we'll use is that technology is an investment of time, energy, and materials into complex configurations which don’t occur naturally, to attempt achieving survival-enhancing and brain-pleasing goals. In modern culture where survival is pretty much assured, tech is increasingly about neural stimulation, and ‘brain pleasing goals’ mostly consist of “time minimizing” or (financial) “profit maximizing” pursuits. Technology has created extended human phenotypes, which make us functional giants with superpowers - even as individuals. Occurring on the scale of virtual-world evolution rather than biological evolution, this made us super-predators so quickly that other species had little time to adapt. Indeed, humans are now the top animal predator in all major earth ecosystems. Clubs, fire, spears, guns, computers and other tech expanded us from a species with a small ecological footprint to the kings of terrestrial vertebrate biomass. We haven't just eaten all the prey; we've used technology to eat the inedible, using - in effect - land to convert fossil energy into food, and thereby vastly increased the earth’s terrestrial vertebrate biomass (which now includes humans, their livestock and pets) far beyond historic levels. In the recent past we have experienced a heady advancement of technology, from microwave ovens to cell phones to satellite television and GPS. We take for granted lifestyles and security which are, for most Americans, better and healthier than those of kings and queens of a few hundred years ago. It is a deep cultural belief that if we face a problem, new inventions and technology - aided by a free market - will eventually puzzle it out. New heights of utility and wonder will surely ensue! That is, we live in a culture which believes there will soon be flying cars. Diseases will disappear. Happiness will increase. We believe the future will have more cool techno-things than the present. We so envy the denizens of the future that some of us have literally had our heads frozen, to be revived and stuck onto new bodies once that inevitably becomes commonplace. Our societal narrative is not tethered much to the

physical world but rather more to an imagined progress story about our cleverness and destiny paving a techno-path to paradise. Such beliefs affect the way we think about and address problems. Because if everything in the future is easier, cheaper, and more wonderful; it must logically follow that IF we continually put off problems, eventually we'll reach a technological point at which they'll be easily solved. That is, the horizons of the practical will move progressively closer until technology becomes indistinguishable from magic. Indeed, most electronic technology already seems akin to magic: most people would be no more able to build a microwave oven from scratch than could a gorilla; (and a gorilla if shown a microwave, could presumably learn to heat a banana in just a few minutes). Indeed, what could possibly go wrong? But the "technology as magic" narrative tends to highlight the beginning and end of a process but neglects the important reality in the middle. Complexity has costs, and high complexity has high costs. These costs are paid in energy and in low-entropy materials such as rich metal ores. And once you run out of rich ores - which arguably we mostly have - you need to then go to the middle grade stuff, which takes more energy. Thus technology – like anything else in our economy we value, first requires an energy input. Without additional energy, to create and to power it - most high technology would be useless. And not only is high technology achieved with energy, but most technology achieves its effects by expanding the ways to USE MORE energy. This has worked pretty well for the last couple hundred years, because we have been able to access more primary energy each year than the year before. This was possible because we have been using technology to accelerate the rate of extraction and burning of what we call "fossil fuels." Our stunning technological advancements would not have been possible without the ginormous scale and versatility of fossil energy. The ‘work’ that is done by using even small amounts of energy-dense oil, gas or coal is orders of magnitude higher than what can be done by the equivalent dollar value of human labor. In a historic and small-scale sense, technology is about making our lives easier and more interesting. But more recently, seen from a top-down perspective, technology has been focused on expanding the entire economic system to use more energy to raise GDP, and as it succeeds, and our economy grows, we become more dependent on higher energy inputs, (not less, as some technology-focused analysts might have you believe). Moreover, it turns out that this technological march to distant horizons is not just a victory parade. There are diminishing returns to investment in complexity. We

humans were able to dramatically expand our ecological niche in the early days by learning to make spear points from flint. A hundred years ago, scientists could make big discoveries with home-made equipment on tabletops. Today we need a multi-billion-dollar Large Hadron Collider even to fill in a few blanks in esoteric theories that have no immediate or projected material value. The low-hanging tech fruit has been picked. And as we reach real limits on net energy and resources, the horizons recede as well. With human society now all-in for permanent growth sustained by technological fixes, we are creating a nested predicament which automatically outgrows the energy supply, which is the very thing that more technology can't fix. This risks creating a lot of highly dependent human biomass which is “all dressed up with nowhere to go” ecologically.

Modern technology can basically be separated into 4 types.

First, there are the inventions that combined fossil energy with mechanization to replace physical tasks that humans used to do manually. Coal fired steam engines, spinning jenny weaving looms gave way to gas powered lawnmowers, automobiles, cow-milking machines, smelting furnaces etc. Because energy was so inexpensive, we could add huge amounts of it (inefficiently from an energy perspective) to raise the real and perceived standards of living. Some combination of higher wages, higher profit margins and lower-priced goods rippled through a global economy of ‘consumers’ in what economists termed a “virtuous cycle” of humans wanting, being able to afford, and buying, more. This type of technology seemed like it ‘saved energy’ because things became subjectively easier, (substituting a huge amount of exo for a little bit of endo) but the reality was that the total energy used increased

substantially, as now there were millions upon millions of lawnmowers, automobiles and microwave ovens, each representing a new baseline requirement for its owner, and each needing constant fill-ups or incoming electricity and replacement - sooner or later. So this ‘technology’ sharply increased societies’ baseline energy metabolism – these things could not exist without a large infusion of excess net energy from somewhere. Second, there are the novel inventions to create pleasing brain rewards that humans had never had access to (or imagined) in earlier generations. Things like iPads or electric blenders or video games. Obtaining the temporary novelty, leisure and play experiences these devices provide never required energy in the past but now requires substantial societal energy inputs. For example, the server farms required for the US internet use approximately 18% of our electricity.76 In addition, technology created new ways to please our brains through increased social status and sexual signalling, such as pleasure yachts which don't actually need to go anywhere, designer handbags, enormous cars, gourmet foods, and various sorts of non-essential social signals. On top of that, such status and signalling means that everyone feels like they need their own cars, lawnmowers, automobiles and (soon?) smelting furnaces because it's ‘higher status’ not to need to share. And as you’ll recall from earlier in the semester, there is no "that's enough" feedback mechanism in the human brain to curb this sort of consumption: we experience social wealth as relative, not absolute. This ever-escalating "relative wealth creation" has grown far beyond the material and energy flows which are needed for happiness and security, and continues to escalate exponentially. This means a growing societal energy price, and a growing cost to the natural world. The net result is that the average American in 2015 has an energy metabolism equivalent to a 30 ton primate.77 Think about it. Third, you may get the idea from this essay that its authors are some sort of Luddites, opposed to technology. Not so! We love gadgets and use technology as much as others, but we’re trying to present it here as it exists, not as we might fantasize about it. And in fact, technology doesn’t always result in more energy use. It can and does allow us to use energy and resources more efficiently or develop new energy sources. For example, power plants have gotten more efficient and can generate more power using the same coal or natural gas input than they did 30-40 years ago; in many processes, waste heat is recovered and put to constructive use. New developments of solar cell technology allow the panels to capture a larger capacity of incoming sunlight. New seismic and drilling techniques allow access to fossil resources that might have previously been unavailable.

Fourth, however, such technological refinements tend to be incremental, and subject to rapidly diminishing returns as the tech matures. Power turbines are chasing improvements of fractional percentage points at high cost in complexity and rare materials. Incremental doesn't scale well against exponential: it's hard to detect any signal at all of "energy saving" technology when looking at the energy consumed, and waste generated by humanity as a whole. And a 20% increase in engine efficiency means that after a doubling in, e.g., gasoline’s cost from $2 to $4 per gallon, the effective price drops only to $3.60. In a biophysical world where we need energy and natural resources to find and extract other energy and resources, technology is in a race with depletion, and so far is losing.

Jevons’ Paradox – a.k.a the Rebound Effect

It's counter-intuitive that “more efficient energy use” could cause more total energy to be used, but that has historically been the case. There's no indication that any non-fossil energy generation has slowed the extraction and burning of fossil energy. Moreover, in a system of market capitalism such as we now live in, there's a clear mechanism underpinning this phenomenon, known as the Jevons' Effect or the Rebound Effect. Any increase in efficiency in using a resource lowers its cost in relation to alternatives and makes it more desirable, leading to increased demand and higher use.78 How does this work? If a new super-efficient car is developed which gets the same gas mileage as the previous models but costs ½ as much, instead of reducing the energy used in society it tends to increased gasoline and energy use. This happens for 3 reasons: 1) more people can now afford to own a car, 2) people who own these cars can drive more miles because they can afford more gallons of gas and 3) they will use the money saved on ‘car allowance’ at Walmart buying other items which contain embodied energy. Moreover, to the extent more efficient cars MIGHT use less gasoline in total, the effect of that in the current supply/demand system would be to lower the price of fuel so that those driving high-fuel-consumption vehicles were effectively subsidized. The rebound effect with respect to efficiency has a time dimension as well. Nobody is currently thinking about designing, let alone producing, 200-year solar panels. These might be hugely more efficient in terms of total power channeled during the device’s lifetime, but if society (currently) considers the value of the second hundred years to be inconsequential, why pursue it? This is part of our prevailing mythology, which is that energy always gets cheaper. The notion that usable net energy will be far more

precious in the future is a very alien one to current culture, and really part of our core story. But this is a clear example showing that how we define efficiency might make a difference. In short, so far in the history of human exo energy use, more “high” technology leads to more fossil energy use, and all the side-effects of such use. Technology is a vector for more energy use. More technology will require more energy. Less energy will generally mean less (or simpler) technology. So where does this leave us as we attempt to be ‘more sustainable’? We recognize our fossil slaves are getting harder to wake, and we are increasingly alarmed at their breathing and pooping. Those who look at the world scientifically are gradually coming to the opinion that we have to move away from dependence on fossil energy, and the sooner the better. So let’s just switch to renewable energy?!! That has a nice sound to it. Good idea. But as often is the case, the devil is in the details. A thousand years from now sounds like a long time. But it’s only 1/300th of the time that our species has existed in its current physiology. Will we be making solar panels and wind turbines in 1000 years? 500 years? 100 years? If so, a) How the heck will we be doing it? and b) how did we get there from where we are today near the midway point of the carbon pulse? Let’s explore this a bit.

(Rube Goldberg machine by Rebecca Grace Jones)

Renewable Energy - Right answer but the Wrong Question?

Renewable energy. It's something we all know about, right? It's the current phraseology for "non-fossil" energy, and the popular notion is that it's just as good as or better than fossil energy. We agree it's hugely better than fossil energy in the most important way: fossil energy seems to be taking the planet into only its 6th mass extinction in 600 million years, and if its use is continued on the current path for many more decades, the earth will lose most large complex life, and most specialized food webs, to acidified seas, hotter temperatures, ecosystem degradation and rapid environmental change. It's the word "renewable" which is misleading. It’s a phrase, a meme, a story. It implies that energy may be “renewed” and used again. This isn’t the case. We generally think of "renewable" to refer to things like forests, fisheries, and crops, which - short of incredible foolishness (see previous paragraph) - could continue to replenish themselves indefinitely, self-assembling based on natural flows of energy and resources. This is in contrast to things that are inherently temporary. Thus, we don't talk about "renewable clocks" or "renewable gold", because once a clock breaks, you need to fix and eventually replace it. Once you throw your gold ring into a volcano, you don't have it anymore and need to find more gold and make another one. So the things we make are only renewable in the sense that we can start over and make them again if we have the energy and material resources to do so. The first law of thermodynamics says, among other things, that energy can be neither created nor destroyed, but can only change forms. There's no such thing as renewable energy. Energy does a one-way dance with matter as it dissipates, and it only does it once. Once hydrogen and oxygen burn to become water, they won't burn again. A maple tree is renewable. A chicken is renewable. They can reproduce themselves based on solar and hydrological flows (and finding another chicken). A Prius isn’t renewable. What is referred to in common parlance as "renewable energy", then, is the notion that there are devices humans can make which will channel and concentrate useable flows of energy for us more or less indefinitely from the ongoing diffuse flow of fusion energy from the sun. And not just a little bit, but (a) in amounts comparable to what we are now getting by burning fossil carbon and hydrocarbons, and (b) with a relatively smooth societal transition.

That turns out not to be the case, for many reasons. We realize that pretty much everyone in society is telling you otherwise, which is why we also have chapters on the evolutionary basis of human delusion. Nor are we opposed to fossil-energy extending technology; your authors both use it and are campaigning to lower total CO2 emissions. But the way these “alternate” technologies are presented these days leads to some unrealistic expectations. These technologies utilize physical mechanisms to tap solar power, wind power, wave power, geothermal power, fission power, fusion power (which doesn’t yet exist), and hydroelectric power. With the exception of some ancient water and wind powered grain mills and small dams, none of these facilities have ever been built in any other way than burning coal, oil, and gas to provide the required infrastructure. They require materials which are irretrievably lost each time they are built. They depend entirely on the existing fossil-fueled global extractive, shipping, processing and manufacturing system. They generate environmental pollutants which are harmful. They are built of materials which in some cases will be exhausted in a fairly short time. Many of them provide only intermittent power, and they all eventually break down and need to be built again from scratch. But built how? All these methods generate only electricity. We do not have a global electric mining, processing, and shipping infrastructure. Yes, it's theoretically possible to build a replacement global infrastructure based on electricity, using fossil energy, but it'd take a long time and would use the energy we're now using to do all the other stuff we do. And it probably wouldn't supply as much energy as we're used to. It would require huge storage batteries, and these wouldn't be the trendy Tesla lithium batteries, for the world has far too little lithium. They'd have to be built out of much more common elements, meaning lower charge density and higher weight. And those batteries and that electrical infrastructure itself would have to be replaced every decade and every several decades respectively. It is far from clear that this can be done at all, but entirely clear even on first-blush analysis that it won't be done to the scale of fossil-fueled industry today. Since it exists in the deep background of our day-to-day lives, it's hard to realize just how extraordinary the built infrastructure of the last several centuries is, and the degree of complexity and interacting systems it takes to extract, say, a pound of neodymium from mines in China, refine it, and make it into a wind turbine magnet halfway around the world. It's not just the wind turbine factory which must exist, but the entire infrastructure which supports it, and that is a fossil-fueled system. (Moreover, the very phenomenon of world trade requires that all parties maintain confidence in the webs of currency and guarantees continuing, and that they remain on good terms with one another.)

If a billionaire set out to build "renewable" energy devices of any kind using electricity only, avoiding all inputs which directly or indirectly relied on fossil energy, and doing it at a net energy gain, it is likely he or she would fail. Indeed, we hereby make that challenge. It's not an obscure demonstration: it's what the entire world seems to be relying on. Those riding near the top (and bottom and middle) of the amoeba all assume that it'll be easy, and it won't be. It's not entirely clear that it can be done, because many things which are - at least in theory - physically possible are not financially and politically possible. And then the next test for our billionaire: using only the equivalent power from the devices he or she builds, build their replacements. This includes the mining, refining, transportation, manufacturing, labor, administrative overhead and other necessary factors. That is, a microcosm of the ostensible "renewables" Plan A for the world. Except that not only would it need to be done, it would then need to be scaled up at warp speed to supply an existing civilization which has evolved to require growing energy inputs each year to prevent it from crashing into fiscal depression. It's easy - and common - to find experts who can rhetorically handwave any problems away. Everyone, after all, likes to hear good news, and today’s experts are generally highly specialized in a single field. But if one actually looks at the scale of energy now used, and does even simple calculations of replacing fossil energy with nuclear or other energies, the number of required facilities is daunting. Where will the energy come from to make them initially? Clearly, that'll be fossil energy, and energy diverted from other parts of our society and economy. Will we sacrifice to build them for all the world's poor, or maybe just sacrifice enough to build them for the wealthiest societies? What about time lag? If we, for instance, decide we need tens of thousands of fission reactors, or billions of PV systems with batteries, how and when does that happen past the writing of the sentence? And what will the resulting societies look like? Today we drive fossil-powered cars and trucks on roads made of asphalt (made from oil), over bridges constructed when oil was cheap, now rusting and not being replaced. Harry Houdini used to thrill crowds by having his hands and feet locked in chains, then being locked in a barrel, and then being dropped in a river. The thrill was in seeing him somehow survive and emerge unscathed. This is not a bad metaphor for our current energy situation.

That's the problem. These aren't renewable energy resources. The mechanisms we’re currently building are fossil-extenders. So, there's one more concept we're hoping to introduce to you: thinking about things in terms which describe what they really are. Renewables are more aptly named - ‘Rebuildables’. And like anything else, you can only rebuild things when you have the needed resources. Don’t get us wrong, we (Nate & DJ) really like the idea of “fossil fuel extender” mechanisms which convert a smaller amount of high-quality fossil energy to a hopefully larger amount of lower-quality and more intermittent energy pulled from primary and secondary solar flows. We both have houses with PV. We like them and they save us money, even if most of the savings was from state and federal tax credits. However, it’s important not to consider them magic. They are built with fossil slave energy, and to date have not slowed the amount of coal and oil burned by much if at all. They are one more thing we do with energy, along with racetracks, Disneyland, projection tv’s, disposable forks, etc. The mechanisms – such as solar panels - do not stand apart as something which will exist forever. Current solar panels are built to last 25 years. When they are replaced the world will have less oil, lower quality ore grades, and other hurdles from limits to growth. Basically, we have both a narrative fallacy and a time fallacy in place. We are beginning to understand that continuing to burn fossil carbon and hydrocarbons will greatly degrade our world, so the discussion has naturally turned to “using renewable energy” instead. However, the realities of this narrative are little-appreciated or understood. It is possible to conceptualize a society in which human activities are powered by natural flows of energy, diverted to human betterment by mechanisms we can replicate as they wear out using what’s available to us. However, that society would not be the society we now have. Not remotely. It would likely have orders of magnitude less energy throughput, and consequently significantly lower per capita consumption due to fewer natural resources available (since they take energy to mine and purify). As one example, we currently live in a society in which we consider “baseload” to be important - to be able to run heavy appliances at any time of the day or night as whim strikes us. This is nothing humans evolved to need. Our bodies are highly resilient and highly adapted to intermittence of supply, and all humans prior to a few hundred years ago adapted their lives to the sporadic nature of rain, sun, light and dark, seasons and tides. Indeed, these defined the human experience. Yet a huge amount of what we now feel we need is the ability to have energy in any quantity when we wish it. That comes at a high cost - flames constantly burning, turbines constantly spinning, networks of energized wires constantly humming. It may be hard to remember that regular healthy

people don’t need that and have never needed that. And that we don’t need these niceties is a good thing, because they’re highly temporary whatever we do. The enormous benefits we have received from the “easiest to find and extract” fossil fuels are due to their extraction cost being thousands of times cheaper than human labor. ‘Renewables’ (built using fossil carbon infrastructure) are still plenty powerful, but their energy output is lower and more sporadic, and so goods and services made with them will cost more. Similarly, depending on the country, a residential solar system costs between $10,000 and $25,000 without batteries. How many humans can afford solar power (or windmills or geothermal) now? The richest third, at best. Renewable energy mechanisms are currently mostly the toys of the rich, and that may tend to become even more the case. Future societies will need to use lower-energy, repairable tech like electrified railroads, diesel tractors using veggie oil, bicycles, and broadcast radio; but not likely airliners for the middle class and cars for everyone etc. With large populations there will have to be far less meat consumed (with its high energy costs), and even then the post-fossil-power world will not support as much human biomass as we have supported during our brief experimentation being detritivores. Our mechanisms will have to be fewer and more repairable. This isn’t hellish: it’s what your grandparents grew up with (though their world was less damaged). Indeed, this is the best-case class of possible scenarios available to us.

Match Game - Earth System Stocks vs Brain Flows

Simply put, our economic system, currently, is based almost exclusively on finite stocks while the brain rewards we seek as humans are flow based – as in we seek daily a continuous flow of feelings – moving towards pleasure and away from pain – and, surprise - it is impossible to stockpile these feelings – though we ‘feel’ as if large digital bank accounts can accomplish this. My (NJ) former billionaire clients, after making a multi-million dollar investment score on Wednesday, woke up Thursday all eager to do it again in a different or novel way – they weren’t after the millions, they were after the feelings. How to invest in and harness energy, and be able to repeat this process indefinitely, within planetary (and species) limits so that we obtain the majority of the experiences we evolved to seek is now the challenge of our generation(s). It's difficult for us to imagine how the temporary wealth of excess energy has altered the way we think about things. We tend not to think of things in energy terms at all, because recently that has not been a limit that has mattered much. We’re living in very

unusual times. But for most of our history, energy has meant the difference between life and death; and that time will come again. In the meantime, our modern shamans (mainstream economists) entirely misunderstand the energy basis of life and civilization, leading to some very bad societal choices. Even if solar panels were made on the moon by aliens and handed out to us for free, the civilization we could maintain with them would be vastly different than what we have now. And they are NOT free. They are a way to trade high-quality transportable robust fossil energy carriers for slow-release intermittent high-complexity-based electricity. They aren't renewable, unless the only time horizon you care about is the next few decades. And they aren’t self-renewable; they can’t make more of themselves. The “green dream” of a society which looks like today’s, but in which we all drive plug-in electric cars, cannot exist at the scale our media and renewable energy promoters imagine. It runs up against a great many limits very quickly once coal and oil are taken out of the picture. Expecting our current power infrastructure to transition easily away from fossil energy is about like expecting a baleen whale to be able to switch its food from krill to swordfish. The whale isn't built for it, there aren't enough swordfish and they're hard to catch, and they put up more of a fight. The fact that swordfish contain food energy is simply irrelevant to the whale. It "feels like" there are many choices to power our growing civilization, and it's just a matter of which is most convenient to us. That's not the case. Our CURRENT civilization can ONLY be powered with primarily fossil carbon. (and mostly liquid fuels, at that). If using other energy sources, the civilization and society would be far different. Thus, the real question is how to maintain a civilization, not this civilization. If we keep burning fossil carbon, we will change the planet into something which may or may not support humans and other large complex life. "Adaptation" is a very temporary thing; air conditioners and electricity will last only as long as the infrastructure lasts, maybe a couple hundred years, maybe far less, while the earth would be screwed up for hundreds of thousands to millions of years, with large life having to re-evolve from small life – if it could - over tens of millions of years. How, exactly, to switch to ‘renewables’ is thus a very important question.

The First Hundred Watts

As a peek ahead to the last week of the semester, it is worthwhile to realize that the first 100 watts gives a human most of the benefits of exosomatic energy. The additional ten thousands of watts provide rapidly declining benefits (other than

keeping up with the watts the Joneses use). Electronic communications can be maintained on a few watts. LED lighting extends daylight hours for 10-20 watts. Microwave ovens are efficient and don’t take long. A life lived embracing intermittence can be modern and sophisticated, as evidenced by many who live with electricity exclusively with small PV systems or windmills now. How do they do it? By ramping down their consumption in a rational manner and getting to know their energy slave on a personal level. There is currently a movement to divest from investment in fossil energy. Yet if we demand baseload power, we are demanding CO2 pollution. The energy companies are currently just providing what we, the citizens, insist on. But at some point, many of the doors we think are options will be shown to be locked or have brick walls behind them. At that point we’ll have to ask some hard questions of ourselves, and what we want for our future and how to attain it. What we euphemistically call renewable energy today is great, but it will not continue economic growth, not reduce emissions (much) and is above all, merely rebuildable, not renewable. Yet rebuildable how many times, and for how long? These are the sorts of questions one runs into when thinking about Reality101.

The Blind Amoeba Driving our Bus – Why Reality 101 can only be grasped by an Individual Mind

Tropism - /ˈtrōˌpizəm/ Noun: the turning of all or part of an organism in a particular direction in response to an external stimulus. "When I consider the lack of cooperation in human society, I can only conclude that it stems from ignorance of our interdependent nature. I am often moved by the example of small insects, such as bees. The laws of nature dictate that bees work together in order to survive. As a result, they possess an instinctive sense of social responsibility. They have no constitution, laws, police, religion or moral training, but because of their nature they labor faithfully together. Occasionally they may fight, but in general the whole colony survives on the basis of cooperation. Human beings, on the other hand, have constitutions, vast legal systems and police forces; we have religion, remarkable intelligence and a heart with a great capacity to love. But despite our many extraordinary qualities, in

actual practice we lag behind those small insects; in some ways, I feel we are poorer than the bees." Dalai Lama Surely all the information we’re learning in Reality 101 is known by many people, who work for governments, institutions and corporations. It’s natural (and comforting) to believe there is someone in authority who sees the whole picture, that facts and synthesis are filtered up a chain of command so that better societal decisions are made, and the collective wisdom is transmitted culturally to society. But what if this is a false assumption? What if the ability to see (and act on) the whole picture exists only in the province of individual minds? Let’s start with an example. A few years ago, I (NJ) was involved in presenting an energy/financial related risk to the Department of Homeland Security. The risk was real but kind of obscure. We spent weeks on the phone talking to a mid-level staffer about it. Eventually we spoke to his boss, and after 3 months we ended up spending 14 hours in an airplane hangar (we didn’t have security clearance), to rehash the logic and consequences to a larger group. Each person involved could only comprehend the details via a one-to-one human conversation from scratch, not from data, charts or 2nd person recounting. Ultimately, everyone assembled agreed with the risk but the decision-maker in charge was too busy/strapped for time to integrate this risk into the larger scope of their work and scrapped the initiative. Onwards. This was a learning experience for me in several ways: 1) the most senior levels of government have way more access to information than the common man, but they are still ‘common men’ physiologically, i.e. their brains have the same biases and limitations, 2) decisions (important ones) are made for political reasons first and scientific/practical reasons second, 3) there is no unseen, aggregate ‘super mind’ at high levels which knows more than one individual does.

Let’s explore this a bit. As noted earlier, we humans tend to create imaginary entities and imbue them with ability and intent. There's probably no single physical phenomenon which has not been anthropomorphized and treated as a god, demigod, angel, demon, sprite, alpha primate, etc. Throwing virgins into volcanos might actually have made sense, if you thought you were communicating with a capricious deity. And we often do think that in some way or another! It emanates from humans’ penchant to attribute ‘agency’ where there is none. When it’s dark out we are much more likely to think a shadow is a burglar than think a burglar is just a shadow. We see faces in the clouds and monsters in our closets because we are fine-tuned evolutionarily to err on the side of false positives when detecting the faces and shapes of hiding predators and prey, and we're also personally sneaky enough to assume that other people probably are too.

So that's one shade of crazy with a good evolutionary basis which is quite well known in the psychological literature. But there are many other such biases - and the doozy is a related one that doesn’t show up in the long list of known cognitive biases we learned last week. Call it the ‘illusion of aggregate intelligence.’ Maybe you can come up with a better term. It refers to the fact that we tacitly assume there is a higher level of conscious decision-making and reality-parsing than single human brains, somewhere above us in the monkey hierarchy (technically ‘ape’ but monkey sounded better). If we humans find ourselves in possession of important information, we tell as many other people as we can, and once we have, we're pretty much done, other than continuing to do what everyone else does. Ever notice how satisfying it feels to blog? Whatever bee is in your bonnet, blogging gives soothing and immediate relief. Or chatting with everyone we know and expressing our opinion. Indeed, the main function of most advocacy organizations these days is to inform the other monkeys, because we are confident that if everyone knows everything, the best decision will somehow surely be made. We go to conferences and everyone shares expertise on related topics, and there is an implicit assumption that the ensuing ‘consensus’ of higher understanding and intelligence will lead to better policy. The problem is, this usually doesn’t work. It just feels like it should. These behaviors probably worked great in tribes of 150 or fewer people back when we were hunter-gatherers, which pretty much was our entire history as humans. It worked because there was a functional group decision-making process which involved alpha-behavior humans who were dominant, or who had the trust of the other dominants. That is, it functioned to alert our cohorts and to convey important decisions to the alpha individuals, who then quickly made decisions. So "enlightenment" of this small group got the job done. But unlike the behavior of the social insects which is simple, limited, and emergent, ours was often still the result of a single-brain process. And there's a good reason for that. Other than single brains, there is literally nowhere thinking gets done. Thinking doesn't exist outside individual brains. Yet because we each subjectively experience consciousness, sentience, self-reflection, and analysis, and also partly because we have hyperactive agency detection sub-routines running in our minds, we tend to assume that somehow thinking DOES exist in larger entities, and we attribute it to them. "The will of congress," "the wisdom of Mankind," "the support of Wall Street," "Scientists will figure it out," "a conspiracy of the 1%," etc. Thus, we keep using the same tribal mechanism that "feels right": communication. Surely, we feel, if there's a problem with poor decision-making, it

must be the result of poor communication of information (or a competing uber-entity). So, we shout louder. And wait for wise decisions, often indefinitely. We may not like the imaginary ‘ubermind’ we're appealing to, but we get a secure feeling knowing it's there. Because the alternative would be that there's nobody steering this bus. But here's the deal. The "intelligence," consciousness, conscience, and ability to sort complex propositions can only exist at the level of an individual mind. There's simply nowhere else for it to occur. So on the large scale, humans in aggregate either make utterly dopey decisions which display the simplicity of protoplasmic tropisms, or the decision is made by an individual mind, somewhere, which steers the tropisms. It is because of this that in large human groups, all decisions are reduced to a few binary choices, because processing information in a group so large is very slow. The propositions which can be meaningfully crunched down into one or a few binary choices are inherently simple. That’s one mechanism the amoeba comes from (it’s instructive to watch the 435 members of the US House of Representatives casting yea or nay votes on some large bill that has only been read by their assistants. And that’s what they do A slow series of yes/no decisions about complex systems you have no real understanding of is a good description of the decision-making of an actual amoeba). As a metaphor, Nate & DJ have long referred to this phenomenon as the “human Amoeba” (and were delighted to see that EO Wilson recently penned the exact same description). Amoebas are simple, immortal life forms which not only don't have a brain, they don't have a nervous system; in fact, they're a single cell. They move toward and away from stimuli and engulf and digest creatures far more complex. Now maybe the human amoeba has more than the functional awareness of a swarm of bees, but one thing it DOESN'T do is think, or choose between complex propositions based on their relative merits. Like an amoeba, it can contain and digest complexity without participating in complexity. Consuming a more complex organism doesn’t make the amoeba more complex, it just makes more amoeba. So, a human committee may contain a brilliant scientist, but that doesn't make the mini-amoeba brilliant. It either defers to a single mind or acts like an amoeba. The bigger the amoeba gets, the less likely it is to do the former. To some degree, the larger a decision-making group gets, the dumber it gets. Having a brilliant human inside a group of humans makes little difference unless the group dynamics cause that person’s directions to be followed.

We can take good guesses at how this came about. Around the time humans developed agriculture, we also underwent a phenomenon termed ‘ultrasociality,’ wherein our strong tribal social behaviors started to combine in new ways as our population and resources increased.79 The needs and wants of the individual were gradually suppressed in favor of growing a surplus for the group/tribe. Back 10,000 years ago, this meant cooperating, dividing the expanding labor force into different roles, including growers, processors, tradespeople, guards, politicians etc. It was thus around this time, already well along in the development of our species, where we began the morph from disorganized bands of hunters into a superorganism. The goal of this superorganism - the human amoeba - was to respond to stimuli – just like in nature where ecosystems and organisms tend to move towards low entropy energy sources, so it was with humans after the agricultural revolution. We lumbered forward seeking to generate surplus – largely agricultural products - for our tribes/villages/cities/nations. We sloughed along, slowly for a long time, slurping up low-entropy goodies and empty continents, and eventually the super snacks of coal, oil and natural gas, etc. The amoeba is rather mindless, but it’s hungry. (we’ll be getting to this next week) Fast forward to today, and mankind in aggregate remains a functional amoeba in most ways. For all the complexity which may exist in individual brains, its aggregate behavior on large scales is scarcely more complex than that of a basic heat engine or bacterial culture. It doesn't care about the emergent, aggregate effects of its actions because caring isn't something an amoeba can do. Oh, it can do simple things like trying to eat another amoeba (culture), or resist being eaten (war), but any intelligent contributions are exclusively the domain of single-mind plans. Thus, when faced with issues beyond an amoeba's competence - and that's a really low bar - it simply continues its mindless oozing tropisms. That's how we get to emergent situations like hair-trigger mutually-assured nuclear destruction; clever organelles acting in concert with an amoebic agenda. We can individually have very clever thoughts, but in practice we almost always do what those around us do, think what they do, ignore what they do, link arms for the tropisms. Yet we generally consider our imaginary uber-entities to be in control, because what could possibly go wrong?

By living as part of a global market-based interconnected culture, we are each part of a metaphorical amoeba – “smart” humans cooperating in pursuit of individual profits and self-interest, but the larger the group we cooperate in, the less it is able to understand and integrate complex issues. Some other summary points following from that logic:

\*\* there is a prevalent fallacy that there is some emergent wise entity which steers human fate, be it groups of humans, imaginary constructs like “Wall Street”, “Scientists”, “Gods”, angels, the “invisible hand of the marketplace”, or “mother nature”. It’s natural to suspect there are secret societies running the world; this is a side effect of the “someone is driving the bus” fallacy. While there are many fractionated groups who are maximizing their own perceived self-interest, that’s all they’re doing. Nobody is actually at the wheel, and no one is more aware of this than the people who find themselves in the “top” positions. \*\* the enlightenment of individuals itself does not prevent the emergence of serious foreseeable predicaments, or cause them to be dealt with logically, because at levels much larger than individuals or small groups, complex propositions cannot be parsed (or acted upon appropriately). \*\* this creates a sort of “emergent stupidity” of humans in aggregate relative to individuals and small groups, in which the maximum complexity of what may be abstractly considered rapidly declines as the size of a group of humans goes up. \*\* it is not true that the smartest and most capable humans will somehow take the reins and make the best decisions for humanity. This expectation is merely an artifact of our tribal past. \*\* the “amoebic behavior” of humans at mega-scale – analogous to simple bacterial tropisms - is the net result of our having the same “common denominator” gene agenda driving our most intense reactions to the world. This includes an intense capuchin-like drive for distributive fairness and perceived egalitarian process rather than logical outcomes. \*\* these uninformed “amoebic tropisms” are not the only possibility at large scales; though they are most apparent during times of plenty when “fairness” and “stability” can be pursued despite the inherent efficiency penalties they impose. \*\*Potentially, the most effective organizational structure is a dictatorship due to the high complexity which may in principle be modeled by a single brain; but such a structure also amplifies the effects of “gene agenda” drives, bias, and fallacies existing within that single brain. \*\* individual minds are the smartest things around. Tribes can in principle be quite functionally smart if they quickly allocate their decisions to the person known to be the best at dealing with a given situation, and this “fits” well with human modular minds. This is why it “feels like” problems have been dealt with when information

about them has been generally disseminated. However, this breaks down in groups larger than classic tribal size because nobody knows who that person is in a huge group. It is in such a vacuum that perceived familiarity and recognition can break the tie, and popular personalities may become candidates for alpha status even if they’re airheads. \*\* your authors enjoy democracy, but democracy may tend to be inherently in thrall of the gene agenda since in large groups it is probabilistically biased toward the simple “common denominator” emotions of the limbic system regarding blaming outgroups, skewed attribution of agency, and the rest of the cave-man “brain rewards” package. \*\* In addition to the tropism-level behavioral complexity of the “aggregate amoeba”, the economy can be metabolically modeled with simple tropism-like equations as a questing heat engine analogous to a replicating bacterium. (that is, the human race can be characterized as a “simple heat engine” which, on a large scale exhibits “tropisms”, i.e., extreme behavioral and metabolic simplicities.) The key takeaway here is this: an understanding of the big picture can only occur in an individual mind. As distressing as it may seem to realize that there is no driver behind the wheel of our civilizational bus – not even a secret cabal of bankers or supervillains, aliens or illuminati – there’s actually some good news hiding here. One of the main reasons it seems impossible to deal with the human predicament is our assumption that human nature and behavior will undergo revolutionary change in response to learning about crises and limits. And that clearly ain’t happening. It’s easy to believe, then, that since enlightenment hasn’t worked, that nothing else can be done and that the situation is hopeless. Because it still “feels like” once we have educated everyone, we just need to wait for them all to do the wise thing, and the bus will start being steered away from the cliff. And that won’t happen. It may never have happened to resolve a complex problem; perhaps it’s just a strong attractor for our interpreter modules to use retroactive stories, due to a simian tendency to demand “fairness of process” over competent results. But brilliance can still exist and be acted upon in small groups, and by individuals. That’s where hope lies. Perhaps quite a bit of hope.

The Maximum Power Principle and the Human Superorganism

In class readings, we frequently refer to the aggregate of modern human society as a ‘superorganism.’ An entity of 7.5 billion functioning as a hive has 2 major implications: 1) (described in above essay) the topics of Reality 101 can only be understood in the mind of an individual, not by society at large and 2) the global economy functions as an energy dissipating structure. It is this last point that we’ll briefly expand upon below. At the intersection of energy and time is ‘power.’ In nature organisms that have steady access to an energy gradient tend to have evolutionary advantages.
