Some have proposed as a “fourth law of thermodynamics” the ‘maximum power principle,’ whereby biological systems develop so as to increase their energy-degradation rate. In that conjecture, biological growth, ecosystem development and evolution represent the development of new dissipative pathways for energy. For instance, in a forest transect, the coolest area when measured by thermal scan is the oldest growth grove of trees, which have grown tall and have thick limbs, best able to capture and degrade incoming sunlight via photosynthesis and respiration from their leaves or needles.80 The hottest area would be that with the least complex structure on it – e.g. a gravel road winding through the forest and fields. Life tends to find and degrade an untapped energy gradient. Sunlight…and fossil carbon, are both such.

Under MPP formulation, humans and human systems are natural organisms, and as such generally adhere to the maximum power principle and its related tenet the Maximum Entropy Production Principle (MEPP).81 In nature, power is basically energy or calories per unit time. In human systems since we have a cultural overlay it’s not just calories but also money, reputation and others social drivers – per unit time.

About 12000 years ago we morphed from “time minimizers” into “energy/profit maximizers.” Tribal societies at the time were aggressively egalitarian, with an emphasis on “fairness of distribution.” But something happened, in conjunction with, but not necessarily due to, the agricultural revolution. Humans began to act more as a ‘hive’ than as a collection of numerous bands of individuals, a transition towards ‘ultrasociality.’ Such a ‘hive’ was not a conscious organism but rather an alternate organizing principle which

behaved in some ways more like a single creature specialized in the production of agricultural surplus. As this progressed, ever-larger groups abandoned traditional tribal dynamics in favor of hierarchical systems which began to allocate their surplus according to social power, as opposed to need.

To the extent “ultrasociality” worked for humans as a competitive survival edge – extremely well – it strongly rewarded those groups which suppressed within-group competition as opposed to encouraging it. With agriculture, cooperation for “the good of the group” became cooperation for “the good of the superorganism” in which the well-being of individuals became secondary.

Once agriculture expanded as a human enterprise, competition between groups favored those groups which harnessed and directed a greater proportion of photosynthetic productivity. All else being equal, an organism (or superorganism)

with the energy to make more “game moves” will tend to prevail over an organism with the energy to make fewer.

12,000 years ago, ‘good,’ as in ‘good for the group’ meant ‘physical surplus,’ usually grain or other storable ag output. After the fossil-carbon bank account was opened, the improved quantity and quality of “game moves” enabled by fossil energy quickly began to favor those groups who (combined) had the highest velocities of resources and money, and thus could more effectively recruit and organize fossil-slave labor. Today ‘surplus’ is no longer the edible food it was during the Agricultural revolution, but a digital account, stacked and amassed virtually each day in the bank ledgers and personal accounts around the world.

Due to the effectiveness of cheap energy, the elements of our societies which have used it to grow complexity, infrastructure and production have prospered. Pursuing such prosperity has in turn accelerated energy acquisition and use. Our first modern hiccup came circa 1970 when declining energy productivity (lower average EROEI), instead of causing a natural shrinkage of the world economy due to limits to growth, caused us to instead choose “debt” as a way to stimulate continued high levels of consumption, at worse EROEI’s. It was probably no coincidence this happened exactly when we left the gold standard, the last biophysical tether to our currencies. For each year since 1965 we’ve added more debt than we’ve added GDP, and since 2000 or so this has accelerated.82 At the same time, global trade –the outsourcing to areas of cheaper production and reaping the benefits from comparative advantage between nations - increased from 20% of world GDP in the 1960s to over 60% of global GDP by 2013.83 These benefits from globalization and subsequent deregulation of financial markets added further inertia to the system.

Eventually, the ease with which we could pull resources forward in time using debt led to lower and lower debt productivity (less and less GDP per additional dollar of debt), to the point that central banks had to take over. Now China, Russia, Brazil, etc. are following the exact same model of declining debt productivity as the G7 nations. In the past few years governments and central banks have subsidized our consumptive lifestyle with gargantuan financial interventions: low inflation interest rates, direct monetary liquidity to institutions, guaranteed credit markets (housing, student), explicit and implicit too-big-to-fail guarantees, ongoing government deficit spending, ongoing central bank balance sheet expansion, etc. All the while, wealth inequality is creeping upwards, as the top-down government support keeping the economy afloat is regressive. 90% of people in America since 2002 have flat or declining incomes while prices for most things are higher.84 This is the case for most populations in the developed world.

The superorganism has, in effect, used money, technology, trade, debt, and rule changes - anything its clever self-interested organelles could cook up - in order to continually access more energy and to expand the complexity in an almost completely globalized society. We have gone from declining energy productivity (EROEI), to declining debt productivity, to declining access to growth to the majority of people, to declining benefits from central bank policies. We’ll soon run out of choices that continue physical growth, but in order to keep the hive buzzing around the rich feeding grounds which are fossil carbon we can still use “Orwellian productivity”, in the form of rule changes, definitional changes for GDP and general creative seat-of-the-pants responses. For instance, Italian government has recently added cocaine sales and revenues from prostitution to their GDP tally in an attempt to maintain the GDP/debt rations mandated by the European Union.85

Viewing our global economy as a superorganism is to recognize it is unlikely we will ever consciously choose to reduce our energy use in aggregate. Most subconscious human preferences correlate with more energy use (see left). Additionally, novelty, our focus on the present, social conformity, rigid belief systems and cognitive biases all combine to suppress active reduction in energy throughput. Yes, total primary energy use will begin its inexorable decline in the next decade or so, but only after we have exhausted psychological, physical, financial and social methods for extending it, whether we consciously recognize this or not.

In short, maximum power could be the real invisible hand.

Up through period A, we applied cheap energy to more and more processes and grew our economies until energy extraction became too expensive for desired consumption level During Period B, we moved to globalized system of trade, reducing prices of most goods. Also, we racked up increasingly large private sector debts Period C introduced central bank and sovereign guarantees, quantitative easing, artificially low interest rates and new government credit and deficit spending. Period D is a continuation of increasing wealth inequality, but since 2008, only the top few % of demography are making any strides. For most people growth is over, even though gross energy consumption continues Period E – coming up – will mark new definitions to GDP, new rule changes favoring immediate over future consumption, an era of ‘Orwellian productivity’

(Note from Nate: TaaL insisted on crossing projects and commenting on this essay) TaaL: I recognize that the Max-Power hypothesis was formulated by very intelligent and distinguished human scientists, but I’ll exercise my prerogative and note that I do not consider it the 4th law of thermodynamics, or any law at all. Rather, it’s a tendency, an attractor. Humans seem to like finding tendencies and calling them laws, because humans who discover

laws of the universe tend to become respected and famous. But sometimes the universe just lets things happen multiple ways, without giving a hoot which way things turn out. Hurricanes can form: does that mean it’s a law that they form? No. Slow convection is another way for heat to pass through a system of air, water, gravity, and angular momentum. From my point of view, human ecologists have focused on energy – which is necessary – without also focusing on the quality of game moves made with that energy. To the extent all game moves in life are equally high-quality, the organism with the more energy will get more moves and will indeed tend to prevail. But one good game move can often beat three so-so game moves, so energy is not an absolute determiner of advantage, and there goes that 4th law of thermodynamics. There is very little embodied energy in a rattlesnake bite, but it can be a higher-quality game move than two bear-bites. The world is a complex place, and energy – while vital – is not the sole determinant of success. That you have not yet folded that into your ecological theories is perhaps not surprising, since you’re a young species. What difference does this distinction make? All the difference in the world, because the human future depends on improving the quality of your game moves – now nearly random – while your power throughput inexorably diminishes. This is where sapient thought comes in and can lead you to a lower-energy existence which is – at least potentially – subjectively higher-quality, and far longer-lasting. The human gene agenda does tend to err on the side of using ever-more energy, but this isn’t a choice which can continue to be made. The good news is that your game moves, at present, are terrible, so like a child learning chess, you have a whole lot to look forward to.

Section IV - The Environment

Believing in modern mythology, and being blind to energy realities, also means living in denial of the heavy costs to natural systems this entails. We’re living as though the temporary energy extravaganza is a “new normal” when it’s anything but. The long-term human story - if there is to be one - will be a story of reverting back steady-state energy income and outgo. Yet previously in human memory, we have had many natural resources and systems which made the planet bountiful for us and the other species which have inhabited it alongside us. These, it turns out, are exquisitely vulnerable to the side-effects of our accelerating energy use, implying that our future may not look like our past.

Rosebud in the Anthropocene

(NJ) When I was growing up in southern Oregon, I couldn’t wait to get home from school, grab my dog and a backpack and lose myself in the Siskiyou hills, exploring creeks, finding salamanders, noticing all the lichens, the insects, the deer and other animals. I remember always being on heightened alert because there were mountain lions in those hills. Though I never saw one, I always imagined a cougar crouched in a tree just ahead, and kept an eye on my terrier to see if he would smell something and bark an alarm. In those rolling foothills, a 10-year-old boy felt alive. Now there is a subdivision over the entire hill and the creek is cemented in. (DJ) I grew up in suburban Indianapolis in the early 50's, with my backyard abutting a little piece of the Pleistocene: Bacon Swamp. Other than the lack of wall-busting multi-ton megafauna it was largely unchanged from thousands of years before, and had it lasted another decade it would have been surely set aside as a nature reserve; but it was filled in and paved over by the 60's. The wildlife density and diversity of my 1955 Indianapolis backyard is something that in more recent years I've only seen in the Costa Rica cloud forest in 1991, and I've heard that since 1991 those cloud forests have had a major amphibian die-off, so there's no telling where such diversity and abundance might be found now. But those who grew up in the same area only 15 years later than me have utterly different memories, and the stories of the old always seem exaggerated compared with the world we see now. In the iconic movie Citizen Kane, the main character had wealth, fame, and notoriety but always longed for something missing in his life. The audience hears his dying words ‘Rosebud’ and only at the movie’s end (spoiler alert) do we discover “Rosebud” was the name of his childhood sled. Despite his subsequent wealth and fame, Citizen Kane’s formative and perhaps most intense memories were of his childhood, with simple pleasures, like sledding on a snow day. It is in this spirit that we have chosen to collaborate on this book, and subsequent projects. Wild nature, the way it calls to us, the ways it impacted our earlier years, the ways we believe it is a vital part of who we are as people, and who we are as a species connected to all life, is

our Rosebud. We think it would be good if more people could feel this. Though perhaps they do.

Biologist E.O. Wilson coined the term ‘biophilia’ to represent the bond with nature that humans have embedded in our genes. His hypothesis was that since we spent tens of thousands of generations without cities and structures, we have an innate love and pull towards natural settings. Just like we share a lifelong link with our own mothers, so too do we have such a deep bond with wilderness, now culturally or circumstantially suppressed for many. Whether or not he’s right, it’s clear that nature, and interactions with other kinds of life, are what we evolved for. While supernormal stimuli can temporarily “shout louder” in the form of video games, Pringles and loud music, they are no real substitute for a natural habitat. Perhaps “biophilia” is inherently redundant, like the word “oxyphilia” might be to describe people who ‘like’ oxygen. But if so, the word is at once unnecessary and insufficient. We don’t just “like” nature, we’ve been sculpted as part of it, including the thousands of mental sub-modules seeking to recognize and be reassured by our part in nature. Anti-depression drugs are a poor replacement. The human experience has always been defined by the regularities of seasons, tides, moonrise and day length; the irregularities and intermittencies of weather and the interactions with other species. We are designed to react and survive in such settings, as they have forever been the mirror which allows us to see who we are. But in the last half century or so, much like King Midas, we have discovered the power to turn everything around us into money. Only to find, as he did, that by doing so we could lose all that mattered. Our “time bias” causes us not to think much about just how temporary the things are which now distract us from the natural world, and the fact that human futures will for the most part be lived without such things.

What’s An Armadillo For?

We (your authors) often ask young people questions like ‘What is an armadillo for?’ They often hem and haw and eventually come up with answers like ‘boots?’, or

‘wallets?’ They cannot help but anchor their thinking with an anthropocentric lens – I ask ‘what is an armadillo for?’, they hear ‘what good is an armadillo to humans’? Students eventually get around to asking questions like “if a given species went extinct, would it really matter? Why?” It always makes us a little sad to hear the question, but it's legitimate. And a typical "environmentalism" answer du jour is to imply that dire unknown things might happen to humans if a minnow, an owl or another species is lost forever. Certainly the answer to such a question isn't simple. Life exists in a web of complex feedbacks, so there is no immediate answer to the question "what is an armadillo for?" Those of us who have even seen an armadillo have generally seen them only as road kill, drawing flies along the interstate. They're amazingly ancient-seeming critters, a mammal with a shell. And they reportedly don't play well with traffic. One of our friends claims that when an armadillo sees a vehicle coming it freezes, and when the car gets within about 10 feet it screams and leaps straight up into the air, causing a direct splat into the grill of cars and trucks. This is almost certainly bullshit, but it passes for a large part of our brains have filed away under armadillos. What will happen to the human world if the armadillos go away? Probably nothing we’d understand. Maybe something. Odds are we wouldn't notice it for long, even if it might have been important in the long term, due to our shifting mental baselines. Yet the armadillo world would disappear forever, all that it was and could ever be, and that isn’t nothing. It was a trick question. Armadillos aren’t “for” humans. They exist due to having sprung from the same chance events, and distant ancestors, as we did; and against long odds have navigated into the present world while 99.9% of all species became extinct or speciated. Armadillos’ evolved answers to life’s challenges may someday – when we’re wiser - cure humans of cancer or plagues, or their existence may not affect us at all. Yet every complex “armadillo” which is lost in the mass extinction now underway takes us closer to a world composed of insects and bacteria, and farther from the world of the large-species interactions which shaped and defined what we are. Think for a moment how delightful it is that armadillos exist to share the planet with us. We don’t know most of their story, but what we do know is fascinating. If you took a walk and encountered an armadillo, your day would be enriched. At the point we decide they have a value apart from what we might do with their shells, we may find that while not being “for” anything, they make the world a more interesting and valuable place in which to live.

One of my (NJ) greatest current enjoyments in life is to go for a walk with the dogs in the morning in the forest near our house and remove the memory card in the infrared wildlife camera stationed on a tree. The unexpected reward from seeing what bird and animal shenanigans happened in the night in the nearby woods gives me great anticipation, and often joy. In the last year we’ve seen bears (above), coyotes, fishercats, turkeys, deer, badgers, dancing rabbits (above), and all color and variety of birds. How many people today get primal satisfaction from the affirmation that, at least some parts of our natural world, are still robust and thriving. Yet how ironic, perhaps, that at this point in history I need to resort to high technology to see the wildlife which was abundant and visible without such mechanisms 200 years ago.

Shifting Baselines

“We live in a zoologically impoverished world, from which all the hugest, and fiercest, and strangest forms have recently disappeared; and it is, no doubt, a much better world for us now that they have gone. Yet it is a marvelous fact, and one that has not been sufficiently dwelt upon, this sudden dying out of so many large Mammalia, not in one place but in half the land surface of the globe.” Alfred Russell Wallace – 1876 And then, decades later: “Looking at the subject again, with the much larger body of facts at our command, I am convinced that the rapidity of the extinction of so many large Mammalia is due to Man’s agency acting in cooperation with those natural causes which at the culmination of each geologic era has led to the extinctions of the larger, the more specialized, or the most strangely modified forms.” Alfred Russell Wallace - 1911 “Shifting baselines” refers to the fact that everyone defines "normal" as the way things were when they grew up.86 So as the natural world is increasingly impoverished, we typically only notice the losses that occurred in our own lives and consider the tales of our elders to be exaggerated stories. This sounds like is just an aspect of nostalgia, but it's a real problem, because not only do most of us not really know what the world used to be like, but we can't track it scientifically without some original baseline. Reading books, and scientific journals helps, but again is our slow, neocortical thinking, not the emotional, fast acting behavioral responses. Our feelings about nature cannot help be linked to our own life experiences. And these baselines have been shifting for some time now. Author Baz Edmeades notes: “Twenty thousand years ago, huge condor-like birds with sixteen-foot wingspans were tearing open the carcasses of hippo-sized ground sloths here in North America with heavy-duty beaks. Mammoths and mastodons were giving themselves dust-baths and pushing over trees. Cheetahs were hunting a large variety of pronghorn and forked-horn antelopes. No less than five other kinds of big cat were living on an extravagant assortment of camel, llama, deer, horse, musk ox, bison, goat and sheep species. With its giant bears, giant beavers, giant armadillo-like species, giant tortoises, and its giant ground-sloth species, North America was, without exaggeration, a super-Serengeti containing many more big-animal species than present-day Africa.

As I continued my run, I thought about how strange it was that the picnickers and joggers around me knew little or nothing about all this. How odd, I thought, that they should know more about the dinosaurs which disappeared sixty-five million years

ago, than about the marvelous array of big mammals and birds that were seen, hunted – and no doubt named – by the first humans to arrive here in North America around twenty thousand years ago."87

There's no real mystery to why so much megafauna is gone: it coincides with humans colonizing one area after another and killing off the larger animals first. Indeed, humans kill off animals of all sizes; but it's harder to kill every single member of a small species than a large one, because the biomass tends to be more scattered. Being large and encountering humans is almost always a deadly combination. The eradication of other species has accelerated in the past few centuries; with a huge amount of wild biomass disappearing since Europeans came to the New World. Fisheries biologist Jeremy Jackson suggests that, based on observer accounts at the time of Columbus, the green sea turtle population was approximately 660 million turtles.88 The Tortugas Islands (Spanish for Turtles) were named such because of this fantastic abundance, and some tales speak of sailors walking from shell to shell on over-crowded beaches. Indeed sea turtle used to be a primary food source in the Caribbean. Approaching 700 million is in contrast with the estimated 88,000 nesting females in existence today.89 88,000 “feels like” a lot though, doesn’t it? Even if it’s 4 orders of magnitude below the original population. We have a hard time imagining 10,000 times as many green sea turtles as today, so it seems unreal, imaginary, like a fairy-tale account despite being documented history. The Tasmanian tiger is an example of an extraordinary kind of fossil - moving pictures - this critter is as extinct as the stegosaurus, but here it is walking around.90 We’ll have color high-def fossils of many species, though the ability to view them may be transient… Much of the Earth’s wild biomass has been lost in what should be recent memory. Estimates of original bison populations in North America range from 15 million to 10 billion - three orders of magnitude of difference in estimates - due to shifting baselines and the tendency to underestimate historic abundance based on what it "feels" should be normal to us at present.91 Again, the stories of the ground shaking, and a sea of bison horizon to horizon seem like tall tales now, even if our great-grandparents (including perhaps some of yours) saw them. It is estimated we have lost half of the world’s wild animals since 1975.92 In the past 30 years, Europe has lost over 400 million birds, or around 30% of the total population.93 And, on a micro-scale, as human population doubled since 1970, we have lost about 45% of global insect biomass.94 This "great dying" has accelerated so fast that most of the earth's large complex life has died since you were born, and as college students you may survive most of what’s left. What a crazy sentence to write.

And the lack of baselines isn’t just relevant to our “feelings”; it has real implications for science, because we kept no organized records of what formerly existed, and thus have no benchmark for what might constitute a healthy population. Since DJ and Nate were born, the whales of earth have largely been eradicated. Cynically via institutional fraud and sometimes crazily, often without any market for the products, nations of large-brained, social creatures were shot with harpoon cannons by the millions with modern factory techniques. Again, there were no original population counts, and the eyewitness accounts by whalers on old sail-driven whaling ships surely couldn't be true because the vast number of whales reported were so unlike we find in the seas today. Even though “counting whales and writing the numbers down” was their primary business. And with oceans acidifying rapidly, whales may be irreversibly on the path to extinction from food web collapse by the time today's high school students are in their 50's. I (DJ) have looked closely into the eye of a full-grown whale, held a young humpback in the surf as it made strange complex noises which reverberated through my body. Such stories may soon seem as fictitious, as improbably remote, as tales of hobbits, dragons and treasure. And I've known dolphins better than most humans have had a chance to, and immersed myself in the toxic knowledge of their reality and their declining status as the 6th great extinction reaches into their existence with great claws. The possible extinction of such creatures may seem an abstract worry, and bit of environmental alarmism, but it’s quite real. In the 1990's my group was working with Chinese scientists to try saving the Baiji; and we had a plan to relocate some of the cute little guys to oxbow lakes in China to keep the species alive, since the rivers they had evolved in were choked with human commerce. I couldn't get funding for it. The Baiji dolphins are all dead now, as remote from human lives as the dinosaurs. But this sort of example makes it real; flesh and blood self-aware, conscious dolphins as real as we are, whose history was as long and difficult as our own, converted to forever-gone in the course of a couple decades. And it's that kind of failure which makes for a high attrition rate in this sort of activist career, because the failure is irretrievable, and difficult to bear. To make a difference you need to understand the entire situation, and understanding the entire situation can bring a profound spiritual malaise which drains you. And your stories, to younger ears, sound exaggerated. Some of my (DJs) campaigns to slow the killing of wild species have succeeded. I am occasionally asked (“occasionally” since it’s now ancient history by the pace of human events) “how many dolphins were saved?” by those efforts. The answer is that neither I nor the world can ever know, because no human knows how many pelagic

dolphins existed before any particular at-sea holocaust began. In ending the pelagic driftnet fishery, many dolphins were saved, but the only dolphin-mortality estimates were from the UNFAO, which guessed between 300,000 and 1 million dolphins per year, without having any initial population sizes to compare with. Nobody had ever counted them, even roughly. Similarly, the humpback whale has just been removed from the U.S. “endangered species” list since its population has “rebounded”, even though this “rebound” probably places it at a small fraction of pre-harpooning levels that we will never know. While fishing in Florida in the early '60's, I (DJ) found the profusion of sea life to be dazzling, even though it had already been heavily overfished by that time. It was where I set my baseline, and it was wondrous, like a fairyland of large moving life which lit up my young brain which had grown up fishing for pond bluegills. The big fish pretty much piled up on deck as fast as people could reel them in, some of them nearly my size. I went back in the 90's and that reality was just gone, but there were still people taking the same boats out to fish, bringing back small amounts of tiny fish... and proudly taking pictures of them. To my baseline it's an incalculable loss; to a young person catching some colorful tiny fish it is still exciting.

At this point, humans and their farm animals account for over 98% of all terrestrial animal life.95 That means we outweigh wild vertebrates by a factor of 60! When a person first hears that, it's unsettling. It may be unsettling to you. But that's the cumulative effect of it all: lost ecosystems, which – being lost - we soon forget. Each generation accepts an impoverished new ecological reality as its baseline. In human terms, it is hard to judge the “importance” of the loss of any individual species. We assign utterly no economic

value to intact armadillos, let alone ecosystems. Most species are just in our way – of corporate profits, jobs and progress. Unless there is a market, the non-human earth species have no intrinsic value to modern human culture. If we could, via some magic, bring back 5 billion passenger pigeons to North America with no cost other than a 1-

cent national sales tax, would we do it? Fill the skies again with dark clouds of them, as existed when the USA began? It’s doubtful we would, because there's no longer a place in our minds for such a wonder. We have no memories of them because the last one died 100 years ago –ergo we don’t miss them. By all means take a moment and visualize the process of trying to get such a tax passed to bring back all those poop-dropping birds. Wouldn't happen, would it? Yet America is different and less special without them, and the nutrient distribution system they represented has altered our forests and farmland in ways which may – in retrospect – be missed once we no longer have unlimited amounts of fossil-energy-generated fertilizer to apply. And we are ultimately supported by those formerly-intact ecosystems. The "carbon pulse" of energy and destruction from burning fossil carbon will be over in the blink of an eye in historic terms, and - unless huge changes are made in human actions - will be likely marked by a visible horizon in future rock strata, with abundant fossils below and anoxic mudstone above. When we've burned all the oil and no longer have air conditioning, reliable rainfall and supermarkets, we may find that losing those species actually did matter, since they supported the complex and truly renewable systems which allowed us exist without burnable fossils.

Beyond baselines, what is the absolute worth of other species?

So how should we figure what those other critters are worth? Our lives were defined by these other species as we became what we are, and humans living in isolation from other large complex species would experience a very different existence than the one we evolved in. Some people think it sounds like a fine idea – less dangerous, cleaner and more predictable. Others find it traumatic, lonely and gray. Some people consider their dogs and horses to be their best friends, others consider the same animals to be only sources of meat or home protection. Having known many dolphins personally, my own experience is to think of wild dolphins akin to an aboriginal people – a different culture but beings with inquisitive and humorous minds, and with self-awareness in the same sense that we possess it. But many commercial fishermen think of them as floating hunks of meat at best and competitors at worst. And most people don't think about them much at all. “In 1976 in Hawaii, I (DJ) was taking data in a university experiment designed to test a dolphin's visual acuity. Under the experiment's design, the dolphin would be simultaneously presented with two different-sized targets, one on the left and one on the right. The dolphin had been "trained" that she would receive a fish in reward

whenever she correctly pushed one of two paddles corresponding to the larger of the two targets. The way the experiment was set up, the scientific observer was supposedly unseen by the dolphin. If the dolphin pressed the correct paddle, I would press the "positive sound" button, a high and presumably pleasant-sounding beep, and throw a herring through a 6x6 inch hole in front of my face. If the dolphin got it wrong, I would push the "negative sound" button and there would be a klaxon buzz, followed by no reward. The dolphin in question, who I knew well, developed rules of her own. She would get all the answers right for perhaps 12 trials in a row, and stockpile the fish on the shallow tank bottom. Then she would (obviously intentionally) start getting 100% wrong answers. Whenever I would push the klaxon sound she would wing a herring through the hole and smack me in the forehead. In fact, she ended up shaping my behavior until I would hesitate a LONG time before pushing the "negative sound" button... and she would wait, patiently, knowing I had to do it.... sometimes as long as 2 minutes... and SMACK would go the herring on my forehead. When she was out of ammunition, she would go back to getting the trials 100% right again, to build up more herring ammunition. (Of course, this particular "visual acuity" data, if examined quantitatively, showed that this dolphin was virtually blind.) I ended that day with tiny silver scales all over my complexion, and an increased awareness of the poor state of the art in dolphin research." It is these experiences with other species that are not easily internalized or understood by reading a book. And certainly the fisherman who view dolphins as meat, or machine-gun and bomb them or cut their throats on general principles just to see them die, are unlikely to ever interact with them or consider them in this way. Whose experience is right? Which view of dolphins’ role in the human world is the more correct one? There is no easy answer, and maybe no “true” one, as the universe doesn't dish things out in rights and wrongs. Rather, it goes to the values system of our individual virtual worlds, the stories we live by, our self-image and our internal image of the world around us. We descended, after all, in an unbroken chain from the same hominids who depopulated the world of large fauna in the last 20,000 years. Now 7.5 billion strong, we're on track to challenge the great planetary extinction crises of earth's multi-billion-year history. Our history with other large species has been a matter of competition, desire, fear, behaviorism, empathy and abstraction. We're descended from meat-eating hunter/scavengers who fantasized about possessing the species around us, painted cave walls with abstract images of them, and defined ourselves by our prey and the

other predators we feared. We learned to understand the minds and behavior of those other beasts, enough to outguess them, to dependably stampede bison off cliffs, build fishing lures and traps, and imagine ourselves flying like birds.

Dog is my co-pilot

One of our greatest successes - or perhaps the wolves' greatest successes - was forming a symbiosis with wolves who later became dogs, and other deepening symbioses with animals to do work, provide dairy products, meat and eggs. Such leaps drastically increased the niche for human-biomass carrying capacity. Both of your authors have shared most of our lives with dogs. Dogs are descended from gray wolves (and other now-extinct wolves) and their global population now numbers over 500 million.96 Dogs are proof that cross-species empathy, understanding and communication can occur, and it’s a wonderful thing. Some of our best friends have been canine. To each of you reading who has spent time playing, caring for, or just hanging out with a dog, you are thus capable of empathizing with the broader natural world, with creatures outside your house, or backyard, or even country. They are out there, and their lives and futures quite literally dependent on human actions in the same way our dogs are dependent on us bringing them kibble. And this brings up a flipside of our empathy for dogs and cats etc. which we should probably put in perspective as well. To a certain degree, many of us use dogs and house cats as a surrogate for wild nature. House cats now share with humans the status of the ocean’s top predators... plankton, zooplankton, larvae, tiny animals, small fish, larger fish, big fish, gigantic fish, and then kitty-cats, what a bizarre trophic food chain. Dogs are, we hate to admit, also wildly overpopulated carnivores from a biophysical standpoint, and many have been morphed into bizarre phenotypes. Our dogs and cats eat heavily from our livestock... humans get the prime cuts, but they get the rest… The “energy footprint” of an American poodle is higher than many of the

human nobility of 16th century Europe. Each Pomeranian probably has a goodly number of fossil energy slaves. They really only “look good” from an environmental perspective if they displace the need for human reproduction. The answer probably isn’t for billions of people to all have dogs, but to reverse the estrangement from nature which leaves such a void for dogs to fill. And, eventually, to have a smaller, sustainably-sized population of humans and dogs living at any given time, enjoying nature and the sight of other species together. On the Other Hand These days, "civilized" humans are very removed from interaction with other species in traditional ways, although we still deeply fear the large predators which for the most part no longer exist. When tourists go for a swim in the ocean, it's clear that the biggest worry is about sharks. The USA averages about one shark attack fatality every two years in total. Indeed, there have only been 548 confirmed shark attack fatalities worldwide since the year 1580, while an estimated 372,000 people still drown each year.97,98

Clearly, the risk of dying from shark attack has always been preposterously lower than simple drowning, and now that the sharks are mostly gone from the seas, the odds must be lower still. Yet the dread we feel is real, and that translates into a largely irrational desire to have them eradicated.

And what the hell, we might as well eradicate armadillos too just to be on the safe side. A rather disquieting bit of research was done showing that many drivers will swerve their vehicles to kill inoffensive wildlife like turtles (or armadillos) when the

opportunity arises.99 That is, a tendency to want other life to not exist. So perhaps the truth behind the apocryphal story of armadillos screaming and leaping in front of cars is less about armadillo brains and more about a human "death swerve" heuristic in favor of opportunistic killing as an end in itself. Certainly, the 6th mass extinction isn't explained simply by our eating things. Nobody ate Dodo birds, they didn't taste good; but from all reports they were great fun to beat with sticks, and that hobby wiped them out in short order, as it has countless other species. Humans take pleasure from killing that is entirely distinct from food needs. For many of our kind, it’s fun to kill things; we may recoil at that assertion, but that reality plays out all around us. Moreover, the balance between empathy and abstraction can be an odd thing, even in humans who "feel like" they care about other animals. In the same way people will donate to a starving child but not to a group of starving children, empathy is something one extends to an individual, not a species. In the USA, it's nearly impossible to raise funds for saving a species that nobody has seen; recall our inability to raise $50,000 in the early 90's to create lake reserves in China to prevent the baiji's extinction. However, if instead we’d purchased a one-legged owl and started the "Hoppy the Owl Trust" in the USA, allowing other humans to visit little Hoppy in his walled enclosure and feed him mice, we'd have had an ongoing income of that much every year, with residuals from the incredibly cute "I Hop for Hoppy" t-shirts. When a perhaps-somewhat-handicapped humpback whale swam up the Sacramento River, people were moved to tears by the antics of "Humphrey" and he became nationwide news, with everyone caring daily how he was doing, and fully engaged in how to turn him around to safety. Millionaires fell out of the trees with gimmicks and ideas. At the same time, this did nothing for general issues of whale conservation, which languished with few dollars and only fringe interest. "Humphrey" was more real, and worth more to people than his entire species. While Humphrey became a "person" to them, his species remained an abstraction. This abstraction, when combined with a general tendency to kill things off, is why most large creatures are now gone from earth and the rest are on their way out. It requires us to go out of our way, to be inconvenienced, to NOT kill things off, and the self-interest in doing so just doesn't seem apparent. Abstraction is a funny thing, and of course we apply it to members of our own species. The only places which haven't seen "ethnic cleansing" recently are the ones which did it long ago. We have a long history of killing and eating those who aren't in our "in group"; we're all descended from cannibals. Indeed, more recently than we may think: during Imperial Japan's invasion of China, a good part of the diet for the troops was cooked Chinese people.100 This has been pretty well deleted from the sanitized historical accounts we are presented post WWII, but the Chinese certainly

remember it well. The Imperial Japanese felt they were descended from the sun god and that other humans were descended from monkeys, so there was no logical reason not to chow down on them. It even severely freaked out the German nazi's on the scene, whose "master race" paradigm was not principally known for its celebration of human phenotypic variation. So empathy and abstraction can vary wildly between human cultures. I (DJ) must admit to sometimes feeling a bit alien, culturally, due to having generalized my abstractions, and my empathy. One story: I spent 6 months of several hours per day in the water with a particular dolphin, becoming friends, and came to think of dolphins as an alien but roughly equivalent kind of people. This isn't a typical experience in most human lives. Subsequent to that I've interacted with many other dolphin species, such as pseudorca, false killer whales. Although they are large enough, with toothy jaws, to instantly kill a human with a single bite, I've experienced them intelligently interacting, as well as approaching me underwater with jaws open, with my only safety being in the minds of these wild-caught carnivores, their decision not to hurt me for the sake of doing it. And kids respond to them as individuals as well. But I also have footage, obtained by one of EarthTrust’s undercover operatives, of a member of that same species being slowly killed for fun by two men in a shallow bay with a piece of steel rebar, a coke bottle, and a pocket knife while a crowd nearby watches and laughs. To them, it was funny; to me seeing it was no less disturbing than seeing the torture-vivisection of a human would be. Clearly, our different experiences had generated different abstractions in our minds, and different thresholds of empathy for another species. (Quick work saved the rest of that whale’s pod from the same fate and caused their release back to the wild, but that’s a highly unusual outcome.) We're now on course for a world in which the largest extant animal may well be the cow. Elephants and rhinos, whales and whale sharks, all are being exponentially eradicated, and zoolife will be on the menu on the downslope of the green revolution, as this century progresses. How far will our baseline shift in coming decades? Will your children get the same wild feeling of nature that we did in the Siskiyou mountains or Indiana countryside by seeing a squirrel, or a pigeon, because they never knew how much more there was? Whether or not we extend our empathy past our species, or pull it back again to our narrow in-groups, it's perhaps worth taking some time to wonder about what sort of existence we might have on a hotter world with scum seas of slime and jellyfish, noxious gasses wafting off the seas, and insects, rats, raccoons and a few others species our only companions. And ask ourselves: whether an expanded empathy right about now might be in our own self-interest before things go that far.

Other species exist in the physical world, while the only thing we can ever directly experience is our own virtual worlds. Rather than consider other humans to be robots, we take the mental leap – unprovable – that they are beings just like we are; and more profoundly, that we are like they are. It’s a threshold of our personal cosmology: a rejection of solipsism in deciding to treat others as real conscious beings. It was not so long ago that in our country slavery was justified due to there being supposedly huge differences in human races. I was told in my youth by an old teacher that black people didn't feel pain the way white people did, and I'm sure he believed it because his empathy for the two colors of humans felt differently to him and his mirror neurons. The threshold of our empathy and of our abstraction of outgroups (within and without our species), has been and will be a dynamic thing. “Touch the earth, love the earth, her plains, her valleys, her hills, and her seas; rest your spirit in her solitary places. For the gifts of life are the Earth’s and they are given to all, and they are the songs of birds at daybreak, Orion and the Bear, and the dawn seen over the ocean from the beach. When the Pleiades and the wind in the grass are no longer a part of the human spirit, a part of very flesh and bone, man becomes, as it were a kind of cosmic outlaw, having neither the completeness nor integrity of the animal nor the birthright of a true humanity. We need another and a wiser and perhaps a more mystical concept of animals. Remote from universal nature, and living by complicated artifice, man in civilization surveys the creature through the glass of his knowledge and sees thereby a feather magnified and the whole image in distortion. We patronize them for their incompleteness, for their tragic fate of having taken form so far below ourselves. And therein we err, and greatly err. For the animal shall not be measured by man. In a world older and more complete than ours they move finished and complete, gifted with extensions of the senses we have lost or never attained, living by voices we shall never hear. They are not brethren, they are not underlings; they are other nations, caught with ourselves in the net of life and time, fellow prisoners of the splendour and travail of the earth.” Henry Beston

Our Faustian Ocean

“Don't it always seem to go, that you don't know what you've got ‘til it's

gone” – Joni Mitchell

Sometimes, in our dreams, we can fly. In our imaginations, we glimpse magical beings

in fantastic worlds. But why should we settle for illusion? In this place, still, all it takes to experience another real world is the decision to do so.

And thus, I spent a large portion of my 20’s under the sea in a weightless, alien yet familiar world, interacting with rich communities of sea life. An ape wearing fins, blending in. Add a mask and snorkel and it is astonishingly easy for a primate to become a sea creature. Equipped with a subcutaneous fat layer rather than fur, with an evolved diving reflex (where did that come from?), a human can fairly quickly learn to be at home in shallow seas. And that I was. Only limited by daylight and thirst, on occasion falling asleep far from land surrounded by an immense calmness which is hard to describe. There, a person could still find the abundance of wildlife that old stories tried to describe about land; everything in the field of vision alive, moving, interacting, behaving. Octopus, eels, enormous turtles, rainbow parrotfish, crabs, clouds of colorful fish, large, fast-moving jacks; twitchy barracuda, the

protruding eyes of bottom-dwellers, the long antennae protruding from holes in the rocks. And through it all I could fly like a slow-motion bird. The seas contain so many stories we have no notion of, nor good language to describe. As a side-project to my conservation work, I put together a research lab to try some things nobody else was doing. In 1991, that lab became the first to successfully test a non-primate (a dolphin) for self-awareness based on primate criteria. Not because I had advanced degrees or qualifications, but because nobody

else had gotten around to doing it before; everyone “knew” that only humans and a couple of our cousin-apes were self-aware by the criteria of psychologists, and so that was what our culture believed. Something shaped like a fish couldn’t have a conscious, abstracting mind. Yet as soon as it was tried – within seconds - the dolphin enthusiastically passed the test, and all the textbooks on self-awareness were subsequently rewritten. My doing this had far less to do with expertise, than with my realization that we humans don’t actually know what we pretend to know. Our biggest blind spot is that we are unaware of the unbelievably breathtaking extent of our blind spots. – – DJ White 11/24/2015 Note from Nate: in this course, we don’t do advocacy, but rather learn how first principles of the physical world relate to the human predicament. But in this particular case, it would feel sociopathic to frame the “big trade” described below in neutral language and terms. We’re still contacting experts trying to gauge the pace of acidification and other ocean impacts, but the information we have so far leads to the conclusions expressed here.

Our Blue Planet

“We are tied to the ocean. And when we go back to the sea, whether it is to sail or to watch - we are going back from whence we came. “ John F. Kennedy In our solar system, and all other star systems with planets we have detected, there is nothing remotely like earth’s living oceans. They’re what defines the planet and have defined us. It’s where life originated and has flourished across deep time, and it’s still where most of the life we know of in the universe exists. It is home to the largest creatures and largest brains, and oldest cultures which have ever existed on earth. (hint: and they aren’t ours) From deep space our planet looks like a dim blue star. From a million miles away seems like a small blue marble. From directly above it, one sees a water world, dominated by three coexisting phases of water. 70% of Earth’s surface is covered in water - the most important ingredient supporting life other than oxygen. (And by the

way, the plankton living in today’s oceans provide over 50% of the world’s oxygen).101 We came from the oceans, first swimming, then crawling, then running and now walking upright, but our origins are in the sea. The salt balance in our blood matches that of the ocean. The density of the human body is about the same as ocean water. The chemical composition of our cells has similar mineral make up as does seawater. In the womb, our developing babies still have ‘gill-slits’ a carryover from our watery ancestral environment in deep time. Indeed, our bodies carry captive seas around with us, with our cells functioning much the way ancient sea creatures did. But, other than a flat blue horizon and an ocean breeze on our coasts, the seas of earth are invisible to most of us. We know that’s where fish come from, and where various creatures live, but for the most part that has seemed a separate reality from the human one. Almost another planet, within which different rules apply than those which govern land animals. Until very recently in the human story, the seas have been very mysterious and scary, the abode of monsters and infinite depths. But our modern pell-mell supernormal civilization leaves little time to reflect on the gradual but inexorable changes we are wreaking on the signature ecosystem of our planet.

Very few of us think of the 3D vastness of the seas as a real “place”. Except for a few birds temporarily aloft, life on land lives what is mostly a 2-D existence, confined to a thin interface between land and sky. In contrast, the entire volume of the seas may be inhabited and traversed by living creatures; 328 million cubic miles of living habitat. Thus by volume, the oceans make up more than 99% of the livable space of earth. They cover 71% of its surface. Their average depth is over 12,000 feet and they hold

97% of the planet’s water.102,103 Yet the Earths seas are - seemingly - considered expendable to the species doing the fossil-burning, because without the burning we wouldn’t have as many new cell phone apps or be able to melt cheese in a microwave at 1am. The ocean is a world of strange lights, languages, and untold stories; but it is more a world of sound than of light, since light travels only limited distances through it, whereas sound can travel thousands of miles. Whales can hear one another speaking thousands of miles away... or could before the advent of man-made noise in the previously-quiet frequency ranges they use. Those wavelengths are now filled with the clatter of diesel engines, huge propellers, and booming sonars to detect enemy ships. The floors of the oceans have until recently been covered in reef communities and other fragile ecosystems; fractally rich in the same sense as tropical rainforests, but in many cases far older, growing with the slow creep of calcite and aragonite crystallizing one molecule at a time at depths surface light cannot penetrate. We have dredged most of them into trackless wastes with steel cables and nylon nets, without them ever having been seen or photographed, and spreading layers of suffocating mud over the sea bottoms in the process. When photographed many years later, they remain barren. Another trade: the destruction of ancient worlds for fish sticks that are only “so-so” but add to GDP. These days, despite the huge leap forward in sonar and deep-sea research, only about 1/20th of 1% of the ocean floor has been mapped well enough to find volcanic vents or sunken airliners.104 The entire seafloor has been mapped, but only to a resolution of about 5km.105 This compares to a resolution of 50 times finer -100 meters – on the surfaces of Mars and Venus, where we can discern far more detail. (This is due to the resolving difference between radar and sonar). When one is talking about living creatures, a 5km resolution would miss just about everything interesting. What is down there?? We seem to think we know enough to make permanent decisions about it. Film-maker James Cameron (Avatar) had it right: the seas are really another world that we barely know but have been destroying anyway. Like our atmosphere, they have always seemed too vast for mankind to affect, and too remote to care about, the denizens far outside our sphere of empathy. But the seas are finite, and we underestimated our own capacity for mischief, once our armies of tens of billions of fossil energy slaves got busy. And only very recently is this profound reality becoming recognized by some who are paying attention.

Overfishing and related threats have caused the oceans fish and animal populations to be cut in half since 1970. Pelagic fish ‘stocks’ like tuna have declined to only 10% of historical populations. Sea turtles have declined from numbers in the hundreds of millions a few centuries ago to under 100,000. Recent marine science shows mangroves, coral reefs (home to 25% of ocean species) and seagrasses that underpin ocean food webs are in steep decline.106 We’ve overfished, polluted, trashed and heated the seas. Like many of Earth’s ecosystems, we have been treating the principle as if it were interest, and like a college trust fund frat boy, have been living ‘way beyond our means. And now, as we start to sober up the morning after, we’re about to sign a new contract to extend our orgy of consumption – at least through our own lifetimes.

Life in the Oceans 2015: The Big Trade

In the history of human affairs, art, and literature there have been some infamously-bad trades. The dicey and perhaps apocryphal sale of the island of Manhattan by “someone” to the Dutch for 60 Guilders. The contemporary trading of young girls into slavery in exchange for heroin and cocaine. Dr. Faust trading his soul to the devil for 24 years of luxury and knowledge. The promise of a first-born child to Rumpelstiltskin in exchange for spinning straw into gold. But the biggest, worst trade of all time is ongoing as you read this sentence: We are trading away all large complex life in the oceans for perhaps the next million years, in exchange for about an extra 50 years of burning fossil fuels. It’s a deal even Dr. Faust might have been horrified by. How so? Currently we have a new use for the ocean, which is utterly incompatible with its other human uses: fossil carbon sink. The ocean naturally breathes CO2 in and out as part of natural flows. The CO2 from gigatons of burning fossils aren’t normal flows, but it gets absorbed anyway. The oceans thus become a ‘buffer’ which will allow us to burn fossil energy longer while heating the planet more slowly. They absorb over half of the CO2 our fossil slaves breathe out. We’ve generally considered that to be good news, because it has slowed the pace of global heating, which implies to us that hard decisions needn’t be made in the near term. We can “kick the can” down the road for a few more decades and avoid any immediate cuts in fossil energy use. No harm in another degree or two of warming, eh?

What’s the catch? Over the course of a single human lifetime, most large complex\*\*species that we know of in the universe are thereby consigned to being lost permanently, large complex sea life in general is lost for millions of years, and in exchange we get a temporary partial and declining buffer of the climate effects on the scale of decades. (\*\*There are large organisms that are NOT complex: e.g. giant jellyfish, enormous mats of fungi, stromatolites.) The reality is fairly simple – it’s a trade of the oceans full of large vertebrates for the likely duration of human existence, versus the (self-issued) human license to burn fossil carbon for another century or less. Ocean absorption of CO2 won’t prevent heating of the climate but takes the edge off it and makes it seem more gradual and politically tenable over the course of decades. The problem with this calculus – aside from its apocalyptic stakes – is that our current species-wide conversation is not even considering whether we should choose not to do it. The odds are high you haven’t even heard about this trade, put just this way, even though it’s one of the most fundamental realities of Earth existence right now...

Sea Life for GDP.

It seems like such an obvious thing to do. To the extent the seas act as a CO2 buffer, this is seen as a fortuitous thing – a boon. You’ve probably read about using the seas this way but framed differently. There was recently a “good news” article about research showing that Antarctic seas are taking up CO2 faster than previously. A typical sentence in the conclusion of these articles is along the lines of “Fortunately, the seas mitigate global warming”. Yet it’s only good news in a “Rumpelstiltskin” sense, because the seas are not unharmed by such absorption. Rather, they are transformed. The CO2 becomes carbonic acid, and like a gigantic stomach, the seas increasingly begin fizzing away the parts which dissolve in acid. Things like shells, skeletons, coral reefs... in other words, many of the organisms

at the base of ocean food chains, and those which form the fractal 3D structure of subsea habitats. Not that it’s a perfect trade, of course. The same CO2 pollution which will acidify the oceans and lead to trophic collapse will - at a bit later date - also sear away the rainforests, melt the glaciers, and make parts of the earth too hot for humans or crops, and submerge the areas where much of the world’s human population now lives and farms and fishes. That’s part of the same trade for our fossil carbon-hedonic habit and the conveniences we’ve recently come to take for granted. (On that trade too, we’ve finished signing 2/3 of our (species) name on the contract’s dotted line). It’s just that the oceans’ ability to support creatures with calcium carbonate skeletons will go first. The first irreversible threshold, the loss of aragonite skeletons, has already begun in high latitudes, and under continued extraction-for-GDP as a human aspiration, could be locked in for the entire world oceans within your lifetime. That puts your generation in an uncomfortable position, to say the least. Faust’s heirs: you didn’t make the deal, but you have to decide whether to engage with it or let it play out. Without some sort of extraordinary change in human societal inertia, it will. It is. We’ve suggested that you be very skeptical about certainty, and you should be. But few things are as certain as basic chemistry. The oceans are acidifying, apparently at least an order of magnitude faster than they ever have, even in past mass extinctions, in earth’s entire history.107 Once it goes into the air, most CO2 will eventually enter the oceans and become acid. And there it stays for a long time. So, in what sense is all of this a “trade”? It’s a trade of convenience: if we decided to care, we’d have to slam the brakes on burning fossil carbon very soon and dramatically transform the way our societies are powered, and how much comfort to expect. Soon, as in by the time you’re in your 30’s.

50 Years of buffer for a Million Years of Slime?

Earth’s ocean soaks up fossil carbon’s CO2 quite quickly. But de-carbonizing the seas is anything but quick. Historically, after the coral and shells are lost to acidification (with accompanying marine mass extinctions), it takes hundreds of thousands to millions of years for the slow process of silicate weathering – rain wearing away alkaline continental mountains - to gradually raise the pH to the point where it becomes possible to build shells and reefs again.108 Of course, by this time all the large animals are long gone... as a million years is a long time to go without eating. But evolution can create large complex life in the seas again, right? Except that it might not:

By the end of the Devonian, "there were fish called arthrodire placoderms with large slashing jaws that were the size of school buses, and there were relatives of living tetrapods, or land-dwelling vertebrates, that were almost as large," Sallan said. "You had some vertebrates that are small, but the majority of residents in ecosystems, from bottom dweller to apex predator, were a meter or more long." Then came the mass extinction, which all but eliminated life on the planet. More than 97 percent of vertebrate species were wiped out. Sallan and Galimberti found that, following the extinction event, body size declined and continued downward for much longer than they expected -- at least 40 million years. "Some large species hung on, but most eventually died out," Sallan said. "So the end result is an ocean in which most sharks are less than a meter and most fishes and tetrapods are less than 10 centimeters, which is extremely tiny. Yet these are the ancestors of everything that dominates from then on, including humans."109

Large complex life is not an inevitable result of evolution, but one and only one possible result of long periods of environmental stability. Large animal life with complex brains is the rarest occurrence of all, seldom produced by evolution over billions of years. There is no reason to think that something like the cetaceans will ever again exist in the universe after we trade them away this century. And other simple and aggressive species, which are evolved to opportunistically displace large complex life and prevent its resurgence, already exist. “It probably won’t happen slowly, where we can watch for signals and milestones to decide when “enough” has occurred and it’s time to take action. Those signals and milestones have been all around us for decades and we have chosen to ignore them. It will probably happen more like a house of cards, where one card falls, causing others to fall, and so on. Pressure from above, unstable foundation below. We have, over the decades and through wanton disregard for consequence, turned our ecosystems into neighborhoods of card houses. So many populations of so many species are hanging on in dwindling numbers that it is hard to imagine that they will be resilient enough to endure a reshuffling of the food chain. And as one species collapses, so will those above it. Like the classic game Rock, Paper, Scissors, each of the perturbations vexing our oceans can be argued to overarch the others. But the reality is, they are severally and collectively part of an integrated system that both feeds and is fed, supplies and demands, gives and takes as part of the cycles of nature. Carbon dioxide in the air leads to warming and acidification . . . Acidification leads to flagellates and jellyfish . . . Warming leads to stratification, which leads to hypoxia . . . Hypoxia changes the water chemistry, which leads to flagellate blooms, which lead to jellyfish . . . Warming also enhances plant and animal growth, which leads to blooms, which lead to hypoxia . . . Warming also reduces oxygen in the water, making respiration more difficult, which leads to jellyfish ..Overnutrification leads to flagellates . . . Pollution leads to flagellates . . . Flagellates lead to jellyfish. Rock, paper, scissors.

It appears that, in many cases, jellyfish blooms are merely the most visible part of distressed ecosystems. In many cases, jellyfish are a symptom rather than a cause. As phytoplankton shift, so do copepods, and as smaller copepods dominate, fish decline. Perhaps in some cases jellyfish move into a vacant niche space, whereas in other cases, jellyfish are the last man left standing in an ecosystem where higher-energy animals can no longer thrive, while in still other cases, the ecosystem shifts to give jellyfish the competitive advantage. Anyway, jellyfish flourish.” – Dr. Lisa-Ann Gershwin

Earth’s oceans. Worth keeping?

We treat the oceans as an afterthought, a neighborhood we have outgrown; good for recreation, property values and healthy food, but pretty much an optional accessory to modern lifestyles. And we have some negative feelings about them too: hurricanes, tsunamis, sharks and the occasional Godzilla are from the seas. Actually, Godzilla is an apt symbol of our historic feelings about the seas... we depend on them, but never felt we could trust them. We still have stone-age brains which evolved on the plains of Africa, and our feelings come from those deep parts of us. Even people in land-locked states worry about shark attacks. There have been fewer than 3000 human fatalities documented from shark attacks in all of human history (since the year 1580), while roughly 100 million sharks are killed annually by humans, primarily for an expensive soup made from their fins for its mythological properties; the rest of the shark carcass, dead or often alive with mortal wounds, is thrown overboard.110 Our culture is mostly fine with that, because they have scary teeth, and our fear of predator teeth is an ancient brain module running in parallel with

our snake-detection systems that make our unconscious decisions. The way we feel about the oceans is linked to deep unconscious systems which respond to predator teeth. Said differently, we possess snake/predator aversion modules, but lack ocean-health neural modules. We were sculpted to manage the tribe, not Earth’s ecosystems ecology, yet we increasingly are mismanaging them. Sound pollution. Chemical pollution. Heavy metal pollution. Radioactive waste pollution. Plastics debris. Fertilizer-produced dead zones. Coral bleaching from warming. Dredge fishing. Military sonar. Subsea explosions, conventional and nuclear. Perhaps because they seemed bottomless, limitless and boundless, we have not treated Earth’s seas gently. A majority of large complex biomass that once swam there is already gone, even before the oceans become more acidic. Half of ocean life has died in just the last 45 years. Yet this only sets the stage for one of the most severe assaults on large complex life since our planet formed: acidification at least ten times as rapid as any which has occurred before in the history of the seas. And when we’re talking about life trying to adapt, the speed of change becomes hugely important. The faster it is, the harder it is for large, long-lived species to adapt., and the faster it is, the smaller and simpler the creatures which survive it, and therefore become the new basis for future evolution. Most of these have not evolved to be much different for hundreds of millions of years: because they didn’t need to. This human generation has a surreal question to answer: is it worth keeping the oceans alive in the sense they have been during our entire history as a species? As extraordinary as that question may seem while being posed, the current answer is even more bizarre: No. Our economies and GDP calculations see insufficient value to living seas to prioritize their continuation. It simply isn’t being done. You’ve heard of “global warming” ... not that our species is doing anything much useful about that either, aside from hand-waving, blogging, and making speeches. But likely you haven’t heard much about “ocean acidification” until this class, and certainly not that it’s the deadlier and more imminent effect of CO2 profligacy. Because a slime ocean won’t kill us. (at least not immediately) Yet doesn’t such a trade at least deserve consideration? Shouldn’t we ‘fess up to the fact that it is, indeed, a trade which we’re consciously making, and which need not be made? Should parents not tell the story to their kids, and ask whether the trade sounds like a good idea or a bad one?

Would a 5th grader change her future life trajectory if this story were told? Or would her mother chastise the school about being alarmist? What are the appropriate boundaries for alarm?

The Problem with Blind Spots

Most of how we experience the world happens in our minds. The corollary being: most of the physical universe thus exists in human blind spots. The evolutionary reasons for this are easy to understand: evolution designs a functional, surviving brain not just by designing clever modules, but by excluding everything which is irrelevant to an organism’s function. Blind spots evolved because they are useful: a frog’s retina does not even see things that don’t move, allowing the frog to concentrate on things which do, and things that move = calories = survival to a frog. Similarly, our senses and minds evolved to ignore the things which didn’t matter to the immediate survival of a hunter-gatherer tribal primate. It makes sense then that we don’t notice the gaps: our minds stitch together the small pieces of the world we do see into a subjectively-seamless experience. The maintenance of this illusion, and of our evolved blind spots, is so central to our mind function that we don’t even notice the cognitive change if half our neocortex is removed surgically. We know the seas of earth exist, but for most of us they exist primarily in a blind spot; not as a magical world which comprises most of the life we know of in the universe, but as something external to our needs and wants, with no discrete value to us. And there’s another world which exists in very much the same blind spot as the deep sea: our own deep future. The existence of the trillions of human childhoods which could happen over the coming million years if we simply stopped doing what we’re doing this century and did something sane instead. Those future people exist to us in the same remote, abstract way as the creatures in the seas, and their existence is riding on exactly the same decisions. X\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_ “We, the undersigned, (Homo sapiens), agree to trade our Oceans, for…………………”

A post script from DJ

We opened this draft for you honors students with a reference to my own getting to know the seas while around your age. I hope some of you have had, or will have, such a chance. If you want to, you’d better hurry. But to that hope I offer a caution: you

may find that you come to deeply love the seas of earth, what they have been and even today – diminished – still are. And you may find that you come to love and feel empathy for their creatures. A profound part of Reality101 – which is to say the real world – is that it is richer and more special than our species is generally willing to conceive. And love, together with self-respect, can move you in ways you would not have planned. It’s hard to have empathy for something like “the living seas” because they don’t have a face. We don’t think much about issues which don’t have faces. So here I’ll offer one face of the big trade: dolphins. They are a good face for human-generated acidification because they are equivalent to us as beings, have lived with large and conscious brains for many millions of years longer than we have, are friendly aboriginal societies which have never harmed mankind, and may be consigned to extinction this century as one small part of our trade of fossil slave largesse for healthy seas. One of my best friends and teachers during my life was a dolphin. I found, to my amazement, that dolphins are people in every sense of the word, less clever than we are in some ways and far smarter in others. The fact that we share the world with such beings – who have had large brains and almost certainly a sense of humor since long before humans existed – is both profound and incredibly lucky. They are friendly aliens with minds and abilities very different from our own. The most fearsome ocean carnivore is the orca. Great white sharks flee in terror at the faintest hint of an orca’s presence, and for good reason: a fight between an orca and a full-grown great white shark is like a human versus a restaurant buffet – no fight at all. Orcas eat polar bears and walrus. If you look at an orca skull, you’re reminded of a tyrannosaur. Yet humans can swim with them safely. There has never, to my knowledge, been a documented unprovoked attack from an orca on a human being. (I hope you pause for a second to consider just how extraordinary that is.) I have a scientist friend who dives with them while they’re feeding. To me it’s surreal that nobody much makes note of these aspects of reality: the existence of disciplined, advanced alien minds on our home planet. We are seemingly unable to recognize genius if it isn’t technological, which is why I had to create an entire lab and hire a PhD ethologist to scientifically demonstrate something which was utterly clear to me from knowing dolphins as people: they’re not only self-aware by simple criteria, but have an advanced concept of mind. Perhaps more of one than we do: otherwise, orcas would pluck kayakers like we pick berries. I’ve been in a deep pool interacting with a wild-captured 17-foot psuedorca (example

shown below) which playfully took my body in its mouth, and could have instantly bitten me in half as they do to 200-lb tuna.

The only thing between me and a grisly death in the maw of a roughly-captured wild beast was the fact that these giant jaws and teeth were controlled by a sophisticated mind, and I relied on that. (Though I admit that one’s assumptions flash before one’s eyes as you see such jaws open and coming at you underwater). As mentioned previously, I have also seen what happens when this same creature falls under the control of humans, and it is not pretty. We do not naturally extend the same courtesy to other minds – or even recognize them unless they act alongside human convention. While writing this essay I find myself thinking about the extraordinary footage from 2012 of a wild dolphin approaching human divers at night for help removing a fish-hook and line from her fin. This was the act of a sentient aboriginal with complex theory of mind, who voluntarily sought out and put herself entirely under the control of a fearsome alien species, at night, under glaring lights, for a painful operation, while holding her breath. This one video should have caused hundreds of scientific papers to be written, because the number of advanced cognitive traits, and logical deductions, necessary for that dolphin to do that is breathtaking. It’s the act of a person recognizing other persons, and expecting cross-species empathy. (and unless dolphins had such empathy, they could not in turn expect it.) If IQ were a relevant measure between species – it isn’t - is her equivalent IQ 30 or 300? What is the dolphin’s concept of humans? Does she recognize that the scuba divers with lights and air tanks are another instance of the creatures that make and use hooks and

lines? If so, how? Recall that South American natives – even with a fairly advanced civilization – considered Spaniards with metal hats riding on horses to be magical beings, and thus totally misjudged them. Yet this dolphin nailed her assessments, even of a highly technological species with whom she’d had no common ancestors for 70 million years. Did she deduce humans’ ability, intentionality, tool-using, and empathy on her own – which would be astonishing, a flash of insight and serial deductive reasoning comparable to those of Sir Isaac Newton and other historic human great thinkers – or did she learn such abstract and foreign ideas from other dolphins somehow, which would be maybe even more astonishing? Yet this hard evidence of advanced mind – dare I say genius – is currently just a bit of YouTube trivia like vids of cats in boxes and dogs chasing lasers. This dolphin would likely have slowly died of her entanglement without a series of brilliant, sophisticated, and accurate deductions about terrifying aliens which she then had the courage to submit to. Can you imagine the mind which could make such deductions, control its own fear and pain, and come out the other side of the experience having achieved the desired goal? And having tried to imagine it, can you imagine how poignant it will be that this youtube clip could easily outlive her species? OK, end of digression, back to making my point... Understanding and acknowledging the intelligence of cetaceans which was ignored by my species meant that I had an obligation to learn about the way we were interacting with them and of course that wasn’t pleasant. We have been wiping them out, in their millions, since I was a child. No particular reason, it’s just one of those things we humans do while pursuing other goals.
