But accompanying my awareness of these things, I found my life re-aligning itself in order to stand in the way of that juggernaut. Not that I necessarily could stop it, but I found that such a decision was ultimately a very simple one, even if scary. That has meant that for most of my life I’ve been living a parallel reality, dual worlds; seeing my species get richer, more certain and less satisfied, while watching cetacean species diminish and vanish. As this unfolded, I found myself at odds with some very scary humans like organized criminals and governments. But there have been victories along the way, and because of them millions of dolphins are still alive in the seas at this writing. Minds in the waters, singing songs we don’t understand. It turns out that there are more things than dolphins that reside in our blind spots. If we learn to step outside these blind spots and engage the world in ways which actually work, rather than which “feel like they should work” to our hodgepodge

modular brains, a few true “Homo sapiens” can make a big difference, and Nate and I will touch on this more as the Reality101 semester winds up. But this is one reason why the “big trade” – the unremarked sacrifice of large ocean species for roughly an extra 50 years of “buffer” of the climate impacts of fossil energy – hits me very hard. Not only because of the imminent loss of a world of coral kingdoms through which I’ve flown in dreamlike waking wonder, the dance of living creatures surrounding me which often seemed like fantasy. But because one part of the big trade, if it continues, is simply that the cetaceans could not be expected to survive it. As high-metabolism, slow-reproducing species at the top of their respective food chains, they’d be among the first to go once acidification dismantles the basis of existing food webs. Each dolphin species has a distinct and unique sort of mind, and the loss of any is a loss as great as that of a hominid species. (not that we greatly mourn the loss of other human species, or other apes, these days, though I hope you’ll agree we should). A pseudorca has a different sort of mind than a bottlenosed dolphin, who has a different mind than an orca, who has a different take on the world than a rough-toothed dolphin, who is different from an Amazon river dolphin. We’ve outlived (euphemism there) the other human species (though genetic analysis shows that I have 2.9% Neanderthal DNA, which I find obscurely pleasing. With our smaller brains and motivating myths, was our species smarter or just more ornery?) In the cetaceans’ case, we’re not just talking about one race of self-aware beings, but many. Scientists from our little lab also tested pseudorca and orca for self-awareness, and they passed. Of course. But what a basic test that is for creatures with brains larger than our own, who have millions of years more experience with using such brains to deal with Reality101. My own reaction to the “Big Trade” – while describing it in exactly these terms in these writings to you Honors students – is “Hell No.” But altering the course of the aggregate human amoeba will take actions and engagement that I’m getting too old to pursue on my own. Writing this stuff for you guys is a part of a commitment I made to a particular dolphin long ago; and that’s not some sort of journalistic, poetic, or romantic license, it’s simple truth. The gross metric of GDP, and the banality of fossil-fueled excess, is to me a poor substitute for humans sharing a lonely universe with friendly, curious minds unlike our own. We currently spend billions hoping to find bacteria on Mmars – and we count that as a healthy part of our GDP. At the same time, we take 250-year-old minds, in brains twice the size of our own, and make them into dog food (Bowhead whales). And now that there are too few to be worth hunting for pet food, we’re trading them away.

There are other ‘people’ besides us. I hate the thought of them going away. Our own species and future will be less complete without them. We are presently sacrificing them for self-indulgent and dubious reasons. Their minds cannot affect the course of human events. Yours may. – DJ

DJ with his dolphin friend in 1976. (Yes, she was the one who nailed him in the forehead with the herrings)

CO2: Playing in Traffic

This is the chapter in which we describe how we live on a wonderful planet, and it would be a great shame if we screw it up by overheating the air and water, acidifying the seas, and killing off the large interesting species of plants and animals we have previously relied on. Let’s face it, you’ve seen a lot of nature documentaries. So you probably expect, here, a pretty much boilerplate description of how our planet used to be an Eden, but how lately we’ve been mucking it up via pollution and mischief, so we really should stop being such jerks and just let mother nature get back to nurturing us. And that’s true enough, as far as it goes. But it skips over a rather large question which we should really consider: does nature - the rules of the physical universe, as manifested in ecological communities of life on this planet - actually have a tendency to be beneficial to the continuation of life, or is it more likely to kill itself off? And is the answer to this hidden by a logical fallacy almost too big to be recognized? These questions are seldom asked, because we clearly evolved on a planet which has been a rich source of food and materials for us and our evolutionary predecessors, and life has taken many forms on the planet in the last 3 billion years. Many aspects of the world are Eden-like, even today in their diminished form. It “feels like” a system which should be inherently stable, if only we stopped perturbing it and trusted mother nature. And indeed, “mother nature” has clearly done a better job at keeping a healthy planet alive than humans have, to date.

But what if generalizing based on the history that we and our forebears have experienced is a logical fallacy? That’s the implication which will be explored here, in a chapter which is nominally about climate heating and the acidification of the oceans, the evil-twin CO2 issues. Why bother to make such a point? Because it helps make the case that perhaps - instead of residing in a system which is self-stabilizing and will always heal itself - we are likely to exist on a knife-edge of ecological, chemical, and thermal balance which we cannot expect to be stabilized by some sort of mindless daisy-world beneficence to automatically save us from our own excesses. There’s a case to be made that we need to distrust the things which got us here: both the genes and their agenda, and the presumption of a cosmos, a planet, and a self-stabilizing global ecosystem which is unerringly beneficial to large complex life such as we. The ecology and life of this planet are wonderful – and likely even rarer and more wonderful than we think - but are not inherently durable as systems. The genes which drive them are just as likely to back off a cliff as climb a mountain, for they are both blind and unconscious. Only the mind can anticipate and understand, and potentially stabilize the processes of life in a universe which is frequently inimical to it. This is, then, a further argument against following the urges of the gene agenda, in favor of developing a rational deep-time agenda for earth-life. An argument that without the direction of mind, earth life is likely to run itself off the rails. No, it’s not some boneheaded argument in favor of reductionist geoengineering for short-term effects, but a realization that the backward-looking “agenda of the gene” is inherently unable to even try to stabilize an ecosystem, or attain any other future goal. But how could that be? How could something as basic as the inevitable stability of nature be a fallacy?

Anthropic Survivor Bias: The Biteypuss Fallacy

We (DJ and Mrs.) once had a truly awful cat named Biteypuss, and only part of her story is salient here. Raised indoors, she hated the indoors, and upon becoming adult inflicted many injuries and types of damage in order to go outside. However, once outside, she didn’t hunt sparrows or mice, but instead played with trucks and cars. She somehow acquired the notion that they were insubstantial wraiths which existed to amuse her, and no amount of restriction could keep her out of the busy street. There she would lie on her back on the yellow lane-dividing line, merrily contorting like a cat which had already been hit by something, but only batting her paws at the tires which missed her by inches. Whenever we became aware she had made it to the street

again, we’d run out, stop traffic and pick her up, and get bitten and scratched in the process. The amazing thing was that she managed to do this pretty much daily, for significant numbers of months - without ever being disabused of the notion that the careening metal objects zooming by were just big cat toys which were meant to be played with. The odds of her remaining un-flattened, and even unperturbed, for as long as she did had to have been extremely low - like thousands to one against. That cat should have been road kill within a week. Improbably, her story ended well; we managed to get her accepted by a fishing research station on an island with no roads. She lived a very long life, and never did figure out that cars and trucks could hurt her, much less that we weren’t her enemies for marooning her. However, her experience was atypical for aspiring roadkill, and said nothing significant about the actual risk of lying in the road, who her real friends were, etc. Simply by surviving, she acquired a skewed frame of reference. We live on a world which until now has been well-suited to life. However, the point of this essay is that That could not be otherwise, because complex beings such as ourselves can only exist on a planet which has had the time, stability, and luck to have hosted life for billions of years. Thus, any conscious beings evolving anywhere in the universe will look to their planet’s history and necessarily see something like an Eden in its past, even if that was incredibly unlikely. However, that past does not constrain its future in any way. So here’s a seldom-asked question: is this universe welcoming and inherently hospitable to life such as human life, or is it more hostile to life, with its main concession being that its rules don’t preclude life’s possibility? The next question is: once life has gotten started, does it automatically modify its planet to make it more conducive to life, as was popularized by the Gaia theory, or does it just do a random walk in a minefield which could equally well end poorly? Or at the opposite extreme, does life tend to be suicidal? Geologist Peter Ward explores that latter possibility in The Medea Hypothesis, and it’s recommended reading. The point of this essay is not to repeat his reasoning, but to note the basic logical fallacy which could hide such a possibility from us in plain sight.

So is all life alike?

First of all, we should clarify the exact question we wish to answer: what is the definition of “life” which actually has meaning to us? This turns out not to be a trivial question. Clearly, current life’s beginning was simple single-celled animals such as present-day bacteria. Moreover, they have always been, and are today, the main story

of life on earth. We pay more attention to lions and tigers and bears, but bacteria are the more effective human predators. Their biomass can be reduced hugely and they can still bounce back. Indeed, even once the earth’s oceans inevitably boil away in the far future as the sun brightens, the bacteria living slow-motion lives deep in the rocks of the crust will survive a long time. It’s not impossible that Mars might have such life inside it even now. But does that matter? Is that really the sort of “life” we care about emotionally? There’s a big difference between bacteria and large, complex life like trees and animals. The former are resilient whereas the latter are very fragile. In the rapid changes of a mass extinction only one thing is certain: large complex life goes away first, for much the same reason basketballs are more robust against earthquakes than are wine glasses. The conditions which large complex life requires to exist are much more demanding than what bacteria need to exist. These include very stable conditions which don’t change rapidly, because such creatures reproduce very slowly. The largest mammal which made it through the last big mass extinction was the size of a squirrel with a tiny brain, and all today’s mammals are descended from those diminutive survivors. The geological record indicates that simple single-celled life started on earth quite quickly after the earth had cooled enough to make it possible. So it’s possible the universe might be reasonably welcoming of single-celled life forms, to the extent that they come into existence pretty quickly, and quickly rebound when nearly killed off. But large complex life is a different story. It took a full 1.5 billion years to develop organelles like chloroplasts and mitochondria which made possible the plants and animals we know today. That’s a long enough wait for these unlikely events that it’s clear that they might easily have not occurred at all in the window of time in which the earth was in the temperature range for life to exist. And large complex life hasn’t had an easy time of it since then. Based on the inexorably expanding sun, in the course of life on earth, bacteria will get about 5 billion years of existence. Large complex life (things big enough to see, including bugs) will get about a billion (if it’s lucky), which is roughly half over now. The window of existence for conscious, self-aware brains might be immensely shorter, particularly if we leave it to the genes and the universe to write the story… as we’re now doing. Our motto in all things seems to be “so far so good.”

Central to the anthropic survivorship fallacy is our reliance on history as a predictor of the future. As noted, we look back and see a planet which has provided for our

every need, and that of our ancestors, and allowed us to grow that most unlikely of organs, a large self-aware abstracting brain. The point of the “survivor bias” fallacy is that this is the only pattern of history an evolved self-aware brain could ever see in its history even in principle, so it says nothing about whether it was a likely outcome or a ridiculously far outlier, like Biteypuss’s lifelong certainty about the insubstantial nature of trucks. So, taking a step back from the seeming reassurance of history, what would we logically expect to see in a universe which was inherently hospitable to intelligent life, and in which a “Gaia” effect stabilized the ecologies, temperatures, atmospheres, oceans, and environmental variables of a living planet once life got a foothold? Well, we’d expect to look around and see other planets in the solar system with life. Both Venus and Mars presumably had the initial conditions for life and have been cross-seeded with earth bacteria (and we with theirs) due to meteor strikes. Their environments should have been stabilized and gone on to develop intelligent life. We’d expect to see the signatures of oxygen atmospheres in extrasolar planets. We’d expect to pick up radio waves from intelligent beings talking to one another. And if there was unlimited energy awaiting the discovery of intelligent minds, we’d probably expect to see actual evidence of intelligent aliens having visited our solar system. In Earth’s history, we’d expect to see the quick stabilization of the planetary biome and a quick and orderly progression to large-brained beings, appearing fairly early and commonly in the earth’s fossil record. What would we logically expect to see in a universe which was hostile to intelligent life? We could not exist if there had been a full extinction event, but we’d expect to see many catastrophic near-extinction events for complex earth-life. We’d expect to see evidence of past cataclysms on our planet and on other planets in the solar system. The planets around us would be devoid of large complex life. No extrasolar planets with oxygen atmospheres would be seen. There would be no hint of radio or any other communication by other intelligent beings, or any solid evidence they had ever visited our solar system. There would be no fossil record of large-brained self-aware beings prior to recent evolution after the latest mass extinction. And this is what we see. Looking at the past mass extinctions - were they unlucky one-off events, or inherent to life on a world such as ours? Do living systems self-stabilize, or are they at all times poised on the brink of arbitrarily large extinctions? Here mathematics gives us a clue: the pattern of earth’s past mass extinctions follows a power law relating an extinction’s size to its frequency of occurrence, just like earthquakes, forest fires, and other systems whose behavior derives from the fundamental structure and connectivity of the system’s parts. This could indicate that the earth’s biosphere is poised for a potential mass extinction at all times, just as the San Andreas fault is

primed for a possible earthquake. The stability of large complex earth-life, and the recent friendliness of Earth to conscious life, may not be inevitable but have been just a lucky break. And indeed, the “deep questions” about the universe seem to be converging on “anthropic” answers: there is probably no reason that the laws of our universe and the masses of our elementary particles are so fine-tuned for life’s existence. The odds are so stupendously against such fine-tuning that it seems we are living in a Biteypuss universe after all, a rare livable universe among a preposterously greater proportion of universes in which not only life but solid matter could not exist. Admittedly, this could seem like an abstruse logical detour for this course, but if true it could have practical and immediate implications for our current subjects.

Anthropogenic CO2: Global Heating and Ocean Acidification

It’s has taken a bit of windup to get to the ostensible subject of this essay, because it’s hard to internalize it without an appreciation of the knife-edge of environmental stability upon which complex communities of life - and particularly large intelligent life - balances. Life itself can alter a planet to make it more hospitable to life or less so (remember the stromatolites?). Since most life is as brainless as a rock rolling down a hill, it doesn’t automatically alter a planet in a Gaia-like way to better accommodate future life. It’s at least as likely to do something that kills itself and all other life off. But - as noted - no self-aware being will look to her planet’s past and recognize that, because the fact of her being alive means such a killing event didn’t happen on her planet. However, if she decides to believe she can depend on “Gaia-like” effects, or inherently robust environmental stability, to safeguard the planet’s future, she’s taking a real risk. The “survivor effect” fallacy of perspective must always present a reasonably clement planetary history to any conscious observer evolved on that planet, though any footprints of past mass extinctions should be her clue that things can go badly wrong. The earth exists at the inside edge of the “habitable zone” around the sun, purportedly about 1% away from being in the zone which is expected to cook a planet to lifelessness. Even if the existence of clouds ultimately cuts us a bit of slack, that zone is moving outward as the sun inexorably heats up, so there’s that. Indeed, life has kept earth from that fate so far. Unlike our sister planet Venus - which has a principally CO2 atmosphere - life has bound up most of earth’s past CO2in deep layers of carbonate rocks, precipitated by the skeletons of microscopic life over deep time. Free oxygen in the atmosphere is also a product of life and is what gives rise to the ozone

layer which protects life from solar ultraviolet radiation. This was not “planned” by bacteria seeking stability, it was emergent from dumb luck. However, life on earth has also done things like killing off other life with poison gas - oxygen was toxic to most life when photosynthesis first got going, and it caused massive dying. The seas can also go stagnant and flood the air with hydrogen sulfide gas produced by purple bacteria which rise from the deeps whenever conditions allow. Life also caused the earth’s oceans to freeze from poles to equator for huge swaths of time by pulling too much CO2 out of the air, locking the planet in ice. If not for the fortuitous existence of undersea volcanic chimneys providing chemical energy, that would have been “it” for our ancestors. Life on earth survived these and many other past “oops” situations when life nearly did away with life - again, not because the odds were necessarily good for life to survive, but because if it had not survived, we wouldn’t be around to figure out the history and talk about it. Suffice it to say that life itself can do either thing: make a world safer for life or more hostile to life. Perhaps neither Gaia or Medea, but just stuff that happens. It turns out that this is not just an abstract thing to muse upon, but a significant aspect of the current human predicament. We, like any self-aware life which evolves in this universe, look to our evolutionary past and see a planet which has largely been benign toward us, even to the extent of killing off past dominant life multiple times, principally involving CO2 variation. We therefore conclude that such benign treatment of humans is a built-in feature of planet earth, and that nothing life can do - we can do - would likely alter that. In other words, we think a lot like Biteypuss. Science, however, strenuously says otherwise. It’s now looking as though most of the past mass extinctions on the earth are correlated with the amount of CO2 in the air being altered.105 It seems to be the main “mass extinction” control lever, and it is mediated by life. If too much CO2 is removed from the atmosphere, the oceans freeze from equator to pole, killing off large, complex life. If life allows CO2 to rise too high, the planet heats and stagnates, killing off large, complex life. In the oceans, high CO2 has a different but still-lethal effect. It acidifies the water, dissolving calcium skeletons. This causes mass extinctions of life on all scales which require such skeletons, and rearranges the ocean food webs, which tends to kill off large complex life. The effect of CO2 heating is that the seas stratify and stagnate, becoming toxic soups of bacterial slimes and simple creatures which don’t need much oxygen. Again, large complex life is killed off. As seas become anoxic, purple bacteria pump out ever-increasing quantities of toxic H2S gas, which kills off life of all sizes.

So if the geological past shows us anything, it’s that rapid change is both hostile to conscious life, and also an inherent possibility of earth’s ecosystem. If it teaches us any single lesson, it’s “don’t screw with CO2.” So of course we have created a global industrial civilization whose primary effect is to inject CO2 into the atmosphere and oceans as fast as possible. All else that humans do is secondary in scale: to a good first approximation, humans function as CO2 catalysts, with any other attributes being rounding errors. The oceans are now acidifying at least ten to one hundred times faster than any previous time on earth, including the worst parts of past mass extinction events. The climate is heating at a similarly rapid pace. Scientists expect children born today to outlive coral. This essay won’t add to the hundreds of thousands of research reports documenting the onset of a 6th mass extinction, and the certainty of these basic physical effects following basic cause. However, it’s written to note that high-energy fire-symbiote life - to survive its own excesses - needs to be intelligent enough not to feel too secure that the earth’s ecosystems will somehow automatically “fix,” “accommodate,” or “stabilize” any CO2 excursions we find convenient to emit. That’s simply not what they do. They can just as easily make things worse as better, and indeed science seems to be finding about a dozen scary and plausible positive feedbacks for CO2 heating and acidification for every negative feedback which is suggested. Life in this universe seems to be a chancy thing, and self-aware life as a large complex creature the most chancy of all. It looks like we’ve been incredibly lucky to get to this point. We need to wonder whether we should keep doubling-down until an inevitable loss or take our winnings and use them to stabilize the ecosystem for large life. Our large brains give us the potential to use this information to steer a course into the future, for ourselves and other complex life. However, those large brains are significantly imprinted with a backward-looking “gene agenda” of urges and biases. That evolved agenda makes it “feel better” to keep emitting CO2 while ignoring the intermediate and long-term future. The notion of living without extensively burning fossils is no more palatable to us than “moving to an island” was for a snarling Biteypuss being carried onto a boat, even though that was one more lucky break for one of the world’s luckiest bad kitties. She got away with being in total denial of the danger of speeding vehicles, by having no conception that she was an outlier among millions of flat cats with similar delusions. Which may help explain why there are no radio signals from aliens detectable in space.

Section V: Synthesis

So how does this all fit together into a synthesis, and why is doing that important? While in our minds we tend to think of aspects of reality as separate things, everything actually happens at once, all aspects of the physical world and human behavior interacting with each other to create emergent realities. We need to understand these emergent effects if we’re to have any idea of what’s really going on around us, and what future we’re actually heading towards. It turns out that this knowledge is significantly at odds with what nearly all humans now believe, and that’s important.

Seeking Homo Sapiens

“Humanity today is like a walking dreamer, caught between the fantasies of sleep and the chaos of the real world. The mind seeks but cannot find the precise place and hour. We have created a Star Wars civilization, with Stone Age emotions, medieval institutions, and god-like technology. We thrash about. We are terribly confused by the mere fact of our existence, and are a danger to ourselves and to the rest of life.” E.O.Wilson, 2012, The Social Conquest of Earth Welcome to the top of the mountain. Dear students of Reality, we made it, via switchback, rope, steep climbs and descents through scree. The view is dizzying, but hazy in the distance (future). The first principles you’ve been exposed to on our ascent underpin the most relevant issues that will affect your lives on this planet, this century. Below is a summary of the milestones we passed on the way up, and when combined, what they imply.

We are stardust - an outcome of once-in-a-galaxy circumstances and deep time. From the big bang to the formation of earth 4.5 billion years ago, the molecules and atoms within ancient cosmic explosions coalesced and self-organized. Life, unexpectedly and perhaps against tall odds, arose from the hostile and variable conditions, endured 9 mass extinctions and myriad minor extinctions in life’s history and arrived here, with us and including us, today. From single-celled cyanobacteria to fish to reptiles to animals to dinosaurs to mammals, the catalogue of earth’s creatures is a kaleidoscope of colors, shapes, sizes, habitats and behaviors. All these organisms

seek energy, and move towards and away from other stimuli relevant to their survival. Our biology is thus an emergent property of random physical interactions through history that have created the proximate rules that explain our world. Life: probability, complexity, and fragility There’s something very important to realize about the nature of life. This truth resides in one of our many blind spots, so you may not have thought much about it. But evolution has not been a one-way march of simplicity to complexity. Rather, the main story of life was and still is simple life: single cells. They were first on earth and will be last by a wide margin, because single-celled life are tough little bastards which multiply at breakneck speed and exist in multitudes. There are more single-celled animals on earth than there are stars in the universe – the current estimate is five billion trillion trillion – that’s a 5 followed by 30 zeroes. Simple life seems to have started on earth almost immediately once conditions were right, and has never looked back. Relative to multicellular life, it is thus high probability, low complexity, and low fragility. Complex life is multicellular and spans a large range of complexity, becoming more fragile as the complexity rises. Large life is a measure of body size, and is relatively rare and becomes more fragile as size increases. Large complex life with brains is incredibly rare and fragile, depending on very stable conditions to exist, and large complex life with conscious, abstracting brains are the lowest-probability, rarest and most fragile emergent feature of the rarest and most fragile life, which require great body size, long childhoods, dense nutrition, and high complexity. We evolved in a time of stable, advantageous conditions which would support k-selected species with long lifetimes, low reproductive rates, and brains. However, rapid environmental changes favor the opposite: mass extinctions are unpredictable in their detailed outcomes, but very predictable in their patterns: their gross effect is to get rid of large complex life in favor of small simple life. The biggest mammal to come through the KT extinction weighed just a few pounds. The lesson, if we are willing to look squarely at it, is that large complex life is a side-story which plays out in times of stable conditions, when simple life gradually expands into unoccupied parts of the fitness landscape. Periodically, when change happens abruptly, the world can once again be reduced to slimes, bacteria and small vertebrates. In the rules of life, fragility and complexity are two sides of the same coin. We, and the other large complex life which now exists, will only continue if very special conditions also continue. Quite simply, life such as us was not an inevitability but rather like a rare gem.

Energy is (almost) everything.

From worms to watermelons to wolves and everything in between, we are genetically related to all living creatures on this planet. In total there are estimated to be ~10 million species on Earth – though if micro-organisms in the soil, etc. are included this tally of our ‘cousins’ may reach 100 million. The commonality in the lives of all these creatures is their daily retrieval and use of energy. Nature abhors a gradient. Life requires one. Energy originating from the sun cascades through natural systems allowing life to flourish, reproduce and form ecosystems. Though other criteria play a role – like habitat, climate and luck - energy is the primary currency of every one of the ~100 million species which comprise the life systems of the natural world on Earth. Those organisms able to access a high caloric return relative to their energy investment have advantages in survival, mating, metabolic maintenance, and raising healthy offspring. Energy is the currency of life. We are Reality blind. Surviving in the physical world isn’t easy. Something like 99.9% of all species ever to have lived are now either extinct or have morphed into what lives today. There’s a furious, never-ending competition for energy, nutrients and habitat space, in which there are winners and losers. This life and death struggle for ongoing existence is the crucible from which currently-extant species – including ourselves – emerged. In order to survive, we have been honed by evolution to function perfectly for the niche in which we evolved. This has meant erring in many very specific ways which made us fitter for the savannahs even as it made our perceptions less-true about reality. (Evolution selects for fitness, not veridical perception). Our minds are well-evolved to be members of small hunter-gatherer tribes in a wilderness, yet we now attempt to run a planet, with many of our evolved tendencies being poorly suited to it (so far). Facts don’t move us, feelings do. And feelings have a strong evolutionary origin. Consider a hound – if she smells a rabbit, what that smell does to her brain and behavior will have a more intense effect than other stimuli like food, sleep, shelter or even human commands. “Smelling and chasing small animals’ is what the hound was selected for. In the same fashion, the crucible of the Pleistocene selected us for: ~intense deference to social pressures for approval, acceptance and status

~intense in-group defense and support in parallel with out-group demonization ~ a long list of cognitive traits that made us valuable tribemembers historically but which manifest as blind spots today, including: delusionality, time bias,

optimism bias, and conformity bias. These combine to make our mental experience of reality greatly diverge greatly from physical reality ~vulnerability to having our mental systems hijacked by modern stimuli many times more potent than what we encountered in our past, crowding out the slower, longer term (and ultimately important) goals

The presence of these genetically pre-programmed tendencies – some weak – some strong, has major implications for addressing sustainability, resource depletion and environmental problems. Among them:

There exists a large and important disconnect between our virtual and physical worlds

Our brains construct personal virtual worlds, which is all we can ever experience, and what we rely upon to be successful in our day to day lives. These virtual worlds often have little correlation with the reality of the physical world. All the things that feel important to us exist in our virtual worlds, yet their very existence depends upon conditions and events in the physical world. These two worlds function under different rules, yet we blur the distinction, if aware at all

Our belief that others believe is the glue that holds the human superorganism together

What we rely on when anticipating the reactions of others is not whether an organizing belief is true, but whether others reliably believe it to be so. We’ll trade a house for green pieces of paper, or the transfer of one’s and zero’s in a bank’s computer, because we rely on others’ beliefs about the green paper and digital numbers to be the same as ours. This sort of “belief in belief” has served our species well in helping it develop into a “superorganism” which is formidable and able to undertake tasks larger than individuals ever could on their own. All human institutions, such as the stock market, the corner store, international trade, and much else are networks of shared beliefs upon which we rely. The thing to realize is that the utility is not in the beliefs themselves – there’s nothing special about green paper – but in the perceived authority of the belief itself. Human societies can get equivally high utility out of shared beliefs regardless whether the beliefs themselves correspond to the physical world or are nonsense. Over time, this ‘belief in belief’ has functioned to make larger human superorganisms (pursuing surplus) possible. And it may have been the edge which caused us to displace other human species in the relatively recent past. Yet with our species now in control of a planet’s ecology, organizing around nonsense beliefs is having important physical ramifications.

Human happiness is not a function of physical wealth

With brains keyed to consider tribal resource distribution a matter of survival, we seek relative wealth, yet absolute wealth is what we actually need. In that sense, we’re wired for greed and the pursuit of excess: The intensity of the feeling of want is greater than the intensity of satisfaction once we get it. And once we get something, we fear its loss more than we value getting more. We care more about our perceived wealth compared to the Joneses than whether Jones is happy or healthy (or sane). In short, our happiness is not very correlated with resource consumption and stuff, yet modern culture (and advertising, and Mom) tells us it is. It isn’t the “stuff” that makes us happy, it’s feeling like we got a “better deal” than the other tribemembers. This was strongly adaptive as we evolved in times of frequent scarcity. But now there’s no evolved mental limit on “how much is enough” because there was never a reason for such a thing to evolve.

We solve physical world dilemmas first by using social sorting mechanisms. Etc.

In addition to measuring our ‘worth’ as human beings compared to “Jones” we also look to Jones to get cues on what the right course of action is. Because we are incredibly social (“pansocial”) we make decisions about real world problems based on what our friends and high status persons are saying and doing. If our friends and the elite in our tribe make poor decisions about the physical world, we are extremely likely to as well. This effect is so strong we barely notice it - if others dismiss something, like smoke under a door, we dismiss it too. It’s our evolved default, and it inhibits the spread of beliefs which are not pleasant while reinforcing those which are. Most humans function with no scientific thinking or reasoning at all using this default, even many who are highly educated. When we hear new information, our first reaction is to observe who else believes it, not how well it conforms to first principles.

Enlightenment via more information doesn’t work Facts don’t move us, feelings do. Feelings come from old, deep brain modules which pre-date our species. If a fact concerns something which we did not evolve to consider salient, we don’t generate strong feelings about it. A surprising number of auto fatalities are caused by insects crawling onto the driver: we have no instincts to fear speed, but do have instincts to shake off bugs, so a spider on your foot ‘shouts louder’ than the sight of an oncoming truck.

It “feels like” informing our other tribe members of new worrisome information should be enough to solve any problem, which is why our social movements strive for universal enlightenment (and why blogging and tweeting is so darn satisfying). However, it doesn’t work. New information is sorted by an “interpreter” module in our minds depending on whether it conforms or conflicts with existing beliefs, and is accepted or rejected based on that classification. Receiving conforming information does the same thing as receiving conflicting information: it maintains and hardens existing opinions. Human activism has largely failed because it is based on what ‘feels right’

and other unexamined assumptions In addition to relying on the ‘enlightenment model of social change’(above), we currently prioritize perceived fairness of process over the effectiveness (or even sanity!) of group decisions. We have narratives about how things happen in the world, and those narratives don’t always correspond to the way things do happen in the world. Yet we primarily seek social recognition confirming that we’re striving admirably, instead of focusing on actually achieving stable new system states quickly. Thus, so far, the “social ecosystem” of current human activism is mostly about tribe dynamics and founded on unexamined assumptions. There may be ways to significantly improve upon it. (in fact, we have some to suggest)

We are Delusional about Being Delusional The default agenda of the human gene, ensconced deeply within every human brain, is now the enemy of complex life in the deep future, including human life. We can coexist with delusionality, even enjoy and celebrate it. But we can no longer afford to run a planet based on it. Quite simply, homo sapiens

isn’t here yet. We are an amazingly competent species, which has forged bold new paths away from our humble beginnings. And, as we travel further away from our hunter-gatherer past, our biggest blind spot is that we have very little appreciation for our blind spots. We feel like our concept of the world is largely correct, admirable, seamless, and complete. We’re evolved to feel that way, with a “rationalizing” module which retroactively stitches together information from thousands of other brain modules. This means that many things are effectively invisible to us. In Reality101 you’ve been introduced to just a few of these things. (Do not doubt that there are many more still invisible to Nate & DJ: we keep finding more, and we’re just two guys with stone-age brains thumbing a ride in the “sapiens” direction. Our collective blind spots may hide amazing truths and pathways we yet have no inkling of, and one of you may one day point them out to us, and others).

Economic theory is False (Well the important parts):

Energy makes the world go round – Energy is what ultimately allows and constrains any living system and it can only be substituted by other energy. Yet mainstream society (based on the Cobb-Douglas/Solow models in economic theory) believes capital and human labor completely explain our economic growth and productivity. They do not. $10 worth of gasoline is treated the same as $10 worth of donuts or underwear but can perform the work of a man working for a year. We thus conflate the dollar value of fossil energy, with the ‘work’ value. Our society confuses energy’s cost of acquisition with its contribution to our current living arrangements. (This is a huge fallacy: for example, do we think oxygen is worth nothing to us because we don’t pay to breathe it?) Fossil energy is the backbone of industrial civilization and will get progressively more expensive this century, with or without including environmental externalities. With ~99% of the physical labor done in the human economy currently done by inanimate technology, the majority of which powered by fossil carbon compounds, this fallacy has major implications.

Most of our modern technology is just a vector for using more energy– We have created amazing gadgets and conveniences, but most of our technology is about inefficiently (energy-wise) replacing activities humans used to do manually at a cost of much higher primary energy inputs, as well as coming up with new wants, needs, and social signals. Some technology, like efficiency improvements at power plants or new solar cells, do enable us to stretch our consumption a bit further, though that tends to be incremental and decreasing.

There are qualitatively different sorts of technology. Those which result in better ways of doing simple things – such as sterilizing surgical implements or making bicycle wheels – pay great dividends on a continuing basis. However, those which pile complex sequences of action on top of other complex sequences of action tend to be very specialized, fragile, and energy intensive. Complexity costs. The low-hanging fruit of simple tech has largely been plucked, leaving us creating ever more byzantine “Rube Goldberg” machines upon which we depend for “growth”. Most modern organizations see technology as the answer to all our problems, but the way technology presently functions is primarily as a spigot-widener for a larger energy footprint and higher neural ratchet of short term dopamine, etc. The upshot is that (most) technology increases, not decreases the total use of energy by society, and this has held true for hundreds of years and will remain true as long as ‘more GDP’ is our cultural aspiration. So it’s presently unlikely that technology will be the answer to declining energy availability, though it may expand our total energy use for a while. These days when you see the word “technology”, it generally means “another way to use energy”. As energy scale itself diminishes, what we choose technology to accomplish will be of paramount importance

Price, cost and value are different – There is an important difference between the ‘price’ of energy (to consumers) and the ‘cost’ of energy (to extractors). The true cost of energy is the cost in natural resource –not dollar - terms. A new oil well in Bakken requires 100 train loads of sand and 1200 truckloads of water. To say it costs $75 to extract also misses this wider boundary story of reality. And ‘higher prices’ do not automatically create new supplies of energy. The important thing to realize about “energy cost” is that it isn’t abstract like dollar cost, it embodies physical inputs and absolute limits. Any energy sources which cost more energy than they produce might as well not exist. Any energy sources with small surplus vs their cost will not provide the economic benefits we currently assume will continue. Given what a barrel of oil can accomplish, it’s ‘value’ is orders of magnitude greater than it’s price (when used in moderation).

Money and financial capital are just markers for real capital – Financial capital is just a marker for real capital – comprised of built, social, human and natural capital. Our economic rules treat all the benefits from nature as ‘free’ inputs and there is no offsetting cost for declines in natural capital. Thus, a nation which cuts down its forests and pollutes its water generally has a better GDP than one which doesn’t, despite being increasingly

impoverished in real terms. Energy cost is our real ‘cost of capital’. Money is just a marker, and an increasingly imperfect one.

Uneconomic growth – occurs when the increase in the ‘bads’ outweighs the increase in the ‘goods’, i.e. where the marginal costs are larger than the marginal gains. When we include ‘the bads’ our net decrease in metrics of (overall) quality of life have been occurring for a long time. But since many of the ‘negatives’ (like oil spills, rape counseling, prison guards, depression medication, animal shelters, deforestation, etc) create ‘output’, the societal goal of increasing GDP continues to be attained (and approved of).

The price of non-renewable natural resources is determined only ‘at the margin.’

This gives completely false social signals about our future. Due to monetary and political distortions the price of oil can diverge greatly from its cost and value. For what it can accomplish, pricing this magic at the clearing bid on the last unit of supply/demand --far below its extraction cost or value to society - is a ginormous misallocation. But it is efficient, and clears the market, so it ‘makes sense’ to the shaman economic analyst, and the superorganism powers on. The single biggest flaw in economic theory is that we only pay for the cost of extraction for our most valuable inputs to our economy – not the cost of creation or pollution. Banks do not function as intermediaries, but create money from thin air. The government doesn’t create most of our money. Over 95% of our money is created by commercial banks when they make a loan – this money pops into existence without a similar amount being deducted from anywhere else in the system, and with no direct accounting link to energy or natural resources. Yet the interest on the loan is not created, requiring continued economic growth to service monetary claims. (If this all sounds too messed-up to be true, we agree. A lot of reality exists in our blind spots, but this one is now easily verifiable).

Debt is non-neutral to economies.

Economists assume that GDP is the same whether we used debt or cash to attain it. Debt is thus assumed to just be an ‘intertemporal consumption preference transfer’ between 2 parties. In reality it is just an ‘intertemporal consumption transfer’. Debt is a bit like a stimulant drug to an economy: it causes power to flow towards the now by pulling it forward from the future. Yet it’s ultimately based on the flawed premise of future periods having more energy and resources to keep the promises which have been made. This all

suggests that dislocations from energy blindness will first manifest in the financial markets.

Microeconomics does not reference or integrate our evolved nature – The theories of micro-economics currently being used to make our most important societal decisions embody distorted models of human behavior, decision-making, and ignore the biology of our evolved needs and wants. We are other-regarding (not purely selfish and individually focused), we are unequivocally NOT rational actors, and we conflate wants with needs. “Utility,” in a world replete with fossil magic, basically means ‘whatever.’

The economy is a fully owned subsidiary of the environment. Conventional economists treat the environment as outside of our economy. GDP counts both the ‘goods’ and the ‘bads’. Monetary gains from natural resource extraction are privatized but damage to our natural commons are borne by the public, future generations, and other species. Our real stock market is our air, our soils, our forests, our oceans, and the biodiversity we share the planet with. This stock market has been going down for over a millennium and has been in slo-mo crash mode since you’ve been alive. Two stock markets – one an index of consensus belief stemming from the agenda of the gene, and one comprised of physical reality. Which one should we focus on? Economic “laws” were created during a no-analog non-repeatable period in human history as ad hoc rules of thumb and are mostly based on false

assumptions. Stable non-growing economies had been the norm for thousands of years in human history. Tapping the stored labor in fossil sunlight changed all that. A 7-fold increase in population in two centuries accompanied a 7-fold increase in per capita consumption. Yet we have been told by economists this is due to cleverness and hard work (and because we are Americans). The ‘rules’ and ‘laws’ created during this era assume this unique period is permanent. But correlation does not equal causation. The fossil carbon pulse will be a blink on the human timeline, comprising at best a few hundred years, and we are now at the inflection point at which net energy has stopped increasing every year. So the “special circumstances” which made those economic rules useful guides will soon and rapidly cease to exist.

There is no plan B – The “invisible hand” that is created by millions (now billions) of individuals pursuing self-interest continues forward until it cannot. Nowhere in modern\*\*

economic theory is there a blueprint for what to do after growth stops. The game is the plan. \*\*Adam Smith did anticipate growth ending, but this is not often discussed.

The above list critiquing economic assumptions about our future is not exhaustive but gives some insight that the core beliefs underpinning modern society have some large holes. Someday, a new economics more tethered to our evolved behavioral drivers and to our natural resource (and sink) balance sheet might emerge. In the meantime, the above disconnects lead to some implications: The fossil slaves supporting industrial civilization will either quit or be fired this century – Fossil carbon-magic was a one-time endowment on human time scales. Most of our carbon slaves will be leaving us this century. If we do manage to wake the remaining ones they are only strong enough to power a less complex society and will likely in the process create an extremely desolate natural world. The choice is timing. Some generation will have to invent life without such an army of helpers, and to “make do” with whatever condition the natural world is in by then. Liquid fuels (i.e. oil) are the lifeblood of modern civilization. Despite oil currently being at $50 a barrel to the public, oil extraction costs are up ~17% annualized from 2002-2012 (down some 20% since 2013). The IEA recently showed that if no new investment occurred, global oil production would drop to less than 20% of its current levels by 2040. The availability and price of oil will call the tune on most costs and constraints in society in coming decades. Only ~20% of post-conversion energy in USA is electricity, so most renewable energy does not address the specialness of liquid fuels and near term peak in global oil production. This is due to the qualitative

difference between concentrated, indefinitely storable liquid fuel vs. all other energy sources. Think “transportation” and all it implies. Pricing oil in dollars means that it is now beginning to bounce between “too cheap to be worth extracting” and “too expensive to run our current economy.” Continued Economic Growth and Mitigating climate change are incompatible – Human societies are currently functioning as a gigantic heat engine oxidizing fossil carbon and hydrocarbons. All “growth” makes climate change (and associated crises) worse – even growth using ‘renewable’ energy, whose own growth and energy footprint have not reduced the burning of fossil materials in the global market system. It’s a bit like an obese person eating more so as to have enough energy enough to begin a diet. Despite the talk on ‘decoupling’ we can’t grow our way to a smaller energy footprint, that’s an offshoot fallacy of current economic assumptions. We can ‘solve’ climate change via a smaller economy Equality/Fairness/Eliminating Poverty and Mitigating climate change are incompatible goals - It’s currently seen as a social necessity to frame the answers to all questions in terms of greater fairness and representation, but in the context of fossil-fueled overshoot this many ultimately be suicidal. Eliminating poverty, achieving happiness, and returning to a healthy world are all best approached via a deep time ethic. The sort of spite-oriented “capuchin fairness” which resents the perceived distribution to others is best left with the other excess baggage of evolution. (\*\*unless all people alive today, the NPV of all future generations, and other sentient large life are part of the formula for equality – then equality and fairness could be compatible with climate mitigation. Or, if most of our exosomatic surplus went away) This knowledge –any knowledge – about our situation is important but not sufficient. Too many activists and organizations rely on ‘information deficit’ theories of social change. We need creative pissed-offedness and intelligent raging against the runaway planet-eating amoeba. And creative, inclusive and kind plans for when the amoeba gets smaller. We might have to stop doing what “feels like it should work” and do the things which actually DO work.

The Monkey Trap

About 200 years ago we humans stumbled upon a monkey trap which had been set by chance events long before mammals evolved. This enormous reserve of fossil sunlight that would become the anthropic “carbon pulse” was both a treasure and a curse. Our energy surplus – gross energy less its energy costs - has been both large and cheap for most of that 200 years,

so much so that we learned to ignore it and instead credit our own cleverness in using it. We designed our economies around the expectation of more net energy to burn each year. Organizing our infrastructure, our institutions and our expectations around this assumption was a mistake. Most humans don’t yet realize that – even award-winning economists and hedge fund managers. And especially politicians and civic leaders. We call it the carbon pulse because it’s ephemeral on the scale of human existence, a few centuries of crazy growth and its aftermath. We humans believed it could go on forever, because we’re really good at believing happy things. However, the real physical world runs on rules, and those rules sharply constrain what we can do. Thus, our actions have consequences. We’ve used our “free energy” to have a lot of short term fun with little thought to the future, because we assumed the future could take care of itself. This will now have repercussions, like it or not. Using helium to fill party balloons, speak silly high voices and venting the extra to the air, sets in motion the helium’s escape from earth’s atmosphere. Humans in the future won’t have an easy way to get helium if they ever need some for some yet-discovered use, because it is created one molecule at a time by slow radioactive decay. By deciding it has no central usefulness and venting it to the air, we’re thus constraining the options of future humans, (though we obviously don’t think about it in this way). Now scale this example past helium to every concentration of resources on the planet, every rare element and non-renewable resource currently used in human commerce. We have largely used up everything which is easy to get to, under an assumption that increasing prices from human “demand” will provide us the ability to permanently get at them. Oops. We’ve depleted our topsoil and allowed it to wash away because with cheap energy and fertilizers we didn’t need topsoil, just an inert medium to hold the plants upright for fertilizer and pesticides. Oops. This is true, one way or another, for about everything we need to keep societies going. The discovery that our big mistaken

assumption - that $1 worth energy is the same as $1 worth of cupcakes or crayons - will come at a hell of a price. This invisibility of the fossil slaves who have made most in the developed world into pampered royalty – has a million ramifications for the economies and social arrangements of the future. As liquid fuels deplete, transportation and shipping will become more expensive and problematic, meaning that the world will effectively become a much larger place again other than for the uber-rich. It will no longer be nearly free to ship things halfway around the world, and “globalization” will slowly (or not so slowly) recede. Many businesses will increasingly be nonviable, from airlines to specialty stores. The human enterprise in general will need to contract and simplify. More local food and resources. Higher prices, scarcity, and sometimes unavailability. We’ll initially experience these energy-driven economic changes as fiscal chaos and crisis; as recessions, depressions, poverty, financial crashes, and whatever new words are invented for unprecedented economic dysfunction in coming decades. These will for the most part not be blamed on the root cause – increasing remoteness of energy and raw materials – which will probably remain largely invisible to most humans - but instead on human scapegoats causing conflicts, social upheaval, violence, and illogical responses. As nations and a species we will find that both large and long term projects are more difficult and energy-remote, their horizons receding from our grasp. And while on the surface we will try to replace our fossil slaves with mechanized ‘renewable’ co-workers, there’s really no such thing as renewable energy. There are, instead, mechanisms which can divert power from solar flows or radioactive decay. These mechanisms are invariably made using fossil energy. They are not renewed, they are replaced at a cost in energy, materials, and complexity which is not necessarily repeatable indefinitely. They currently function as “extenders” of fossil energy. Things won’t be the same without the energy slaves. No air conditioners. No airliners. Just air. On the largest scale, it’ll mean that the carbon-pulse expanded terrestrial biomass – principally of humans and their livestock – will have to diminish toward levels sustainable on a changed earth without fossil magic, which will initially mean less meat to eat, and eventually mean a drawdown in human population numbers. There are many traditional ways a population can diminish, and relatively few that are pleasant. Managing this global crash-landing in a sane and humane way will be one of the big challenges for newly-awakened homo sapiens. We could be talking about a population decline from 10 billion to 8 billion if humans manage to dramatically lower our average consumption. But sustainable levels over time are likely closer to 1-1.5 billion, with wide error bands, which would mean that a lot of history could happen in the coming century +/-. The carbon-pulse population overshoot will be humanity’s largest crisis ever, because after peaking in coming decades the population

will never be this high again. Ever. It’s one of the biggest bottlenecks of all.

The Real Stock Market

This carbon pulse has expanded the human niche, but has also had huge effects on earth’s ecology. The terrestrial vertebrate biomass has been grossly inflated by various ways of transmuting coal, oil, and gas into food. At the same time, we have expended huge amounts of energy to capture and convert terrestrial and oceanic creatures into desirable foodstuffs. Thus, Man and His Livestock have largely displaced large vertebrates in wild nature. On land these wild animals – as a percentage of the total - are now only a rounding error. The total vertebrate biomass (of man plus nature) is grossly unsustainable, and we will gradually be returning to trophic pyramids based on solar flows. Our industrial sector has introduced new pollutants such as heavy metals, bioaccumulating chlorine products, plastics, antibiotics, and hundreds of thousands of other things into food chains. The heavy breathing of our fossil slaves is changing the atmosphere and oceans to conditions which have never existed during human history. CO2 levels are at 410 and rising, tripping positive feedbacks like methane from melting tundra, decreased albedo from lost ice, and forests burning rather than sequestering carbon, each positive feedback taking us farther from the possibility of slowing or reversing the heating. It appears the oceans are acidifying at least ten times faster than ever before in the history of our planet, dissolving shells and raising the imminent prospect of food web collapses as coral and other organisms dissolve away for hundreds of thousands of years, a cascade of events which could be “locked in” midway through this century. The oceans are warming and stratifying, holding less oxygen, which favors jellies, bacteria and slimes over vertebrates like fish, seals, and whales. 200 years ago, if the most devious and sociopathic humans had put their minds together to craft a way of deliberately destroying Earth life and a livable human future, they could not have done better than the Carbon Pulse, the merging of a fire-using ape species with millions of years of accessible stored flammability. The “mischief” which has been enabled by the carbon pulse has led to what is on course to become the 6th mass extinction event in the last half-billion years – and this even before the climate heats and the oceans acidify and stagnate. We pay high prices for tiger penis soup, whale burger, dolphin bacon and endangered-tuna sushi. We burn rainforests and wipe out their denizens for trendy products, and create enough fish net to circle the globe many times, dredging away the coral in unseen habitats. Out of sight, out of conscience.

We face these prospects with minds still in thrall of nonsense beliefs, hyper-stimulation and tribal social status, a bit like what might happen if one gave nuclear launch controls to hamsters and smeared peanut butter on the red buttons: they would be unlikely to associate the taste of the peanuts with the bright flashes on the horizon. Similarly, we buy crab legs at Costco without much thought about how they got there. Tantalizingly though, it is not quite impossible for us to reverse course and save a world, and not incidentally save a deep human future of trillions of childhoods, but that possibility is tipping precariously on a high windowsill in a strong wind. Pretty much all the humans you meet are missing large sections of the puzzle, and even those who discuss such things look no further ahead than a single lifetime, even when considering effects which play out over thousands of years once committed. And poignantly, it’s not like we get to choose between energy services and the natural world. The excess net energy is going away in either case, and our descendants (and maybe us) will have to live with the world that remains. If we don’t find it within ourselves to stop burning voluntarily though, there won’t be much around to live with and amongst, as the other large complex species which have defined us and shared our ancestral trajectory, will be gone. We still have hugely more energy than our bodies need, but a tightening of individual energy budgets will “feel like” we are being impoverished because we’re attuned to relative rather than absolute wealth. Rather than focus on the fact that what we have is still amazing, we tend to fixate on the perceived wealth we will lose, while our actual wealth – a living world –implodes. The way this all plays out will be substantially determined during the productive years of your lifetime. You do not live in ordinary times.

Bottleneck?

Traditionally, a “bottleneck” is the term which applies when a species has almost gone extinct, but managed somehow to survive with enough individuals to rebound, thereby successfully navigating ‘the bottleneck’. Many species now extant, from cheetahs to humans, have faced such precarious bottlenecks in their past. They’re called “bottlenecks” due to the shape

of the population curve, which traces the shape of a ‘bottleneck’ as numbers approach zero. An open bottleneck means survival, a closed one means extinction. We’re surrounded by an accelerating mass extinction now, of our making. Species are going extinct at tens of thousands of times the normal background level. Many more are in their own narrowing bottlenecks. The other apes, the primates, the whales, all terrestrial megafauna. The species which are specialized for rainforests. The ones which have nowhere to go as their habitat is lost. It’s not an overstatement to say that all large complex life on earth is approaching bottlenecks, and our turn will inexorably come as the carbon pulse peters out. But that’s only obvious to those who learn to see reality as it exists. Yet your authors’ use of the term is broader than that, for we use it not only to refer to populations of a species, but for entire ecosystems. And we also see many bottlenecks related to the human experience. The net energy bottleneck is one. The amount of food which will be available in the future is another. There will also be bottlenecks in the level complexity our societies can maintain, the degree of complexity of our technology, the scale of projects we can undertake, and of many other qualities of human existence. The human “bottleneck” thus refers to the imminent narrowing of human options, resources, and population forced by the hard limits of natural physical law. Thinking about these bottlenecks presents us with a radically different framing of “what we should do” with the time and energy we have. The point we have to make is that while nobody can know exactly what will happen in the future, we can know a lot about what won’t happen. The future will have a 4-dimensional shape – a time dimension – just as there is a 4D shape of the past existence of the cheetah, which passed through a tiny bottleneck and thrived again (and faces another one now). The shape of human existence – the area under that curve – will determine whether the human story is ending, or, alternatively, is barely starting. Pretty much everything we think we know is incomplete or wrong and needs to be reconsidered in

light of the brevity of the carbon pulse, and the reality of deep time. We need to think hard about what our feelings trick us to do, and think deeply about consuming, reproducing, and why we do what we do. Because the gene agenda of humanity has become the mortal enemy of a decent human future. It’ll be nothing less than the fight of our intellect versus our feelings, played out in billions of minds for the highest possible stakes over a very short timespan. Your authors will dedicate their lives to helping our species transcend the gene agenda and hold open the bottleneck to get our species and others past the temporary crisis and into the deep future. There’s a good chance we’ll need help.

Thinking About The Future

As we begin to think about becoming homo sapiens, we find ourselves in many predicaments which were not foreseen by the brains of our predecessors. There’s no reason they would have been – we’re evolved to be efficient and formidable hunters existing in small tribes, and science and the wide-boundary systems integration it allows is a product of iterations and energy surplus only recently available. Yet it’s clear now that we’ve gotten ourselves into an existential pickle. We can either ignore it for one more generation and seal the deal, or we can engage with a bigger set of crises than conscious mind has ever had to deal with. Heck of a choice, huh. Yet it isn’t hopeless. In addition to the obvious fact that it hasn’t happened yet and is not yet inevitable, there’s a lot more stuff hiding there in our blind spots, and some of it may be cause for hope. One thing, for instance, is that the biophysical nature of depletion and energy cost is such that our use of fossil energy will decrease whether we plan it or not. This is something the IPCC, and economists, don’t understand. It will not be sufficient to solve the problems, but it’s something. The only thing that has caused downturns in the rate of fossil energy use is recessions and depressions, and those will be coming with a vengeance. That’ll (probably) be good news for other species, and uncomfortable news for those of us who like the smooth way our societies now run. But there are other misunderstandings that will hinder us. Our culture assumes that large and complex problems will be easier to solve in the future. The opposite is true. They will become harder, and this needs to be planned around. Our “experts” these days are mostly specialists and reductionists. Reductionism is a powerful analytical tool, but it has taken over our cultural definition of expertise, leading to idiot-savant style decision-making. The real world exists as a synthesis, and

our mental models of that world must also become so if they’re to have real predictive value. We need to re-think everything, because currently we’re working with a lot of wrong answers, piled on top of wrong assumptions. It’s possible to use “filtered thinking” to sidestep the long list of human biases, fallacies, illusions, heuristics and other evolved predilections which causes us to follow insane narratives. There are “first principles” which are known which lead to clear deductions about what can and can’t happen. They do not tell us outcomes, but constrain those outcomes in knowable ways. This is science. Denial and Nihilism are two sides of the same coin, functionally equivalent. They are attractors for our evolved heuristics to resolve dissonance. We must walk a path between them. The future is probabilistic. The relative odds of future outcomes change every second, based on events. There’s no wise overmind, cabal, or secret society running things. Aggregate human actions currently function like an amoeba, with simple tropisms. Human society currently functions as a giant heat engine. We assume that to change its course we need a consensus agreement, but that’s mathematically impossible working with billions of stone-age brains; and as crisis becomes evident many will default to their primate programming. Yet we have not yet truly tried the “sapiens” route. We haven’t yet done anything which should be expected to work. Individuals and small groups can sometimes accomplish what larger groups can’t, steering the amoeba in wholly new directions. There is not yet a science of effective activism, so we do random things that “feel” useful, even though many of them are self-defeating. The scale of a problem is less important than the structure of that problem. We need to invent and perfect the science of steering large-scale events, in a humane and altruistic yet pragmatic way. (Your authors think they have some ideas along these lines). The first sapiens will need to coexist and care for those who adhere to the gene agenda, while doing whatever is necessary to navigate the bottleneck. A reasonable philosophical basis for the new species – homo sapiens – may be derived from basic principles of life and awareness in the universe. We need to choose what is sacred and live with a commitment to it. No answers - yet. From a deep time perspective, what humanity does (or doesn’t do) in the next few decades will impact the next hundred thousand years – and beyond. We’re figuring

out a lot of things not to do, but don’t yet know exactly what to do. But we now have a reasonable sense of what the full picture looks like. Acknowledging all pieces of the elephant is more important than having immediate answers. Indeed, most people focusing on just one aspect of our situation – say poverty, or climate change - are probably several steps behind reality. (Side note – it is often reasonable strategy to focus on a subset of the system, but if this isn’t done from a synthesis overview, the strategy may be flawed. For instance, most social activists these days believe in the same flawed economic theories and cultural myths that those they oppose do.) We admit we don’t yet have answers to the coming bottlenecks, but the more people who sign up for ‘we care about the future of humans and complex life but don’t yet know what to do’ the larger sapient demographic there will be. Once the commitment is made, the answers become a big priority, and motivated humans have done and will do amazing things. Motivated homo sapiens may do even more. Before any debate - let alone decision - can be made on ‘what to do’ there first needs to be a discussion on ‘what is the goal’? As a carryover from our agricultural past, where the goal was ‘food surplus’, our modern hominid goal is essentially quarterly earnings and increasing GDP. All societal decisions are made with this as the centerpiece of what our human culture aspires to. Even art, and medicine and education – pursuits that should be a priority in any sane culture, have been parsed into dollar goals. And our current institutional plans are to maintain all aspects of society pretty much the way they are, but do so with renewable energy instead of fossil fuels and with more wealth equality. It sure sounds good. We (Nate and DJ) aspire to humans creating something like sequential 100 year plans with humans and most remaining large complex life coexisting on a world not terribly diminished from the one we now have. To get there we have to pass through (a series of) bottlenecks. Advocating and steering things of value through those bottlenecks will need champions and creativity. We don’t yet know how people will champion such futures, but it will first entail understanding the full shape of Reality 101. Realizing that we don’t have the answers puts us many steps ahead of those who are embracing incorrect answers that don’t correspond to physical reality or the reality of human behavior. We are in a no-analog event in planetary history. It may seem that some of these descriptions and prescriptions are a bit glum. We’re aware that pretty much the entirety of the balance of your educational experience, from kindergarten through college, is shot through with optimism bias, consensus blind spots, pervasive presentation of unexamined cultural consensus beliefs, and a constant barrage of news and conversation which hails primarily from the deep feelings of the gene agenda. Therefore, we’ve considered it a responsibility of this

course to give an honest look, filtered of bias, and have been fairly ruthless in trying to attain that. Bright, pro-social, educated people alive today and engaged with the future are the first generation of our species – of any species on this planet – that knows: where we came from, how we got here, what we need, what we’re consuming, how we’re impacting the planet, and what paths are possible and desirable. This is the first and necessary step towards true sapience. Once we get beyond the uncomfortable fact that some human misery is baked into the cake in the coming 50-100 years, we can have experts of all kinds working on ‘consilience’ –the jumping together of knowledge – to create a political/spiritual/social movement that results in a livable future. There is not one issue but many. But if humans want to transition from ‘clever’ to ‘wise’ we need to look beyond the politically correct consensus trance, beyond what seems ostensibly fair and equitable, beyond what brings us immediate status and respect, and stand for inherent good. Things look dire, but the world is not yet fully broken.

Predictable Bottlenecks in a Probabilistic Future

As of this writing, there are 2,666 emoji’s to facilitate cell phone texting conversations, but there’s no generally agreed simple symbol to represent “the future” in the entirety of either our written language or our mathematical language, despite the fact that our collective destiny lies there. Since a google search didn’t’ come up with anything to represent our collective future, we offer the “t” (standard physics for time) with a forward arrow on the crossbar, to signifying time forward.

™ Something that is at least as important as those 2,000+ emojis, we’d like to think. By this point it should be clear from the course is that we humans have an increasingly problematic relationship with the future, primarily because we never evolved to need one. Our gene-programmed agenda, the primary determinant of how we “feel” about things, is still calibrated on the tacit assumption that we exist within hunter-gatherer bands facing scarcity on African savannahs. That situation didn’t require a lot of abstract thought about futures farther away than a week, or maybe next year at the outside. Yet like all animals in a highly-competitive ecosystem, our grancestors on the savannah had their perceptions about “what will happen, what won’t happen, what might happen” in those timespans reasonably well-calibrated

against physical reality. Nature, red in tooth and claw, has a low tolerance for functional delusion relating to the real world. These days, we exist largely detached from any immediate consequences associated with actions more subtle than stepping in front of a train or off a cliff: our eusocial society protects us from most missteps, and in turn has allowed us to believe a lot of stuff that is wildly out of relation to the physical world and its possible future states.

A Probabilistic view of the Future

One thing we can know about the future is that we can never know exactly what will happen in it. We know that it will exist as a series of single discrete outcomes, but the nature of forward propagation of events in physical and biological systems has elements inherently unpredictable in advance. Furthermore, the predictability decreases exponentially as we look farther ahead in time, even in nonliving systems, as uncertainties compound.
