This nature of complex exponentially-expanding interrelated systems on long timescales triggers a powerful urge in the human mind to stop thinking about them and instead go do something else, preferably something pure “gene agenda” (insert whatever you plan to do after reading this essay here). The contemporaries of our ancestors who were thinking about distant future bottlenecks – if any - likely became leopard casualties while their tribemembers were eating warthog marrow, telling entertaining stories, and sneaking away to the bushes. It was almost always more useful to use heuristic mental shortcuts, most of them boiling down to “the future will look a lot like today, only maybe a bit shinier”. This has left us with an evolved default pathway of thinking about the future in a manner grossly disconnected from the real physical specifics and implications of the current situation. The actual correctness of a theory about the future has, for almost all of human history, been far less important to survival than has social conformity of such an organizing idea by tribe members. A tribe favors something inspiring, not too complex-seeming, easy to communicate, and generally positive. How does one convey anything useful about our future that meets these criteria? Enter humanity’s greatest double-edged magical sword: language. We primarily think and communicate in words, which requires us to instantly build mental pictures out of words we hear from others. A phrase like “the thunder turtle is big as a mountain and made of shimmering purple glass. And she rides rainbows and eats nonbelievers when they let their guard down” creates a vivid, real-seeming picture in a human mind. This type of thinking worked spectacularly well for small bands of humans in a world with bountiful resources. The sacrifice of an accurate, veridical (corresponding with reality) organizing myth was small price compared to the benefit of social cohesion which

belief in it provided. Today our (and others’) future is held hostage by purely verbal logic; grammatically-correct sentences which resolve our dissonance and anxiety but may be physically absurd (instead of turtles it’s rocket travel from city to city, etc). A common “verbal logic” description of the future you’ve certainly heard is that “anything can happen”. Such a prevalent assertion makes the future seem essentially random, and thus easy to disregard by our dissonance-quashing evolved mind routines. Clearly, though, this is dead wrong. Because anything can’t happen. Most stuff we can imagine can’t happen, and even more stuff certainly won’t happen. From unpacking this we can derive much more predictive value than from our generally-relied-upon narratives. Even our own brains function by following the rules of deterministic physical reality, a bit like a really complicated tangle of electroneurochemical mini-avalanches. Yet like other systems infused with chaotic process, we can’t predict exactly what WE will think in the future either. The best we can do with our brains is to construct what we’re calling a “probabilistic view” of the future which we adjust over time. We have the significant advantage of existing at the time of the greatest amassment of scientific knowledge and objective truth ever reached by our species. From very near the peak of energy available to human society we can look farther backwards, and extrapolate more correctly forwards. Even if we can’t exactly know the future in advance, there are lots of aspects we can know about it fairly precisely. We are able to “bound” the scope of imagined outcomes by applying lenses of thermodynamic possibility, aggregate probability, and some common sense. With these bounds we are then able to effectively reduce the future from something vast, monolithic, and incomprehensible to a range of possibilities that we can be fairly sure of. And all without having to resort to flying turtles or their narrative equivalent.

Can’t and Won’t Happen: Absolute, Chaotic, and Random aspects of Reality

Knowing what can’t and won’t happen in the future really narrows down the possibilities of what can, and that turns out to be a big deal. This is based on the properties of physical reality: some stuff is absolutely precluded, and other stuff is so unlikely as to be effectively impossible. These are the “can’t happen” and “won’t happen” respectively. That may seem like a trivial formulation, but most of what our species currently believes about the future is significantly at odds with both of those. For instance, in the images above, there are some wooden shapes and sizes you could carve out of that starting block of wood, and some you couldn’t. That’s obvious enough from the picture, but to the extent we have any societal plans for the future, they – in large part – amount to believing that we will carve a full-sized car out of a small block of wood, because instead of pictures we think in words. Can’t Happen: If a proposition about the future runs afoul of a basic law of the universe, like the laws of physics, chemistry, or thermodynamics, it can’t happen. For instance, if you contributed to the crowd-funding campaign for a small battery-powered underwater breathing system, you are a) no closer to breathing underwater in the future and b) have also blown your money. Such basic “rules of reality” may be thought of as “absolute” limits to reality, and thus to the future. Another category of “can’t happens” is based on history – those frozen sequential accidents which underlie all that we experience - because the things which are possible in a given system today are significantly contingent upon what has happened

previously. By the same token, specific aspects of the future are contingent on which things do or don’t happen now. If we blow off an atmospheric fusion bomb, then the background radiation will inevitably be higher in the future. If we accidentally kill off a species of tree snail, the future won’t have any of them. If we burn all the accessible oil we can find, then future humans won’t have much. And so forth. This is quite basic – a corollary of the “can’t have your cake and eat it too” principle – yet is often ignored. It can generally be referred to as path dependence. Won’t Happen: If a proposition is ridiculously unlikely to the point of preposterousness, it needs to be treated as functionally impossible. Yes, essentially impossible things happen all the time – such as each of us being born – but any particular essentially impossible thing which hasn’t yet happened should be treated as actually impossible in terms of worrying about or planning around it. This part of reality stems from the existence of nonlinear, chaotic, and random aspects of the systems we’re enmeshed within. (A meteorite could drill through my roof and kill me before I finish writing this sentence, but …... it didn’t.) We have previously used the example of all of the oxygen molecules around you happening to move to your ceiling so you suffocate; physically possible but probabilistically impossible on a scale shorter than quadrillions of years. Or, using another example previously mentioned, there is nothing physically precluding someone building a 500-foot-tall cheese statue of a grizzly bear in Pittsburgh within 20 years. There will presumably be enough cheese in the world; with enough money one could erect scaffolding, affect elections to get the permits, and so on. Yet it is entirely reasonable to assume that in 20 years there will be no 500’ cheese grizzly in Pittsburgh. That is, there are a bunch of propositions not impossible based on the laws of reality, but which are effectively impossible based on considerations of aggregate probability. In the case of the giant cheese grizzly, this would amount to a whole bunch of events having to fall just the right way for the cheese grizzly to come into existence, each with their own individual relative probability of happening. Just about any complex bit of human endeavor requires a number of complex things to happen in a certain sequence. In the case of the cheese grizzly, you could come up with your own list of what those might be: zoning, permitting, structural engineering, design approval, public backlash, political opposition, cheese acquisition, cheese storage and fabrication, insect repelling, weather protection, liability, decommissioning, etc. Let’s say conservatively that building a 500-foot cheese statue would require 100 necessary steps. The aggregate probability of the project being finished would be each of those fractional probabilities multiplied by all the others, so unless all of the steps were

pretty much a slam-dunk, the ultimate project goal would quickly recede toward impossibility.

A Probabilistic framing for the Future

One classic example of an intractable unpredictable chaotic system is the weather. When your authors were kids, weather reports would make absolute predictions on what the weather would be in the coming day or week, at the cost of being wrong a lot of the time. That is, they’d say “thunderstorms tomorrow” and might be correct 60% of the time. These days, they have generally switched to a probabilistic forecast, such as “there will be a 60% chance of rain with thunderstorms”, which actually conveys much more information than the prior absolute predictions: we know the predicted likelihood of a given outcome, which is a more nuanced prediction. It would be great if we used exactly this sort of methodology for referring to all aspects of the future, because it’s the most useful way to conceive of it. Just like weather, human-connected systems have significant elements of chaos and nonlinearity, which makes the whole human enterprise on earth effectively chaotic. That means that the most useful and correct way to think about the future is probabilistically, as an array of possibilities with various estimated likelihoods of happening.

The usefulness of partial future knowledge

In many cases knowing the general shape of what’s likely to happen is much more useful than knowing the specific details. Consider a parked SUV in a Dallas parking lot at noon in summer, inside which someone has locked a pet poodle. There are both linear aspects and chaotic aspects to this situation: the position of the poodle inside the SUV is chaotic because the poodle’s brain is a chaotic system. So, at any given future time, there is no way of predicting its position, other than it is “somewhere inside the SUV”. On the other hand, in the simple situation described, the poodle’s position isn’t relevant for most classes of useful prediction, since the laws of physics will quickly heat the car past the point where the poodle remains chaotic & breathing. So even without the poodle’s position being known, some very useful and prescriptive predictions can be made solely on the basis of predictable effects. Frantically trying to model the precise poodle behavior in a 3D rendering of the car interior on a supercomputer would be much less helpful than finding and yelling at the driver to crack a window, or if necessary, breaking the window yourself. Even without knowing the ‘exact’ future, we can know enough about it to be useful. (this example is also reasonably analogous to the debate on climate versus weather, with those who disregard the science of greenhouse heating essentially focusing on poodle behavior).

The Walls of the Bottle

Since humans find it easier to think about things when they can imagine a picture, we’ve illustrated an abstract “bottle” which contains all our world’s future possibilities. The “walls of the bottle” demark the limits between the outcomes inside – the stuff that can happen – and the outcomes outside, which is all the stuff that can’t or won’t happen. There’s no way of predicting exactly which outcomes will ultimately manifest at any given time in the future, but they will lie inside the bottle and not outside it. This becomes a relevant framing, because a great many futures now imagined by our friends, families, governments, institutions and societies lie outside that bottle. In other words, just because a given future “sounds nice” has no correlation with whether it resides inside the bottle of future possibilities. Any actual future we achieve will be based on the reality we have NOW, and we’ll need to proceed to it in a series of possible steps, each of which can actually physically happen. (You might be getting that déjà vu feeling that this is a bit of a problem for a species like ours which is beset with multiple blind spots about reality).

....and Bottlenecks

Another reason our “bottle of the earth’s future” concept is useful is to provide a mental tool for envisioning changes to the walls of the bottle, such as bottlenecks. Of course, the rules of reality – physics, thermodynamics, and stuff – won’t change. However, the ramifications of these rules when combined with our actions can absolutely change, because the nature of reality is that it is contingent on what happened previously. For instance, we can know that if armadillos go extinct on earth,

then there will still be no armadillos on it in 10 million years. If X amount of CO2 is injected into the atmosphere, the ice covering Greenland will melt within a range of years Y. If we run out of minable phosphate, the total amount of food which may be grown by human farmers will face an absolute Liebig limit at lower than current levels, and so on. Thus, the “walls of the bottle” can move in and out, and some things which were possible yesterday become no longer possible tomorrow, and things impossible yesterday are suddenly possible today. Some, like a global thermonuclear war tomorrow, would contract the walls of the bottle very abruptly; others, like a gradual loss of pollinating insects, affect it more subtly. Anything that will happen this coming century – and beyond, will happen inside this ‘bottle’. The walls of this bottle will be boundbounded by many things, but primarily will be constrained by the amount and type of energy used in human systems. As we descend on the downslope of the Earth’s one-time carbon pulse, a large number of bottlenecks will be experienced. The ‘bottleneck’ terminology is traditionally used in biology to describe such things as “population bottlenecks” and the related “genetic bottlenecks” in living species, but we use it here in the more general sense to describe similar aspects of other replicating systems. If you look at a population graph over time, you can see where the “bottleneck” concept came from... the area under the curve diminishes, narrowing to a “bottleneck” shape. For instance, supersonic airliners expanded in popularity in the latter part of the 20th century but then went extinct, as did “floppy disk drives” and other complex creations which were once widespread. Many human languages have become extinct, and a few are hanging on in a “bottleneck” of very few users, with an uncertain future. A bottleneck event can re-expand to greater numbers of possibilities, or continue closing until there are none. Thus, a “bottleneck” as we refer to it, is a potential near-extinction event, for any complex replicating system. For many things: species, socio-economic systems, infrastructure, knowledge, ecosystems, etc. the 21st century will represent a bottleneck event.

So, What ARE the Probabilities of various futures?

Even if a thousand synthesis scientists each drew up their best predictions of the probabilities of future outcomes, they wouldn’t agree exactly, because there’s no way to be perfectly right about such a thing. The degree of correctness would vary with each scientist’s mental models and assumptions, and the degree to which their mental models were in fact well-correlated with physical reality and able to account for the chaotic, contingent, and random processes. So beyond the short term the models

would increasingly diverge from each other. However, they WOULD agree on the broad-brush dead-obvious points, which is why one sees the frequently-quoted statistic that 97% of climate scientists agree on human-caused planetary CO2 heating. Such an exercise would be of more than academic usefulness, because we’re affecting the shape and size of that “bottle of possible futures” each and every day, narrowing the possibilities of what will be possible tomorrow when a (hypothetical) sapient species would instead be working to broaden them. When predicting aspects of future reality based upon this “bottle of possible futures”, the broadest and most basic are the easiest to see, with more specific predictions becoming more speculative. So let’s start with the biggie:

The Great Simplification

The “Great Simplification” is a term we’ve invented to refer to what is arguably the biggest and most important event in human history of all time, so of course you haven’t ever heard of it before... its name OR its existence. It exists at the intersection of multiple human blind spots. If its existence became generally acknowledged, societal weirdness would peak very quickly and unpredictably, because it’s a lot to take onboard, a veritable sinking Titanic of impossible Christmas ponies which the consensus trance has taught us were safe and real.

The Great Simplification is simply the inevitable downside of the “carbon pulse” graph, during which centuries of “more fossil energy slaves each year” crests the

roller-coaster peak to “fewer fossil energy slaves each year” until the human species is once more in a steady-state economy tapping direct and indirect solar flows. This will, of necessity, involve the systems we depend on becoming less complex because there will be less net energy to service that complexity. (It doesn’t mean that our individual lives will seem simpler, because convenience, too, is currently achieved using energy-generated complexity.) One might well refer to the upslope of the Carbon Pulse the “great complexification”, because humans and human systems have gotten bigger and more complex using all that free net energy. We tend to invest in complexity as a way of overcoming problems. This, as noted by Joseph Tainter, tends to work fine for societies until it doesn’t, and we’ve had a good long run. But as we crest the peak of all-time annual usable net energy now or soon, we will find that a lot of our basic assumptions about systems no longer work the same way. On average, each year there will be less stuff to go around rather than more, so it will no longer necessarily make sense that investments should earn interest. Indeed, the entire basis of “money as debt” becomes really problematic at the point it is ever realized that the energy and materials to pay those debts never existed in the imagined quantities.

The above graph shows one plausible possibility for our energy future, as a result of the prior actions of the ‘superorganism’. We have quite a bit of oil, coal and natural gas left, and plenty of viable ‘rebuildable’ technologies, but in combination not enough to continue to grow economies at sufficient levels to maintain the financial

claims, expectations and complexity. Leaving climate and environment concerns aside, if the above trajectory manifests, global GDP in 2050 would be roughly 60% of its ~2020 peak. Many other possibilities exist. The “Great Simplification” is an accurate term, because the net effect as available energy ramps down is to make everything both more expensive and more enegetically remote, causing systems of all sorts to simplify or fail. It is also perhaps a bit of a euphemism, in the sense that “simplification” is about the least-dire word one could come up with for a set of networked calamities which could easily be a real planetary shitshow. Like, what could possibly go wrong when “peak humans” at between 12 and 15 billion encounter “inexorably declining food”? Will we react as a sapient global society and become vegan monks to avert a humanitarian disaster of unprecedented scope, or.... something else? On one level, this is just one paragraph in an elective honors course... but on the other hand, it refers to an inevitable predicament which will face the human species at its all-time biomass-bubble population high during your lifetimes. YOW, right? And if you tell people about it, they won’t believe you. (DJ and NJ say “Welcome to our world”). So predicting broad-brush, based ONLY on increasing energy remoteness due to “best first” energy and ore use, and well-known extraction trends, one can expect: - less net energy (and more expensive energy in real terms), beginning sometime in the 2020-2040 timespan and proceeding on an uneven downward slope for ~200 years or so - peak and declining everything relating to physical resources, due to peak and declining net energy - food (corollary: population? Rise of global veganism? Giga-famines?)

transportation (corollary: globalization stops working, Walmart stops working, suburbia etc) - basic infrastructure maintenance (corollary: bridges, plumbing, roads, sanitation, etc)

complexity (corollary: internet, new drugs, fancy surgeries, etc) - manufactured goods - communications networks - heating and air conditioning - physical wealth - etc - a break from the most-dire IPCC planetary heating scenarios, which don’t yet incorporate such concepts as EROEI or credit and which predict emissions based

solely on energy demanded. (but even the likely scenarios are still pretty dire, particularly if extended out to 2200+ as they must be). - a boon for the most remote wild species, far from human populations, once energy gets too expensive to go get them and shrink-wrap them in the gourmet aisle. A curse on the less remote wild species adjacent to human populations, as hunger and political instability push conservation laws and treaties past the breaking point - greater reliance on renewable food resources like healthy fisheries and soil (oops...) - greater reliance on predictable rains, stable climate and temperatures (oops...) - the failure of all plans now extant, of any kind, which are heavily based on the neoclassical economic assumptions of continued physical economic growth and expansion much beyond the next decade or so. (Such as the current economies of major nations). - We humans will probably experience these effects first via fiscal instabilities in the money system, lessened reliability of the more complex networks, lessened availability of choices, greater poverty etc. - We will erroneously blame this on other groups of humans, as well as trying to claim more resources... somehow. Traditionally, this has meant wars. This is a really short list. Clearly, this entire course could easily have concerned itself ONLY with the basic implications of the Great Simplification and still just scratched the surface. Yet without the rest of the course, you wouldn’t have the conceptual framing to even be able to see that the points above are dead obvious.

Dealing with This Reality Stuff

So we’re now drilling down to the reasons this course exists. It’s not to depress a few lucky honors students. It’s because this century could turn out a lot of different ways, and many of them suck bigtime. It’ll help you to know this going forward, whatever you decide to do. Moreover, the most probable scenarios (at present) fall into the “really undesirable” shallow end of the pool. That means that if the “the agenda of the gene” corrals us into continuing “business as usual” without some impressive course corrections, this is going to be one really messed-up century in more ways than we could easily count.

The Great Simplification is inevitable, always was, from the moment we began basing the way we exist on fossil slaves. We’ve just all managed to miss it until now because we’ve treated finite stocks like unlimited flows, if we’ve thought about it at all. Yet although this sounds like terrible news, it’s really good news, in the sort of “pain in the ass” way that getting a timely diagnosis of a treatable condition is good news. Because a lot of the worst stuff isn’t locked in yet. There are some absolutely great human futures still reachable inside that “bottle of possible futures”, even if the walls are getting ever-closer to them and have foreclosed on some others. The things we do in our lifetimes change the relative odds of those futures. Sometimes a little, sometimes a lot. And not just “en masse” things but actions by individuals.

So What Can be Done to Raise the Probability of Better Futures?

There’s potentially a LOT to be done, as you might expect from this event - the Great Simplification - being among the most impressive all-time events in human history. There are still huge degrees of freedom in how it all turns out, and a lot which can be done to improve the odds of better futures arriving. Humanitarian efforts – as noted, human populations are limited by available food. How high will the global population go.... 12 billion? 15 billion? 18 billion? Now contrast this with reasonable estimates of what sustainable populations may be after this century; perhaps 1-2 billion, perhaps less. How that great difference in population size is navigated in this century will be the biggest humanitarian challenge humans ever face, even if our species survives another 10 million years, because there simply won’t be the excess energy and resources to grow the population this big again. The biomass bubble will deflate as converted fossil energy increasingly leaves the biosphere. There will be many ways tragedy can be averted: converting to local-source vegetarianism, diverting energy from wasteful industries to food production, taking societies off 24/7 baseload power and adapting them to function on smaller and more-intermittent energy flows. Mass extinctions – The looming 6th mass extinction on Earth seemingly will occur if “business as usual” continues until we’ve burned all the carbon we can get out of the ground. Moreover, today’s wild species protection laws and nature reserves will seem quaint in the latter half of this century as billions search for food and the stability of governments will depend on supplying it. Since the “Great Simplification” implies that we’re going to have to make do with less stuff anyway, there really isn’t a valid tradeoff of the interests of wild species versus “growth” economies because “growth” economies are going away no matter what, and not that far into the future. If we

throttle them back sooner, we can prevent the worst degrees of a mass extinction. In these strange and fraught times, nearly anyone can choose to save multiple species if they decide to as a life goal. And there’s a bright side from the GS perspective, because if these species make it past the “simplification” bottleneck, they may become ‘extinction remote’ as humans will not have the cheap energy to access the homes of all the world’s species. Societal knowledge – There are many important scientific, cultural, artistic, and other achievements of humanity that could conceivably be lost in the convulsions of the Great Simplification. Data now consigned to spinning hard drives packed ever-denser with information will increasingly become difficult to access and even maintain. There is plenty of time – now – to make sure the important stuff doesn’t become lost, and this would be one of many very worthwhile things to do. Societal Cohesion – Psychological limits to growth will be every bit as challenging as physical limits. As anxieties increase as we pass social limits to growth, many people will revert to tribal behaviors, not trusting (and blaming) those in out-groups, including the media, government etc. It will be important to maintain our core institutions of science, media, and education and to promote and defend civil discourse. Social Foresight – While the energy available to our species will be falling from it’s “carbon pulse” peaks, it is not inevitable that we backslide into former modes of societal functioning. It would, for instance, be a great shame to see institutionalized slavery rise again, or a return to the systematic oppression of women and minorities. Our energy and resource lenses show that this is a reasonably likely possibility in parts of the world, as nations tend to turn inward again. Knowing that ahead of time could, in principle, allow societies to avoid falling back into it, which would be one of the greatest social justice victories possible. Nobody is even yet discussing it since it falls within our social blind spots. Carrying capacity – Nobody worries about natural carrying capacity these days, the ability of our farms and other food sources to produce calories without a 10x boost of fossil energy inputs, but it will be vitally important by the latter half of the century and beyond. Healthy soil, enough fresh water, healthy insect and worm populations, these should be treated as the treasures they are rather than being dumps for herbicides, pesticides and inorganic fertilizers until they’re no longer available. And not just on isolated farms, but in the prime growing fields of entire nations. There’s important work to be done.

Energy– So how will our societies be powered? People talk about an all-electric society powered by “renewables” or by fission reactors. Yet those fission reactors aren’t being built, and there are serious problems with relying exclusively on intermittent wind and solar. But this pales next to the lack of an electric infrastructure: our current civilization only uses about 20% of our energy as electric power. How would we rebuild everything we do to be mostly electric, and is that even possible? We see that “renewable” power is getting cheaper, but that’s the view from a very different global society than the one which will soon prevail. Eventually we’ll have to ask hard questions that reside in the large realm of wants and needs – as individuals and as a culture. Do we really need 24/7 baseload for every house, or might it make more sense to have houses use a lot less energy, generated nearby from intermittent sources, while consigning baseload to industrial processes which really require it? Many similar tradeoffs will become relevant in coming decades. Money – So what happens to “money” and our intricate financial system, ultimately based on the dependability of future energy to repay debts being incurred now? What even happens to capitalism when investments can’t be expected to generate interest from year to year? It’d probably be a good idea to ask these questions ahead of time and come up with a series of blueprints rather than just have the whole system grind to a halt some Monday. Transportation – We in the USA currently live in patterns which require food and supplies to be delivered to our homes or nearby stores through networks of primarily petroleum-burning vehicles running primarily on asphalt roads. At any given time, most of our food and supplies are on ships and trucks, with local stores having only a few days’ worth on hand. However, in coming decades this will become increasingly less practical as oil becomes less available. Electric-battery semi-trailer trucks aren’t really comparable in power and range to today’s diesel trucks, no matter what Elon Musk claims. The expense of flying will tend to make seating tighter, airlines consolidate, and will ultimately spell the end of hub & spoke airline models as fewer locations are served by air going forward. Jet travel for the middle class will recede. Regular old low-speed trains can be run electrically and efficiently, but we’ll need to re-lay most of the tracks which formerly existed and electrify them, and in so doing, alter our living patterns to focus on trains rather than trucks and cars. These changes don’t need to be draconian, and in many cases will offer sanity and a slower, welcome lifestyle, but as of yet -in the ‘hungry amoeba’ stage of cultural aspirations, they are not yet even on the drawing board. Globalization – Having essentially free transportation has given us the ability to source products from the whole world. This has arguably increased total wealth, but it has also meant that we’ve become dependent upon distant manufacture for many

essential items, losing the capacity to supply our own food and manufactured goods locally. But it’s clear from an energy, money, and materials lens that the level of ‘globalization” we have become used to will be going away, which implies that we need to anticipate that and re-localize the production and acquisition of the things we want and need, hopefully in a planned way rather than a gridlocked emergency. Critical System Failure Modes – Our narratives about the future cause us to believe that people in the future will be richer, smarter, and more technologically astute. This makes it seem reasonable to “kick the can” on solving large ranges of problems, putting them off into the future. However, through the “Great Simplification” lens it becomes clear that future generations will in general be poorer, probably have less access to networked information, and have less energy to deal with big problems. So practices like leaving huge amounts of ‘spent’ nuclear fuel rods in swimming pools outside fission reactors becomes obviously a bad idea which is getting worse all the time. Some things need to be dealt with while energy and resources are still relatively cheap and abundant, and while emergency personnel will still drive toward a nuclear accident instead of getting their families away from it. Just one of many possible examples. Hiatus – We conduct our societal affairs under a tacit assumption of absolute continuity on all fronts. Electricity, liquid fuels, internet, phone systems, financial systems, sanitary systems, emergency services and so on are expected to be viable in perpetuity. However, as systems “simplify”, many of these will tend to become more erratic and less dependable, with potentially cascading effects affecting other systems. We could start having conversations about being resilient to hiatus, but for reasons of ‘denihilism’ we haven’t started these conversations as a culture yet. And so on.

One Honors course to rule them all?

We don’t know the future, but we have reasonable confidence of what it will not be. The peak in fossil sunlight flow rates will mean major changes in our lifetimes, and a brief survey of possibilities was listed above. We can be reasonably sure the average energy/material throughput for Americans – and global citizens will decline in coming decades. It’s important to point out that a 30% drop in material wealth per capita (for those in the United States and Canada) brings us back to 1993 levels – a 50% drop to 1972 levels. How we respond to this energy descent as individuals and as a culture will be a deciding moment in our history. Given we have an over 80:1 exosomatic surplus buffer, there remains a great deal of benign, and even excellent futures still on the table, but they won’t arrive without effort.

It would have been quite a different story if Gandalf had told Frodo that Sauron had the ring, and that all was lost. It really could have been a pretty short book with nothing but orcs, dark clouds, sellout wizards, evil, and negativity. There would be no reason to walk to Mordor or do much of anything besides getting drunk on whatever hobbits drink, much less consort with elves, dwarves, or other dicey characters. But that isn’t our story yet, and needn’t be. The world isn’t irretrievably broken, the Great Simplification has barely started, and there are quite a few people who are discovering exactly the shape of our predicament, and the nature of the things which could substantially change them.

That’s right. R101 is a metaphorical “ring of power”. Tag, you’re it.

Denihilism Why Most People Disbelieve/Ignore Our Societal Challenges

With all these societal blindspots with respect to energy, technology, the state of the environment, why aren’t people responding? If not to climate, wild species and ocean change then to the energy, financial and systemic risks we face in coming decades? Alas, the road to sapient thinking is neither level nor straight. It might be better thought of as a narrow path between yawning sinkholes which swallow up the unwary. That’s because certain classes of error are a good fit for our evolved programming and cultural stories. That means they feel right for us to believe despite being at best counterproductive and at worst wildly wrong. Below is a list of 7 of such cognitive ‘attractors’ – heuristics to which the human mind defaults when confronting uncomfortable or threatening information.

“The experts tell us not to worry” (Reductionism ad absurdum)

“Reductionism” is the practice of examining a complex object or system by breaking it down into its simplest components, and it’s a very useful tool in many situations. For instance, it’s easier to understand mineral crystals if you understand something about atoms, and easier to understand some things about a human body if you understand how a single cell functions. A person studying anthropology doesn’t usually need to know much about chemistry, music or seamanship, she’s better at her job focusing solely on anthropology. However, there’s a downside to excessive reductionism, because emergent characteristics of an object or system can’t all be predicted by looking at the simplest elements. For instance, one could not have predicted the existence of vampire bats by knowing the characteristics of subatomic particles and gravity. A bunch of really important stuff is entirely invisible to any particular reductionism lens. The reason this is important is that almost all main vocations which support our current societies are based upon extreme specialization, and fractionation of knowledge. The very nature of “expertise” in our societies these days is some scientist who knows everything there currently is to know about one incredibly narrow slice of reality. That person will often not have any idea how the world works on the important levels at which we experience it. For instance, Steven Hawking, an expert on black hole cosmology, believes it’s a good idea to terraform Mars so we can move there. This is an idea which is energetically, technologically, and probabilistically impossible – and trivially so - based on a simple back-of-the-envelope synthesis of

scientific disciplines. But our culture lacks high-status “synthesist” experts to point this out. Ultra-specialists win Nobel prizes, while practical synthesists are lucky if they get an underpaid job teaching high school science. Moreover, they need to pick up this skill on their own, because the very notion of being a “scientifically sophisticated generalist” is not one to which society presently assigns value. Money, tenure, and status currently accrue to those who become savants in a narrow slice of some discipline, and contribute to the surplus-generating activity of that entity/corporation, even if they have below-average functionality in wider-boundary thinking. This leads to a society in which there are tiny islands of rigorous science loosely woven together with what are essentially fairy stories. Experts generally have NO IDEA how their own expertise might fit together with that of an expert in another discipline… because it seldom comes up! These societal choices have led to society-wide blindspots. Yet “worrying about blindspots” isn’t a thing we think about either because…it’s another blindspot. If various reductionist experts don’t get excited, we don’t either, even if lower-status scientists make a more cogent logical case. There’s something analogous to a real tribal status dynamic at play. So if Elon Musk describes a “Hyperloop” high-speed train, or the use of giant rocket ships for cheap passenger travel from one spot to another on the earth, we believe: we bid up his stock prices and line up for tickets, even if the proposals themselves don’t pass even a basic scientific “sniff test” for real-world feasibility. And in doing so, we tacitly take onboard an unrealistic set of expectations for the future. There is no “Synthesist General” position in our government – or any government - responsible for relegating nonsense propositions to the sidelines, because it hasn’t occurred to us that we might need one. So excessive reductionism is an attractor which can – and does - pull us in the direction of oversimplification, and of overlooking the obvious fact that the world we inhabit is a complex place with a bunch of emergent stuff happening all at once. We are marketed the wonderful traits of an elephant ear, or foot, or tail and in doing so miss the fact that the entire room is full of elephants. Or perhaps more apt, alligators we have somehow ignored by focusing on elephant parts and not looking down.

There was a popular movie in 2006 called “Idiocracy”, in which people of lower intelligence outbred those of higher intelligence, and via democracy created a really stupid nation. That’s not our problem, but we seem to be achieving a somewhat similar effect by different means: not idiocy, but willful ignorance. Ignocracy?

We graduate high school students who have heard nothing about ecology, and college students who don’t know where food or money really comes from. Our societies pursue “permanent growth” without any real idea of what that implies. It simply feels better to be ignorant, non-rational and optimistic about most things, and we have mostly gotten away with it for quite awhile now. This is not to say that humans are intrinsically boneheaded. We’re “evolved to be wrong” in many ways which gave us historic advantages, but we can actually be pretty clever. That cleverness generally comes in response to challenges or crisis, however, which means that we don’t step out of our comfort zones until some problem has arrived on our doorstep and is actively harshing our mellow. It would be perfectly feasible to design an education system which taught children how to design a future they’d want to live in, and being kids they’d take it onboard and deal with it pretty well. However, we haven’t done that yet. And without the language, terminology, and skills to think about the world as it really exists, it will be very difficult for our social species to plan for even the obvious challenges. And so, our ‘ignocracy’ ignores our challenges, at least for now.

Hyperoptimism/tech singularity

We humans evolved with an optimism bias as a way to stay healthy. There was good reason for this: we had very little control over the big things which affected our lives, and it’s far healthier to not be stressed-out all the time. So rather than worrying about whether we would catch enough antelopes to eat next year, we just assumed that we somehow would, because it feels better to be happy than be worried (and via suppression of cortisol and other stress hormones, that also keeps us healthier). Plus worrying about next years’ antelopes didn’t affect their availability anyhow. It’s STILL healthier to be happy than worried all the time, but the situation has changed a bit since our optimism bias evolved. In particular, human actions are now the greatest force determining what actually WILL happen in the future, on issues from antelopes to nuclear bombs to a changed world. That being the case, we really should spend time being concerned about our collective effect on things, but we still have that “don’t worry – be happy!” bias we inherited from our time on the African savannahs. It feels like we shouldn’t worry, even when we should. This bias of ours can reach some pretty crazy extremes. For instance, when you combine this hyperoptimism with “excessive reductionism” (above), you get societal

beliefs like the “tech singularity” which is actually believed in by a lot of ostensibly educated bright people. Under the tech singularity scenario, computers will keep getting faster indefinitely, leading to us all uploading our minds into immortal robots in the coming decades. This tale is a powerful attractor for some folks, because the story implies all future environment/energy problems will be solved or won’t matter. Why would immortal robots need healthy ecosystems, a comfortable temperature, other species, or fish in the seas? Indeed, this attractor is so strong that people are now paying to freeze their dead severed heads in liquid nitrogen, since of course immortal robots will quickly figure out how to bring dead heads back to life in a brighter future than that which prevailed when the heads were detached, and will for some reason feel obligated to. Even those who don’t believe in full-on robotic immortality can be pulled by this attractor into believing various miracles of technology will inevitably be brought into being by some sort of transcendentally advanced human cleverness, despite the fact that such miracles run counter to the laws of the universe. When scientific reasoning is attempted by those who are stressed about the future, but who lack a systems understanding of limits, it can morph into a mythology pretty quickly. A very persuasive one to some people. And most of us have an optimism bias at work regarding the future, because we inherited it. It’s not that easy to recognize that our feelings come not from rationality, but from ancient coping mechanisms from the savannah.

It Doesn’t Matter Anyhow – (Nihilism)

Another strong attractor, when a human feels bummed out about something which seems difficult, is what we might call nihilism... the feeling that “oh well, we’re screwed -nothing really matters anyhow”. It may be too late to solve X problem, and if it isn’t too late we probably can’t fix it anyhow. And if we did fix it, problems Y and Z would still exist, so what’s the use? I wanted a future with more stuff and less stress, but if I’m going to have less stuff and have to work harder, why should I bother to care about any of the rest of it? It’s true we humans tend to consider anything that “feels depressing” to be roughly equivalent to all other situations which “feel depressing” and thereby establish sort of a false equivalence between all such states. And that can make any particular challenge seem overwhelming. During the great stock market crash of 1929, a large number of investors jumped out of high windows to their deaths. Why was this? Because a little ribbon of ticker-tape had informed them that their abstract net worth had diminished. They were still healthy animals in a wonderful world, but were so overwhelmed by the abrupt

hedonic down-ratchet of their imagined relative status that their neurons shouting loudest caused a messy death on the pavement to seem preferable. Because if that one bad fact was true, nothing else mattered to them. Ergo, jumping out the window must be a good idea. Most of us don’t take it that far, but the same evolved circuits exist in every human mind. Some event or circumstance feels true and final, but it isn’t. All futures for the broader world are NOT equivalent, nor are they yet determined. The same goes for one’s own life. At times, understanding and describing the probability distribution of the future can be distressing, but just like a spider on your arm can feel scarier than a nuclear war in the moment, our brains can trick us into sub-optimal decisions, that in clearer (future) moments, might in retrospect seem poor, or even crazy.

Sociopathy – “After my kids die, I don’t care if the world blows up”

A very close friend of the authors, when discussing climate change, ocean acidification and the carbon pulse, stated ‘well after my kids die I don’t care if the world blows up”. A shocking statement perhaps, but probably one that behind closed doors might be commonplace in modern human cultures. That is, the impulse that nothing matters unless it directly affects you and your immediate tribe and in the near term. Those who feel that way would probably object to the label, but let’s call this what it is: sociopathy, the lack of empathy for, or conscience towards, those you don’t know. This is a very efficient attractor, because it removes much of the complexity one might encounter in the world, and fits well with our tribal minds. Attitudes like this actually aided our fitness, back on the savannah, to consider those outside our immediate tribe to be antagonists, or irrelevant, because very often they were. Those who felt this way were more likely to survive and become our ancestors, so such a tendency became “baked in” to our programmed feelings. So looking out for oneself and one’s family “feels right” in a very strong way that looking out for one’s species never can... because that feeling comes directly from the ancient illogical imperatives in our brainstem. Indeed, it feels SO right that human history has been shaped by wars, massacres, and expunging of competitors. In the time between wars, this sentiment can manifest as simply not caring about the future, because there’s no emotional connection with a person one hasn’t met (or who hasn’t yet been born). Such unseen future persons are outside the tribe (as a cave-man mind conceives of a tribe) and therefore inconsequential.

As far as this goes, it isn’t incorrect; but it is sociopathic, because it leads to a decision matrix via which one is willing to derive personal advantage from actions which cause serious consequences for others. This is not only a terrible way to run a planet, it’s a pretty sad and isolated way to exist. That doesn’t mean it’s necessarily uncommon, though: a fair percentage of those who DO expect societal disruptions are attracted to “guns and gold” as opposed to community.

The Inverted precautionary principle

Consider two things: a parachute harness and a set of pants suspenders. These objects have the same function: holding something up. Yet a parachute harness is engineered to be more than ten times as strong as it needs to be, while a set of suspenders is engineered to be exactly as strong as it needs to be. Why is this? It’s an example of the precautionary principle: if the consequences of failure are severe, you want to really err on the side of “not failing”. So pants suspenders and parachute harnesses are not considered interchangeable. If one breaks, your life ends after a long plunge and a lot of screaming. If the other breaks, your pants fall down. So a precautionary principle makes a lot of sense in situations with unrecoverable failure modes. For instance, it’s not desirable to give nuclear launch codes to a sociopath. In our day to day lives, we often adhere to a precautionary principle. We’ll pay a bit extra to design cars with air bags and seat belts even though MOST of them won’t ever crash. We take the lead out of house paint on the possibility that some child might eat paint chips in the future. We build bridges and airplane wings to handle more G force than we expect them to encounter. We fill airships with helium instead of hydrogen. Because failure isn’t really an acceptable option. However, all these adjustments have been made in response to terrible accidents in the past; people falling out of parachute harnesses, bridges falling down, dirigibles exploding, airplanes having their wings fail, etc. We are less astute about entirely foreseeable problems which haven’t caused calamities yet. Indeed, our optimism bias (above) leads us to assume that calamities which haven’t happened recently WON’T happen, so we don’t prepare for them. This leads to something we refer to as the “inverted precautionary principle”, under which the burden of proof is placed upon any future scenario which doesn’t sound delightful. Which is to say, we invert the precautionary principle and require a high standard of proof that anything negative could ever happen.

These days, reductionist science is significantly organized around the odds of a given proposition being true, termed the “confidence interval”, with the most popular confidence interval being 95%. This standard is so pervasive that it’s applied by many even to potentially catastrophic outcomes in which a precautionary principle would make a lot of sense. So, for instance, our institutions insist on a 95% confidence level on something like the disappearance of shelled organisms, and resulting oceanic food web collapse, due to ocean acidification... when even a 1% chance should be enough to galvanize our actions. It’s the equivalent to saying that parachute harnesses should be built to the standards of pants suspenders unless it can be PROVED that we will ABSOLUTELY die otherwise. And since many things can’t be as easily tested as suspenders, we can employ that “inverted” precautionary principle as a mechanism to avoid doing anything, or thinking stressful thoughts. This sounds abstract, but it’s a real thing. For instance, in the early days of the space shuttle, the program managers estimated roughly a 1 in 100,000 chance of catastrophic failure of a shuttle launch. The engineers felt it was more like 1 in 100, but didn’t communicate this since the question wasn’t asked of them. A recent NASA study synthesizing what is now known places the actual initial per-launch odds of catastrophic failure at 1 in 9. Yet an order of magnitude could have been easily estimated by a reasonably competent 4th grader, based on the known failure rate of large solid-fuel boosters, which was roughly 1 in 100. Since failure of either of the two solid-fuel boosters would be catastrophic, quick math leads to an estimate of 1 in 50, which is a lot closer to ballpark reality than the numbers which were used by the decision-makers. So even rocket scientists can be affected by optimism in a way that creates a skewed precautionary principle. (And in a reductionist world, there are a lot of different kinds of rocket scientist).

It’s someone else’s problem

In a highly-specialized society organized around wasting surplus energy, most tasks in society are not done by most people. If your toilet malfunctions, you call a plumber. When your trash can is full, specialized trucks driven by trained trash engineers come and pick it up, and thousands of monkeys separate it into recyclable categories. (Or there might as well be trash-sorting monkeys as far as we would know). If you get an impacted nose hair, it’s off to the nose-hair doctor for an encounter with a nasal scope. And so on: pretty much every conceivable thing which needs doing has had some specialist assigned to do it, so we have come to expect that everything which should be done has had experts assigned and paid to do it by the invisible hand of marketplace demand and capitalism.

So when we are faced with the thorny problem of limits-to-growth simultaneous with planetary limits-to-sink, one attractor is the immediate assumption that someone else is working diligently and competently on these problems. If the free marketplace hasn’t accomplished that, then surely some governmental commission has been empowered to do so, using the best science. And if THAT hasn’t happened, then surely the richest 1% of humanity, in their ambition and opulence, will figure a way to deal with it, since they have the most power and the most to lose, and are presumably engaged in powerful secret societies to control the world anyway. And if all THAT fails, well, God/gods/angels/beneficent space aliens/Gaia must have a plan which is so super-duper that regular mortals couldn’t understand it, but which will work because miracles ALWAYS work. The thing is, though, that the “invisible hand” of the marketplace isn’t about looming future problems at all. Democratic governments are spectacularly stupid in some ways, and being proactive about future problems in perhaps the biggest way. There HAVE BEEN governmental commissions established and packed with scientists: we ignore and de-fund them. The richest 1% are, if anything, more clueless than the poorest 1%, because they can afford to be; and their secret societies – to the extent that they exist – are mainly selfish and stupid. They don’t really need to deal with problems, they just need to stay rich enough that the worst impact of any problems continue to fall on everybody else. And gods/angels/aliens don’t solve problems in the physical world: planning on miracles is entirely the same as not planning at all. So it SEEMS LIKE problems which don’t happen in our own households, within our personal sphere of skills and comfort, are someone else’s job. It’s just that for most of them there IS NO someone else empowered to deal with them, so that “mental attractor” of saying “it’s someone else’s problem” and deciding to ignore it can be a problem for US. Because when everyone considers it someone else’s problem, nobody does anything to deal with it.

We face a series of complex, abstract, threatening and urgent but non-immediate problems. This combination is almost the perfect storm for our brains to ignore and reject. The processing which goes on in a human mind is a bit like small claims court: it isn’t so much about truth or justice as about the emotional efficiency -the mental resolution of contentious issues so they’re no longer contentious. A human mind in a state of dissonance doesn’t feel good, so it takes real effort to maintain a state of dissonance for long. For that reason, there are all sorts of heuristics within a human mind to break the deadlock and stop thinking about the something that’s causing the dissonance. Some of the heuristics are super-illogical, but it doesn’t matter to us, because being in a state of dissonance causes stress, like having a snapping turtle latched onto a toe or a wasp trying to lay eggs in your ear canal. So pretty much anything which causes that sensation to go away has a good chance of being accepted. Looked at it this way, it’s easier to appreciate a simple truth which might seem like a contradiction: Denial and Nihilism are functionally similar mechanisms with the same end goal. A person who denies there is a problem, and a person who thinks that problem is hopeless, may disagree (strongly) over the details,. but both have resolved their dissonance over the issue and in so doing avoided having to do anything about it. In the real world, it is a small minority of people who are willing to suspend the dissonance-avoidance brain mechanisms available to them and to engage with a stress-causing situation to resolve it in the physical world. That they must generally do so these days within democratic systems that inherently sideline minority opinions is a

structural problem with the way we design our societies, with roots in our ancient brainstem function. In conclusion, all of the above dynamics populate two mental poles 1) there is no problem, “XYZ” will solve these issues, or 2) it’s too late/too big – there’s nothing we can do. These two poles can roughly be described as denial and nihilism. And they are functionally equivalent. Why? Because they both resolve dissonance by obviating any personal behavioral change. And the result of resolution of dissonance by billions is that the underlying economic/environmental challenges remain. Please help to change that.

7 Flavors of the Future

By now anyone with a smartphone or a television has heard the term ‘sustainability’ and has at least a vague notion that humanity may be running into some sort of environmental or resource limits. But beyond these headlines, the ‘notions’ that are in peoples’ heads are almost as many as there are peoples’ heads. In conversations with bright, well-intended folk about the future, we are struck by how different peoples’ outlooks for future are, and even how different the vocabulary and language is. Pretty much ALL these people think they “believe in” a science-based future - and simultaneously think those who disagree with them do not. This highlights something important: the way the universe works is that all the things described by what we call “science” simultaneously affect the way things turn out. However, the way our culture mostly uses science is as islands of valid description surrounded by oceans of fairy stories, with science treated as though it were magic (or, to quote a Kurt Vonnegut character, “science is magic that works”). The very nature of expertise in today’s societies is reductionist. Your neighbor may be a specialist in programming the targeting systems for cruise missiles, working from a home computer without ever seeing a cruise missile or knowing anyone targeted by one. We use cell phones and microwave ovens, but it is quite beyond us to build one ourselves. We treat them as “magic that works”, which is why chimpanzees can learn to use them. The end product functions simply, so we’re able to easily accommodate them into whatever story we use to describe the world, even though the complex physical rules used to design them are quite beyond us. So like all humans for the last 200k years, we tend to pick the story that most fits with our temperament, circumstances and expectations. But now we flavor it with a bit of science. The only problem with this is that ANY aspects of the real world which are ignored invalidate the synthesis results. Below we group peoples’ beliefs about the future into 7 broad categories. While the intentions of all groups below are ‘a better future’, there seems to be little overlap between groups. In other words, many people expect a ‘more sustainable future’ but with wildly different understandings of what that might mean, what we should do to get there, and the likelihood of achieving it. Let’s take a brief tour through some of the more popular flavors of the future we see around us.

Fossil Cornucopia

The earth is an enormous place with incredible amounts of resources. It’s just not credible that humans have used up more than a tiny fraction of all that, or really ever could. I’m always reading about new oil discoveries in various parts of the world. Peak Oil has been thoroughly debunked, wasn’t it supposed to happen all the way back in the 70’s? Plus, why would all the IPCC climate scientists be so worried if fossil fuels were going to become scarce any time soon? If you look at the data, the trend has always been more oil and more materials used every year by humanity. That’s not going to change- when we have high prices it ignites human ingenuity, then we find more oil, and gas, and coal, and everything else. I’m tired of hearing that we’re about to “run out” of oil. As long as the oil companies are allowed to keep drilling, we’ll have all the energy we need for a pretty good future for a very very long time. Key Assumptions/Beliefs: We have enough fossil fuels to sustain economic growth for thousands of years. Human Population in 2200: 15 billion with 14 billion too many immigrants Human average wealth in 2200: Far richer than today Human average happiness in 2200: Far Happier than today Condition of Earth in 2200: OK How we’ll deal with pollution and ecological problems: Somehow. Big Advantage: we don’t need to change a thing except getting rid of environmentalists and buying off the sand people Fatal Flaws: Ignores the increasing energy cost of acquiring energy. Innumeracy. Optimism bias. Motivated reasoning. And what about the other species?

Neoclassical economics: GDP forever

There’s really nothing to worry about. The science of economics clearly shows – using methods perfected over the last century – that resources will continue to become available in any quantity we may demand. If prices go up the market will develop substitutes - this goes for energy carriers like oil, coal, and gas, as well as every other substance we may desire. The genius of capitalism is that a free market makes these sorts of things happen automatically by unleashing the full potential of human ingenuity. That’s why the USA has long been the world’s leading nation with the highest wealth, because we’ve beaten down efforts to interfere with the price mechanism and technological response. Economic theory shows no fundamental obstacles to growing to many times the size of earth’s current population, with GDP doubling every 20 years into the future perpetually. Sure, in 500 years from now the economy will look quite different than today’s – information and efficient use of materials will be a much larger component- but we’ll arrive there, nudged and

occasionally pushed by the invisible hand, with things you might not be able to imagine today. Key Assumptions/Beliefs: Human demand creates unlimited energy and resources from thin air Human Population in 2200: 100 billion+ Human average wealth in 2200: So damn rich we won’t believe it, GDP increasing for billions of years into the future Human average happiness in 2200: Far Happier than today Condition of Earth in 2200: Great. We’ll be so rich we’ll have plenty of money to fix any environmental problems that might arise. Big Advantage: Magic Fatal Flaws: Batshit insane. Elevates invalid principles to status of physical law while ignoring actual physical laws. And what about the other species?

Green Dreams: Wind & solar renewable

The environmental toll taken by burning fossil fuels, and the danger of nuclear power, clearly dictate the necessity of immediately switching to 100% renewable energy. Fortunately, there is more solar power hitting the land in the state of Texas in a single day than is used by mankind all over the planet in all countries. Solar and wind are already cheaper than fossil fuels and will only keep getting cheaper. If we can only muster the political will to make the switch, we can provide new jobs, a cleaner (and smarter) environment, and grow our economies faster than we currently do with costlier fossil fuels. We don’t have to choose between economic growth and environmental harm, we can go to 100% clean green energy which will create more jobs and higher living standards. The main challenge is spreading the good word and divesting from fossil fuel company stocks- since their stranglehold on the political system and imperative to make obscene profits is the main thing holding us back from the transition to renewables. But that is a matter of time - the renewable revolution can’t be stopped, and is now inevitable. Key Assumptions/Beliefs: We just need to stop burning flammable fossils and replace them with wind & solar electricity. Human Population in 2200: Stabilized at around 10 billion Human average wealth in 2200: A bit better than today. GDP increasing slowly and steadily Human average happiness in 2200: Somewhat happier than today Condition of Earth in 2200: Pretty good since we got our act together, earth is healing from the bad stuff we did last 2 centuries

How we’ll deal with pollution and ecological problems: Renewables and the march of human enlightenment cause us to creatively fix them. Big Advantage: Renewables and electricity is easy, efficient and profitable! Just need to convince the ignorant it’s in their best interests and gain political traction Fatal Flaws: No such thing as renewable energy; only rebuildable mechanisms which create only electricity. No clear way to actually do it without FF’s. Might power a nice civilization, but certainly not THIS civilization. And what about the other species?

Glowing Future: Nukes

Solar energy is fine when it’s daytime or with nice weather near the equator, and wind power is fine when the wind is blowing, but those aren’t what our grids need. They need baseload, and the only proven non-nuclear way of supplying baseload is burning fossil fuels or using hydroelectric dams. But fossil fuels are running out and trash our environment, and dams have already been built in all the best places. The next logical step on the energy density ladder is nuclear- it’s a no brainer really. There’s plenty of Uranium, and even more Thorium which could be made into thousands and thousands of years of energy. And beyond that there’s fusion, the power of the sun, harnessed and controlled by our scientists right here on Earth. Once we discover how to do that (and we’re so close!) we’ll have millions of years of power just from the deuterium in seawater. There’s a glowing future on the horizon if we can just clear away some red tape and get it done. Key Assumptions/Beliefs: Unlimited energy from fission and fusion plants by 2050. Human Population in 2200: 10-20 billion Human average wealth in 2200: About the same as now, more or less Human average happiness in 2200: About the same as now, more or less Condition of Earth in 2200: Fair, since we stopped burning so much carbon How we’ll deal with pollution and ecological problems: Running society on nuke electricity ends CO2 pollution and acidification Big Advantage: plenty of uranium, thorium, and deuterium. Fatal Flaws: Would need huge numbers of nuke plants, starting decades ago. It isn’t happening at all. Can’t build fission plants without FF’s. They only make electricity. Intractable problems with nuke weapon proliferation, waste management. Current fuel practice is mega-accident awaiting a power loss. Fusion reactors don’t exist and may never. If they do they’ll be enormous and prohibitively expensive to scale. And what about the other species?

Tech Dreams: Star Trek Future

There’s no real need to worry about earth OR energy as long as we get our priorities straight. Our only problem at the moment is we’re trying to do too much stuff at once on one planet without looking at the right long-term goals and possibilities. It’s becoming clear that our ability to reproduce, and our innate cleverness in developing new ways to feed ourselves and solve thorny problems is eventually aiming us at the stars. Cleverness is what has always separated us from animals, and there’s more humans – and thus more cleverness – today than there’s ever been at any time in history. If there’s one thing the universe has showed us, it’s that cleverness, ambition, and a bit of time, always finds a way. Our universe is so full of energy – all we need to do is reach out and take it! It’s just a matter of time before our scientists do just that and lead us towards a future of abundance. Then there will be no holding us back from an interstellar land rush. (with 70% likelihood) Warp drives – not today, perhaps not in our lifetimes, but eventually, for sure! Key Assumptions/Beliefs: Science discovers unlimited energy, which takes us inevitably to the stars in a manifest destiny Human Population in 2200: 500 billion total on multiple planets Human average wealth in 2200: We no longer use money, just replicators. Everyone has unlimited wealth. Human average happiness in 2200: Deliriously happy and used to it. Sex with beautiful android servants, all humans philosopher-poets Condition of Earth in 2200: Like a beautiful park, with dinosaurs and mammoths and extinct species re-created. Most people live in orbital cities. How we’ll deal with pollution and ecological problems: Move off the earth and terraform other worlds, outlaw pollution. Dolphins, elephants, gorillas get right to vote. Big Advantage: antimatter and dilithium crystals are wonderful Fatal Flaws: there is no source of antimatter in this particular universe, and dilithium crystals aren’t a thing. No new source of available power found or even expected by scientists in last 100 years. Excessively reductionist logic, doesn’t account for assholes, probability, or human nature.

Utopians: Egalitarian colony

The only way to save the earth is to change the way we interact with each other. Our environmental problems derive from the unfairness of our current social systems, our toxic racial and patriarchal structures. We have to first have a just and fair world before we can be sustainable. There is no ethical consumption possible under capitalism, so the solution is to drop out of society and live life on your own terms, to be the change you want to see. Organic farming doesn’t harm the earth. Eventually

everyone will see how much better we’re living, and a tipping point will be reached whereby our race’s destructive and authoritarian urges will be abandoned. Key Assumptions/Beliefs: Peace and Love Human Population in 2200: Lots of awesome peeps! Human average wealth in 2200: We’ll be rich in the things that matter Human average happiness in 2200: Everyone’s happiness will be above average Condition of Earth in 2200: probably great, maybe radically so How we’ll deal with pollution and ecological problems: We’ll all live peacefully and the earth will heal with us, we have the power of song, crystals, herbal brownies, and group hugs Big Advantage: Zero cognitive dissonance, easy drug access, polyamory, smugness, high subjective karma Genetics: 60% Sativa/40% Indica Fatal Flaws: All that toilet paper and other stuff you get at Walmart means people aren’t really detached from the big machine, and will totally move back to the city by the time they’re 30. Farming is hard work. Pretty much nobody envies this lifestyle (yet), so any tipping points are remote. The world isn’t fair.

Muddle Through OK

I really don’t know much about what will happen, nor do I care much about it. The future is just the future. People always get worked up and scare themselves about this or that future calamity/shortage/disaster but it always seems to work out somehow. You know, a day at a time. I don’t really see any reason our lives won’t be pretty much like they are now in 50 or 100 years, as long as nothing really crazy happens. I guess if the oceans rise we can walk to higher ground, and if tuna go extinct we can eat sardines or something. Every human generation worries about some darn thing or other, but things always have turned out OK in the long run. They probably always will. My cousin believes we’ll get flying cars but I’m not so sure I really need one since they might crash a lot. There’s nothing in the paper or on TV about any scary futures, and nobody is getting really excited about anything other than the usual kooks on the History Channel. And why are you asking me all these questions about the future? Don’t you have enough to worry about today? I swear, I always get stuck sitting next to people like you on a plane. If I give you my peanuts, will you stop talking? Key Assumptions/Beliefs: Things usually turn out OK. Human Population in 2200: Dunno. About like now probably. (How much is it now?) Human average wealth in 2200: Not sure, but probably about like now. Human average happiness in 2200: About like now, some things better, some worse.

Condition of Earth in 2200: Not perfect but nothing really bad happened either. Some species gone, a shame but we won’t miss ‘em much if we don’t know they are there now. How we’ll deal with pollution and ecological problems: Some solar panels and wind, frack gas, and whatever seems to work. Scientists will think of some stuff. Maybe geoengineering and air conditioners. Big Advantage: Seems like this is what will happen whatever we do, according to the people at the office Fatal Flaws: no consideration of the actual nature of energy primacy, ecology, finance, or human behavior.

Ultimate Tech: Utopian Singularity

The implication of Moore’s Law is that the future will be significantly defined by ever-increasing transistor density in integrated circuits. This, in turn, allows data storage and information manipulation to increase exponentially. Such an exponential increase will soon reach and surpass the number of connections in a human brain, making it feasible in principle and in practice – inevitable, really – to upload human minds into immortal bodies. This is the logical “singularity” of human technology: to reinvent ourselves and transcend biology entirely, as cybernetic beings free to explore reality at our leisure. We’re the first generation of true immortals, and it’s an exciting time to be alive. We are richer than our grandparents, but the exponential advance in technology in recent years, and in coming decades, will make us look like peasants relative to the material abundance of our children. Key Assumptions/Beliefs: Moore’s Law continues forever, accelerating all aspects of human progress. Human Population in 2200: 20 billion or so, many of them living in immortal robot bodies Human average wealth in 2200: Why would an immortal robot need wealth? You don’t get it. Human average happiness in 2200: However high we set our brain controls, presumably turned up to 11. Condition of Earth in 2200: Who cares? The earth only existed to bootstrap us into terminator drag. We have a cooler virtual reality now. How we’ll deal with pollution and ecological problems: Robots don’t need flowers, friends, oxygen, or fish sticks Big Advantage: We think it will happen SOON, like before we die if we eat right & exercise. If not, we can just freeze our heads ’til then Fatal Flaws: Gross disconnect from nearly all aspects of reality. Sociopathy. Fundamentally a cult. Frozen heads don’t do well after thawing.

There are tens of millions (or more) of well-meaning, creative, pro-social people working on what they think the future needs. The point of the above exercise is not only that good people can disagree, it’s that there are a heck of a lot more ways to be wrong about the future than to be right about it. But even without knowing anything about the future, one could infer that all the demographics characterized above can’t be right.

We tend to think about things we can’t see in verbal phraseology which passes easily from one person to another and triggers associations and coalescing narratives inside our minds. Many more examples could have been made: the future as predicted by adherents of major religions, the future as predicted by various reductionist disciplines, the future as predicted by political candidates, and so on.

What we’re missing, to a large degree, is a set of mental filters to weed out the things which can’t happen and which won’t happen; that is, the things which are precluded by the nature of physical reality and probability. Even then, the chaotic aspects of human systems will cause the actual future to remain a probability distribution, but it will be a distribution of actual possibilities as opposed to fantasy narratives.

Having all these disparate visions of the future is not all that useful for assuring that the future which actually arrives doesn’t suck. Ideally, most of us should be working together towards futures which are still possible to achieve. That is the challenge of our time.

Section 6 -Where do we go from here?

So. We’ve seen that we live at a crucial time, and have a number of problems which boil down to a clever ape species following evolved “feelings” off a cliff, due to the happenstance of finding a jackpot of flammable stuff. The society around us is driven by mythology which conforms to those feelings, rather than to logical or ethical planning for the future. So how does an individual trapped within a near-mindless “superorganism” transition into a sapient invididual with some degree of independence from this "consensus trap” of people around us?

Imagine 101

A Note to Students: certain things are expected of an honors class, and after all we’ve exposed you to in this one, you have every right to expect a list of “things to do”. And we’ll honor that implied social contract, with a lecture by me next week and a list of possible things to do and things to avoid. We have, after all, exposed you to things which would cause any sane person to feel a lot of mental dissonance, so it would be more or less tradition for us to now - as the finale to the show - pull a rabbit from the top-hat, giving you “the answers” in a time-honored college tradition.
