And that leaves us shuffling our feet a bit, because this course wasn’t designed to get you to do specific things that we or anyone suggest. It was to impart more autonomy, not less. If we’ve done it right, you’ll end this course paying attention to fewer imperatives imposed by others, and more attention to why you’re doing the things you’re already doing and planning to do. You all have been given lists of “things to do” since you were little kids, and those lists are pretty long by now. Getting another long list from us during finals week, however thoughtful and well-intended, is probably not what you need. Knowing the basic shape of what’s really going on

around you, including what’s hiding in our cultural blind spots, may be the better deal. Our intention in this course was not just to shine a light at some basic realities our species ignores, but to make you conscious of the way you think, the way human society works, and the evolutionary roots of it all. And the reality of how this interplays with energy and ecology. To tempt you to become a more autonomous proto-sapiens individual by offering you choices which have never been explicitly presented to you before. To provide a few tools, and a context, with which to answer the basic question “who am I?” That’s what it all comes down to, right? We actually don’t have “the answers”. But we think we have some partial answers and a method to hone them. We have a lot of ideas which may get traction, and some theories of how to affect things which may work, but that material hasn’t been included in the course, as there’s just so much which could be packed in. It takes a certain amount of chutzpah to think you can construct a course labeled “reality”, to be fairly complete, and to then to also “stick the landing” the first time it’s taught, so thank you for bearing with us. Telling you now “what to do” might edge past chutzpah into hubris. But we’re not planning to just hit you with all this “reality” stuff and then abandon you. If you wish, you’ll still have access to us via email, and we will be publishing a far more complete version of this stuff. And not just to “write a book”, but as the written basis of logic for a paradigm shift to underlie a series of projects and programs with which we hope to shift things that have so far have proven intractable to pro-future earth-advocates. Maybe you’ll decide to keep in touch with us. Maybe not. You may decide to pursue a bit more normal course than we do, or try to forget this stuff entirely, or to discover things more creative than we’ve thought of and give us recommendations on what to do. That all needs to be your own decision. So with that disclaimer, here are a few end of semester remarks to get you started thinking.

Someone Needs to Know This Stuff

You’re probably wondering, on some level, why the hell we’re doing this to you. This course, this information, this unsettling, less-filtered gaze upon the underpinnings of physical, evolutionary, and probabilistic reality. And the even more unsettling implications for a human future.

Of course, you probably understand one obvious part of the rationale: someone needs to know this stuff. And while a lot of people currently know individual pieces of it, these pieces haven’t assembled into a whole in many human brains yet, which means that society is not coming up with workable answers to some very pressing problems. Our living world urgently needs homo sapiens, and we haven’t reached that standard yet. We don’t expect all of you to “drop everything” and become superheroes - (maybe just a few of you :-) - but knowledge and synthesis of first principles is a start. Being temporarily sad, overwhelmed and/or scared is the rational response to first hearing about this stuff. In comparison with what your culture believes, these situations not of your making are depressing realities. It is our hope that any initial shock and sadness at learning about the energy/behavior/environmental collision is something to be experienced, witnessed, catalogued and then passed through - because a sapiens mind is a terrible thing to waste. In the history of this world until very recently, no individuals of any species have ever integrated the things in their head that you have been taught in Reality101. And knowing these things is pretty much a necessary prerequisite for coming up with real-world plans to take our species to a decent future, bending it away from the unconsciously destructive agenda of the gene. Your awareness of these things makes you important not only to your family, friends, civilization and species, but, if truth be told, to all extant complex life at a very crucial time. In the previous mass extinctions on earth, there was no possibility of intervention and changing course. This time, at least in principle, there could be such a possibility. So, what are the first steps in engaging with the future?

Monsters, Myths and Hope

In the legendary Greek myth, Perseus had a predicament to deal with. His mission was to kill the monstrous snake-haired Medusa; yet mortal man could not behold the face of the gorgon and live. Those who saw her were turned to stone. As the story goes, he solved this by only looking at her indirectly, peering at her distorted reflection in a golden shield while hacking with his sword in her general direction as he advanced ass-first into the fray. Monsters don’t generally fare that well in such legends, but in the real world our money would have been on Medusa. In learning about Reality 101, you have gotten hints that there is no specific villain in our story, and that many of society’s knee jerk reactions on ‘what needs to be fixed’ are misguided, or flat out counterproductive. Perseus’ bold ass-first charge in the wrong direction based on “not looking at things clearly and directly” is the current human, and thus activist default. Our blind spots see to that. Problem is, they cause us to see monsters where there aren’t any, miss monsters that are there, and to waste our time and energy on things that have no chance of actually slaying the monster. In the case of Perseus, there was no actual reason to fight a monster to death in the first place, his actual adversary was a creepy king who wanted to marry his mother and manipulated him into a fight that was designed to kill him off. But perhaps the real victim of the story was Medusa, who had started her life as a beautiful maiden, was cursed with snake hair and inability to interact with humans, and ultimately got blamed and beheaded for no particular reason. If you’ve never before looked at it from that perspective, don’t be surprised - humans deal with myths the same way we deal with everything else, taking our cues from those around us.

Understanding the core issues- who is the Medusa, who is the creepy king, and how to strategize on the future game board full of monsters (obvious or otherwise) where you and some like-minded individuals are game pieces is the first step beyond Reality 101. Clearly, by our creating this class, and by honing a paradigm for a homo sapiens approach to our existence in deep time, you can tell that we don’t think it’s useless. Far from it. We still think that there are huge degrees of freedom in the potential outcomes. All fantasy futures will not be - and never were - possible, but there is still probably an attainable range from “quite decent” to “horrible”. Steering a path to the decent ones is the challenge of our age, species, and world. But it’s a narrow path in many ways: there are a lot more ways to get it wrong than get it right, and the “agenda of the gene”, wired deep within the illogical and invisible ancient structures of every human brain, will constantly seek to divert us from such a path and to thwart sane plans. Those humans who take the sapiens leap will have to initially contend with the greater majority who haven’t, and who are hostile to the very notion of short-term sacrifice, even modest sacrifice, in exchange for a deep future they can’t envision and won’t live to see. Even more difficult is “threading the needle” between personal denial and fatalism, staying energized and psychologically healthy while making adult decisions on behalf of our species (and others). It means looking directly into the face of the gorgon, and not at the distorted reflections which seep through our evolved bias, fallacies, and illusions. Yet it must be attempted, and soon. So far in this course we’ve peeled back some of the layers of obscuring distortion, giving you a progressively clearer view of the actual problems facing our species and world. We’ve also shown you examples of intelligent people who find personal certainty in denial or fatalism and thus avoid actually doing anything useful. Yet there’s no reason to think that we humans aren’t fit enough to look at reality honestly. We became who we are by facing some daunting realities. We are kick-ass primates who until recently have dealt with some very hairy, scary realities. Plagues. Famines. Mile-high ice sheets and blizzards. Horrible parasitic diseases. Sabre-toothed tigers, dire wolves, cave bears. We kicked their asses into oblivion and made houses out of mastodon bones. So at what point in evolution did we become aristocratic weenie debate societies, academically regarding our navels, unwilling to take risks or endure hardship, throwing cucumber slices at one another with capuchin howls as we meekly commute to make-work jobs? The big shock is not reality itself, but in abruptly finding out - after much of your life - that you’ve been told incorrect, incomplete, and wildly overoptimistic stories about

the world by those around you who never questioned that what “feels good to believe” might not in fact be true. We think that if kids were taught the realities of energy, evolution, and ecology from a young age, they’d adjust to it; (even at the same time being more than a bit annoyed with the situation they’re being handed). And we’d better get going on that. But we’ve dallied far too long to wait for the next generation, which means that some of us need to drop our shields and look our gorgon in the face, and resist being turned to stone by the dissonance-avoiding heuristics which urge denial, fatalism, depression, and inactivity. We may even find, by doing so, that the gorgon we’re ostensibly preparing to fight isn’t the real enemy. We’ve met and read a lot of smart people who feel that we’re doomed, because we’ve tried everything and nothing has worked. The 6th mass extinction is proceeding, CO2 emissions are still headed up like a rocket despite people flying around to conferences making nonbinding commitments that take effect only after they’ve retired, and people driving hybrid cars that need steel, asphalt, lithium and water to cool the electric plants. Activists have tried their best, it seems, but nothing much has happened. Our thesis here is that –as a species - we haven’t even started doing our best yet. That is, we haven’t tried anything which would be reasonably expected to have a positive effect on our future. We’ve just done a bunch of social–posturing stuff which “feels like” it should be useful, but really isn’t. So now it’s time to start doing stuff that works. So why haven’t we solved the 6th extinction, the CO2 problem, or any other problems associated with the Earth’s carbon pulse? - the “enlightenment” paradigm of passing more and better information widely is our default, and it hardly ever works. It just feels like it should. The problem isn’t an information deficit, it’s the way human minds deal with information. - if “nice” was mandatory as a practical methodology, there would be no life-saving surgeries, no dental care. Why do we automatically mentally exclude all possibilities which aren’t “nice” in all practical ways? At this point we’re mitigating a predicament, not cutting a birthday cake. - there is currently no science of effective activism. It’s still at the “alchemy” stage of everyone trying random things without a real sense of deep underlying principles. There’s no reason one can’t be developed, but the nature of social movements is such that it’s not really being pursued.

we assume that the bigger a problem is, the more difficult and complex its solution will be. That doesn’t necessarily follow. It’s often the structure and not the size of a problem which makes it tractable or intractable. - small groups and individuals have far greater capacity to transform many kinds of large-scale issues than do large groups, because they can efficiently manipulate more variables more quickly while intelligently pursuing a more complex plan. Yet activists and their funders tend to be idealistic about “fairness of process” which generally means addressing problems with large, egalitarian, slow-moving committees ill-suited to affecting rapidly-reacting systems, which rapidly become gridlocked into mediocrity by internal social-status dynamics and gamesmanship.

And why should we think there’s hope? Here is a very short list:

because we haven’t really started trying. So far we’ve just been talking, blogging, and fiddling.

because the worst outcomes are not yet locked in.

because we don’t all have to be ineffectual, timorous wusses. We don’t need to

be terrified of what others may think of us. We can leave that mode any time. What are we saving up our adrenaline for?

because we don’t need all the things we think we need to be perfectly happy and healthy.

because despite living in what amounts to a temporal-sociopath society in which future human childhoods and conscious wildlife have roughly the status of bacteria, we can transcend it and choose to care about the future.

because if given a choice without being indoctrinated at a young age, kids will choose future life. The reason this course was shocking is not that humans can’t deal with reality, but that you were hit with reality after two decades of being told a different story

our interpreter modules actually have potential super powers if they can be liberated from the core brain feelings of the default gene agenda.

because solving our problems doesn’t depend on everyone in the world agreeing. They never have, never will. We don’t need consensus to change the world, we just need to change it. The world has always changed without consensus agreement.

because the same blind spots which create the problems also hold some solutions. Our thinking is constrained and in a rut. We build conceptual boxes around what we think is possible, and they become self-fulfilling by causing us to not try

We don’t need aliens or angels. In principle, people can be the surgeons, the hard-decisions aliens. There’s no one else to do it. We just need to step up.

sapiens vs fecundus? Cultural evolution can work incredibly fast

only now do we understand the shape of the full picture. We never have

before. That has power. Go.

because sapiens have inherently more complete and workable abstracting tools

available to them. More complete mental models of reality have inherently better predictive value.

because wrangling the amoeba has always been done, and we shouldn’t shrink from it. Failing to try means that those with no such scruples, following the gene agenda, win by default.

because this is no longer a jungle tribe, we can let go of those instincts and go beyond them. The snakes aren’t the problem at this point.

The amoeba does simple things, so minds must steer it. Whose will it be?

Homo? Sapiens?

In this course we’ve talked about a “sapiens” agenda, as opposed to the default agenda of the gene. But wait a minute, we are already “homo sapiens”, right? Well….

The genus was created, and the species named, by C. Linnaeus in 1758. He deserves credit for including humans as a species at all, since most of the world then (as now) doesn’t really consider humans to be animals. Still, there has been some science done since then which has put things in perspective. As Diamond points out in The Third Chimpanzee, creating the genus “homo” isn’t indicated based on taxonomic rules, it’s more like a self-awarded vanity license plate (“IMGR8”). The reality – if we don’t bend the rules applied to other species - is that there would be three species of chimpanzee; chimps, bonobos, and humans. So without the designer plates, that’d be Pan troglodytes, Pan paniscus, and Pan sapiens. But that “sapiens” moniker doesn’t really wash either, since so far we’ve been clever but not wise. Maybe Pan pyrophilus for our tendency to set things on fire? Pan delusio - the deluded ape? There’s no shortage of qualities which might distinguish us from the other chimps (what’s latin for “wears pants”?); but if we’re honest “wisdom” isn’t among them.

When asked what he thought of western civilization, Gandhi replied “I think it would be a good idea”. Perhaps we should think of “sapiens” in a similar way: something we need to aspire to, and quickly, as the primate “gene agenda” and the “lighting things on fire” proclivities have combined to create some impressive impediments to a desirable future. Actually, Gandhi might be a decent example of a sapiens agenda: a person who used intelligence, empathy, bravery and nonviolence to accomplish the seemingly impossible: winning a war against long odds without fighting it. We will

need at least some sapiens to attain their namesake if we are to reasonably navigate things of value through the various physical and ecological bottlenecks of the 21st century.

Deus ex Machina

A hoary old plot device, from ancient Greek and Roman drama, was the “deus ex machina”. It was a scriptwriting kludge that guaranteed happy endings. You could create a terribly tragic plotline in which awful things unfolded… then at the end, a god (deus) was lowered by cable (ex machina) from above, and passed a miracle solving all problems, bringing murder victims back to life and righting wrongs, so all the actors could dance around happily and exit stage left. If you find this unsettlingly familiar, it’s because it’s pretty much “the current plan” for dealing with modern human problems.

Most humans believe in real gods and angels and expect them to function in this role in the physical world. Any time now. Others believe that beneficent aliens are watching and waiting until the absolute last moment to swoop in and save us with better technology. And many others trust in metaphorical or pseudoscientific gods & demons, such as the “invisible hand of the marketplace” and expect them to bring about permanently rising prosperity underpinned simply by the increased demand existing in billions of chimp brains. Yet all these imaginary entities exist only in our virtual worlds – the fact that they are absent from the physical one poses a problem.

Clearly, nobody is playing the role of “wise guardian angel” or “beneficent alien gamekeeper” at present. But to return to that Gandhi quote, “… it would be a good idea”. The notion of clear-seeing minds, guided by altruism, love of Earthlife and logic, which step in like loving parents to set right human mistakes and return the world to beauty and bounty for a long and happy human future. We apparently need such beings to become a part of our storyline, and they are clearly late to the party. Alas, aliens are not hiding among us and probing midwest farmers, nor is there an emergent species overmind watching and preparing us for the future - benevolent, unscrupulous, or otherwise. What does exist are the amoeba-like tropisms of a growing human heat engine which emerges as the lowest aggregate common denominator of seven billion chimp brains competing for status, wealth and stimulation.

However, as Gandhi showed, human events can be steered even on large scales, not as much by committees or representative bodies, but rather when individual minds are used effectively and non-delusionally. And that’s where most hope lies: in individual sapiens minds. Someone must step into the role of beneficent aliens and

steer our world away from dismal futures. The only possible candidates are individual human minds. Like yours.

Towards Sapience

The future is not what most in our culture/species expect. We are developing an extensive, but not exhaustive nor definitive list of suggestions that fit the physical, ecological and social roadmap that is ahead of us. We have broken them into 3 broad categories and below briefly share an overview without too many specifics on:

Economic responses –how to better navigate a world with less surplus 2. Psychological responses – how to not go crazy in a world seemingly gone

crazy 3. Natural system responses – how to play a role in the defense of the living

It should come as no surprise that ‘natural system response’ is ultimately the most important to your authors, and really it should probably be to everyone who has any ecological literacy or hope for the future beyond their lifetimes. But as you know, time bias and other blind spots make the environmental problems sound like whispers compared to the other economic and social shouting on our plates. And, truthfully, without our basic needs – physical and psychological – taken care of, the earth probably won’t have too many champions. Essentially, there is no “one size fits all” solution – and we will need butchers, bakers, candlestick makers and eco-jedi all in symphony to craft a liveable future.

Economic Responses

The society around us is still pretty much all-in behind the belief that the economy can grow forever based on sheer human demand, detaching itself from any need for increasing energy and materials. (Our expert economists say these things, so they must be true). For that reason, the stock market – which measures aggregate perceptions – is still acting healthy, along with the property market, financial markets, and wide belief in continuing globalization. We believe that our money, and our debt, and thus our growing societal wealth is backed by that permanently-growing prosperity. It’s a set of beliefs which has been true for what seems like a very long time, so we tend to think of it as a “first principle” law of reality. But as you know or at least suspect, it isn’t one. Our large blind spot has hidden an army of invisible fossil-energy slaves which have created essentially all the extra prosperity, convenience, biomass, and wealth markers. And they’re going away soon, because the real “first principles” of physics and thermodynamics require that they

must. Their departure won’t be obvious at first: we measure our world in dollars, not in net energy. Yet as the net energy available to our species crests the depletion curve and takes a shark-fin dive back toward sustainable solar flows, our fiscal systems will become ever-shakier. There’s currently massive debt overhang – debts which there will not be sufficient energy to ever repay. That debt has been incurred to keep the party going awhile longer. But there’s a funny thing about ponzi schemes: anything that can’t go on forever, won’t. Thus, in your lifetime there will be impressive monetary gyrations leading to discontinuities, and such fiscal convulsions will be how humanity initially experiences the reality of diminishing net energy. This will probably be interesting to live through: there’s no telling what mix of good and bad ideas may be adopted by a society which doesn’t really understand what’s happening. Certainly the underlying gene agenda which has our species in thrall will cause a number of emergent phenomena which have been seen before: the sorts of things which fill our history books. It would be good to also see a robust Sapiens response, but if so, it will have to play out in situations where many people are angry, afraid, resentful, and somewhat desperate to retain what they feel they should have. This is one of several ways in which this course could help you and your loved ones: if you know to expect this, you can prepare for it in terms of personal resilience, both psychological and practical.

Psychological Responses

In Reality101 we’ve done our best to present an honest first-principles synthesis of our evolved minds, and how they have so far interacted with the world and with the carbon pulse. It’s not too extreme to say that the beliefs of our society are crazy by physical-world standards, thought they seem normal to most of the people we (and you) know. We’ve gotten away with such a disconnect due to the nearly unlimited “do overs” enabled by our fossil slaves. But as they go away, there will be a lot to deal with in addition to the demise of the dominant mythology of perpetual growth. A lot of things will change. (Remember the old Chinese curse: “may you live in interesting times”?) You do live in interesting times, and they’re only going to get more “interesting” from here on out.) But you can prepare for that psychologically by expecting the changes, not setting yourself up to depend on business-as-usual, making your life decisions in an informed way, and deciding what sort of life would be most meaningful to you, since these are the times you were born to. The human future will be significantly determined by the gene agenda, and past history shows where that leads in times of change. We are hopeful a Sapiens agenda

will also become a player. There will be a lot fewer of them, but they’ll be playing chess while the others are playing checkers... badly. Will we become less hooked on supernormal stimuli, or will society go ever deeper into designer drugs and electronic stimulation? Will we maintain high standards of medical care and long life expectancy, or will that be dialed back? Will families tend to stay closer together rather than fanning out across time zones? Will we embrace diversity, or will we default to blaming outgroups when social stresses arise? Will we move to even crazier belief systems in order to cope in stressful times? Whatever the answers to those questions, bear in mind that even when humanity undergoes what are in retrospect momentous events and changes, they mostly seem pretty slow when they’re happening. Most people who lived through the “depression” of the 30’s and 40’s had happy lives and didn’t know they were part of the Great Depression until later. Just ask them – many are still around and the skills they used then might well be useful again sooner than we think. Events like world wars effect some people intensely, but to most people they are the backdrop to far more pressing events in their local sphere. The lesson to take from this is that even though the coming decades will be historic in many ways, you will live through them one day at a time. It’s important to have the shape of reality in your head, but you don’t need to confront it in its entirety every day. Rather, it should become a backdrop for planning, expectations, and defining your own success as a person. Immense transitions can be accompanied by a lot of laughter and good experiences, even if they’re subsequently labeled as revolutionary by people in the future.

Ecological Responses

With regard to the human predicament, nothing makes sense except in light of the carbon pulse, a very temporary aberration from the normal rules of carrying capacity. This has disconnected the top of the trophic pyramid from energy and material flows and has generated environmental hockey sticks everywhere we look. This pulse is inherently temporary. Keeping it happening for a long time isn’t even an option. So the things which might preserve a living world are the same things we will have to do anyway, just slightly sooner and less traumatically. Being alive now at the inflection point in the carbon pulse - and therefore at the great energy/biomass collision and disconnect - is not like being alive in the fairly recent past or fairly near future. Your generation really is different, and not just because its nitrogen (proteins) was made in factories and you are literally eating transformed fossils. In the past we had very little knowledge about systems and very little about how our minds actually work. In the future there will be much less ability to steer

different courses due to receding horizons. But the coming 2-3 decades will be a time in which the course of earth events will be contested as though we were fighting for air. Due to social media, civic freedoms and the continued presence (and assistance) of billions of fossil co-workers, individual humans still have huge reaches and degrees of freedom in their ability to change the world. The far-seeing systems altruists – and perhaps there are one or three of you in this class - need to get very good at steering things. This is not something one would seek out, it’s more like being on an airliner and hearing the stewardess come on the PA system and ask if there are any pilots onboard.

Let’s be honestly aware that – current headlines to the contrary - the environmental movement has failed so far, in terms of being remotely adequate to the scope of planetary system loss. Its ideals have remained ideals, disconnected from the physical world. This doesn’t mean it’s impossible to change the course of events, just that it’s difficult with the cultural baggage and beliefs we carry around with us. In other words, don’t assume that the current failure of social movements to adequately deal with the human and planetary predicament exhausts all possibilities. We are evolved to pass information to other tribe members and to expect prudent decisions to result, but for many reasons that doesn’t work well with huge diverse populations. Enlightenment per se isn’t much of a useful tactic to change the course of events, despite that being the primary assumption of most activism. Finding out what doesn’t work is productive, as long as we can admit it and learn from it.

Nate and DJ do not have the answers, and this course isn’t about supplying them. But we have some observations which can lead to rough suggestions to get you started.

First and foremost, know why you’re doing what you’re doing. Be mindful not just of society’s blind spots but your own evolved tendency to rationalize and pick up conclusions and aspirations from those around you. Once you decide what matters to you, you should be able to analyze how your actions and decisions square with that. We live on a full planet ecologically speaking and are still adhering to small planet rules. From just being a better person and helping your community to being an Earth-life ambassador and everything in between, we invite you to participate in the future.

So, What to Do? (A preliminary grab-bag of ideas)

Part 1 -An Introduction

So. We’ve arrived at the end of the semester of our brief but packed survey of Reality. Here is where me and my friend DJ suggest ‘what to do’ with all this information. Let’s start with a question that some of you might be asking (though perhaps not): what if we’re wrong? I doubt any of you disbelieve much of the core aspects of what’s been presented: that the evolutionary basis of our minds is relevant to our current global predicament, that energy and resources (not money and cleverness) are the foundations of the modern economy, that oceans and Earth’s atmosphere are absorbing excess CO2 to their detriment, and that wild species and ecosystems are being severely impacted by human beings. The nuance and potential uncertainty in R101 lies within our secondary inferences: how much of our behavior is genes vs. culture and volition, how close are we to the end of growth due to energy remoteness and its repercussions, how

close are we to Heatlock tipping points and other environmental bottlenecks, and similar questions. We don’t know these answers - no human does - and as I’ve stated, given the absolute number of facts and assertions we’ve covered this semester it is virtually certain we will be wrong in retrospect about something. However, it’s likely most of what we covered is reasonably accurate, and the puzzle we’ve assembled together will keep its general shape in coming years and decades. It’s for you to decide – after more study, more observation, and more self-reflection - how our probability distribution of future trajectories, risks, and ethics matches – or differs – from your own predictions. The point is that you now have the tools and concepts to make such predictions, and to see the value in doing so. Energy and economy, human evolved behavioral drives/ constraints, and the other species and ecosystems of Earth – these subjects comprise the Reality 101 synthesis. Now we’re at the point of the semester where we figure out how to integrate these topics in a holistic way and come up with navigable paths, personally and for our world So let’s get into it.

First off, you were just exposed to an intellectual synthesis of the world situation - knowledge of the fundamental way things inter-relate. This is our global Reality, but your own reality - and how you engage with it - will likely be much more comprehensive than just knowing our situation. Equally (if not more) important will be your mental state, your psychological resilience, your daily behaviors, your skills, your emotional and physical health and your strategies, experience and abilities. Understanding Reality 101 concepts, as it turns out, is just one of the pieces of the wider circle that will be your personal reality – ‘being’ (how to live your life) and ‘doing’ (how and whether to try making an impact) will fill out the circle. What

follows below is a non-linear grab-bag of ideas that may lead you down some new, exciting and hopefully impactful paths on the ‘being’ and the ‘doing’ part of the

future. Surfing, Bobbing or Sinking

Acquiring a deeper knowledge of our human reality can seem like a bit of a mixed blessing. Once one digs a little deeper, to transcend the nonsense narratives of popular beliefs and expectations, one quickly begins to see a sharpening outline taking shape: the outline of fast-approaching inevitable consequences to a society based significantly on nonsense narratives. This clearer view isn’t, seemingly, for the faint-hearted. The challenges and discontinuities facing our future can seem intractable and overwhelming, and speaking about them can put us in an awkward social limbo due to their dissonance with consensus beliefs. So what to do? It’s clear that humans don’t automatically retreat from challenges. People have been making up thin excuses to climb tall, dangerous mountains for thousands of years at least. The mountains’ mere existence simply challenges some of us. Yet even so, the sudden appearance of unexpected mountains in our path - metaphoric or otherwise - can be daunting. Their “unexpectedness”, in turn, emerges as a direct consequence of our evolved modes of thinking: optimism bias, the Christmas pony effect, and a list of biases and fallacies as long as your arm. So what hope is there? Quite a bit. We call ourselves homo sapiens, and sapience is definitely something we can aspire to. Part of this is learning how our own brains work, and why. As we start down that path, it quickly becomes clear that the

“feelings” which evolution built into our core brains in the deep past are a mismatch with the human existence which confronts us today. And that includes “feelings” about scary futures, which are anchored on very subjective standards. If magically transported above the arctic circle to a landscape of ice, snow, wind and sea, a person from industrial society might assume they had been given a death sentence and simply sit there waiting to freeze, while an Inuit placed in the same situation might consider it a beautiful day filled with promise. Alternately, finding oneself a quarter-mile offshore in huge breaking ocean waves would strike many people as a hellishly hopeless situation; but a body-surfer might consider it one of the best days of her life, and the best place in the world to be on that day. That is, there are far more situations which seem hopeless to us than there are situations which are actually hopeless. This has much more to do with the way we think, and the extent to which we understand the situation we find ourselves in. The thing which makes us feel hopeless is not having mental tools for dealing with situations that seem novel and threatening. There’s no particular physical difference between an Inuit, a surfer, and an office worker; they simply have worked on very different skillsets and are confident in very different situations. The rest of this century will be a lot like what a surfer experiences: constant change will increasingly BECOME the landscape, but in ways which can largely be anticipated, and which obey physical law and deep drivers of human behavior. This will tend to throw existing advocacy, activism and accomplishments into turmoil, because those are largely predicated on continuation of the social, physical, governmental, and monetary infrastructures we have become accustomed to and consider stable. However, it could also – in principle – provide huge leverage for those who learn to surf those waves of change. ---------------------------------------------------------------- Big step back. Why am I teaching this class? The initial DECISION to teach it was because I (and DJ) feel strongly that the synthesis story is vitally important to the way things may turn out, and hope to be able to communicate it effectively. If we can do that, then a few out of each group of 20 students may run with this stuff in their own ways – ways that could makes significant change on our environmental and social issues. That was the goal. But once I put my “teacher” hat on, that is no longer the (sole) objective. From the first day of class until the final lecture my role is to better equip you all as human beings for the cultural transition(s) ahead of us. That is no

small feat - as my voice is but one of many in a cacophony of chaos in a busy college life, and a voice that is saying things which are not always easy to hear, and not immediately obvious how to implement. The coming Great Simplification and its peripheral events will be like nothing we’ve experienced in many generations - if ever. Some people will be shocked and overwhelmed by news and events into frozen inaction. But others will navigate the ups and downs with aplomb, while moving events along the way. It’s my hope that going through this class will at least give you adequate ‘treading water’ capabilities, and perhaps for some it may be like providing a metaphorical surfboard. So, if you follow the surfing metaphor, the following pages are something akin to a “Surfers Guide to the 21st Century”.

The Superheroes of the 21st Century

One of the things I (NJ) will never forget from traveling is that in the poorest countries, most stricken by disease and hardship, where you would expect children to be sad and miserable, I encountered the happiest, most genuine kids I’ve ever seen anywhere. In Zambia, in cities where there was 50%+ HIV/Aids and incomes were less than $100 per month, the children beamed at me with ear to ear bright smiles. In poor forest villages of central Ecuador, the children worked hard, but then also played hard, about 50 of them sharing one soccer ball, kicking it around the village and encountering various chickens and obstacles to laugh and remark about. What did these young humans have in common? And what did they have different than most American children (and young adults)? They had an understanding of their reality and the reality matched their understanding. And so they found time for joy and play and work and discovery.

A few of you might now be asking yourself: ‘how can Nate be so mean and teach me all this sad and scary stuff’? As I warned the first day of class you might be simultaneously thanking and cursing me well into the future for opening your eyes to view the modern human enterprise with Reality lenses. But it doesn’t really have to be scary or depressing or ominous – the scary part is the DELTA between what you (thought you) knew and what you now understand. If all Americans were taught about reality the way a Zambian or Ecuadorian child was, then what we are experiencing right now would just be another challenge to navigate, with zeal and passion, as opposed to a near paralyzing mountain of dissonance from what our cultural beliefs are. It’s not that Reality is so bad – it’s that it differs dramatically from the cultural stories we’re being told, and have been told since we were born. It’s probably pretty clear by now that the gene-agenda “business as usual” scenario, unmodified, could result in simply awful outcomes. Continuing as we have, extracting and burning flammable fossils and pursuing relative wealth, will wreck the oceans and climate right about the time we run out of most physical wealth and start engaging in the energy “poverty wars” which may quickly turn nuclear; with the aggregate potential of ending the experiment of human civilization, and with it perhaps the “age of mammals” as we have known it; all within the lifetimes of a child born today. So with that as the “Plan A” perhaps it would be worth facing a bit of angst for a better “Plan B.” Modern environmental efforts are flailing because both the story and the 'ask' don’t go deep enough. Scientific articles about OA or climate or biodiversity-loss spend 98% of the article/movie painting a picture of how bad things could soon get and then 2% in the conclusion saying 'the time for bold action is upon us'! The bold actions implied are usually run-of-the-mill capuchin-pleasing social dynamics – that resolve dissonance but accomplish little. Your authors think the time for bold action was many decades ago; now is the time for desperate, sincere and creative action, undertaken with discipline, focus, and seriousness. If there were to be such a class as Reality 201, it might be a course designed to assist Reality 101 graduates to become real-world catalysts who can put all the stuff they just learned into useful practice. One doesn’t win a chess tournament playing with checkers rules, and today’s universities are churning out no shortage of very-bright graduates who have very little idea about the realities which will shape their future and that of their country (and planet). The world doesn’t really need more of those types of college graduates but does need more superheroes and catalysts - persons with qualitatively-greater ability to affect events on all scales than most people do.

Perhaps the biggest focus of R101 is the nature of human blind spots and systematic errors about how the world actually works, how and why the things around us actually happen. Not all R101 graduates will choose to personally engage with the “great challenges” of human futures, but even those whose life-course is largely unaffected may still find themselves just a bit more “super” in terms of their level of awareness and planning, and this may help shape their expectations and their future prospects, that of their families, and of their communities. Just as humanity is no longer surprised by hurricanes due to geosynchronous weather satellites, incorporating a top-down integrated view of reality can prevent unpleasant surprises in many ways. Now that we’re used to weather satellites, few are depressed by the ability to predict, monitor and chart hurricanes. It’s a good thing, not a depressing thing, even if it does cause us to “think about” hurricanes more often than we otherwise would. In the below pages are some of our current thoughts on how the integration of Reality101 principles – especially with insight and effort into your brain’s workings and potentials - can improve your own lives and contribute to Good in our modern societies and natural world. For all we’ve learned about human behavior, it should be clear we - as a species can’t ‘change’ our basic natures. But it is now possible to use our knowledge of whence we came and how we tick, to steer events – in our neighborhood, in our city, and perhaps in the forests of our region, and just maybe in the azure bays in an ocean halfway around the world a thousand years hence. Minnesota, the United States, the World, and the Future could all use some

Superheroes about now…

Part 2 -Getting Used to your new ‘Reality Lenses’

In the movie ‘They Live’, Roddy Piper puts on some magic glasses and suddenly sees the hidden messages that were all around him, but when he takes the glasses off the “cultural spell” returns. In a similar way, you now possess the ‘magic lenses’ to see the world through new energy, environment and human behavior/cognitive bias filters. Though it will be intense and scary at times to see reality this way, it is akin to looking 2-3 cars ahead in a snowstorm when conditions are dangerous – such

foresight will not always prevent an accident or predict events with 100% certainty, but by using these 3 lenses you’re much more likely to properly assess what’s going on. Your now each have several pairs of new lenses which can help you view our world with different insights and different focal lengths. The ecological lens obviously has the longest timeline. The energy/economy lens is more intermediate in the distance it sees. And the evolutionary psychology/human behavior lens is for “up close” daily interactions with other human beings – and with yourself. What follows are some suggestions on how using your new prescription might lead you to different routines and decisions than a ‘monetary’ or ‘what they say on TV’ lens.

The Consensus Trance Lenses

Wearing the same lenses as everyone else can be problematic when it comes to understanding the way the physical world works, because – (wait for it) - most people don’t. So instead of having a scientific worldview, we believe what conforms to what our ingroup believes. Take two kids: one lives in rural Iran and one lives in rural Alabama. Just by knowing this one fact, we have high statistical odds of knowing what they believe about the fine points of the universe’s history and future. Modern economic theory believes humans are independent arbiters of fact. If so, it’s a freaking amazing coincidence that everyone believes what their friends believe. And it’s not just their friends who influence them.

Our minds are biologically and culturally pre-programmed to conform. This leads to some enormous blind spots which we grow up entirely unconscious of, constantly reinforced by the cross-checking validation we do with those around us, through body language, imitation, verbal inflection, and frequent communication. If you happen to find yourself in a room full of fools, it will be your strong inborn tendency to conform with them. (combine this with the capuchin-like “fairness imperative” from the Gene Agenda and you have a recipe for democracies which do dumb stuff).

These tendencies made for kick-ass aboriginal tribes which functioned as an effective superorganism. But now that we’re living in groups of thousands, millions, and billions they tend to “damp out” creativity and novel thinking while enforcing conformity with existing consensus belief systems and the “status quo”. In fact, the reason which many of the problems we face “feel impossible” is that they would require individuals to violate consensus norms and “be different” in some ways in order to accomplish them. In effect, our evolved default lenses are “unicorns and rainbows”, which is to say magic and optimism. That’s really fun to believe as kids, and while we stop believing in literal unicorns as adults, most humans cling to our figurative unicorns; the myths around which our social consensus is constructed. There’s nothing wrong with looking through such lenses, but there’s a time and a place to change them...

…With the Simplest Changes

For some of you, winter break couldn’t have come any sooner. The content in this class was a sledgehammer and you are going to be happy to get far away from it and back in the warm cocoon of Christmas and conventional societal distractions. And. That. Is. OK. Normal even. (For us too).

You can still hold a positive view of the future, get a good job, stay in mainstream society, and ensconce yourself in the excitement and fun that 20-somethings usually do. But while still wearing the happy pink tinted lenses, you can remain ‘woke’ to the issues raised in this class. You can’t help but know about energy, behavior and global ecosystems at a deeper level than most of those around you. You have a huge advantage in life because you understand these things, but that doesn’t mean you have to think about them 24/7. History tends to roll ahead slowly in our subjective experience. People who were kids during the “great depression” mostly don’t remember any great depression, because everything was normal to them, and life was

dealt with a day at a time. It will for the most part be that way going forward, with the “Great Simplification” still invisible to most people, though you’ll see it as the mover behind events.

When you find people who are open to discussing and learning about these issues, engage with them, befriend them. When, one day in the not too distant (but perhaps plenty distant-feeling to a 19 year old) future, the Great Simplification begins to more obviously unfold, perhaps you’ll have had time to process the content in this class, and in this essay, at a more personal level and will have thought about productively engaging with it in some way. Perhaps at that time you can emerge as a ‘sleeper’ and contribute at a time when the world needs your knowledge. In the meantime, you might consider being a closet ambassador for sanity on some of the core issues raised in class. You now know the drivers and constraints that your modern stone-age mind possesses. Me vs Us, Us vs Them, belief, habituation, focus on the present, empathy, creativity, knowledge-of-what-has-come-before, etc. As Paul Ehrlich mentioned in class, human culture and reaction to events has and WILL change very fast in future – how it changes and where it settles is an emergent property of the people dancing at the time when the music is temporarily halted. Do dance moves that accentuate the brighter side of our natures. The world has changed with less. In an effort to be human, BE REALLY HUMAN in the best sense. Then

dance to the various tunes that life will present.

Seeing the World through the Energy/Economy lens:

But there are many other tints of glasses (other than pink) that were handed out in this class. Here’s an overview of how you might view your future and the human economy with your new ‘energy’ lenses. \*\*Recognize the Surplus around you. Whether you think in terms of EROEI (or prefer TaaL’s term ‘Derr’), seeing aspects of our world as products of energy surplus can help inform decisions about what may prove viable in your own life, your community, or your country and world. (As well as what might not be viable). We are living during a period of enormous energy surplus. As we allocate a greater percentage of our economy towards energy procurement, this implies smaller surplus available for marginal pursuits. Allocating energy (and time) towards its best use will be important.

\*\*Use energy foresight – As we recall from the milk/technology example, higher energy costs don’t adversely impact low- and intermediate-power situations all that much. But processes that have high energy intensity (fully automated milking in above graphic) are extremely sensitive to higher energy costs. In a similar way, your personal universe will be sensitive to higher energy costs in coming decades based largely upon how energy-dependent your lifestyle is. Large homes or vehicles, long commutes, long-distance relationships (unless you embrace intermittent embraces), and complex energy-intensive gadgets and infrastructure will all require more energy (and in turn, more money) than simpler, more flexible arrangements. Avoid highly energy-intensive lifestyles and behaviors and you will tend to avoid energy-related consequences.

\*\*Notice and invest in Real Capital - The first thing that Reality glasses will highlight is that money is just a marker for many of the things you really care about. The financialization of the human experience in the last 70-80 years or so has obscured this fact so it’s not always visible. On your journey to redefine what

‘wealth’ and ‘success’ mean to you, think about creating stronger tethers to natural, built, social and human capital. And invest time and resources accordingly. \*\*Recognize and minimize meaningless consumption – Once you internalize that the “wanting is a more intense sensation than the having”, this gives you great power (in theory) over your purchases and consumption profile. \*\*Towards TRUE FREEDOM: If you have the discipline, try a goal-oriented voluntary simplicity, with a far lower consumptive footprint. The benefit of this ISN’T that you consume less, that’s an aesthetic consideration which ultimately makes little difference while our current economic system remains the same. Thus, in and of itself, it’s just a lifestyle choice. The real potential benefit is that you can – in principle - use the money you save thereby to fund your own ongoing primary career as a self-directed steerer of events, or do anything else that has meaning to you. Most people live near the limit of their means because that’s the social signal they get from those around them, and a way to signal status and inclusion. Yet there’s really no reason to do so. Most people, by the time they’re 30, are well-enmeshed in a hierarchy of financial and time commitments which preclude much freedom of action. This means they need to worry about keeping a job they may not like, doing right by their 2.5 kids, waking before the sun rises to commute in traffic, and be constantly afraid of losing what they have. Such folks will have little personal flexibility as the economy begins its 200-year process of simplification, and few moments to spare for thinking of issues outside their own crisis management. However, for those who learn to live and be happy on absolute wealth, those times needn’t mean a decline in lifestyle. This course of action is available to pretty much anyone in America; you don’t need to be born rich, have connections, or be brilliant. You don’t need to wait for some windfall or lucky break. You just need to teach yourself a bit of discipline and not allow your hedonic ratchet to by hijacked by social imperatives which aren’t imperative at all... they only “feel that way” due to your evolved programming. \*\*Don’t just stand there – be useful. In the future you will have a choice to be active or passive with events. If you choose to, your knowledge of systems and synthesis can make you invaluable to those around you. You can help them learn to prepare for the long energy downturn and its accompanying fiscal storm clouds. You can learn skills which will have pragmatic value to your family, neighborhood, and town. And you will likely understand what’s going on better than those around you. A “Reality MacGyver”. Pick some skills you already have and refine them – whether its cooking or “small engine repair” or music or computer programming or being a good listener. The point is to actively be useful, as opposed to not. \*\*Localize and regionalize supply chains – the future will be a lot more local, as globalization driven by essentially-free energy progressively slows down. As the

world becomes a much larger place again, national and regional economies will increasingly disengage with far-flung trade. If this is planned for, it needn’t be a problem. If it isn’t, there will some very rough adjustments. Having shorter (and lower-carbon) horizontal and vertical supply chains will be the needed trend. \*\*Help create cross-generational technology – not stimulating gadgets – We’re near the all-time peak of net energy use – meaning that we still have great ability to make the energy downslope less traumatic for your generation and those to come. We can invest energy to terrace fields for non-mechanized farming over the coming thousand years, can make dwellings which don’t have to be rebuilt every 60 years and which passively retain heat in the winter and coolness in the summer. We can rebuild canals, build out low-energy electrified railroad networks. We can build the low-energy infrastructure we’ll need. But our societies currently aren’t doing that, because those societies are effectively delusional about energy and most other things, and expect our current wealth and “growth” trajectory to continue increasing indefinitely. We need to shed the fantasy of the “future rich” and realize that the people of the future – including our older selves and family members – will not have the many invisible energy slaves we now take for granted, or for that matter the human wage slaves on the other side of the world who are now making most of our stuff, delivered to us by nearly free energy. The decisions made, and actions taken, during your productive lives will make a huge difference in what future generations experience. \*\*Become more flexible and adaptable –In a world of declining surplus and uncertainty, reducing the complexity in your own life probably makes you more adaptable to change. This goes beyond energy and monetary changes – and crosses over into the psychological aspects – consider a NTROPI (neutrotransmitter return on Personal investment). Simpler, for many reasons, is probably better.

Get to know an “energy slave”

As we’ve discussed, energy is mysterious thing, more a quality that stuff has rather than being “stuff” itself. The essentially invisible nature of energy has made it an easy fit for one of humanity’s ubiquitous blind spots, which is why energy illiteracy is the norm even among high-status highly-educated people. If you think about it, the inability to comprehend energy and its ramifications is behind most human confusion about what the future holds. For instance: baseload power. That’s the current societal distribution system, supplying essentially unlimited amounts of electricity to any individual at any time, on whim. The main reason that things like “solar PV panels” can’t fully power today’s civilization is that they produce power when the sun shines, and with a high degree of

intermittence. They may produce a lot of power on Tuesday, but only a fourth as much on a cloudy Thursday two days later. This would seem to imply that humans will always need to burn fossils, or to build humongous energy-storing batteries somehow, because how else can baseload be maintained? Well of course there’s a basic fallacy in play there. While some things, like industrial production of solar panels, do need uninterrupted power for some parts of the process, we really don’t need huge baseload to exist as humans. Heck, we don’t inherently need exosomatic energy at all. Once we have well-designed houses and clothing and blankets and stuff, our actual needs for wattage are pretty minimal. High energy draws are in many cases a “supernormal” stimulus we’ve gotten used to, like watching it snow from a steaming back-yard hot tub in the dead of winter. That’s a fun thing to do, but it’s hardly an absolute constraint we should design our energy systems around. Have you ever spent a month camping, with only basic food? What you find is that a healthy human can maintain a high quality of life without baseload power, without luxury food, without the internet, and without any real-time fossil slave magic needed. Indeed, many people who spend a month that way later consider it to be some of the best time of their lives. Intermittent access to food, water, electricity, communications and commerce is what we’re evolved for. Moreover, having re-set one’s hedonic ratchet with a month in nature, one can then deeply appreciate simple luxuries like clean flowing tap water, a shower, a good rocking chair, a cherry pie, or a party with friends. The fact that we don’t need the stuff we currently demand, in order to be happy and healthy is the best news there is. It means that while the net energy our societies have will be dropping for the rest of your lives and long after, this doesn’t mean that quality of life can’t improve at the same time. So how best to come to some sort of reasonable balance with the lowered number of energy slaves in your future? We recommend that you start by getting to know one up close and personal.

Conjuring up a personal energy slave

Here’s the deal: you get the most benefits of electricity from your first hundred watts. After that, it’s all rapidly-declining returns. The difference between “no electricity” and 100 watts is huge: potentially communications, lighting, cooking, refrigeration, and much else. The difference between 10,000w and 10,100w is essentially negligible in terms of an individual’s life-quality. The nature of energy use in today’s society is that we can use 10,000 watts of electricity without seeing or otherwise experiencing the heat and combustion products necessary to supply it to us, which might be 30,000w or more in combustion energy from burning fossils. Yet our “hedonic ratchets” are set by having high energy consumption for marginal increases in subjective convenience. Our cultural obtuseness about energy means that we don’t even think about the fact that – since it’s finite - we’re depriving future people of their basic 100 watts, as well as the climate and ecosystem which they would have been able to depend on. Here’s our recommendation: build yourself a small solar energy slave system and get to know its personality, what it can and can’t do, what it costs, and how it works. If everyone did this, we would take a large step forward in energy literacy. Moreover, it could provide not only skills but a useful degree of resilience which could come in handy going forward. And it’s fun, really. You can do it with a single solar panel, say 100 to 200 watts, mounted in your yard, on your roof, on your doghouse, or anywhere the sun will hit it directly. You won’t need permits because it won’t be wired into the grid. To that you’ll add a cheap charge controller and a 12v battery, and a small inverter to make that into AC power. The budget will be a few hundred dollars. The result will be a nice little energy slave which, when the sun in shining, can kick out as much energy as the legs of a human athlete, without getting tired.

With such a system you can charge your electronics, run a stereo system, cook (within reason, no turkeys) with a microwave oven, run a small refrigerator, pump water, power a fan to cool you on a hot day, or do anything else with modest energy requirements which comes to mind – just not all at once. And if the grid goes down, your system will still work, unlike all those houses you see covered with grid-tied solar panels, which go down when the grid does. Those people will be coming to YOUR house to recharge their phones! A system like this can be a good primer in thinking about energy flows, because you probably can’t run the microwave and the freezer at the same time. Then again, a freezer has thermal inertia, and will stay cool just fine when powered intermittently, even overnight if it is well-insulated. LED lighting doesn’t use much power, and won’t drain your battery too far at night. Modern computers don’t have to use much power... this essay is being created with offgrid power from a very similar system (only with a few more panels) running a mac mini computer on a large low-wattage display, with no energy storage between the surface of the sun and the written word. However, you do have to be conscious of whether the sun is shining, the state of charge of your battery, and what things are “on” at any given time. It’s less automatic. Whether that makes it a pain in the butt, or more fun, is an individual choice. But clearly, it’s possible. Baseload is not an inherent human need. Once one has picked up a few tips on lower-energy lifestyles – and has gotten to know a small energy slave’s personality – intermittent “renewables” can be seen for what they are; a miraculously cool thing, and what they’re not: a way to keep baseload happening indefinitely. To our ancestors, 100 watts of power would have been like a genie in a lamp, granting many wishes every day. If we have fewer energy slaves going forward, and our kids and grandkids have fewer still, that doesn’t mean they’ll be doing badly. It may just mean less energy wasted. And they may appreciate it if we start practicing energy moderation before then, because you can’t run an air conditioner on 100 watts, even if

you need one.

Your Pleistocene Lenses – Turning Brain Bugs Into Features

We evolved. And just like our eyes, muscles and feet are products of evolution, so are our thought processes, mental modules and prepared social learning. If you understand only this about yourself and others, you have more insight into human-human interactions than the majority of our species. But can you use this to your advantage? In the past, humans have had little to work with but the subjective experience of existence: our brains came without user manuals, and what it “feels like” is going on is often nothing like what’s really going on inside them. It feels like our virtual worlds are a seamless representation of the world we perceive around us: in fact, our brains assemble

an imaginary “user interface” for the physical universe out of whole cloth, with no more absolute correspondence to physical reality than the icons on a computer screen have the billions of microscopic transistors, electron flows and magnetic fields we are interacting with when we use a computer. These virtual worlds have humongous “blind spots” which evolved to be a good fit for “savannah-salience”, the things which worked best for a tribal primate in Africa, and which defaults we still inherit. It’s only in very recent years that we have understood the way modular mind has evolved to function in humans. Every human mind consists of more recent layers added over ancient layers, and all these contain specialized modules running constantly and without subjective effort in the service of special functions and purposes. The deep/stupid layers like the limbic system are the root of all our most powerful feelings, and of behavioral rewards and punishments for hewing to or departing from the genes’ agenda, while the more recent layers are involved with socialization and the manipulation of abstraction. What we each seem to experience is something like being a small conscious “pilot” riding somewhere behind our eyes, making the executive decisions for the body and person. That’s not what happens, though. There is no such unitary executive ‘self’;

rather, that’s part of the (relatively) seamless illusion. In fact, there are thousands of voices clamoring in any given head, but only a few systems which can interact with the outside world. Ancient modules may no longer have positive survival value, but they retain their deep connections to motivation and feelings which the conscious mind inherently lacks. The left cerebral hemisphere is the only one which can talk, but without the input of the separate individual in the right hemisphere, many of its perceptions would be altered. We evolved a self-regarding module, and a related “public relations” module, which give us the ability to think of ourselves as though “seen from outside” and compete more effectively as social beings. As it normally functions, this system creates and continually updates a “personal storyline” of ad hoc confabulation to explain our decisions and actions. It’s “ad hoc confabulation” because the “conscious” module has no access to understand why the other brain modules – some of them a lot more powerful, others more deeply connected – made a given decision. Yet our experience is of unitary consciousness and a seamless perception of world. This illusion is so robust that one doesn’t even notice if the link between cerebral hemispheres is surgically severed, much like the blind spots in our fields of vision where the optic nerve connects to the optic disk. Do you see blind spots in your field of vision? They’re there, but the illusion is that they aren’t. Indeed, your eye only sees a tiny part of the scene in front of you, the rest is assembled in memory from tracking samples. In such a way, our entire experience of conscious existence is separated from the physical world by huge blind spots which made no difference to a tribal hunter, but could matter hugely to a world-controlling species. We need to learn, in the words of a prominent brain researcher, to take our perceptions of the world seriously but not literally. Evolution got us to where we now are. It’s an irony of reality that our evolved minds must now learn to detach in some ways from their evolved default feelings if we are to continue the experiment of human civilization and science. Indeed, it’s our evolved defaults which have put us into a series of nested predicaments which require extraordinarily fast cultural evolution to not destroy our environment, our cohort species, and ourselves.

Getting to know your Modular Mind, and Strategies to make the best use of it

“Intra-skull realpolitik” refers to the development and learning of new sorts of self-awareness, methods, and techniques to take the evolved brain you were born with and bend it into the service of rationality, ethics, and logic as consciously decided by the formerly-subservient “self-aware” social modules; while retaining the deeply-meaningful non-rational aspects which make human lives worth living. This is necessary because “following our feelings” is what got us into various messes. In our current situation, it’s a recipe

to be overweight, overpopulated, over-stimulated, overmedicated, and passive in the face of dire situations which don’t directly trigger the strong motivating emotions of the limbic system. It feels good to do what feels good. That’s a truism as well as a tautology, but it has sufficed as a core philosophy for many people. It would seem the corollary to that is that departing from “feel good” brain rewards will make us feel bad, but that’s only true in the short term, because we can learn to change the things we get brain rewards from, bringing our other modules and the deep stupid powerful systems of our core brains in line with our conscious preference. Your authors will be speaking here mostly from personal experience, but we expect this subject with a funny name to have a bright future, if humans are to have much of a future at all.

Social points or environmental stability?

Earlier in the semester we learned about how our brains are products of conserved ‘circuitry’ which contributed to our historical survival. Modern brains now have a ‘neo-cortex’ which can process facts, create science and intuit abstractions about our future. But these thoughts and feelings – arising as they do from a relatively new part of the brain - are not as intense as the routines of resource acquisition, social rank, and short term focus whose time-worn ruts were present from the beginning.

A case in point. Everyone in this class is worried about the long-term effects of CO2 and pollution on the oceans and our ecosystems. As new information comes to light we are eager to share it with our friends, families and colleagues – ‘Look at this! Something isn’t right about polar temperatures this month! We need to change our behavior!’ (expecting of course that OTHERS will be the ones to change). When it turns out to be a false alarm, and that our environmental situation – at least for the day – gets a reprieve, those who have shared the scary information widely feel shame and embarrassment for having called attention to something extreme that didn’t occur. This is a perfect example of modular minds in action. Our social status module gives us a much more intense reaction than ‘the polar ice cap is melting while we are alive’ module. To any other of the 7.5 billion humans alive and any number of billions that will live in the future, one person’s social status alteration from being right or wrong is completely irrelevant next to the monumental changes underway in our natural world – but to the one individual in question, it doesn’t ‘feel’ that way. Different parts of the brain – wanting to be right – and caring for the environment – both active simultaneously (a majority of French citizens currently support both reducing CO2 emissions and the Diesel riot protests). Odd combinations like this, that make us ‘root’ in different directions, occur every day. What’s going on here?

Embrace your modular mind

So as long as we’re stuck with having triune brains with evolved modularity, we might as well make the best of it. Get to know them and coexist peacefully, figure out which systems should never get their hands on the metaphorical car keys, and deal with the uncomfortable fact that our stupidest and most primitive modules – those we share with reptiles - have the greatest influence on our emotions and motivations, while our more rational impulses have no direct line to emotions or motivations. To realize that the brain’s “behavioral rewards and punishments” such as neurotransmitters are doled out to reward a brain for doing things which may no longer, in the 21st century, make any sense. To recognize that a helluva lot of physical reality, and its very nature, is invisible and counterintuitive to us but will determine the fate of humanity’s future.

Realpolitik: A term to describe or prescribe politics based on strictly practical rather than idealistic notions, and practiced without any sentimental illusions

We refer to this process of coming to an accord with our internal entities as “realpolitik” because we are unable to overrule the brain’s defaults by sheer force of will. The conscious/rational subsystems of the mind will lose that contest in the short term every time. Rather, one can engage in a gradual process to bring them into line with one’s ethics and scientific/rational awareness, which is more like congressional lobbying or wild-animal training than it is like using “will power”. The result can be worthwhile: it’s possible to train your brain to “feel good” about things it didn’t originally “feel good” about, and “be averse to” things it formerly had no interest in. One can also enlist the connections to the deep mind for motivation, bravery, and dedication to your chosen goals, while continuing to enjoy the emotions which have little to do with logic and science - like pride, love, meaning, self-respect, feelings of reverence and spirituality, and joy - without having them conflict with your rational agenda. Since this is an unnatural thing to do as far as a brain is concerned – like learning to play a harpsichord – it’s gradual and subject to continuing improvement throughout a lifetime. But knowing the way your brain actually works is an advantage people never had in the past, and should help with insights.

“Go down into the crater of Sneffels which the shadow of Scartaris touched before July. Then you'll reach the centre of the Earth. I have done this. - Arne Saknussemm." ‘Journey to the Center of the Earth’ by Jules Verne Making oneself useful to the future – your own or the world’s - involves becoming at least quasi-sapient, and this requires self-awareness far beyond the simple test of mirror self-recognition which today tests for that basic benchmark. One needs to be

self-aware of the way their mind works, and learn to experience those modular parts working in different ways. That is, to see oneself not as others see us, but as others can never see us. There are deep parts of the brain which are powerful, simple, and stupid. These can give you great power of will or take it away from you. They exist on their own terms and follow their own skewed logic, wild animals you can never domesticate, but which you can come to an accord with, the way you might if you had to share a cave with a grizzly bear, several wolverines, and several dozen screaming monkeys, all deranged in such a way to be unaware of one another’s existence - only of yours - yet still crowding one another. You need to throw raw meat to some, learn to scratch the others in the way they want scratched, learn the soothing things to calm the monkey screams, so as the weakest being in the cave you eventually emerge the dominant arbiter of the cave’s dynamic, leveraging the strengths of each off the others. This is possible because none of the others actually have goals to do so, they just want what they want and are not even minimally self-aware. But their imprinting about “what they want” is terribly powerful, and almost impossible to directly resist. This means being a bit Machiavellian with them, teaching them gradually to eat and lust after different things which you provide, a semblance of their original desires packaged in a different way. Without them in your corner, you may blog a good game but you won’t do much else. It’s not easy, but like climbing a mountain it’s hardest before you’re sure it can be done. Once you realize others have done it, it’s like booking a sherpa to help climb a mountain with known trails. Of course, the standard human condition - in this metaphor - doesn’t even see the mountain, much less the trails. At the very least, this process takes a lot of time alone, isolated from other humans, and a great and steady desire to tease apart the inner personalities and what they need. It’s an ongoing process, and your entire life you’re still working with a cave full of beasts which are in no sense tame, but which gradually learn that following the rationale agenda is the only way they are allowed to eat. Maybe not eat what they would prefer, but eat.

Avoiding Denihilism

Once one realizes that “this isn’t really happening” and “there’s nothing which can be done” are dissonance-quashing and effort-avoidance routines built into a human brain, and are not rational conclusions or products of logic, one can avoid them by just refusing to indulge in them (and

“indulge” is the proper term), but there’s a lot of self-reflection which goes into the ability to “just say no” to such basic routines. Of course denial and hopelessness seem like opposites but they really aren’t: they’re peas in a pod, both serving exactly the same end of rationalizing inaction. That’s why one of the best ways not to fall victim to them – and many other such pre-programmed routines – is to spend some time owning “who you are”, and to be always aware that you are a brain (and body) doing something different than what it evolved to do. The measure of your individuality is the extent to which you can take what you were born with and do something extraordinary with it. It’s hard to avoid dissonance-reducing rationalizations without being aware of what they are and how they function in one’s mind. Thus, there exist a number of quite intelligent science-based people who find pleasure and social status/fulfillment in blogging about the futility of trying to alter future planetary outcomes. This means that not only are they not trying to do anything useful to steer future outcomes, but they’re actively evangelizing the very monkey trap they’ve fallen into, becoming a part of the problem and making things worse. Of course, they are able to rationalize to themselves that since the situation can’t GET any worse, they are doing no harm. Once you go down that path, it’s like eating the mythical lotus.
