One can grab an illusion of moral high ground and get social stimulation and status without doing anything difficult or uncomfortable, functionally equivalent to those who deny the same problems exist at all. Ironically, many such folks profess abhorrence of the situation they are helping “lock in”, but they have abandoned rational attempts to direct their minds; their urges predominate and they learn to enjoy their tapeworm-like social niche. If one sees denial and hopelessness/nihilism as two sides of the same coin - two faces of the same dissonance-reducing heuristic - they can be avoided along with the other

“gotcha” biases and fallacies which can make an otherwise rational mind worse than useless. But those who fall into this trap become an “attractive hazard” which can pull in others.

Creative Personal Abhorrence and Delight

In the previous section, we mention the term “creative abhorrence”, which – as you might expect – is another term we’ve created as a part of the new language which becomes necessary to deal with hacking a caveman brain to be effective in a changed world. It’s a minor tactic in the scheme of such things, but is one which is fairly easy to describe. So let’s unpack it just a bit more here: It can be a struggle to contend with the gene agenda successfully, and consciously do something important for the future, whatever that may be. Our gene agenda isn’t made for the future, for other people, or for other species. It was designed by actions taken in past “nows”, which just happened to increase reproductive odds in the contexts when then prevailed. Still, that doesn’t mean it’s impossible to do, or even all that difficult. The problem arises when you repeatedly conduct an arduous decision-making process, weighing the pro’s and con’s over and over again. If you repeatedly roll the dice of “persevere” versus “give up”, it becomes inevitable that you’ll eventually decide to give up. Las Vegas skyscrapers and successful insurance companies have been built because of that entirely predictable “aggregate probability” outcome. This is a familiar experience to dieters. If there is a carton of Haagen-Dazs in the refrigerator, then every time that door is opened, there’s a decision tree on whether to “just have one spoon” or not. Even the strongest-willed person is going to have a

moment in which they’re feeling sorry for themselves, low blood sugar, weak willed…. and that ice cream disappears in a frenzy of ecstasy and quick regret. The easy fix, of course, is simply not to have the ice cream available, so those “Chunky Monkey” or “No Chunky Monkey” dice don’t get rolled so often. (That’s a good reason never to grocery shop while hungry.) A person may agonize for years over whether to eat meat or to be a vegetarian, however, once that decision is made, future decision trees are greatly simplified. A person who self-identifies as a vegetarian does not re-engage the tortuous decision process each time they go to a restaurant or a supermarket. Rather, they have chosen to limit their spectrum of choices through the effective use of negative associations, which constitutes sort of a “learned invisibility” regarding those things, a self-made perceptual blind spot. When this is done ahead of time, with a plan and strategy in mind, your authors refer to it as “creative abhorrence”. If you ask a vegetarian if they want a bite of steak, they will generally show a look of distaste on their face, because the easiest way of converting to vegetarianism is to train yourself to be disgusted by the thought of eating flesh. And you’ve experienced it too. What do you think about cannibalism? Nothing predatory, just eating some person who died accidentally, and whose wallet donor card instructs that his body not go to waste. Presumably, just now you didn’t go through a long checklist of pro’s and cons of whether to chow down on this hypothetical guy’s mortal remains. You immediately thought YUCK, and that was it. In fact, human flesh is reputed to be quite tasty, but there’s a general cultural agreement that it’s disgusting to eat “long pig”, and this disgust (which you’re probably feeling right now) is so effective that in most parts of the world there are no laws against cannibalism. They’re not needed, because abhorrence is such a reliable inhibitor to human behavior. A negative feedback speedbump thrown into the positive feedback human dynamic of consumption. Such speed bumps – and catalysts - of human behavior can be useful when negotiating reality with a primitive, modular mind. “Creative abhorrence” is one useful way of exercising “free won’t” in reprogramming ones own behavior to fall in line with the agenda of who you choose to be, rather than the generally more despicable and moronic default agenda of the gene. The rationale is the removal of the constant “roll of the dice” on many daily behavioral decisions which cause most people to take an easy, boring, self-indulgent and ultimately self-imprisoning path through life.

Lest this sort of tactic sound “abhorrent” to you in and of itself, we’ll note that any “abhorrence” quickly becomes entirely subliminal. You don’t actually visualize yourself eating some other human when you think of cannibalism; you just don’t “go there” in your mind and can rely on the “deep associations” of your non-rational core brain to enforce such a rule without your thinking about it, so there’s no stress and no temptation. This is the case for many vegetarians as well. In addition, one can also establish “delight” associations and train them in much the same way, such as the delight at saving a wild creature’s life, the delight at preventing waste, the delight of scientific accomplishment, and many other things your brain is not programmed by default to feel good about. Your brain has evolved defaults, but is somewhat plastic: you can shape its future decisions, even its future snap decisions, by consciously laying the groundwork. As noted, the first thing we recommend after integrating Reality (and maybe taking some time off to recover from Finals) is deciding Who You Are. Not defined by those around you, but defined by yourself, based on YOUR choice of ethics, the things YOU have chosen to treat as sacred, and the life which, if lived, will in old age cause you to look back and justly admire the life you have lived. This can be, and perhaps should be, a secret you never share, or seldom share. A self-designed “this is who I am” is a precious gift of self-aware mind, and yours for the taking. Moreover, once you decide who you are, what that person would ignore and what he/she would engage, what to stand for and stand against, you will find that few difficult decisions will arise in life, because the “who am I” answers them for you; being true to your own principles will steer you on the path you’ve decided upon, even if the exact path is by its nature not yet clear.

And beyond....

After that, there are many strategies for training your mind in intra-cranial realpolitik to come into line with this “who am I”, and those ways would fill a book. They include learning to recognize your own mind-modularity and the multiple personalities represented by those modules; getting to know the “invisible” parts of your mind by the suggestions they deliver; deconstructing your “PR” module’s confabulated and rationalized storyline, throwing raw meat to the limbic system to bring its deep psychological power in line with your consciously-set goals, and much else. But the creative use of abhorrence is one useful general strategy, and what we’re discussing at the moment. As a counter-example, if you decided that you personally wanted to conserve energy as an example to others - even knowing that global Jevons effects would likely moot

most actual energy savings - you could fill yourself with guilt and angst over everything you didn’t do perfectly… and face it, there would be a lot of things you didn’t do in an ideal way. Add a few more socially-conscious angst-sources like “buying american” “not using the F word”, etc. and you could wind up walking around all day in a continuing state of low-level free-floating guilt and angst for not being more perfect in following your own principles. This, in turn, can lead to lowered self-esteem and a decision to just quit trying. The problem here is that such a person is “rolling the dice” within the context of a dissonant decision matrix every time it comes up, rather than restricting future actions with the creative use of positive and negative associations, which your brain is designed to do quite readily. Have you ever eaten a food while feeling nauseated? You will be unlikely to intentionally eat that food again. Conversely, if you eat a new food while you’re famished, you’re likely to seek it out in the future. Such associations are made constantly in the human brain, and it’s up to us whether that reality is going to be a “bug or a feature” in terms of your quest for sapience. If, for instance, you simply trained yourself to associate any unnecessary energy use with the smell of car exhaust, the image of polluting coal chimneys, the pollution of formerly-pristine waterways, etc. then there is a mild distaste which can be attached to any wasteful use of energy. Not a raging guilt, but a slight counterbalancing negative to go along with the positive stimulus which comes from the energy use. This would cause you to experience relief when replacing an incandescent bulb with a low-wattage one, or just to remember to turn out the lights when leaving a room. Such creative abhorrence, planned for ahead of time, can be applied to any number of situations, habits and aspirations, and it can paradoxically make your life more pleasant because it offloads “cognitive load” issues into the unconscious and effortless “association circuits” within your brain.

A side example from DJ:

Because I decided to include similar associations in my “who am I”, I ultimately found it to be rewarding to arrange my driving so I only go to town once per week, and to modify other activities in similar ways. This doesn’t engender angst but some private satisfaction, because it turns out that by doing so I miss out on nothing but traffic and save a ton of time. It means I drive less than 1000 miles per year, giving my old Honda accord better effective energy-efficiency than a moped: gasoline price rises don’t directly affect me. By replacing “impulsive trips to town” with a pre-planned schedule, everything still gets done and I have more time to do productive stuff. In a similar way, my “trained abhorrence of waste” also led to an enjoyable hobby in which I do most exosomatic-energy-using tasks when the sun shines and I can use energy via generation of non-stored PV electricity matching my usage with the natural cycle of solar energy rather than expecting the grid or batteries to wastefully compensate. With many people, this would be done for social reasons, I suppose. In my case, very few people know about it and I’m writing about it here for the first time on request; because it isn’t about impressing anyone, it’s about the “who am I”. It’s aesthetically pleasing to me to use less energy, to pollute somewhat less, and do a number of other things differently than those around me, not because it saves much energy on a societal level - which is debatable - but because it simply feels better to me. That sort of mental redesign has real staying power, and utterly avoids guilt, indecision, and stress which might otherwise be associated with what could “feel like” a sacrifice. There is no “cognitive load” addition the way there is, for instance, when going on a temporary diet. Because being true to who you’ve decided you are isn’t stressful, it’s stress-relieving.

Modular Transtheism

If there’s an electrified “third rail” in discussions of human mind and future, it’s religion. Since this is a science-synthesis course, we’ve mentioned it in its correct context - a class of unifying belief systems which provide some societal and personal benefits entirely divorced from the truth or plausibility of the central stories themselves. This always loses some readers, because religion has long co-evolved with human mind. Just as our genomes consist partially of bacterial and viral sequences which inserted itself into our DNA long ago, our minds have evolved in parallel with shared mythological belief systems which are still the dominant force in human culture, and the prime conscious rationale (and rationalization) for following and channeling the “feelings” generated by the core brain. There are clearly some downsides to this; a person who believes that the physical world is explained by the premise that the creator of the universe wrote an infallible

book, and “here it is”, is pretty well armored against a scientific viewpoint if he or she cares to be. For instance, the bible says that God promises the earth will never flood again, so that means CO2 cannot make the seas rise. End of story. This utterly negates the fact that geology shows that there never was a global flood, and that the basic science of CO2 and ice has been understood since the 1800’s. This sort of “shrugging off” of science is not practiced by all religious people, but by many you know of (and that we know of). It might be surprising, therefore, to realize that the authors of these materials are not “down on religion” per se. Religion – or more specifically –‘religiosity’ (the numerous aspects of religious activity, dedication to ideas, and beliefs) is one of the most powerful forces which has ever existed to motivate humans, in large part because it transcends logic and speaks to the deep feelings of the powerful ancient brain-layers. If you had a religion which imparted deep feelings, motivation, kindness, and sane environmental stewardship without the outright-crazy bits like talking snakes, arks, living inside whales and similar erroneous physical-world stuff, you’d really be onto something. And in fact, many people are using “modular mind” for religion without actually copping to it or even knowing it exists. All of us know people who both believe in a large standardized religion with some crazy stuff in it, but who also function as moral and rational people with a decent understanding of science. Modular mind is what makes this possible: they don’t just believe one or the other, they believe both, selected by the context they find themselves in at a given time. While such picking and choosing is inconsistent, such inconsistent standards are built in to human minds, and seldom reconciled. Such terms as “self deception” and “in denial” predate the modern understanding of modular mind, by erroneously assuming there’s only a single “real” mind “in there” which holds one belief or another. When in truth, one module may be a Baptist, another a scientific rationalist, and still another a Zen Buddhist, all happily living in the same skull because only one gains ascendance at any given time during the day. There are many cultures in which Christianity coexists with voodoo, with paganism, with science, and presumably other beliefs like leprechauns or Menehune’s. Nobody makes that big a deal about this… that, for instance, in the Philippines a large portion of the Catholics also believe in black magic and curses. These “contradictory beliefs simultaneously held” are exactly how mind modularity works. A reason that atheists are hated is that they insist on reconciling the modules logically, which is exactly the sort of dissonance that human minds hate. Conversely, a person of Religion B is still a “person of faith” and not disliked even if a person from Religion A thinks they are as wrong about their beliefs as an atheist is. The biggest accusation that believers make

against atheists is that they’re “untrustworthy”, which probably derives from an atheist’s lack of belief in the inevitability of divine punishment for sins. This breaks the “belief in belief” pact which allows people to coexist with large numbers of strangers they don’t know. You don’t care so much that the other person believes in your exact sect, but you do care that they believe in a version in which they get eternal punishment in some nasty place if they do terrible things to you. The point is, modular religion already exists and is widespread, it’s just not acknowledged as “a thing” yet, and so exists in limbo. So is “modular religion” sacrilege? Not necessarily. No less an authority than Jesus is said to have opined on the question of metaphysical versus physical issues. Physical coins for paying taxes are a physical world reality, and one should “render unto Caesar what is Caesar’s, render unto God what is God’s”. That is, deal with physical realities like Roman tax collectors pragmatically without expecting miraculous intervention, and deal with spiritual things in a spiritual way. That Jesus (reportedly) didn’t see this as a conflict with what he was saying is a good example of functional modularity, the pragmatic schism between virtual and physical worlds. Thus, in addition to atheist-Christians, we could have some powerful mind combinations: activist-Buddhists, ExxonMobil-climate activists and so on. Once we embrace our modular minds, lots of pathways that are currently closed to use because they cause dissonance and angst, open up. Homo sapiens could use such tools.

So what does this imply, going forward?

Humans evolved to believe multiple mutually-contradictory things at once. If we decide to “own” that and admit it to others and ourselves, it could be liberating, a next step in self-awareness. Indeed, it may be high time to define self-awareness as a continuum rather than a single threshold. So let’s say we, as authors of these projects, were to endorse religion, even while saying that we don’t subscribe to any particular religion and that we recommend using only science and life-ethics for making physical-world decisions. Those ethics, in turn, would derive non-mythologically from simple statements about what is preferable in 4D time to conscious living beings within ecologies. Prayer would be for personal strength and comfort, not the expectation of altering physical-world probability or outcomes. We would urge personal repudiation of the gene agenda. That would have a number of aspects, but one of them would be to choose a religion and keep it in the virtual world of magic and emotions. The pragmatic reasons for having a religion are similar to the reasons for uploading a “patch” to a glitchy computer operating system: to help it work better within the

current class of situations. The subjective reasons are that religion has evolved to fit hand-in-glove with the stable function of human mind, and provide a workable framing for nearly all of our deep feelings. In a way, a religion is a stock user interface for a human brain, to keep it from doing things which will get it in trouble or cause it to not function well. What are the good aspects of religion? There are many: Tying together community, reducing existential angst, stoking the ego, throwing raw meat to various primate-needs for relative status, relative fairness in distribution, deferred reward, assuring punishment of outgroups, providing a free-floating rationale for ingroup altruism and lack of outgroup altruism, breaking dissonance loops on personal mortality, reducing feelings of personal responsibility and obligation, conveying feelings of tribe/family. It provides a context for ritual, for sacred values and feelings. It obviates “why” questions by replacing with “because X wants it”. Without this stuff some of us would be pretty messed-up chimps. The “holy grail” as it were, would seem to be finding a way to achieve these benefits in a way which was not ecologically suicidal to a race of billions of corporeal beings using exosomatic energy; a situation which was far from reality when the religions initially came into being. Religions helped make us very effective primates; too effective for our own good, as it turns out. If we can figure a way to take the best things from religion and apply them to a virtual world, and the best things about science and follow them when dealing with the physical world, Amen.

Absolute versus Relative Consumption

Another tip related to avoiding the consensus monkey trap is is to train yourself to appreciate the absolute, rather than the relative. Did your neighbor get a grape while you got a cucumber slice for the same task? Did he get a sports car while you still drive an old Honda? You could scream, you could attack him, you could resent him,

or you could make yourself unhappy about your relative privation and lower social status. But really, why? Those are all deep-brain defaults which evolved to ensure your survival within a tribe existing at subsistence level on the plains of Africa, in which situation unfair distribution might actually endanger your existence. Those tribes no longer exist, and driving around in a Honda – even if it no longer has “new car smell” - pushed at high speeds in the snow, uphill by uncomplaining fossil slaves is enormous luxury, far more opulent than what was available to royalty during most of human history (and probably most of the human future). So how can you be happy for your neighbor’s good fortune, and happy with your own situation, without becoming a rampaging consumer? Again, this runs afoul of the “gene agenda” defaults, so it doesn’t “feel right” initially, but it really can work. You just need to train yourself to stop worrying about what other people around you have, while training yourself to appreciate what you do have. Instead, try comparing yourself to the average human existence of the roughly 100 billion human lives which have ever been lived, in which your teeth would have been cracked, you’d have carried a fair parasite load, you’d have been at constant risk of attack from predators human and nonhuman, and your prized possession might have been a feather necklace or a flint hand-axe, and a wrap of stinky animal fur to keep you from shivering at night. You’re currently something like 1000x as wealthy as “the historic Joneses”, so it logically follows that being 50x as wealthy probably wouldn’t suck. And 250X-500X wealthy would be quite something indeed. Once one does this, it’s child’s play to do something like living on half a normal american salary; because if your internal neural comparison is not with the consuming apes around you, but with the barefoot shivering apes of most history, it’s easy to do so while vastly appreciating your wealth, even if others might consider you living beneath your means. This doesn’t mean a live of asceticism; one can live a life which is outwardly pretty identical to that of others, just a little bit off the cutting edge. Learn to do your own repair; buy used stuff; learn to cook well and healthily using bulk staples. You’ll be better off, because you’re operating in the range of reward your brain evolved for (well, a fair bit above it, but within reason), which means you’re less likely to need antidepressants or self-medication to feel relatively sane. Yes, it’s a mental trick, but can be done and offers immense benefits with practice. Actively choose who you compare yourself to. And who you don’t. And in addition to neurally, you’ll also be fiscally operating in the steeply ascending part of the reward/cost graph, getting 90% of the pleasure and utility from 20% of the investment. That’s a formula for personal freedom.

What? Personal freedom?

Well sure. Because if you can live on half a normal salary, it means you need no longer be defined by what “job” brings in that salary: you have twice as much money as you need, which means you are NOT dependent upon a rich uncle dying, an itinerant billionaire to take a shine to you, or some granting network to pull your name out of a hat: you can immediately begin a parallel career doing whatever it is that means the most to you. (If this sounds theoretical, it isn’t: it’s exactly the path walked by author DJ.) Moreover, this is scalable: millions of people could, in principle, do it immediately by simply deciding to. It circumvents the Jevons effect which neutralizes individual “lower impact” consumptive decisions, because you’re still spending the money... you’re just spending it on constructive stuff rather than in compulsively feeding your “gene agenda” urges. It’s damn near all upside, just from learning not to care passionately about what those around you are receiving and consuming, and therefore what you’re expected to do to fit in. It really is just that simple.

Make the normal, Super.

We humans live in a ridiculously amazing world right now. Not only are we for the most part not starving, diseased, and exposed to the elements, but we’re surfing the absolute all-time peak waves of cheap exosomatic energy as applied to a virgin planet. This is not such good news for the virgin planet itself, but for humans it means Whole Foods cornucopian shopping, jet air travel anywhere in the world, the internet in your pocket, the ability to have video calls with someone in Mongolia, to have tarantulas, macaws, and border collies as pets. In other words, we can stimulate our brains to transcendental levels. We can jump out of airplanes for fun multiple times per day, breathe a hundred feet underwater while being felt up by a giant octopus and taking selfies, dine on thousand-dollar-a plate sushi from endangered Bluefin tuna, take designer party drugs, and just generally indulge in pleasures beyond traditional dreams of mortal avarice.

Yet chasing supernormal levels of stimulation has a number of downsides. One is, of course, that we’re wrecking the planet and its ecosystems and have kicked off only the 6th major planetary extinction event since complex life began over a half-billion years ago, by exchanging gigatons of burning fossils for microliters of transient brain-chemicals. But even getting beyond the “environmental externality” of perhaps ending human civilization and most large animals in the not-too-distant future, there are downsides for our minds themselves. A key downside is that we quickly habituate to any level of stimulation. That’s one reason drug addiction is a bad thing: your neural system quickly habituates, and requires an ever-higher dose to get the same feelings. Once you reach toxic levels, you can die or go insane, so that’s not good. And pretty much the same mechanism controls all the other supernormal stuff we seek. Our brains evolved to habituate. That’s why millionaires jumped out of windows when the 1929 stock market crashed. It wasn’t a bad day, and they still had their health and a lot of other stuff; but the brain feelings they got from abstract wealth markers (and attendant social status, whim fulfillment, etc) had gone away, so life was suddenly relatively intolerable. This is a salient thing to note as we giddily forge into the 21st century, because as we’ve seen, our exponential grown in the use of energy and materials carries with it the inevitability of that growth ending, and starting a multi-century roller-coaster ride back to sustainable solar and resource flows. Just as “more energy and stuff every year” has characterized the last century, “less energy and stuff every year” will be the theme of most of this one and all of the next one, so it would probably be good to figure out a way for that not to be a massive bummer. Inasmuch as we will still be enormously wealthier than the historic human average for your entire life, that should be do-able. We evolved to be capable of happiness, fulfillment, love, accomplishment, meaning, and all that good stuff just from having enough to eat, shelter, and good company. Most of the best times you’ll ever have will be about exactly those things. So how can we manage our brains to “stay civilized” when some of the things to which we feel entitled start slipping away? And might we do this before they slip away, proactively, so we avoid that stress and empower ourselves to make decisions which lead to better futures?

Resetting Your Hedonic Ratchet

They say the first step is admitting you have a problem. Hyperstimulus traps abound in our modern world: junk food, porn, computer games, compulsive tweeting, shopping, status pursuit, etc. When indulged in, they overfeed a powerful inner moron, turning it into an addict with hands on your main controls, amping up its power to shout louder than your rational will and eventually squelching it entirely. Over time, this ‘ghost of dopamine past’ becomes stronger, craftier and shows up so many times during each day he begins to function as your scheduling secretary. So, before one sets out to make changes in the world, it’s good to exert a bit of control in one’s inner house. (Your personal hedonic ratchet is a very small subset of the ‘cave of the mind’ wrestling match, but one accessible even to those who don’t plan to fully train what’s in the cave). The key to the ratchet is that your inner imbeciles are made temporarily whole, and feel rewarded, not by absolutes but by relatives. Some humans have gotten to the point where they are simultaneously shooting heroine and snorting cocaine, having marathon sex with multiple partners, while being adored by the masses and binging on the finest food. It breaks them, because after all this, they have nowhere further to go to feel better than their “new normal”, and either inevitably become damaged and then slowly heal, or pursue a level of stimulation which physically kills them. However, if you understand the nature of your hedonic ratchet and want it to serve you - or at least not hinder you much - it’s best to set a low baseline level of stimulation to leave yourself plenty of dynamic range without straying too far into the hyperstimulus smorgasbord currently feeding the humans around you.

For this, nothing can beat approximating the situation we evolved for… alone in a wilderness with nearly nothing, learning to appreciate the world and then re-introducing, one at a time, things to be immensely grateful for, like shoes, warmth, a little bit of light, basic food, add a friend or two, etc. So that by the time one gets back to a humble roof and bed, there’s a feeling of enormous wealth, and an entirely valid one. Do this enough times and it’s easier to see the supernormal stuff for what it is, a hijacking and an addiction, and to avoid it all in exactly the same way one avoids shooting heroin, by realizing it’s a preposterous high to pursue, even if others are doing it. We advocate this ‘resetting of one’s ratchet’ periodically, both as an aesthetic and to give one larger flexibility once various societal intermittencies become more common. It’s a bit challenging but very rewarding.

We recommend appreciating things, small things, to keep your human brain well-calibrated. There’s wonder all around. Take some time to get to know about a species of bug, the lizard which watches you when you come out of the house, learn to see the fossils and other signs of deep time around you when you take a walk… and be delighted by them. Know the names of the stars and planets that are above you in the sky at night, and let your mind take you to them based on what we now know. Find out the shoe size of that homeless person and bring her a better pair next time you see her. Make such little things important and they will be, and will keep your sense of wonder well-calibrated for the unexpected. Once having done so, such resets may cause you to feel you’re surrounded by addicts, which is but a small piece of the truth. Learning to achieve, and never lose, the feeling of subjective wealth at having the simple things one really needs is powerful, and opens many doors. And it’s not illusory: a human with food, shelter, shoes, water, warmth, a few friends or family and something worth caring about is as wealthy as a human can ever be. The supernormal is a distraction, a drug. It requires a flow of money, which for most people requires that they pursue money rather than direct their own lives. Remember that the Ghost of Dopamine Past - for most of us – will

constantly be by our sides pointing out something that is higher-level stimulation than these slow but wondrous parts of our lives. Feed the tortoise, starve the hare. You’ll see more of the countryside of life. And be moving slow enough to do some things.

Go on electricity, technology, food, alcohol, social media, etc. holidays

“If you are caught unprepared by a sudden rainstorm, you should not run foolishly down the road or hide under the eves of houses. You are going to get soaked either way. Accept that from the beginning and go on your way. This way you will not be distressed by a little rain. Apply this lesson to everything.” Hagakure

If your brain had come with a “user manual”, it might well have given this advice: do without from time to time. It’s counterintuitive but it’s a good thing to try, for a number of reasons. For one thing, you evolved under conditions of hiatus and intermittence. Your entire body and neural system is tuned for that. By creating cultural conditions in which emotional, physical and psychological discomfort is now almost constantly avoided, we’ve created populations which are heavily medicated by antidepressant drugs and self-medicated by alcohol, overeating, and other things which dull their senses when any ‘departure from comfort’ occurs. It’s amazing how food tastes when you’re really hungry, and have not had high-salt-high-fat-high-sugar “junk” food for a while. It’s nice how you feel after being “off the internet” for a week, and it can be fairly amazing to see the world around you re-emerging once you have been off that quick-stimulus-and-response treadmill for a while, ignoring social-status “likes.” If you’d like to be a bit more extreme, try going without electricity or other exosomatic energy for a while. There is an important point to doing so: you’ll get a bit better handle on what you need, how you feel, and the utility of energy. And become more mentally resilient in the process. In addition to a ‘vision quest’ and a periodic resetting of your hedonic ratchet, we recommend using your knowledge of your brain’s inner workings to overcome some of its drawbacks. One of the core insights of Reality 101 was that our modern skulls house stone-age minds, and as long as we know what this means, in many ways it’s a good thing! Our brains look and act very similar to those of our ancestors of 50,000-

100,000 years ago, but our culture and the technology and available activities are what have progressed exponentially. When we feel overwhelmed, or in a rut, it can make sense to ‘purge’ - not food or drink per se, but the overly intense stimuli in our lives that most in our culture take for granted as normal and healthy. In addition to lowering the daily ‘unexpected reward’ from higher and higher levels of neural habituation, the practices of email, social media, internet and even electricity holidays may be useful practice for future years/decades. It can also be good for the “now,” if you’re one of the few that decides to walk your own path. And, in addition to improving your health and well-being today, it may pay significant benefits down the road. In the universe of possibilities this century, some – even many – will involve intermittent or no access to electrical services. Teaching your mind that this is no big deal because you’ve experienced it in your younger years could be a

large (mental) asset. Environment/ecology lenses:

Much of the rest of this ‘what to do’

essay outlines ecological initiatives or ways of thinking that align with pro-future ecology, so when you view the world with any lenses, first remember this: \*\*There is an environment, and our economy is part of it, not beyond it. Thorstein Veblen famously critiqued the American economy as being comprised of ‘business’ (financiers, speculators, investors, etc.) and ‘industry’ (factories, supply chains, basic goods, etc.). His critique - that in good times the ‘business’ overwhelms the ‘industry’ and becomes the focus of the human economy - is even more apt circa 2018. But the criticism still

employs narrow-boundary lenses. There is a part of the economy – the factoring in of

our natural capital source/sinks - that does not add immediate surplus to our bottom lines, but that lays the foundation for future surplus. Full-world economies need industry, and a much smaller ‘business’ (in the Veblen sense) but also during your lifetimes a third component: future/ecology. And our current culture has lots of industry/business people and lots of environmentalists but very few who understand and can articulate the intersection of both worlds. Join that awareness, and transition. In a small way, or bigly.

The Amoeba Lens: “I’m part of the Superorganism, and I am NOT.”

On first blush, it can seem a bit of an existential dilemma to realize that one is part of a superorganism. And - at that - not a brilliant and godlike superorganism, but one which is none-too-bright, crawling around like a ginormous BTU-seeking amoeba and screwing up a previously productive planetary ecosystem in the process, with no more guiding ethic or introspection than shown by a frog’s kidney. Any brilliant insights associated with our species’ history have been largely the product of single minds, and exist without that brainless collective acting, or significantly able to act, upon them.

Thus, the natural reaction of a prosocial, pro-future individual discovering this is to seek disengagement or escape from one’s destiny as simply one cell in that superorganism. But that’s easier said than done. It’s particularly difficult to disassociate from the metabolic aspects of the human heat engine…. even if one buys a cabin in the boonies and becomes a vegan alpaca-herder, the best one can do is to subjectively “feel like” a somewhat less-destructive part of the amoeba while still being conjoined to it in a thousand ways, from toilet paper to prescription alpaca medicines and emergency services. So such a path means knowingly turning one’s back on the ongoing planetary damage one knows is occurring while pursuing individually higher karma on some imaginary status ledger of respective blame. The thing is, IF one is aware enough to grok the situation that well, now is the time to maximize ones individual impact on the future, not minimize it. A very small part of earth’s biomass has developed brains capable of understanding the things which affect the livability of the planet. Very few of them have had the opportunity to be educated enough to understand it. And only a tiny portion of THOSE few will be able to navigate past inborn cognitive biases and systematic errors to think realistically about what might actually be done to achieve less-bad futures. What a waste it would be for the few minds which make it through all those filters to decide not to try. Big group and Small aspirations should perhaps be avoided… There are a number of bumper-sticker, t-shirt and motivational-poster slogans out there which might be construed as encouraging one to only engage issues on a small scale: two of those are “be the change you want to see”, attributed to Gandhi, and “think globally, act locally” which generally isn’t but could equally well be, since Gandhi never said either of them. So if you envision a world of vegan Alpaca farms, it would follow that you should simply do that and wait for the teeming masses to follow your example, (which they won’t). The latter exhortation strongly implies that the best way of engaging with world problems is locally, which ranges in effectiveness from inadequate to outright silly. If, for instance, you are worried about south american rainforests being plowed under, it makes little sense to spend time focused on the suburb you live in. If you worry about a possible extinction of orangutans and bonobos, there’s not much you can do in your town to deal with it. In this day and age of passports, internet, and peak energy, one can directly engage with nearly anything in the world: “think globally, act globally”. If you can affect a local change which impacts the world in a positive way, by all means do so, but it’s better to aim at affecting a scale far larger than the scale on which you personally live, because that’s the only way your own life will be a “net good” to the future rather than an ecological “lesser evil” by simply minimizing your own impact.

Another Gandhi misquote, about large-group organizing, also comes to mind: "First they ignore you, then they laugh at you, then they fight you, then you win.” This plays to the myth that organizing in large groups may seem futile at first, but inevitably leads to victory. In fact, it feels futile at first because it generally IS futile, and seldom leads to any kind of victory unless within that large group you have a fairly sophisticated stepwise plan coordinated by an individual or small group of prime movers, as Gandhi did. But what right do I have to steer and influence what other people do? To maximize the number of human lives lived in deep time, maximize the total happiness and number of childhoods, and minimize bad experiences, the human amoeba (and its child) will need to be steered; and it can only be steered to specific target outcomes by groups small enough to understand the complexities of the world. This runs up against a social-ethics dilemma: what right do I have to steer other people? The laws of reality, in combination with our aggregate actions for the last couple hundred years, now have a good chance of taking us into realities we would not wish to visit, during your lifetime and beyond. By having a top-down view of what may unfold, you also have – at least in principle – the ability to steer things towards realities which are more humane, fair, and worth living in. But this runs up against a social-ethics dilemma... Well it’s not a right, an honor, or an ego trip: it’s a responsibility, and the alternative is negligence. The person who isn’t drunk has a responsibility to grab the car keys from those who are. The great majority of humans are non-rational, happily non-rational, and wouldn’t want to be any other way. They will be steered as they always

have been steered, by reacting in the way their limbic systems urge them to, because they really don’t want to face any difficult decisions. There never has been any real question of whether they’d be motivated by intelligent metacognitive self-analysis, because they would no sooner engage in that than eat worms. It doesn’t feel good to their brains, while reacting quickly and passionately to inane stuff does. The only question is what or who

their stone-age limbic systems will be steered by: random events, political propaganda, consumer marketing… or just perhaps, someone with an ethical, rational long term agenda who is not so wrapped up in his or her own pursuit and display of self-purity that they actually take the wheel and try to do so. Look around you. There are mere decades in which to make crucial, major changes in the way we exist on earth on all scales. The world will not, on that time scale, be saved by the sudden consensus agreement of a voting majority of erudite philosopher-citizens arriving on-scene in the nick of time. That being the case, it is incumbent upon the 1% who are “woke” to reality to step up. It may seem like 1% is an underestimate, since you know a lot of people who vote as you do. But if anything it’s currently a large overestimate, because most people - even those who seem to agree with you - adopt opinions and positions about things based on social consensus and tribalism rather than an actual process of science synthesis calibrated against physical reality. As we have noted before, even rational goals pursued in a democracy can only succeed via primarily non-rational support. Organizing large social groups to affect the future is primarily about trying to affect the consensus in a democracy - a mechanism which often won’t get the job done, but at least in principle might. Yet we tend to try organizing on large scale for all classes of problems, and many of them are spectacularly ill-suited to being addressed by large organizations or movements. When these large organizations or movements don’t make any real progress, we become dejected, because it seems as though they constituted the last hope for real change. This can lead to depression and disengagement. It’s useful, then, to realize that large social movements are really not all that useful in a lot of cases; because, as noted, very little of the actual steering of the human species is done by conscious consensus or even agreement. To a large degree, there’s no rational steering whatsoever, just the emergent results of billions of individual actors pursuing what ‘feels right” to their inborn brain-reward mechanisms. Such steering as is done by conscious volition is generally done by those who are good at it to pursue relative advantage rather than achieve any optimized future on earth. Some of the points to be made in this essay will seem extremely counterintuitive; so let me note from whence they spring. The author of this section (DJ) had the instructive experience of attempting to affect the exact same medium-scale global issues as (a) the international program director of one of the world’s largest and most famous conservation advocacy organizations, and then (b) as the director of an unknown organization without any funds. The only extraordinary thing about such a

sequence of events is that the success of the small, poor, unknown organization with only a few members turned out to be fantastically higher than that of the large, wealthy, famous one with millions of members, on not just one issue but all of them, which was unexpected. How could that be? Well, the long answer would fill several books that may well never be written, but the short answer is simply that - as stated above - large social movements and organizations are simply not very effective at getting things changed in the real world (even if it feels like they should), while individuals and small groups can be really good at it, even on the largest scales. Opting out of the behavioral aspects of the Superorganism

We’ve noted that it’s nigh-on impossible to truly detach from the metabolic aspects of the questing heat engine which undergirds our collective existence. However, it’s quite possible to detach behaviorally from the “human amoeba” and become a self-directed actor who wrangles what the amoeba will and will not do. And it’s probably not overstating things to say that such individuals constitute the main hope of things turning out OK. Just realizing that the superorganism/amoeba/heat engine exists in the way it really does is a huge perspective shift which very few attain. It is quite reasonable, from that vantage point, to decide to repudiate the amoeba, to be self-aware of our evolved deep-brain defaults and when necessary to override them. This might seem counter-productive to personal happiness, if it amounted to ignoring all that “felt good or right” and doing only stuff that felt uncomfortable. But that doesn’t necessarily follow. In fact, it’s possible to be quite depressed and unproductive

even if following every urge your brain gives you. People who do generally end up with addictions and other consequences. Your “gene agenda” doesn’t seek your happiness, it seeks slavish response. But we’re at our best when we’re about more than just pleasure-seeking after dopamine and other limbic-system behavioral rewards. There are things like pride, self-respect, a sense of purpose, place, and mission; triumph over challenge, loyalty to the people and things one loves, satisfaction at accomplishment, and much more. These things may actually be far easier to find in a consciously-directed life. Moreover, just because society in general may think such a person is a bit of an oddball doesn’t mean one can’t find or assemble a tribe with shared values and goals which can provide the sort of mutual support and camaraderie a savannah-designed mind works well within. That is, it’s possible and even practical to repudiate the Gene Agenda, and your authors recommend doing so. You owe it no loyalty or responsibility, because there is really no “it” to be loyal or responsible to. It’s simply part of the web of unconscious happenstance - luck - which caused your brain to assemble in a way which created your mind and its independent universe. Other than some atavistic limbic-system compulsions and rewards, it offers nothing. Resigning the “gene agenda” means resigning from your part in emergent amoebic tropisms, and potentially steering some aspects of the amoeba intelligently. When doing so, you’ll still be tied to the energy footprint of the collective, but you’ll be using that energy creatively, for rational and altruistic ends which may alter the very nature of that huge footprint.

Be A Little Alien

Any way in which one intentionally disobeys the gene agenda makes them just a bit alien. And that’s a good thing. Boilerplate

humans hewing to the Gene Agenda have had a good run, but are now running toward a brick wall they don’t see. Being 5% alien,

at this point in time, is probably more useful than being 50% more intelligent, because even very intelligent people spend their lives within the consensus trance.

So hey! In the spirit of our advice to be 5% alien: Join AAA: be

an “altruist alien anthropologist”. Pose as human, and enjoy life as human, but be able to switch in and out of alien-

perception mode by wearing Reality glasses and your alter-ego thinking cap. Beneficent aliens are needed now, but none have shown up. Become them. The human race badly needs loving gamekeepers who can operate from a detached perspective at least part of the time.

And the indirect effects... The “consensus imperative” may also have something to do with the tendency of humans, when wanting to solve a problem, to search for (or create) a large group of people – the larger the better, presumably – with which to address that problem. However, this tendency contains the seeds of its own problem, because the larger a “consensus seeking” group of humans gets, the dumber, slower, and less effective it gets, for many reasons. Thus, you can wind up with a “problem solving” group which is spectacularly ill-suited to solving most sorts of problems. However, even after the demonstrable failure of such structures and strategies, it still “feels like” the thing to do, so we “double down” on it, with the “big groups” getting bigger and the “small groups” fading away, even if the small groups are far more effective (which they generally will be). Creativity and abstract planning have enormous potential to deal with problems and predicaments, and we have plenty of those starting to show themselves. If you wish to use that potential, you’d be well-advised to not seek validation from large groups or those who happen to be around you, but to get your validation from first-principles analysis, a synthesis worldview, and logic. This works best for individuals and very small groups of people who come together because their creativity is reaching similar conclusions. This can be the start of a more useful sort of mini-tribe, and aligning with humans it is less inhibiting to seek consensus with. I (DJ) am often contacted by college grads asking how to get a position in a large prestigious conservation organization, to help change the world for the better. My advice to them is generally “don’t”. If you want to make money, go into some other line of work; and if you want to change the world, just do it. Don’t chain yourself at the ankles to a group which is already far along the downslope path of diminishing returns (on resource investment in complexity). It “feels like” joining a large group is the right thing to do, but that’s just the subverting effect of some atavistic tendencies we’d arguably be better off without.

Choose your tribe...

Now it’s all well and good to point out where the “gene agenda” leads us into being ineffective and to make poor decisions. But we are stuck with the brains we have, and we won’t get much done if we’re depressed or unhappy. So to some degree we have to give our deep core-brains the things they need to not be despondent, depressed, bored, or un-stimulated. Fortunately, this can be done in ways which don’t compromise rational life-plans.

Since we “feel best” when we’re “fitting in” with a tribe, it makes good sense to choose your tribe to be in alignment with who you want to be and what you want to accomplish. If your ideals and plans are ridiculed or disapproved by those around you, perhaps it’s time to seek out people who are more supportive, and whose ideas strike sparks with your own in good ways. Within such a tribe, you may well find collaborators to work on projects and plans, and your background stress will be low. Obviously, it’s still good to be wary of too much consensus, and to stay 5% alien when possible, but you can find or assemble a tribe in which this is considered cool. A group of friends who experience life to the fullest and are on the same wavelength on most issues is one of the most stimulating situations a brain can experience.

With all the Lenses removed – Who Am I?

When learning about the interacting economic, energy, and environmental risks which face our species and world this century, it’s natural to feel a bit of panic along the lines of “what the heck do I DO about this stuff?”. Do you buy solar panels? Lobby for a carbon tax? Move to Iceland or New Zealand? Buy an electric car? Buy a ton of canned beans and spam and move to a cabin in the woods with a short-wave radio, or live in a shack and grow a vegetable garden? Buy guns & gold and hunker down as a survivalist? Have a dozen kids and train them to care for you? Become a preacher? Or just “go with the flow” of society and see what happens? It’s probably good that there are still a lot of potential choices available, and the

choices mentioned are ones we’ve seen people making! But the big changes we can see taking rough shape in the future are still moving in slow-motion relative to the pace of individual human lives, and it makes sense to take the time to really think it through, lest you wind up spending your perfectly good life in a way which is less enjoyable or useful than it might otherwise be. The main point is that whatever answer you come up with should be your own answer, with a good fit for who you are and truly wish to be.

That’s the reason a central part of a “what to do” section begins with “who am I?” It’s because this will be a momentous century. We can know, beforehand, the rules which will constrain the future’s possibilities, but not which twists and turns reality may take within those constraints - our complex existence will iterate into the future a day at a time. That means the “what to do” responses may change, and change significantly, based on these changing contexts. The things which make sense to do this week may be supplanted by a different set of goals five, ten, and twenty years from now, as we each observe and react to the systems we live within and alongside. Moreover, since this will be a momentous century, it will have stresses: not just those now apparent, but new and different ones. There’s no way to know exactly what they will be; just that they will present themselves as they did to past generations. Unless your decisions and actions are firmly anchored in who you are, it is likely they will eventually be abandoned. So here’s another Reality insight: what to do really needs to flow directly from who you are. One person may have an entirely different set of what to do’s than her best friend, who’s a different person; and both of their what to do’s will change as the things around them do. It might seem strange for an honors course about “Reality” to ultimately urge you to discover who you are, but in your authors’ experience, there’s no substitute for it. Talking these weighty issues over with others is important and we encourage it. But the questions at the very core of your one existence are of a different class. The issue of ‘who you are’ is a private thing for you to come to terms with based on nobody’s counsel but your own, perhaps in nature, perhaps in a place which has special significance to you; perhaps in a single marathon quest or perhaps in a series of sessions interviewing yourself over an extended period. In a universe which doesn’t care about you, humans, or even life, you have an opportunity to choose what sort of goals, ethics, standards, and principles make sense to you, and adopt them consciously for reasons that are your own. Which things do you care about, do you not care about; which of these things would you take a personal risk for? What will the guiding philosophy of your life be? Do you like and respect the person you have been so far? Why or why not? When you are 50 years older, what sort of life do you want to be able to look back on? What sort do you NOT wish to look back on? What sort of small (or medium) things can you “let slide” - What sort of big things shall you hold as personally sacred, and defend if necessary? Which “gene agenda” urges are you better off re-directing? What balance between the inner world of feelings and the physical world of matter and energy do you wish to have? Will you cede power to your urges or give it to your principles (or some combination)? What is your answer to “why am I alive?” There are no right or wrong answers to these questions in general, but there will be right and wrong answers for

you. This might seem to be a bit of a woo-woo thing to do, but it cuts to the core of “what to do” about our various predicaments, because you should do the things that make rational sense to the person you actually are, to achieve things you will value by your own standards and ethics in the context of an extraordinary period of history. Moreover, to the extent you are following the logic and emotion of the person you have chosen to be, it will be far easier to make life decisions going forward because you’ll know who you are and won’t have to work it out from scratch every time the world throws you a curveball. This may wind up being the most important thing you take from this course. Only something with the depth of a “I am \_\_

x\_\_and I stand for

” answer will be resilient against the bottleneck tribulations. To persevere, your sense of core identity needs to be stronger than the hate, fear, resentment and uncertainty your brain evolved to generate during stressful times. So while some people will go into politics, others will decide to raise a family; still others will chain themselves to a bulldozer or press for a carbon tax, or provide food for the poor, others may advocate for solar panels or endangered marine species, and still others may try setting in motion humane and sapient responses to get their communities through the coming physical bottlenecks. No one person can do everything, or should try. It’s best to first figure out who you are, who you want to be, and then follow that where it may lead using all the skill, motivation, and bravery you can bring to bear in a life worth living. That’s what your authors are attempting, and for that reason. So when you answer the question “who am I?” It should ideally be the person you have decided to be with your one life, a person who you respect based on your own standards, who your older self in the future will be proud to have been. In times when many things are going away, it could be very sad to be alive to see them go. But knowing that you have saved a thing which otherwise would have been lost, is one place hope and meaning may be found. It could be a species that no one knows about, one that everyone knows about, or even an idea, an ethic, an infrastructure, a way of thinking, a limited natural resource, a skill, a culture, a language, special knowledge. A species of bird, mammal, reptile or insect. You could make that your creative reproductive contribution. Only in very special and very strange times can one reproduce other species (see below). These are just such times. Compared to all of our ancestors (and probably most/all of our descendants), we now do have the potential to prevent some major things from being predictably, irretrievably lost to the future; to make things significantly better than they would otherwise be. This is an amazingly creative life-choice to be able to make. So that’s the core of it. To think of yourself as a unique individual with a conscious

mind which intends to significantly direct its own destiny. This is a rarity: many people never consider this, and just do the “gene agenda” thing of subconsciously managing PR with other humans to achieve advantage and hope others somehow fix things. The gateway beyond this is the realization that there is a “you” apart from the way other people define you. This is one reason being by oneself in nature (or anywhere) is a good idea, separated from the consensus signals of constant social posturing and reaction. Without doing something like this, it’s (very) hard to get out of the genes’ clutches and become self-aware by more than capuchin criteria. Once you learn to care about who you are, and who you could be, that opens all the other doors, including the one to deserved self-respect. It’s possible to easily go through life without facing up to whether one likes themselves or respects themselves, not because of self-aggrandizing narratives and ego but simply because they know the truth about themselves and they’d respect and like any such person. It really does come down to your urges versus your principles. If a person always chooses the former, it’s probably reasonable to conclude that he doesn’t actually have principles. But this is for nobody else to judge, and certainly not your authors. Rather, it’s something that a person should conclude for themselves, and hold as a personal core truth. It’s not for social shaming or social anything, it’s for self-respect. Once you figure out the “who am I now, and who do I wish to be” answers, you’ll tend to find your own way by steering in the general direction of your goals via thousands of tiny decisions and a few large ones which will add up. You’ll probably find that there are many things to do which have meaning and align with who you are. Remember, ‘what to do’ makes very little sense except in light of the bottlenecks, which make no sense except in light of the carbon pulse, which makes no sense except in the light of evolutionary psychology and physics. And finally, anything you do makes little sense except in the context of who you are.

A Bottleneck Vision Quest

One suggestion we might suggest is perhaps a bit analogous to a “vision quest”, a practice among some native American tribes in which they spent time in solitude, often accompanied by fasting or the self-infliction of pain, seeking a spirit guide to follow. We say “analogous” because we don’t recommend adding pain, (and any spirits you may dream would be part of your personal virtual world). Likewise, the fasting is entirely optional unless it works for you or you’d like to lose a couple pounds. The important part is to unplug from the social net, and that doesn’t just mean the internet, but the constant high-bandwidth back & forth with other members of your species, all of which is very soothing to the gene agenda but not helpful in terms of getting in touch with the core “you”. The location should be one in which are

inclined to feel inspired: a pretty vista, a spot overlooking the ocean, a starry sky, a journey in the woods that “feels right” to you, or just a place which has personal significance in your life. Then start dissecting the way you feel, the way you think, the things you fear, the things you truly love and care about; the things you regret and the things you’re proud of. The degree to which you’re living your life to impress others specifically and generally. The degree to which your working definition of “success” has relied on society and the expectations of others, rather than what would constitute “success” in your own terms. What are your ethics? Where do they spring from? Do they agree entirely with those of others around you, or do they diverge in some ways? Can you recognize the modular nature of your own mind, examine examples of retroactive confabulation your mind has engaged in to place you in the best light in your own eyes and those of others? Can you feel the fact that your left and right hemispheres are different individuals, and that your mind is composed of many voices wishing to be heard? Can you find cases in which you truly believe utterly irreconcilable things in different modules? And can you accept what you are and take responsibility for who you will be? What would it take for you to become one of your own heroes?

Don’t expect to come up with these answers immediately. Some of them may elude you, and you may find others which aren’t mentioned here. But see if the process doesn’t help you know yourself, as the first step to whatever you decide to do. We recommend that – whatever answers you come up with – you keep them to yourself. They are not for purposes of sharing outside your mind, and should not require any external validation or reconciliation. If they do, then do it again, with

that as an insight/input. We realize that the unique time you have been born into is a powerful and compelling context in which to seek these answers. There will be immense things occurring during the time you will be alive. To some extent, those few people who have been exposed to synthesis science, such as this intro to R101, are a bit like time-travelers from the future who have been granted the wish to be transported back in time before

many changes to the earth and to humanity were irrevocable, to make changes for the better. That’s because this way of looking at the world may well be obvious in retrospect, hundreds of years hence, but during your life it will not be. You are living in the future’s past. This is cutting edge, heretical stuff by the standards of the day, like the revelations of Copernicus.. The pieces of the puzzle you have fit together in your mind are not apparent to most of your species, and likely will not be for a very long time, if ever.

On Reproducing - (other species, ideas, ethics, and maybe babies)

Given what you’ve learned in this class about the Reality of the coming century, the decision whether to have children becomes a non-trivial one. It “feels like” giving birth is an optimistic pro-future thing. It’s the strongest “gene agenda” drive we have, for good evolutionary reasons. Yet reproducing in the current circumstances might be counterproductive. Having children is about instinct, pheromones, social pressure, and that whole package, and

in the current context it precludes doing much for the future, because of the physical, monetary and time resources required to be a good parent. Also, new parents aware of Reality could be knowingly sending kids into a very chancy period in history without much regard for their prospects. Anyone who’s reasonably aware of the world today and decides to reproduce may be doing it for any number of rationales (or no rationale), but it likely isn’t for the kids. So are we about to make some sort of environmental case for you not to have kids? No. The standard environmental view is that having children in a developed country is far and away the most harm you can do to the environment, no matter how ecolate, kind-hearted or brilliant your child may grow up to be. By driving a hybrid car, being a vegan, and using clean power, you would save 488 metric tons of carbon dioxide

during an 80-year lifetime. By having one fewer child, you would save 9,441 or almost 20 times more. This is certainly worth thinking about. But this rationale - to not have kids because of world overpopulation and resource consumption - may not always be as true as it sounds, because lower birthrates - occurring for any reason – are unlikely to be the major control on population going forward. Moreover, the “Jevons”-like effects of consumption will, within a market society, largely absorb small sacrifices and redistribute any conserved resources to others, rather than sending signals - overt or otherwise - to “slow down” the corporations which extract coal, oil, iron and phosphorus from the earth. In the same way that the CO2 emission savings from ‘greater efficiency’ and ‘adding solar panels’ only apply to an individual not an entire system, the energy saved from having one less child will likely be used elsewhere in our system of growth. Thus, our argument for seriously considering whether or not to have children is not this one, but more nuanced. Our reasons for taking the decision to procreate extremely seriously: we love human kids, but there’s no shortage of them. Indeed, ecologically, humans are in plague-population mode and are heading for bottleneck events in which the human population will be reduced, possibly drastically, during this century. If so, that’s a terrible thing to knowingly send a kid into. In addition, raising a child requires and deserves a full effort. But morally, full effort is currently required by someone to try ameliorating the future travails of the kids already born, of kids others will surely have without a second thought, and of other species which may or may not survive the bottlenecks without help. Not having kids – or at least delaying the decision - frees up time, emotional load and physical resources for one to engage in events of meaning during once-in-a-species events. A baby in your 20s precludes a great many choices of action – and impacts – effectively taking you out of the game and pulling you fully back into the Amoeba. As another consideration, the same bottlenecks which will eventually reduce human numbers mean that thousands to millions of other species will have trouble surviving this century. Most such species have not a single dedicated human caring whether they do or not, to the extent of trying to actively help them survive. And probably never will. Think about that. It means that there’s a choice to make: a human can, in effect, choose to reproduce other species than human, by devoting the care and resources which would have been given to human children to one or more other species that person has “adopted”. There are no adoption fees or papers, a simple personal decision is enough to seal it. Ergo, we are not advocating abstaining from reproduction because there are already too many people – we are advocating thinking seriously about the decision, because without kids, you’ll be more flexible, more

effective and more involved in the challenges that need your efforts and skills and passions. The essence of our recommendation is this: don’t be pressured by others, or by society, to have kids. Do it because you’ve really thought it through with their well-being and interests paramount, and have chosen to spend your life bringing them into this world at this time, and adequately preparing them for what they can expect to encounter.

On Reproducing Other Species – (A note from someone who’s done it -DJ White)

Here are some personal thoughts on my and my wife’s decision not to have kids of our own, not because we’d expect you to necessarily make a similar decision, but because it’s at the deep core of the brain’s “gene agenda”, as well as those of the culture and our guiding mythologies. Evolution being what it is, it could not produce anything else. Over-reproduction is the single standard and strategy of the gene agenda; everything else is details. Our myths and cultures need to fit with what “feels right” to do, and the ones which encourage reproduction acquire a numerical edge over those which don’t. The mechanism of evolution is a life-and-death competition for energy and other resources, which is why nature - including human history - is red in tooth and claw. These days humans are essentially genetically identical. The other human species are long gone. Evolution’s de facto “death cage match” battle for allele survival is a terrible idea in the context of a self-aware species, and is pure heartless unproductive gene agenda unworthy of any ethical code worth the name. Someone else’s kids already carry all our genes forward, times a billion. It’s not like sexual reproduction clones us anyhow, at best our kids are a random mashup of their parents’ genes… so what’s imperative about that? Is it the entertainment value of having kids to bond with? The social points we get with family and society for having them? The oxytocin rush of baby smell? A safety net, hoping they’ll care for us someday? None of those are remotely morally persuasive, particularly when one reflects that knowingly inserting kids into a population that may undergo rapid traumatic shrinking could be tantamount to premeditated child abuse. The things we should “want to survive” about ourselves aren’t our hair and eye color, our ability to digest lactose, or our immunity to sickle-cell anemia. Rather, it’s the unique things about us, which are (almost) all products of our minds, and can be culturally passed on to kids. But those don’t have to be our genetic kids. We have done our best to pass our values, our new ideas and aspirations, our re-framings and

priorities, our sense of the sacred, to thousands of people in person and far more by proxy. These are the things that are “us” and that we want to see continue within the human species. Is it, somehow, our eugenic duty to the future to clone our ‘superior brains’ and prevent “idiocracy” scenarios, lest the dim rabble outbreed us? Well, I couldn’t sell that to myself either. What constitutes a superior brain? Were we actually smarter than the Neanderthals, or are we their Idiocracy scenario? At a small scale we can contemplate deep mysteries, but on the large scale that translates to amoebas with H-bombs, and the heat engine goes on. Moreover, who’s to say that a person who is kinder, more empathic and wiser but lacks 20 IQ points might not be a better human than her friends in Mensa? But beyond that, speaking as a person who has spent years in comparative cognition research, I’ll note that, broad brush, there just isn’t much variation in mental capacity among humans these days. Most of the functional variation is cultural. We’ve long since killed off significant genetic variation in the “final exams” of evolution, determined by who kills, rapes, out-cooperates and out-competes who. And of course, the world’s largest brains, which aren’t human, are being rapidly pushed into oblivion by the sheer biomass of the carbon-pulsed gene agenda of the third chimpanzee. Here’s the thing: I’m not speaking against having kids. I’m speaking on behalf of the kids, yours and others. Reproducing is, to me, the biggest moral decision one can make, as well as being in many ways the decision which constrains all subsequent decisions. My wife and I were physically capable of having kids; we both love family dynamics, having come from fun families. But had we done so, we probably couldn’t have done the other stuff we did, like facing some seemingly impossible quests together. We faced the gene agenda versus a sapiens agenda bigtime, and it wasn’t easy; but then neither is parenthood. Again, I’m not pushing this - and have never done so to anyone - just laying out what we decided. But I do recommend that your major life decisions be made subject to clear thinking about who you have chosen to be, in this one life that has been given to you. It’s hard to make any sort of logical case that there “aren’t enough humans” at this point. By putting our effort and energy into other species instead of having our own kids, my wife and I reproduced very successfully in an unusual way: instead of adding to human biomass, our physical progeny are sea creatures. Our efforts of the last 40 years have, evidently, saved on the rough order of a million dolphins, countless whales, seals and seabirds, and a huge biomass of sea turtles, sharks, and other marine creatures which would have otherwise been taken by destructive fleets we succeeded in having banned from the high seas, from drive-kills we have permanently stopped, from illegal commerce in endangered species we have

curtailed. Those critters will never hug us, and we don’t require it. We like knowing they’re out there. Their future, we don’t know, but we know their species have a better chance at what’s coming than they would have had. To us, that’s a worthwhile thing. I won’t say it has been easy, but I’ll say I recommend it. And there are a boatload of humans kids we’ve affected as well. Our intellectual progeny are those who have been moved by our rationales, arguments, and actions. We missed the standard joys and pains of bringing more humans into the world, but having some notion of what those kids and their kids might face, we realized that what was unique about ourselves that we wanted to pass on wasn’t my red hair or my wife’s brown eyes. It was our thoughts and values, and any species which we might be able to save from going away. There was no shortage of red-haired, brown-eyed human kids. Every human kid these days already has nearly our exact genomes in the ways that matter. Is modern human existence really still some sort of desperate competition for “allele survival supremacy over the kids of others” that we’re supposed to buy into? And if so, isn’t that ultimately a fairly half-assed idea to cleave to? I care about the kids of others, and near as I can tell they’re just as suited to represent my species genetically as mine would have been, even without the red hair. Why the heck should I be motivated by some sort of allegiance to red hair? Desperation to consciously adopt the gene agenda really makes little sense, and popping out kids for social-conformity reasons or for our own pleasure ultimately didn’t make the cut for us. Personally, I’ve disowned and repudiated the gene agenda, and have done my best to substitute love, responsibility, and understanding. I realize how corny that sounds, but there it is. I don’t claim to be any sort of sapiens, though I am “sapiens curious”. While I live, I will fight for the existence of the trillion human childhoods which should be lived rather than being discarded as an irrelevant future, and for a living world in which they too might know a dolphin as a friend. There is no motivation but those for my having participated with you in offering materials for this course. I sure as heck don’t get paid for it. Don’t be pressured to have kids. It’s not your responsibility to do it. If you do have kids, be a great parent. Do it, if you do it, because you’ve decided it’s right for them (the kids you’ll have), and is how you choose to spend your one life. Because adequately preparing them for experiencing the second half of this century, as things start contracting in earnest, will be a challenge.

Part 3 - Hitchhiking in the Sapient Direction

Sapience: the ability to think and act using knowledge, experience, understanding, common sense, and

insight.[1] Wisdom has been regarded as one of four cardinal virtues; and as a virtue, it is a habit or disposition to perform the action with the highest degree of adequacy under any given circumstance with the limitation of error
