in any given action. This implies a possession of knowledge, or the seeking of knowledge to apply to the given circumstance. This involves an understanding of people, objects, events, situations, and the willingness as well as

the ability to apply perception, judgement, and action in keeping with the understanding of what is the optimal course of action. It often requires control of one's emotional reactions (the "passions") so that the universal

principle of reason prevails to determine one's action. In short, wisdom is a disposition to find the truth coupled with an optimum judgement as to what actions should be taken. – Wikipedia

Your authors have strongly recommended “Homo sapiens” as a good idea for a species which hasn’t quite arrived yet. We call ourselves “Homo sapiens”, but that’s largely because we invented taxonomy and didn’t care for the notion that we properly belong in the great ape family. We have been very clever, but historically we have generally whiffed on the “wisdom” part of that vanity nameplate. Recently science has given us incredible new insights into the nature of mind, evolution, and the physical world, which makes “sapience” more attainable and useful in principle than it has ever been, as the conscious outcome of pursuing a synthesis worldview based on an ecological ethic. Such self-awareness opens the door, at least potentially, to a long and exciting human future on a healthy planet. However, our cleverness in finding ways to please our stone-age brain-reward systems has resulted in some less-than-wise outcomes, and this was thrown into overdrive when we added hundreds of millions of years’ worth of flammable fossils to the equation. That allowed us to get ourselves into a number of pickles which simply would not have been possible otherwise; pickles which will now require sapience to extricate ourselves and our world in reasonable shape. And it has lit a short fuse: had

all those fossil carbon compounds not been accessible, we’d have had tens, maybe hundreds of thousands of years to pursue sapience. With the “carbon pulse” engaged, that time shrinks to decades: a make-or-break cage match between the backward-looking Gene Agenda and the nascent ‘Homo sapiens.’ Yet you should now be aware it isn’t really possible to just “decide to be sapient” and voila, have it happen. Unlike the “gene agenda” which you’re born with, absolute sapience is a destination at which we can’t ever expect to truly arrive. A behavioral and ethical asymptote. At best, we can identify it as a direction and goal: to be more sapient than we have been, and to continue that pursuit. The Reality101 course has been about prepping for a more sapient future in which we can see the entire chessboard and make our personal moves accordingly. We are all (acutely) aware of the accelerating threats in “metabolic space” to oceans and the ecosphere. And we see daily examples in mischief space of the things our species does with surplus energy- both to the environment and to ourselves and our culture. To meet these challenges, we advocate first starting personally, in ‘sapience space.’ So the decision to pursue sapience is the first step towards Homo sapiens and away from adherence to the behavioral rewards and punishments of the Gene Agenda, to challenge our urges with our principles and win. Below you’ll find some scattershot ideas regarding “hitch-hiking in the sapient direction”, which is what your authors are pursuing and recommend.

Wise Guys: Homo Sapiens Doesn’t Exist Yet

The human race, homo Sapiens sapiens (which means “wise man”), is the most advanced and intelligent creature on earth (\*\*\*at least by its own standards). For the last 20 thousand years, we have increasingly uncovered previously unseen emergent effects by combining fire, flammable fossils, technology, cheap raw materials, extra-somatic information storage, grasping appendages, cultural rather than biological niche adaptation, and the stored solar energy of a hundred million years. There are a lot of adjectives which could be used to describe this species, but “wise” is not yet one of them. So far we are “clever man”, using mind and technology as a tool to satisfy the urges and goals of the gene agenda. A snapshot at this moment might look very successful, but the existing trends - accelerating toward immutable physical limits - throw the intermediate-term thriving and surviving of this species into question. Wisdom is worth cultivating. It is about long-term good judgement based on a useful synthesis of knowledge of mind and nature, stripped of the evolved bias, fears, lusts

and illusions which fight against it. Until we begin actively seeking homo sapiens, we won’t find them. Homo sapiens is still something to aspire to, and soon.

Don’t try to be perfect. Try to be effective.

There’s simply no way to deal usefully with everything an inquiring, conscious and conscientious mind will come to care about, and trying to do so can paralyze anyone by imposing cognitive load, scattered focus and ultimately depression and burnout. That’s one reason that “social shaming” or “social capital” is an iffy mechanism for changing the world; attempting to “do everything right” and project that image to the world. This can very quickly overwhelm a person. Moreover, pursuing personal “karma” doesn’t actually accomplish much. If one decides to go live in a cabin off-grid and only eat self-grown vegetables, boycotting most aspects of industrialization, that may give him or her peace of mind, but it doesn’t fix any problems. Jevons-like effects reallocate resources to others and minimize any positive impacts their partial withdrawal from the big Heat Engine might have. At this point in time, sapient individuals need to maximize their effects on the world, not minimize them. Withdrawing from society to ignore what’s going on in the world is in that sense a bit of a cop-out, an abrogation of responsibility, which is a victory for the Gene Agenda and its array of dissonance-resolving heuristics. A more realistic and useful standard to aim at is to choose one or a few things you really care about and be disproportionately effective in changing them for the better, on a scale larger than your own existence. For all the additional issues you’re aware of, have some reasonable balance and just try not to be a hypocrite. Do your best without subjecting yourself to a crippling load of mental stress. Can’t eat organic? OK. Don’t want to be a vegetarian? Well, come up with something that you can live with. Can’t donate to all the good causes? That’s OK, they probably can’t donate to yours either. You don’t have to do everything. You don’t have to even try to be perfect. Just do the stuff which makes you feel you are who you’d wish to be. (not the stuff you think others expect you to do). Your authors certainly aren’t perfect. We don’t live in remote communes gumming radishes, we have cars and occasionally fly on airplanes. At any given time, there will be a primary project we’re working on, and we’ll be thinking about several others, but we try to be kind to ourselves... because it’s people who find a balance who can get good at changing things. Do we always nail that balance? Nah, we’re not perfect about that either, and nobody will be. You’ll find that there are things you can do which conform to your “who am I” answers, and do NOT add to your cognitive load, like consolidating car trips, using energy intelligently, not eating endangered species,

etc. These sorts of things actually wind up freeing up your time to do more thinks like hike, spending time in nature or with friends, and resetting your hedonic ratchet. You won’t see obvious progress toward your big goals everyday, but you can live in a way which allows you to respect yourself, and the “big goal” progress will become evident on the scale of years.

Don’t quit your day job at Exxon: use it to follow your own principles

So here’s some advice which is about 180 degrees out-of-phase with that of current social activism: consider NOT dropping out, but creatively staying in, this society. This too goes to our advice to keep track of your own principles rather than hewing to social notions of “personal karma” or “purity”. DJ used to work in the oil industry, and NJ was formerly a Wall Street executive. We’ve both long since quit those jobs, but are perhaps better at what we currently do for having had that experience. Plus, if one has a conscience and quits a job on principle, that job is filled by someone else who likely does not share the same principles, and you wind up one more person trying to do good from a position of relative poverty, feeling pure but perhaps not getting much done. The human heat-engine amoeba will not be steered by people quitting their day jobs to go start organic-carrot farms. There’s nothing wrong with such lifestyle choices, but they don’t change any of the macro trajectories. Jevons effects will reallocate the stuff they don’t consume and the money they don’t spend to others, as long as the economy is organized the way it now is, so buying a bicycle is good for your health, but has the effect of making gasoline cheaper for people driving Humvees in the bike lane, which may be less good for your health. (Rebound effects abound). So here’s a notion: if you work within the amoeba – as pretty much everyone does – there’s a way to get around the Jevons effects, and the “mischief space” effects, and ameliorate some of the problems caused by the Amoeba, while giving yourself a high degree of freedom to pursue your own goals. It’s simple in concept, eminently do-able, and so scalable that tens of millions of people could do it – if they cared to – tomorrow, transforming the very nature of society, activism, and creative engagement with the future. The crux of it is a path explored by author DJ: just make the decisions that only half (or some other set portion) of your income from that job is “for you”. Define the other portion as “for your second-career projects”. That’s it, all it takes. It means ratcheting

down the conspicuous consumption from maybe 200x what you need to 100x what you need to survive. That, in turn, just means prudent budgeting rather than taking your cues on expenditures from a social peer group, be it friends, family, or work contacts. Are there happy people living on a small fraction of what you’re paid? Yup, and you can be one of them, while still earning that full paycheck. The extra money makes you an instant activist, inventor, philanthropist, or whatever you desire to be; it becomes the YOU while your day job is just what pays the bills. The way it circumvents Jevons is that you’re still spending all the money you bring in... you’re just not spending it on yourself, as a consumer. Rather, you’re spending it creatively on some issue in human or environmental mischief space which is subject to being changed for the better, which means Jevons effects can’t reallocate it to someone else’s consumption. It’s also a bit of a revolutionary model: these days, if a person wants to change the future, they wind up looking for some way of supporting themselves and discover that paid activist positions are not only scarce as hens’ teeth, but most of them are really fundraising positions in disguise; and if they aren’t fundraising they’re administration. In contrast, a self-directed-self-funded pro-future person is far ahead of the game, personally financially secure and able to – half the time – pursue being who they want to be. This is what author DJ did: while working a job in oil exploration, he set aside half his salary to save whales, and became a high donor to one of the first efforts, in 1974, to shut down whaling; becoming a chimerical creature: a doodlebugger/whale philanthropist. Ultimately he decided to leave and pursue the conservation part of his interests full time, but without spending time getting to know the issues and think about them by being a half-time whale philanthropist, he might not have been able or confident enough to. And by living on about ¼ of the actual funds he was making, he also had a bit of financial pad to make that jump once he decided to. So this can work. Whether it’ll become a broad movement is an open question. It first requires stepping outside the gene agenda to consider the fact that even with no money at all, a person in a rich nation today has better health, security, and nutrition available than the kings and queens of old, with a longer life expectancy. A person on food stamps eats better than medieval royalty, if they’re halfway competent at cooking. That means that living on a third, a half, two-thirds etc of pretty much ANY salary will provide for a respectable and comfortable life plus a budget to fund the freedom of a primary “sacred” purpose outside oneself. It does take a bit of impulse control not to immediately spend all one’s money, but really no more than

one should have learned by third grade. It means being secure enough not to need to spend what one’s friends spend on consumer items to “prove” anything about social status. Really, it could be something a lot of people would do IF it was presented as a respectable sort of life option. There are many plusses to it; one lives more creatively, practices good fiscal hygiene, becomes more personally financially resilient against uncertain times, and much else. But the big plus is that the who you are dimension falls far more within your own control. It’s no longer just theory, you can live it and steer it. Of course, this means getting a cheaper apartment, using a year-old smart phone with a lesser phone plan, driving a less-shiny car, etc, but those are really incredibly minor things in comparison with having a life which is more than a cycle of paychecks and consuming. It’s possible that if it became known that this was being done by a fair number of people, a ton of others would follow. It just isn’t “done” yet, and people don’t like to feel odd.

It’s a shame to shame.

“You drive THAT car? We bought a new Prius. Electric, low emission you know”. ‘Did you know that raising beef is one of the biggest impacts on climate? -I buy organic tofu and love it!”. “I can’t meet you for a movie, I am biking all of our cardboard and bottles to the recycling center”…..Any of this sound familiar? The reason to differentiate this sort of self-funded parallel life from the sniping and shaming of standard social activism is that - for one thing - social activism doesn’t work very well. It creates tribes of people who “feel like” they are superior to others, with or without reasonable justification, but it doesn’t accomplish much. Using that polarizing template, a person at Exxon is automatically on the Dark Side. If such a person were to quit their job to become an activist, Exxon would hire someone else immediately with no impulse to help the future, while the erstwhile activist would find very few paid opportunities available to do anything useful. But if that same person mentally splits her life between the means of obtaining money for living, and the mission which is deep, sacred, and meaningful to HER, it can be transformative, of that person and of the issue. It adds a far deeper dimension to life, an additional way to “keep score” of personal success. We need a movement that doesn’t shame people into tithing 2 or 5% per year, but instead welcomes them to be a full-fledged wonderful human being for having this extra dimension. We simply don’t need all the money (and what it can buy) that modern first-world jobs pay, which is why there is so much overpriced junk being

consumed by most consumers and quickly making it into the landfills. We do what those around us do, because it’s easy not to think much. But thinking has its rewards. Looking into the future, there will be a time in which the “limits to growth” mean that people will need to live more frugally. What better prep for those times than engaging now with a dual purpose, so that one’s lifestyle need not take a painful “hit”, when the standard obligations and debt of a “normal life” suddenly become a trap. Looked at in that way, this sort of choice provides the additional benefit of a personal buffer against hard times, and the acquiring of skills and disciplines you’re likely to need during your lifetime anyway. The only way to make this a movement that welcomes such people is to drop the “gene agenda” trappings of social activism: shaming and judging. Nate and DJ have worked in the oil industry, the financial industry and the charity world, and y’know, there are about as many good people, and selfish jerks, in all of them. We’d like to see a movement in which people weren’t chided for not measuring up to the purity judgements of others. That’s pretty much the standard for capuchin-monkey troop dynamics, and we can do better.

Warriors & Mystics

As noted above, in the current situation, reducing your impact just makes you a lesser evil. You’re still part of the problem. Moreover, since perhaps only one in a hundred people seriously tries to do so, any effect is overwhelmed by others using what they don’t through Jevons-like effects reallocated by the capitalist system of wealth and resource distribution. “Living correctly” is a social response, often to signal to others that you are better than they are, and it’s generally socially policed with shaming,

status markers, and a hierarchy of displayed karmic purity which gives good ‘monkey vibes’ but which does little to solve any actual problems. The only way to be a “net good” for the planet and the future is to have more of a positive impact than the negative effect you have on the world. Aesthetically, it’s good to live lightly as a matter of principle, but that doesn’t come close to making one a part of any solution. To do that, one needs to engage at a larger scale than themselves, to make up for not only their own impact but also the hundred or thousand who aren’t doing anything future-altruistic. This means thinking big, acting big, and succeeding. This is quite do-able, but not do-able by trivial effort. It means focus, discipline, intelligence, and perseverance, with the tenacity and fierceness of a warrior combined with a sense of the sacred. We’ve referred to these roles as “Warriors and Mystics”: not “warriors” in a violent sense, but in the sense of being deeply committed to a principle or something one has decided to hold sacred, like a piece of land, a tradition, a wild species, or the wild oceans. Many times in human history, humans have been called upon to be warriors, but never for such stakes as now exist: not just for our own species survival but for the shape of the living systems in which we evolved. The “Mystic” aspect refers to finding a way to motivate ourselves to rational action by harnessing the power of the deep, powerful illogic of our ancient brain structures. This means committing to sacred stories which are by and for our virtual worlds and have personal meaning to us; while staying conscious of the distinction between the virtual and physical worlds. There’s magic in our minds, and there’s physics for the physical world. Without the element of stories which motivate our spirits, we won’t engage; and without a synthesis understanding of the physical world’s rules, we won’t succeed.

How does one get started making a difference without going crazy?

Here’s the big secret: don’t feel like you have to prove yourself to anyone but yourself, but make your own standards high. Do what you reasonably can in your life, and don’t engage in social posturing regarding it; don’t brag or compare yourself to others, keep it to yourself. But beyond that, specialize: choose one big thing that you are going to stand for and affect, over the years of your life, and let that largely define you as you define it. If you worry about “living as though you were poor”, who are you trying to impress? Is your status as a consumer really more important than accomplishing something

original and self-motivated with your one and only life? Don’t answer: that’s a question for your own musing under the stars. In fact, the things which others do to posture may not make as much difference as they think. For instance, buying an electric car has huge environmental impacts from the car’s construction energy footprint. In many parts of the nation, the grid power is from coal, even if there are solar panels on the roof. So that’s not really helping much. However if you owned a cheaper gas car and simply consolidated your impulse trips to one day a week, you’d consume far less primary energy, pollute less, waste less time, take less traffic risk and offer less to others, not need to make high car payments on a fancy new electric, yet still have the benefits of a car. Of course, setting up your work life so you don’t need to commute a long way to a job everyday can help make that possible… and surprise, if you learn to do such stuff, you might not NEED to commute to that far-away job. Modern jobs are often a hamster wheel which you need because of the things you need to keep the job. One can, and should, do logical “first principles” analysis on the life they are living and want to live. To what extent are they just reacting to what they think they are ‘supposed’ to do socially, rather than piloting their own flight path? Ultimately, once your life has been lived, it’s not about your relative karma or purity, it’s about results. What was the net result of your having lived on earth?

Be kind to yourself

Here’s another radical notion: be kind to yourself. Living at this special time with few delusions is a stressful thing, particularly if you’ve pushed back against the “imposed feelings” of your brain’s reward system and are trying to be a decent, sapient person. This doesn’t mean “be self-indulgent”, which is pure gene agenda. It doesn’t mean to be smug and self-superior, which is ditto. It means to cut yourself reasonable slack and know your limits. It doesn’t help the future if you burn out, and most people who start as idealistically hoping to change the future do wind up burned out, long before they get good at it. This happens when one doesn’t pace him or herself, but throws him or herself entirely into an adrenaline-fueled push for quick world change. Since system-steering takes time and a coherent plan, it’s generally a poor match for adrenaline. If you realize from the getgo that “burnout” is the primary failure mode for those wishing to affect the future, it becomes only pragmatic to prevent it, and one of the best ways is – with the context of your high personal standards – to give yourself some license to enjoy life. Spend time with friends, take days off to recharge your soul

in nature, go be with a dog or a horse or wildlife. Try standup comedy, write songs, build a treehouse. Pace yourself, for steering the future is a marathon, not a sprint. How to know the difference between “being kind to oneself” and shirking? You’ll know.

So what about society?

You’ll note that what we have given here are mostly individual recommendations, and not recommendations for society. There are reasons for that. Chief among them is that society doesn’t innovate, it moves in very simple ways, and it has no mind. Society is changed by individuals who become astute in steering it; an art something like training a bear to ride a unicycle. This requires learning realpolitik – what works rather than what we wish would work – but it’s not inherently cynical. It can be pursued successfully to achieve altruistic, pro-future and pro-ecology goals. Clearly, society should quickly wean itself off fossil slaves and stop wrecking the planet’s ecosystems. Just as clearly, it’s not doing that. Despite all the blogging and conferences and slogans, it’s behaving as a relentless, growing heat engine. This growth will be eventually be curtailed by natural limits, but not in time to save the climate, preserve productive seas, or slow the 6th mass extinction. It’s our hope that society will increasingly be influenced by those with a “sapiens” agenda towards future humanity and other life on earth. Since relatively few human minds will ever pierce through the veil of gene-agenda delusion, this means that those who do will have a vital role to play: they must attempt to have a disproportionately large effect on the world. Understanding reality, and their own minds, gives them exactly that option. Society’s goals and methods need to change if the human story is to end well. The way that will happen – if it happens – is through individual minds deciding to curb their fears and unreasonable desires, and make it happen. There are many such minds in existence as you read this sentence, but most of them are yet unaware of what they can accomplish. Bear in mind that although your authors have some ideas and are coming up with new ones each week, we do NOT represent that we know the best things to do, or even that such things are specifically knowable yet. However, we think that logic, ethics, and a look at the science indicates that some courses of action are probably better and more likely to be meaningful than others.

Also bear in mind that there are three relevant stages to come: (1) the remaining peak of energy/wealth/mischief and illusion of permanent growth. (2) the period when bottlenecks become obvious and then become severe, with less energy and physical wealth, high stress, biomass drawdown, and various helter-skelter human reactions. (3) the dawn of the first day after the bottlenecks pass their narrowest, and the millions of years thereafter. Different strategies and ideas will be effective during each, though degrees of freedom are now at their highest, obviously.

In many ways, a hundred thousand years of cultural evolution will occur in this century. Exciting times, dire times, but also perhaps times of greatness, when our species either rises above the agenda of the gene or goes down with it. Some final summary suggestions from your instructor(s) preliminary grab bag: \*\*The future exists as a probability distribution – expand your capacity for uncertainty, change, and hope. There’s no way to know exactly what’s going to happen. We can know a lot about what won’t and can’t happen, but far less about exactly what will, because its future state will be contingent on billions of decisions and actions. You can’t know exactly what you’ll need to deal with, but you will likely see it taking shape before others will. Learn to use your mind creatively, to think on your feet, and to be centered with a sense of who and what you are which isn’t anchored to the aspects of society which may find themselves on shifting sands. Viewing the future as a distribution with shifting probabilities resolves dissonance and allows you to engage, much like treating weather forecasts as a probability distribution has been adopted as the most sensible way of presenting them. \*\*Avoid blaming as much as possible – take ownership and responsibility Blaming is a gene-agenda default, which is a pretty good way of predicting in advance that it won’t make much sense. The problems you will encounter aren’t necessarily anyone’s fault. Bear in mind that you’re far wealthier than the next generation will be, and so on. Appreciate your luck, and let it help you feel humble. You have a lot.

\*\*Embrace life. Play, laugh and live every day. No explanation necessary. Balance is key and having fun and keeping a sense of humor is vital in these times. In any times. Appreciate how amazing life now is. In addition to embracing life, take time to realize that we are flying, communicating, eating, experiencing life in ways that just a century ago would seem like magic. Don’t lose this appreciation of what we have nor take it for granted. \*\*Try to narrow the gap between virtual and physical worlds (but keep a vibrant, healthy, fun, sane virtual world intact) Know your delusions... and celebrate them. The magic, love, and stories of our virtual worlds are what make us human, and make living meaningful and fun to us. But be aware of physical-world reality and make decisions concerning it with your anti-delusion goggles on. \*\*Grieve, when appropriate. We are culturally trained to think displays of grief and sadness are forms of weakness. I disagree (NJ). When my golden retriever died I felt so lost and sad I wouldn’t have wished that feeling on my worst enemy. I cried, at 37 years old, for a solid hour. And felt amazingly better. (Edit: happened again this semester w my next Golden who was too old to bring to class. Same result). These are not normal times – recognizing the 6th mass extinction and some of our social stresses is beyond the worries we evolved to regularly encounter. Grieving is not only acceptable, its appropriate and probably at times required. And talking these things through with a close friend, even if the talk is sad and doesn’t come up with answers, is itself a beneficial healing response. Be kind to yourself and don’t bottle up what are completely normal feelings. \*\*Lean into your fear – There is very little to fear, personally, other than the loss of expectation of things we never needed in the first place. Fear is a big part of the gene agenda, and it got us here, but at a high cost. Living a life free of fear is worth attempting. We all feel it, but we don’t have to be guided by it or motivated by it. We’ll all die someday, and until then we’ll live. It’s the same deal humans – indeed every animal – has always gotten. \*\*Know your inner reptile - Your limbic system is powerful and reptilian. In effect, you’re riding an Orinoco crocodile which has deep ability to influence how you feel and what you do. But those deep-brain regions can only give you feelings and urges and tendencies, they don’t really “think”, and aren’t conscious. As suggested earlier, learn to live with your deep brain, to throw meat to the modules which need it, but don’t let them steer or define you. Become a lifelong student of metacognition, and gradually bring those deep parts of the brain into line with your desired self.

\*\*Practice “Free won’t” - Build neural structures for when tomorrow becomes today (basically, use your knowledge of time bias and Reality 101 to re-shape the relative probabilities of your future responses) It is difficult to make sapient decisions quickly. However, you can alter the odds of how you’ll react to future quick-decision situations by pre-restricting some future choices. We call this “free won’t” to playfully contrast it with the term “free will”. Many of the decisions we make “feel like” they’re free, but they are highly constrained – and often made entirely – by illogical and even unsavory subroutines burned into the structure of our minds since long before humans existed. However, we can consciously steer this by training ourselves to “not do” certain things, and to be mentally rewarded when we do things we have decided to value. This raises the odds that future quick decisions will be more “sapiens” and less “gene agenda.” \*\*Protect your Mind -Make sure to give yourself balance - and engage with these topics and the future - part-time, because full-time will tend to burn you out. (It’s up to you to define what ‘part time’ means) Find a personal retreat from the craziness of the day. This can be a real place, preferably with green things and quiet, but can also be an imaginary place in your mind if you practice – and eventually master the art of meditation, whatever sort “meditation” might work well for you. Nate’s mental ‘favorite place’ looks something like the below:

\*\*Allow 1-2 mental parking spaces at all (or most) times. Cognitive load (the tendency to max out at 7 chunks of information in your brain at the same time) is a real thing. People who worry about money and are addicted to various electronics show much higher levels of stress and do poorer on aptitude tests – not because they are less intelligent but because they have limited cognitive bandwidth left. If your

neural RAM is all filled up with trivia and fluff, there is no space left for the real, beautiful and meaningful. \*\*Educate and inspire others to change our collective cultural definitions of success -Don’t stand by passively while events and people ‘do wrong’. This happened in 1930s Germany and many other places in history. You already know what you care for and mostly what you stand for. Don’t be imprudent about when and where you take your stands, but don’t be silent either. Humans aren’t ‘evil’, but for the last 12,000 years or so we have been following a novel cultural pursuit (surplus). It doesn’t have to always be this way (and probably won’t be). We – each of us - can write a bit of the Towards Homo sapiens movie script. ~ Avoid the error of seeking to blend with a very large tribe if it doesn’t reflect your core values. Communicate clearly with others when that’s a part of your life mission, but don’t slide into the mediocrity of large groups when you have a choice. This probably also means that it’s best not to seek your direction within a large established organization, even one whose mission seems aligned with your principles, for that will likely prove discouraging. ~Pick a species or issue and commit to it as a sacred purpose, as though it were a child of your own, a core goal of the “who am I”. Never quit. Learn from each failure, learn to understand systems and what steers them. Think in 4D. Be outside the mainstream, unthreatening, implacable. Visit your comfort zone to recharge, but exist mostly outside it. ~If you can’t do that, tithe to a friend to can. Learn to economize as an ethic, and work a regular job and give half to them to direct, working in collaboration. Don’t send it to a big-name group to do administration with; take a role in seeing where it is spent. Make their success your own, while living what is otherwise a normal American life based on a somewhat lower income. The way out of Jevons’ paradox is to redirect a portion of your spending to alter the nature of mischief space. This is original, creative, scalable, and at this point in time far more useful than becoming low-consumptive and passive. ~Take it upon yourself to eradicate some destructive activity which is negatively affecting the odds of a decent future outcome for humans or the world in general. It may be small in scale or large, very localized or international. Accept the consequences of doing this, and make your best attempt. ~Continue to learn how reality works and teach others, to increase the number of reality-based tribes. At the moment, there are effectively very few.

~In the spirit of our advice to be 5% alien: Join AAA: be an “altruist alien anthropologist”. Pose as human, and enjoy life as human, but be able to switch in and out of alien-perception mode by wearing Reality glasses and your alter-ego thinking cap. Beneficent aliens are needed but we don’t have any. Become them. The human race badly needs loving gamekeepers who can operate from a detached perspective at least part of the time. ~Be a pro-future, pro-biosphere ‘sleeper’ who springs into action when something happens. Decide which things should not be allowed to go unchallenged in your reality, and react appropriately based on your own values. ~Bend the systems around you to reach a different state than they would otherwise reach. This can be done in thousands of ways. It can be done to save armadillos or burrowing owls, but also pothole repair or Cub Scout funding in your community. The important thing is that you can affect these systems with your mind, make them respond. ~Inspire others by your example, not your ego. Blaze a trail so others will follow. ~Don’t be daunted by a problem’s scale, only by it’s structure. If you can’t tell the difference, study until you can. Engage as necessary. ~Become relatively ego-free something like a monk, but don’t sink into navel contemplation. Instead, take a step into engagement with the 6th extinction and/or the future of those people and things you love. ~Fight hopelessness. At best it’s a counterproductive “gene agenda” heuristic to resolve dissonance. At worst, it’s a mental illness. ~Reclaim the sacred, the special things that define you… that you can feel about with possessive intensity as a part of yourself, no more to be abandoned than a loved one. It becomes your task to see that special thing through an intense storm which can come only once in the planet’s life. ~Don’t try to be perfect on all issues. Be mindful of your impact, managing abhorrence, but focus on your sacred passions, uncompromising. ~Get out of situations in which those around you disagree with what you feel is sacred. Find your clan, or create one. No solution works unless it can get through the coming bottlenecks. The existential malaise and powerlessness you may now feel is

significantly due to the opinions of those around you. Try being surrounded by people you can encourage to try great things, and who wind up doing the same for you. ~Your focus might be a species, a population, a resource for the future, a forest, a special place, a special knowledge, a prevention of burning or destruction. helium. oxygen. A human culture. wombats. hummingbirds. redwoods. amphibians. butterflies. you don’t have to do it all, but you can do one or two things very well, with passion and competence. Get one nuclear warhead dismantled, one fuel rod sequestered, etc. The power will lie in thousands, then tens of thousands, each doing his or her sacred task to make the future probabilities somewhat better. ~In engaging with reality, be a student in the art of what works. Not what you might WISH would work, but what actually works. Learn the nature of human thinking in groups, and how pivotal humans steer groups. It is not immoral to use these dynamics, and it may well be immoral not to. And we come to this: knowing what you know now, you’re the most powerful sort of being ever to have existed: a self-aware mind, in a society which leaves you free to move and innovate with the resources of a culture at the peak of industrialization and energy use. The limits on what you can do are more defined by the consensus trance of social conformity than by physical reality. It took this world billions of years to produce you, and now here you are, against all odds, living a too-brief life at an amazing and perilous time. You can own your aspirations, and decide what your ethical standards should be. There are countless adventures for the highest stakes hiding in plain sight, in our collective blind spots. Learning roughly where the blind spots are is the first step toward self-awareness and away from the gene’s mindless and self-destructive agenda. And once you learn that, you’re free.

I do not live happily or comfortably With the cleverness of our times. The talk is all about computers,

The news is all about bombs and blood. This morning, in the fresh field,

I came upon a hidden nest. It held four warm, speckled eggs.

I touched them. Then went away softly,

Having felt something more wonderful Than all the electricity of New York City.

~Mary Oliver

You were born into a time in which you can make a difference…

NB: these draft materials contain images which have been dropped in as place-holders as we determine what to use and to secure rights. We are primarly using public-domain and original images, but until we have confirmed full

usage rights for all images, these materials should not be widely distributed. They are made available to reviewers for comment and are not in their final form.

Appendix – A Look Ahead

Vital projects hiding in our blind spots That forehead-slapping moment...

In Reality101, the experimental course we’ve created and taught, the participants start out with a welcome skepticism, and are then exposed to not one or two but dozens of ways in which humans have evolved to get stuff wrong. This leads to a fair bit of dissonance: how can the society they grew up in, and even the teachers of their other college courses be mistaken about some basic aspects of human reality? But ultimately there’s a forehead-slapping moment when the sheer weight of converging evidence shows that it’s probably true, and that it has important predictive and explanatory value. That predictive and explanatory value is used in the examples which follow, which spring from one small but important subset of Reality101: our misunderstanding of energy.

A few examples of unaddressed issues springing from a misunderstood energy reality:

Our society misunderstands energy. This misunderstanding has simple explanations, but massive implications for the human future. What follows is a brief overview about the ramifications of some aspects of these misunderstandings. The upshot is that we need to entirely re-prioritize most of what we’re doing. We would all like to see humans continue to thrive for a long time, in significant numbers and with a high quality of life. However, many of our current individual and collective actions are at odds with these general goals. Humans are to a very large extent working on the wrong things in the wrong ways. We’re doing this because we see the future through a distorted lens of unrealistic expectations, based on shared stories about what seem to be trends based on a very special period we have just lived through. If these misunderstandings were easy to see past, there wouldn’t be much of a problem. However, they’ve been incorporated into our cultural stories for so many years that they now seem self-evidently true. The science which could clarify things exists as islands of specialization in a sea of biased storytelling which feels good to believe for quite understandable evolutionary reasons. Moreover, we are evolved to

focus nearly entirely on the near-term, while the real-world limits we’re up against will make themselves felt over many decades in seeming slow motion, and often indirectly. Over the last few years, we (Hagens & White) have created and taught a course on remedial reality - R101 - which points out these systemic errors and constructs the bones of a synthesis worldview with known biases accounted for. This is being well-received, but as a college course it stops short of connecting the obvious dots to what is indicated about the future. This short overview will look at ONLY a few of the most glaring of the implications; yet even those are sufficient to make the point that we’re currently prioritizing the wrong stuff. And it’ll be capped off by a short list of things which we really need to be on top of... and aren’t. This list will seem surreally eclectic, yet all the subjects derive from the same simple physical truths we have collectively overlooked. This is important because we’re facing an imminent new beginning, in which the systems around us will soon begin behaving in very different ways from what we have grown used to and have planned around. This could be an exciting and upbeat time, the beginning of a true “age of sentience” for mankind. It could be humanity’s finest hour... or it could go in other ways. The stakes couldn’t be higher. A few terms from the R101 course which are important to this quick peek are energy primacy, the carbon pulse, the biomass bubble, sustainable carrying capacity, net primary productivity, energetic remoteness, and money as a claim on future net energy. Fortunately for this essay, these are all straightforward concepts. These will lead us to other important concepts, such as the great simplification and receding horizons. By energy primacy, we simply refer to the fact that all life, all commerce, indeed any movement against gravity or inertia, or any generation of order, is an energetic process and is enabled or delimited by available net energy. Humans and their activity don’t fall outside this reality, despite many fanciful stories to the contrary. Energy sets our physical limits. Having understood this basic, it can be shown that nothing about the world we live in today makes sense except in light of the carbon pulse, a very temporary infusion of energy magic and abundant resources made available by the one-time confluence of cheap fossil energy and a virgin planet. Being individually short-lived, we have assumed that the way things have been for the last ten generations (more energy and resources to go around every year) defined permanent new trends. Without understanding what drove and enabled these trends, this has seemed reasonable, even

if it flies in the face of what we know about science. It’s called a “pulse” because of the relative brevity of its roughly 300-year (+/-) duration, set against a human history (and future?) of hundreds of thousands of years. The carbon pulse has in turn caused or enabled many unusual things. Among these, and something you’ve almost certainly never heard of, is the biomass bubble; a concept so obscure in human parlance that your authors had to invent this term to describe it, yet in many ways it’s a vital aspect of current human reality with a large bearing on how this century will play out, quite relevant to many people currently alive. To wit: the terrestrial (land-living) vertebrate biomass of earth is now considerably larger than its historic size, and most of that is the flesh of humans and our livestock. Put another way, that means not only that the sum total of wild terrestrial vertebrates is now an insignificant rounding error to the total, but that the mass of humans and livestock is higher than the total historic mass of all terrestrial vertebrates since before man existed. The age of mastodons and giant sloths and bison as far as the eye could see, bird flocks blotting out the sun. The abundance of walking, flying wildlife - before mankind existed - amounted to a fraction of our current weight. The key enabler: tapping huge amounts of ancient solar productivity, via fossil energy effectively turned into food via industry, rather than relying on solar energy and natural flows to produce food as the earth has through history. The biomass bubble – which includes our own bodies – is thus a part of the carbon pulse. Its exact extent is open to debate: some scientists have suggested that the terrestrial vertebrate biomass is now 5-7 times historic levels. Yet even if it were only 120% of historic levels, that fact would have enormous ramifications for the human story. The term for the ability of the planet to support animal life is defined by the “net primary productivity” via photosynthesis and is referred to as carrying capacity. The natural carrying capacity of earth for terrestrial vertebrates was smaller in the past than the artificially-boosted carrying capacity we have recently created. The increase in the total amount of edible plants made possible during the last century is generally referred to as the green revolution, but it is popularly attributed solely to technology while ignoring the fact that the technology is highly energy-intensive, essentially converting oil and natural gas into delivered edible calories at a large (10:1) energy cost (growing, harvesting, processing, delivering, cooking). (It uses photosynthesis as an intermediate step which adds near-negligible energy – about 9% - to the total). This highlights a vitally important basic error, because while

technology can in principle be permanent, unlimited cheap chemical energy is definitely not. This would be little more than entertaining trivia except for the fact that the carbon pulse IS a brief pulse in human history, with a down side to the curve corresponding to the up side, and it will soon enough be ending. This will, in turn, pop the biomass bubble as the earth reverts back towards a carrying capacity determined by the net primary productivity from natural flows. Inasmuch as this biomass bubble currently consists of humans and their livestock, the imminence and inevitability of its decline has ramifications for every aspect of civilization, society, and human life in general. Moreover, the sustainable carrying capacity isn’t ONLY about energy, but about all other relevant limits which affect it, such as fresh water, soil quality, stable climate, insect life, soil fauna, and much else. This is worth mentioning because all of these aspects of carrying capacity have been, and are being, degraded relative to the way they existed prior to the carbon pulse, so the sustainable carrying capacity we will be reverting to post-pulse will at least initially be a diminished one. Just how diminished will be subject to our actions. The second to last of the handful of concepts we’ll consider in this overview is energetic remoteness. Put simply, we use energy to get stuff and do things. Much of the stuff we get and do things with is non-renewable on the timescale of human existence. Things like metal ores, for instance. Such resources, locked in rocks, exist in a broad range of purity and availability. Being pragmatic, we use the purest and easiest-to-acquire stuff first, and always have. This means that ALL such resources automatically become more energetically remote as time goes by: it takes more energy to acquire and refine a given amount of finished material - copper, steel, phosphorus, tin, niobium, name it - than it used to. And it always will. This very much ALSO applies to things like oil, natural gas and coal, the source materials for fossil energy, and this all has important ramifications our current culture is still blind to. We’ve been blind to it because it was rendered invisible by - what else? - the upside curve of the carbon pulse. Finite resources have been getting more energetically remote for at least 200 years, yet the annual rate of net energy increase from the increased pace of global fossil energy extraction made the trend SEEM to be in the opposite direction. If that’s the case, you might well ask, why does an invisible trend matter? Well, again, it matters because the carbon pulse curve also has a downside, in which there will be LESS net energy available to human societies every year for a very long time, and we’re likely very near the inflection point now. (The institutions we currently rely

upon to report such an inflection point – the UN, IPCC, IEA, etc - have not done so since they’re incorporating economic assumptions which ironically fail to include energy primacy). Oil extraction increased at 6.6% annually from 1880-1974 – since then it has increased at about 1% a year. (Smil, EIA) There remain significant stores of fossil carbon, but most of the coal and oil remaining has much higher costs in money, energy and environmental terms, providing significantly less benefits to society, and in turn, making many of our activities and plans in coming decades more ‘energetically remote’. The last of the R101 concepts we’ll review in this essay is money as a claim on future net energy. We all know money is important, we all want some, but we don’t really know where it comes from or what it actually is. The odds are good you’ve never heard either of these clearly laid out, even if you’ve googled it. (Or majored in economics or business in college). What money IS, in principle, is an abstract claim on future goods and services, based on faith and trust, which boils down to a claim on future net energy. Those claims are the “money” we use to keep the economy going. When we create more of it, we spend it and doing so stimulate our economies to consume faster, which amounts to burning (mostly fossil) energy faster. So it’s a bit like a person taking a stimulant drug. In society we currently refer to this entire process as “growth”. Where most money comes from, these days, is banks making loans. No kidding. Under current banking rules, when a bank makes a loan of a million bucks, that money pops into existence without being deducted from an account anywhere else, other than being added as an IOU on the bank’s balance sheet. This can seem to be a great way of doing things, because there’s a general societal myth that we have thousands - if not millions - of years of increasing net energy and “stuff” available. We don’t. If a person, a nation, or a civilization misses the fact that future net energy is the basis of money, and creates more money than there ever will be future net energy to redeem it, then there can be a helluva lot more claims created on future net energy than can ever be redeemed. This sets the stage for a bunch of dramatic “oops” moments when this overhang eventually corrects itself, as it must do. This essay includes only a small fraction of the concepts from R101, which take months to learn but are necessary backdrop for this essay. Please refer to the R101 course materials – soon to be available online ion the Bottleneck Foundation website – for a more complete description of the “rules of reality” which underpin these summary descriptions. Those materials also offer a synthesis human behavior

overview which explains how and why we and our designated experts have so many durable consensus “blind spots” about reality, even in a technological society. All we’ll presented here are some very basic conclusions, and show how even those basics turn our current priorities on their heads. In short, despite the fine details of the future being unpredictable, certain major things we expect are definitely not gonna happen, and certain major things we don’t expect definitely will. The reasons our societies believe otherwise are rooted deep in our evolution, in the very way we think. We need to plan around actual reality. For the most part we aren’t doing that yet.

So where does this leave us?

So let’s review: \* Energy underlies life, biomass, and economies. Thus, the “carbon pulse” has solely and directly enabled a terrestrial vertebrate “biomass bubble” by diverting millions of years of stored fossil energy into a bolus of food creation and acquisition. This biomass currently consists almost entirely of human and livestock biomass.

As an obvious but seldom-remarked corollary, the carbon pulse has also enabled wealthy human economies, with the greatest wealth going to those which have used the most fossil energy. We have pretty much ignored, denied, and obfuscated the fact that this wealth has been an absolute consequence of cheap net energy, while accelerating the pace of burning by creating ever-more money. \* As a finite depleting resource, the carriers of fossil energy are problematic over the intermediate term. Not only do they disrupt the climate and acidify the seas, but the liquid fuels on which our societies are largely based are quite finite and will begin inexorable - and fairly steep - declines in total availability within decades, followed by similar declines in the other types of fossil energy source materials. \* For the last ~200 years, we have been on the upslope of the carbon pulse, with the overall trend being more net energy recruited continuously year-over-year. We are now using far more oil than we are finding, and drawing down reserves in the process. For roughly the next ~200 years the trend will be less net energy recruited each succeeding year. The inflection point for that change is around now, give or take a few decades. \* As available net energy to humanity declines, it will no longer obscure the

increasing energy remoteness of all finite non-replenishing resources. These two effects together will mean a new trend of decreasing physical wealth for centuries. \* Our societies are largely oblivious to these points due to evolved idiosyncracies and bias in how humans think about the world, and in particular (don’t) think about energy. These are not wild observations. They are direct consequences of the nature of reality, of thermodynamics, and of basic physics; and of tribal minds which evolved to optimize for the Pleistocene. We entirely understand that this leads to conclusions at odds with prevailing narratives, and we understand why: our societies are primarily social and political, with islands of specialized science floating in a sea of popular stories. The essential difference of the paradigm behind R101 is looking at all aspects of reality simultaneously rather than in isolation. Thus it’s not surprising that it leads to conclusions which are disconnected from myth, which will necessarily be different than those which aren’t. (By all means dig more deeply into the R101 materials, or get in touch with us, for a fuller story).

The emergent consequences of these points include:

There will be a “great simplification” in human affairs as the net energy of the carbon pulse winds down. We call it that because the net effects of increasing energetic remoteness and the end of monetary credit additions will be human systems becoming more simple, less complex, and smaller. That doesn’t mean easier, because much of the complexity we’ve invested in is for convenience. This will affect, in profound ways, the ways we are able to live, as individuals, as communities and as nations. (We use the term ‘great’ because the disconnect with todays economy/GDP will be significant – almost certainly a bigger drop than the 15% global decline in the prior ‘great’ event – the global depression in the 1930s). \* There will be receding horizons in terms of the feasibility of energy intensive activities. We define “receding horizons” as the experienced effect of two synergistic trends described above: increasing energy remoteness of most physical things humans use or desire, and decreasing net energy availability. Our societal narratives have generally assumed that everything will be easier in the future, and that people will be wealthier. That will generally not be the case, which is why we call it “receding horizons”: the practicality of all energy-intensive goals will recede, becoming effectively farther away. Diminishing net energy availability will mean that everything with a physical energy footprint is less energetically affordable, and thus there will be less to go around. Things which require a lot of net energy, and are not necessary for our survival, will be the first to go. (for instance, no air conditioning for

the poor... and a lot more poor.) \* The biomass bubble will deflate. The amount of available food for humanity will track the amount of available net energy, because the excess biomass is created or recruited by that net energy. This means that the population of humans and livestock will decline from a high peak somewhere near midcentury, and that decline will probably seem precipitous, declining by many billions by the end of this century. The actual shape and severity of the human decline will be affected by the types of new technologies, by whether most humans become vegetarians or continue to eat livestock, by the stability of climate and rainfall in growing regions, by the availability of such resources as minable phosphate, by the survival or collapse of world fisheries, by wars and many other factors, but the terrestrial biomass must eventually revert to at or below its historic levels. And as noted, the carrying capacity will at least initially be diminished/damaged relative to where it was historically. \* These things will cause all sorts of disruption to human systems. It’s likely that the first way most of us will experience “receding horizons” is when there are abrupt discontinuities in our fiscal systems. The huge debts represented by money and other abstract claims – currently over 300% of GDP in the developed world - can for the most part never be redeemed, and this will play out in some disruptive fashion along the lines which have been seen in the past, and likely in new ways as well. Price volatility, cascading fiscal industry failures, national currency failures, market crashes, recession, depression, devaluation, revaluation, debt jubilees, and whatever else results from national leaders try to get things back on track. An inevitable energy reality will be perceived as an unforeseen money problem. \* Humans don’t generally understand the deep energy-rooted effects which will be causing fiscal chaos, lessening relative wealth, and lessening food supplies in most of their lifetimes. Our human default is to blame such problems on designated “outgroups” of other humans and to politically organize and seek redress from them. Historically, such pressures have led to unstable nations, regime overthrow, the victimization of minorities and the weak, and wars. Though the magnitude of past events – even world wars - has been tiny by comparison with what’s baked into the cake this century. It would be enormously valuable for humans to understand the inevitability of these processes, and that the effects are nobody’s fault in a malign sense. \* The predictable fiscal chaos, the destabilization of nations, and the preconditions for wars of all sizes, will mean that the current assumptions underlying globalization, treaties, and all other existing webs of commerce and political/diplomatic agreement will become untenable. There will thus be periods of hiatus after which

new arrangements may or may not be negotiated. All these effects emanate from a simple misunderstanding of energy. This is a very simplified listing of inevitable and nearly-inevitable high-level effects. But even this shortlist can illustrate why there are some projects we really should undertake, and others which will have been a waste of time and resources, (\*\*it’s not insignificant to note that even if the science pointing to these effects were mistaken - which it manifestly is not - that it would be necessary to curtail fossil energy use voluntarily on much the same time scale, and with the same effects, to prevent cooking the planet and turning the seas to toxic slime, which might spell the actual end of humanity and much else. The reason the effects will be much the same is, once again, that energy is misunderstood, leading to an erroneous narrative that “renewables” will allow us to maintain current civilization and “growth” without fossil energy. They won’t. For details, see R101 section of ‘Rebuildables’.

So what should we be doing differently? Nearly everything.

Even a basic list of necessary re-prioritizations would start with a “top hundred” and “top thousand” projects. Essentially none of these is now getting attention based on a realistic energy understanding. So what does the Bottleneck Foundation suggest in this essay as examples of societal projects which acquire huge importance through the lenses of energy, ecology, and human behavior as it actually exists? What follow are just 10 examples out of thousands which should get immediate attention. - Our Education system is becoming irrelevant for the future we are facing. Primary and secondary education are a product of energy surplus. Paradoxically, they also are one of the few investments that can contribute to ‘future surplus’. In the near straight line up economic growth over the past 3 generations, there was no societal cost for large numbers of students learning esoteric subjects. In a world of energetic limits and overall declines in economic growth, the curriculums in our schools are going to have to change to be more relevant to a source and sink constrained world. We’ll need children and young adults emerging with an understanding of the actual world they will face as opposed to what it was the past 2 generations. We’ll need more focus on science synthesis, understanding our own minds, on ecological principles, and on the skills which will be increasingly needed in a lower-energy-throughput society. We’ll need to transition back to a situation in which a large percentage of people are directly involved in growing and preserving food, and in keeping a desirable society running without the option of wasting of energy and materials. Ideally it’d be good to have grades 1-9 or so be redesigned to teach this stuff, followed

by trade schools which teach specific skillsets. The capitalist basis for going into extreme debt to become highly specialized will likely become irrelevant, though it’s hard to say whether that transition will happen in 5 years or 40. From such an education system, many of the additional ‘projects’ listed below will make sense to a greater number of people - Foundation and large funder priorities are out of whack. The initial funding available to make the future a better place lies largely in the hands of private and public foundations, in networks of philanthropists, and in NGO’s, which are traditionally the innovative “first responders”. Yet currently those demographics don’t understand energy realities, and their directors and boards subscribe to prevailing narrative versions of the “growth” mythologies of neoclassical economics, under which the corpus of their endowments is expected to grow annually forever. (This is simply incorrect; reliable “earned interest” is an emergent result of more net energy available every year, and that will soon reverse itself on the downside of the carbon pulse). Future fiscal dislocations will make the 1’s and 0’s in bank hard drives diminish and ultimately become meaningless, and any unspent charitable funds will have been wasted when they could have done much good. Of course, by planning for a future far different than that which will exist, much of what they have spent to date will also have been wasted. The bad news: Networked philanthropy is not a sector which is characterized by the ability to easily adjust to new facts, being heavily influenced by network consensus inertia, risk avoidance, highly-specialized advisors, perceived sunk costs in the status quo, the tacit assumption of systemic and fiscal stability, and presumed career stability. This synthesis – elementary as it is – is an almost “perfect storm” combination of concepts these players reject out of hand. The good news: In principle, the smarter, braver, less-networked players could take the lead and eventually catalyze a large portion of the rest. They could start with ideas and pilots base some of the remaining examples: - Avoiding giga-starvation this century. As noted, terrestrial biomass is now inflated over historic or sustainable levels by leveraging off fossil energy, principally oil and natural gas. As liquid and later gas fuels become more energetically remote and fiscally expensive, crop biomass and thus animal biomass (human and livestock) will track net energy down. This could lead to the most terrible famines in history, or be managed in a humane way by diverting energy from other uses and by phasing out industrial livestock operations while having extant humans eat the plant calories themselves. If this is to be done, the logistics and prep work should be planned and started well in advance, because it will be the largest humanitarian challenge, for

the largest stakes, in all history - past or future. (Since human populations will never peak as high again for energy reasons). The bad news - if we don’t understand that this is inevitable, we will fail to plan for it. Major organizations like the UN still view the future with money and technology lenses instead of a biophysical accounting. There may be be little empathy for those who starve in foreign lands while there is a perception of diminished wealth at home relative to the recent past. It will be our human default to deal with this through wars and isolationism. The wealthy will not wish to stop eating corn-fed meat as often as they like. We are wired not to believe depressing things even if they are entirely simple and logical. Even now your brain modules are working to disbelieve this. Furthermore, the carrying capacity of the planet – the number of food calories it can create simply from sun and soil - has taken a big hit while we’ve depended on “green revolution” techniques which are ultimately unsustainable. The good news: The huge amounts of energy currently wasted on unnecessary consumer stuff, and the huge amount of food now fed to livestock, could in principle be a buffer which prevents such starvations IF this is handled as a legitimate world crisis with no one ultimately at fault. There are probably a couple decades to politically and logistically prepare for the really deep declines, and for young people to prepare for the humanitarian challenge of the age, saving billions of men, women, and children from the awful fates nature traditionally provides for population overshoot. We are still near the peak of all-time net energy availability, and can direct more of it to preventing foreseeable problems if we choose to. Even if “gigafamine prevention” was the ONLY project to be undertaken, its ramifications would be daunting, because the knock-on effects of billions of starving people boggle the mind. Gigafamines can be largely averted by planning decades in advance for the inevitable. This project – in many ways the largest and most important in human history – has yet to be begun. - Maximizing human life quality this century. In addition to the looming energy-shortage famines of the latter 21st century, there will be a related problem: maintaining human life quality in a vastly changed world. There are many aspects to this, but high on the list would be the problem of global heating and climate change, which takes on somewhat different attributes through non-delusional energy and behavioral lenses. For one thing, the water supplies for traditional farming areas will be directly threatened by CC, from monsoons becoming unreliable to glacial melt diminishing rivers, just as deep aquifers become too energetically remote to rely upon

at recent scales. This is already realized by climate campaigners, except that they don’t realize it will occur against a backdrop of destabilized commerce and fiscal dislocations, if not outright resource wars. Another thing not widely recognized is that the coming generations will not adjust to elevated heat and humidity the way nations now do at the all-time peak of cheap energy availability. We must expect that just as wet-bulb temperature limits make areas near the equator increasingly uninhabitable, the energy grids which now allow people to take refuge will become less stable, less robust, and more expensive. Our progeny will not be using ubiquitous air conditioning in 100 years, they will be migrating away from areas too hot and dry to live in, abandoning what have in the past been some of the most sustainable ecosystems and farming areas on earth and attempting to resettle in vast numbers where other people already live, and to large degree without the benefit of the high-energy lifestyles and infrastructure we have today. Our myths currently have us believing that in the future people will be wealthier and more capable. This is simply false. It’s within our power to make their lives far easier than they’d otherwise be. - Social Stability - The glue which undergirds our physical societies is “more net energy every year than the year before”, which general trend has been true for over 200 years. As this trend ends and then reverses, there is no aspect of society which will not be profoundly affected: fiscal instability and hiatus, rationing and unavailability of staples, the “rolling warehouse” model of asphalt highways and diesel trucks to ship supplies, a loss of faith in the political system, the opportunism of demagogues, the polarization of segments of the populace against other segments, the migration of population within nations and between them. Even those who are luckier than others will “feel like” they have less than they formerly did, and historically this precedes unrest and revolution. Preparing people for the new reality in advance is a priceless advantage to have, but only if it’s acted upon. - CO2 Campaigns. These campaigns are vital, to allow ongoing human habitation of the planet at some level of life quality, and to prevent the seas from undergoing irreversible food web collapse to jellies and bacterial slimes. However, such campaigns must be recast if they aren’t to run up against realities they aren’t expecting. On one hand, the highest levels of IPCC CO2-release scenarios won’t be realized because global economies will be contracting as liquid fuels become far more expensive, somewhat throttling down energy use in parallel with fiscal dislocations. The IPCC bases its scenarios not upon energy availability but upon human demand, in accordance with spurious econometric assumptions which have no biophysical basis. On another hand, though, the entirely fictitious “CO2-sucking machines” predicted by the IPCC as integral to their mitigation strategies are, thermodynamically, simply fantasy constructs and must be treated as such. At the risk of running out of hands, there’s also the fact that the green narrative of 100% or even 70% ‘renewable’ energy

at anything near current throughput levels is not physically achievable with anything like current physical throughput and economic structures. Not only are they intermittent, but the mechanisms involved are in no way renewable; like an automobile they need to be periodically rebuilt from scratch with fossil energy, and at present mostly liquid fuels. Yet a far greater problem is a different blind spot: very few now realize to what extent the wealth of our economies relies directly upon the enormously cheap net energy provided by burning fossil carbon compounds. Even if one sets aside the fact that there has never been a solar panel, wind turbine, hydroelectric dam, or nuclear plant built with anything but fossil energy, and that it is far from clear that they COULD be, the fact is that most actual value created in industrial economies now comes from leveraging off cheap fossil energy (as opposed to human labor). As we remove barrels of oil from our economy, we will remove the underpinnings of physical wealth and need to deal with that. Alternatives can power a good civilization, but not THIS civilization. Lowering CO2 is the existential ecological challenge of humanity’s existence, threatening not just the coming century but tens of thousands of years into the future, so we’d better get our facts straight. Currently, we’re very confused. It will be harder than we think it will be, yet we still need to do it. If we represent it as easy, we’ll crash into the reality that it won’t be - The 6th Mass Extinction. Ecologically, mankind has set into motion what looks set to become only the sixth planetary mass extinction in the last half-billion years (and 9th overall). Our success or failure to limit its effects will determine the nature of life on earth forever, and some of us are aware of this. However, very few have integrated the inevitabilities of energy decline and fiscal dislocations into the picture, which changes the way we should plan. In particular, environmental campaigners and species advocacy is now done by entities which, like others, share the prevailing societal assumptions of neoclassical economics and capitalism, which predict indefinitely increasing wealth in all nations, stable governments, increasingly enlightened and noble populations, increasing globalization, and stable geopolitics, nations, and treaties. This is simply not what is in store. Rather, based on a reality analysis, we should be planning based on pretty much the opposite of those assumptions. Saving species “on paper” via treaties will not survive the fiscal, political, and resource-shortage crises which will characterize the latter two-thirds of this century. “Protected” species near to human population concentrations will be quickly poached and eaten or sold during the first of many inevitable hiatus events. Energetically remote species will tend to get a break as they become too expensive to go after on an industrial basis. In the latter half of the century, pretty much anything living near dense human populations - from trees to birds - will be consumed. Species which are endangered by this reality - even if now extant in significant numbers - will need to be relocated or be lost, even though that will probably be at odds with current laws and treaty rules. The high seas will become more of a conflict zone as fishing

fleets act in a more piratical manner, accompanied with gunboats and using slash-and-burn type technologies. If we would protect biodiversity, we need to plan for this with the advantage of foreknowledge. The tragedy of the ocean commons, and the race to take accessible wildlife, will be a free-for-all if not prepared for well in advance. - Fuel Rods in Swimming Pools. In the USA and in many parts of the world, nuclear reactors exist to create baseload electric power. With a few notable exceptions, they have operated without serious mishap. However, adjacent to the reactors, in what are effectively swimming pools, are huge numbers of irradiated fuel rods which were previously used. Euphemistically termed “spent fuel”, they are far more toxic, radioactive, and dangerous than the fuel rods were when new. In the US, Japan, and some other nations there is nowhere to send them, so they just stack up there, waiting. The number of curies of radiation in the irradiated fuel rods is far greater than that which is inside the operating nuclear plants. Few people have a problem with that, because our societal narratives believe that the future will invariably be one of greater wealth, higher technology, and more energy. If that’s true, then surely those advanced-technology, money-rich, resource-rich, and energy-rich future people will find something to do with them and we needn’t worry. Yet as we have seen, looking at reality based on peaking and declining net energy, big projects will soon start getting harder - not easier - and they will be increasingly hard every year for a very long time. As this happens, there will also be a predictable attrition in terms of how much of our total energy (and thus money) budget we will choose to spend on emergency response teams and infrastructure maintenance in general. This is salient because constant active cooling is the only thing which keeps those swimming pools from evaporating and turning into radioactive volcanoes venting a witch’s brew of waste. The thing which keeps them from evaporating is more cool water being pumped in, continuously. This, in turn, is accomplished by grid power and backup generators. (What happened at Fukushima was not an earthquake causing reactor failure; it was simply a failure of grid power followed by the backup generators running out of fuel.) If we “kick the can down the road” indefinitely on the “spent fuel” pools, we risk entering a time of failing grids and infrastructure; a time in which plant workers will run away and evacuate their families rather than running towards a potential radiation disaster. There are current efforts to secure better power backup for these sites, but crucially these efforts still operated under the assumption of rising prosperity in the future and leave the irradiated fuel rods where they are. Getting the fuel rods out of the swimming pools and into a remote repository is something best done when energy

is cheap, and civilization is well-funded. If we wait too long, we’ll pass the point when experts run towards a potential meltdown of irradiated fuel, and instead run away from it. Every year which passes makes the ability to relocate these time-bombs more energetically remote and unaffordable. - Diesel Trucks go away. If we believe current societal narratives, there will be no shortages of energy or materials with which to continue cheap transportation of anything and everything.
